# Hive Desktop User Guide <a id="hive-desktop-user-guide"></a>

Hive Desktop (Hive for short) is a desktop workspace for coding with AI agents. It runs coding agents (**Claude Code** and **Codex**) for each project you're working on, side by side, and keeps track of what each one is doing, how many tokens it uses, and the skills and MCP servers it has.

## Getting started <a id="getting-started"></a>

1. **Install Hive Desktop.** Download `Hive-Setup-<version>.exe` from the [latest release](https://github.com/Suremise/hive-desktop/releases/latest) and run it: it adds **Hive Desktop** to the Start menu and the desktop. The installer isn't code-signed yet, so Windows SmartScreen may warn you; choose **More info → Run anyway**. From then on Hive keeps itself up to date (see [Updating Hive](#updating-hive)).
2. **Choose your coding agents.** Hive runs the standalone command-line tools of **Claude Code** (Anthropic), **Codex** (OpenAI) and **GitHub Copilot** (GitHub); they aren't included with Hive. A new install starts with all of them turned off: turn on the ones you use in **Settings → Providers** (the banner at the top links there). **Help → Provider Setup…** finds each CLI and offers a one-click install with the official installer if it's missing, and walks you through signing in (and, for Codex, its one-time Windows sandbox setup). The copies inside VS Code (or Cursor) extensions are not used. See [Coding agents](#coding-agents-claude-code-codex-and-github-copilot).
3. **Open or create a workspace** (**File → Open Workspace…** or **New Workspace…**). A workspace is any folder whose subfolders are your projects.
4. **Mark the projects you're working on** with the toggle next to each project.
5. **Add an agent and start it.** A new project has no agents: **New Session** (Ctrl+Shift+N) adds one on the first connection in your order of accounts and starts it, or use **+ Add ▾** in the toolbar above the panes. The agent opens in the Session tab. Switch to another project and start another — sessions keep running in the background.

**Start small, finish a block, start fresh.** Hive runs well on a small plan, such as Claude Pro. Start with one agent; a builder and a reviewer (see [Card loops](#card-loops)) is optional. A lighter model suits routine work for a project's agents (**Agent Settings**), but keep the Assistant on your agents' model and effort (see [Assistant settings](#assistant-settings)). What uses a plan most is long conversations, since every turn sends the whole conversation again. So work in blocks of a few hours, let the lanes finish, then start every session fresh, the Assistant's too (**New Conversation** in its **⋯** menu): it picks up from its journal and the board. To keep an agent's thread, hand it over to itself in a new conversation (see [Long conversations](#long-conversations)).

### Tips <a id="tips"></a>

Hive shows a **tip** about something it can do when it starts, at most one a day, as a quiet pop-up in the same place as the others: **Try it** does it, **Learn more** opens the part of this guide about it, and **Next tip** shows another. A few tips come at the moment they help, once each: the first time a transcript turns amber, the context passes your Compact threshold, you add a second agent, or you paste a screenshot. Tips about things you already do are skipped.

A tip only shows **when nothing else is going on**: no other pop-up showing or waiting, no agent waiting for you, no finishes about to be told, and no key pressed for 10 seconds. Until then it waits, and one that never finds such a moment that day isn't shown (the day's tip is tried again next time Hive starts). So a tip never comes beside the notification it is about, and any notification arriving closes a tip that is showing. It steps aside while you type in a terminal under it. **Don't show tips** turns them off (**Settings → General → Show a tip when Hive starts** turns them back on); **Help → Tips…** lists them all, searchable, either way. Tips aren't kept under the bell: Help → Tips… has them.

With several Hive windows open, they share what the tips remember: a tip seen, the day's tip shown, a moment's tip used or a command you've run in one window counts in all of them, and stays that way after a restart. Another window doesn't show you the same tip again.

### Moving dialogs <a id="moving-dialogs"></a>

Any dialog (Add Agent, Agent Settings, a card, a question…) can be moved out of the way: drag it by its header. It stays inside the window, and **Escape** while dragging puts it back. A dialog opens in the middle again next time. A question over a dialog moves on its own, and Escape closes just the question. The image viewers stay where they are. When a dialog closes, the keyboard goes back to what had it before, such as the button that opened it. Pressing **Enter** to confirm a name (New Mode…, a rename) never presses that button again, so the dialog doesn't reopen.

### Search and filter boxes <a id="search-and-filter-boxes"></a>

Every search and filter box (the board's search, Settings, the Files and Sessions tabs, the sidebar's filters, tables' filters, Tips, Keyboard Shortcuts and the command palette) shows an **×** at its right while it has text: click it, or press **Escape**, to clear it and see everything again; the cursor stays in the box. In a dialog or the command palette, the first Escape clears the box and the next one closes it.

## Coding agents: Claude Code, Codex and GitHub Copilot <a id="coding-agents-claude-code-codex-and-github-copilot"></a>

Hive calls the coding agents it can run **providers**. Each agent in a project chooses its own, so a project can have a Claude Code agent and a Codex agent working side by side. Hive shows each CLI's own terminal, exactly as it looks when you run it yourself.

- **Turning providers on and off.** **Settings → Providers** lists them with their install state. A provider that is off can't start agents; its agents stay listed, greyed out. Turning one off while its agents run asks whether to stop them now or let them finish. There's no default provider: an agent runs the CLI of the connection it chose (see [Accounts and connections](#connections-preview)), and a new agent gets the first connection in your order of accounts that's on here, whose CLI is on and installed. A project with no agents warns only when no coding agent is installed (or none is turned on). Hive's banner, the status bar and Provider Setup flag a missing CLI only once something uses it: a provider that is turned on but not installed, which no agent uses, just shows as "not installed" in the status bar. Just after Hive starts it is still looking for the CLIs, and anything that needs one (Add Agent, New Session, starting the Assistant) waits a moment for the answer rather than guessing. If Hive is still looking after about 20 seconds, it says so and does nothing: try again in a moment.
- **Setup.** **Help → Provider Setup…** has a tab per provider: install, sign in, and updates. Each command or path it shows (an install command, a sign-in command, the fix for a problem it found) has a small copy button beside it that copies exactly that, and says **Copied**. Claude Code signs in with a Claude plan or an Anthropic Console account; Codex with a ChatGPT plan or an OpenAI API key; Copilot with a GitHub account that has a Copilot plan (see [GitHub Copilot](#github-copilot)). On Windows, Codex also needs its sandbox set up once (without it, Codex asks before every command); see [Codex's Windows sandbox](#codexs-windows-sandbox). **No restart after an install:** installers add their folder to your PATH and often say to restart your terminal. A CLI Hive finds where its installer puts it but not on your PATH (Claude Code's own installer can leave `%USERPROFILE%\.local\bin` off it) works the same: Hive adds its folder to the PATH of the agents and terminals it starts, without changing yours. Hive needn't restart: when an install ends (and with **Check again**) it looks again on the PATH Windows gives new programs, shows the CLI installed, and agents you start from then on can use it. Agents, Provider Setup's terminals and **Open External Terminal Here** also get that PATH whenever Hive starts them, so something you installed outside Hive is there too. Agents already running get the new PATH when they restart.
- **Settings per provider.** Each provider has its own page (**Settings → Claude Code**, **→ Codex**, **→ GitHub Copilot**) about the CLI itself: its path, update checks, the fallback lists of models and effort levels, its price table and where costs come from (see [Models, effort levels and prices](#models-effort-levels-and-prices)), and the opt-in for its mode without guardrails (**Enable the Bypass permissions option** and the like), which says whether connections may choose it. Model, effort, context, permission mode and extra arguments aren't here: each connection has its own. **Settings → Claude Code → Allow background sessions** is off by default: in Claude Code, pressing ← on an empty prompt (easy to do while moving through text) opens its agent view and moves the session into Claude Code's background service, where Hive can no longer see or stop it. Hive turns that off for the sessions it starts; `claude` in your own terminals is unaffected. If a session is in the background anyway (you turned the setting on, or moved it there outside Hive), resuming it shows a notification with **Stop It and Resume**, which stops Claude Code's background job and resumes the conversation here.
- **The icons** in the Sessions list show which provider each session uses; for an agent, hover its name in its pane.
- **Conversations stay with their provider.** A Claude Code session can only be resumed by a Claude Code agent, and a Codex session by a Codex agent. To move work to another provider, use **Hand Over to…** (see [Sessions](#sessions)).
- **What both share.** The workspace's Hive skills (those for project agents), its MCP servers and Hive's own `hive` tools reach every agent, whichever provider it uses. Neither CLI loads MCP servers from your user settings in Hive sessions. Hive never changes either CLI's own configuration files.

### GitHub Copilot <a id="github-copilot"></a>

Hive runs the **GitHub Copilot CLI** (the `copilot` command), not Copilot in VS Code. Any Copilot plan includes it, Copilot Free too. Turn it on in **Settings → Providers**, then choose **GitHub Copilot** when you add an agent. Its agents get what the others get: statuses, file locks, the `hive` tools, the workspace's skills, sessions you can resume, usage and cost.

- **Installing.** **Help → Provider Setup… → GitHub Copilot → Install** runs `winget install GitHub.Copilot` in a terminal. Installed with npm (`npm install -g @github/copilot`) works too. Provider Setup shows the version it found and how (WinGet, npm, PATH, or the path in **Settings → GitHub Copilot**). **Update** goes through the same installer to keep its package record consistent. Copilot's own updater can change a WinGet-installed executable without updating WinGet's record. If WinGet then refuses to remove the old portable package, Provider Setup explains the possible cause with the failed update's terminal and offers **Update anyway**. Clicking it retries with `--force`, allowing WinGet to replace changed files; Hive never does this automatically. Stop all Copilot agents first, as for any update. **Check again** shows the installed version afterwards.
- **Signing in.** Signed out, **Sign in** opens `copilot login` in a terminal: it opens GitHub in your browser to authorise Copilot. With `copilot login --device-code`, you enter a code on GitHub instead. Hive never signs in for you.
- **Which sign-in Hive uses.** Provider Setup says who Copilot is signed in as, and how, in the order Copilot itself checks:
  - **`COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN`**: a token you set in your environment for Copilot (a fine-grained token with the "Copilot Requests" permission) comes first. Provider Setup can't tell whose it is.
  - **Copilot login**: your own `copilot login` (the account comes from Copilot's settings; the token stays in Windows Credential Manager).
  - **GitHub CLI**: with no login of its own, Copilot uses the GitHub CLI's (`gh auth login`).

  Copilot would also take `GH_TOKEN` or `GITHUB_TOKEN`, but Hive doesn't pass these to Copilot agents: other tools set them, and they shouldn't sign Copilot in by accident. When Copilot is signed out and one is set, Provider Setup says so. Hive only checks whether a token variable is set, never what it holds. It reads only the account names in Copilot's settings, and never asks the GitHub CLI for its token.
- **Modes.** **Accept edits** (the default) edits files in the agent's own folder (the project, or its worktree) without asking, and asks before other commands, other MCP tools, web access and changes anywhere else. Copilot can't approve edits in a folder whose path has parentheses (`New folder (2)`, `Program Files (x86)`): there an agent in Accept edits asks before each edit, as in Ask, and Hive tells you so when it starts. **Ask** reads files and runs harmless commands without asking, and asks before edits too. **Plan** plans the work and asks before changing anything. **Allow all** approves every tool, path and web request without asking, in the agent's folder and outside it: choose it knowing that. **Autopilot** is Allow all that also carries on by itself until the task is done, without handing back to you: once a turn has run in it, Copilot approves everything for the rest of the session, even after you switch out of it (Copilot says *Permission policy: Allow All for this session*, and Hive's mode badge then shows Allow all). Autopilot appears only after you tick **Settings → GitHub Copilot → Enable the Autopilot option** (turned off, a connection that chose it launches in Copilot's own default mode and says so). In every mode, Copilot uses Hive's own `hive` tools without asking, as Claude Code and Codex agents do (**Settings → Agent API** decides what they may do). See [Permission modes](#permission-modes). **Shift+Tab** in its terminal switches to Plan and Autopilot and back while it runs; switching between Accept edits, Ask and Allow all needs a restart (and after Autopilot, Ask and Accept edits need one too). In Accept edits, Copilot's own footer still says **Manual Approval**: Hive starts it with edits in the agent's folder approved, which Copilot's label doesn't show (the mode badge's tooltip says so too). The mode has taken: Hive's footer shows the mode the agent runs in.
- **Trusting a folder.** The first time Copilot starts in a folder, it asks in its terminal whether you trust the files there. The agent shows as waiting for you until you answer.
- **Instructions.** Copilot reads a project's `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and `.github/copilot-instructions.md`. It also runs the hooks in a project's `.claude/settings.json`, as Claude Code does.
- **Models.** Copilot Free uses **Auto** only (Copilot picks the model). Choosing a model needs a paid plan; Provider Setup reminds you, since Copilot can't tell Hive which plan you have.
- **Cost.** Copilot counts usage in **AI credits** (one credit is $0.01), not tokens. It writes a session's token totals only as it exits, so **Stop** ends a Copilot agent as you would, with Ctrl+C (it takes a second or two), and the Sessions tab keeps its tokens. Stopped mid-turn, it first cancels the turn and waits for it to end, then presses Ctrl+C again once Copilot shows *ctrl+c again to exit* (**Settings → Advanced → Stop: exit hint**). If Copilot doesn't exit, Hive ends it after 10 seconds (**Settings → Advanced → Stop: exit grace**, under GitHub Copilot). Your plan's monthly allowance is in Copilot's `/usage`.
- **"Third-party agents" on Copilot Pro.** Pro's Claude Code and Codex agents are GitHub's cloud agents, which work on issues and pull requests on github.com. They aren't the local CLIs: a Copilot plan doesn't sign in Hive's Claude Code or Codex agents. With Copilot, Hive's Claude and GPT models come through Copilot agents (on a paid plan).

### Git <a id="git"></a>

Worktree agents, merging, the **Git** tab, what's left to merge on **Merge…**, agent templates with worktrees and **Remove All**'s clean-up all use **git**. Install **Git for Windows**, version **2.38 or later** (git-scm.com). **Help → Provider Setup…** shows a **Git** row under every tab: the version Hive found and where, or **Git not found** with how to fix it and a **Git for Windows** button. With an older git, the row says what doesn't work (for example, squash-merged branches still count as unmerged, so their worktrees are kept).

Hive uses the git on its own PATH, together with the PATH Windows gives new programs. If you install git while Hive is open, or add its folder to your PATH, click **Check again**: no need to restart Hive. Where that git is Git for Windows' own (`Git\cmd\git.exe`), Hive starts the git.exe it would start, directly, which saves a process each time; hooks and aliases work as they do in your terminal. While git is missing, the status bar shows **Git** in red, and the screens that need it say *"Git isn't installed (or isn't on Hive's PATH)"* rather than that the project isn't a git repository. Hive never deletes a worktree or branch it can't check with git: they're kept, and it says why. Claude Code also uses Git for Windows' Bash for its commands when it's installed (else PowerShell).

### Tested CLI versions <a id="tested-cli-versions"></a>

Claude Code, Codex and Copilot update on their own schedule, and a new version can change what Hive relies on. Each Hive release says which version of each it was tested with: **Help → Provider Setup…** shows it under the CLI it found, as **Tested with 2.1.287 · installed 2.1.290**.

- **The same version**: a tick.
- **Newer than tested**: "This version came out after this Hive release was tested. Most updates work; if something behaves oddly, report it (Copy Diagnostics)." Nothing is blocked.
- **Older than tested**: "Older than tested; consider updating."
- **Version unknown**: Hive says it couldn't tell which version is installed.

**Help → Copy Diagnostics** lists the tested and installed versions for each CLI, so a bug report says how they compare, and the Hive Assistant can see them too when you ask it about a CLI behaving oddly.

### Models, effort levels and prices <a id="models-effort-levels-and-prices"></a>

Hive asks each CLI which models it has and what each can do, so a new model, or one your plan now includes, shows up without waiting for a Hive update. Claude Code and Codex answer for your installed version and your account (asking uses no tokens, and never starts a session). **Settings → <provider> → Models (fallback)** says where the models come from now, for example *From Claude Code 2.1.289*.

- **Model pickers** list the models the CLI reports; any other model can still be typed as a custom model ID.
- **Effort pickers** offer the levels the chosen model takes: Haiku 4.5 has no effort setting, Haiku 5.5 takes low, medium, high, xhigh and max (as Claude Code reports them), Opus 4.6 stops at Max, GPT-5.5 at Extra high. An effort you chose that the model doesn't take stays chosen and is marked *(not offered with …)*, with a warning; Hive doesn't change it for you.
- **Default effort.** With no effort chosen, the agent's footer and the pickers show the model's own default once the CLI has said what it is, for example *Medium (default)*.
- **When the CLI can't be asked** (not installed, an older version, an unexpected answer), Hive uses the fallback lists in **Settings → <provider>**: **Models (fallback)** and **Effort levels (fallback)**. You can add, remove and rename entries (mark a model **Older** to keep it behind *Show older versions*), and **Reset to defaults** goes back to Hive's own list, which then follows Hive's updates. The lists stay folded while the CLI's answer is in use; **Show the list** opens them.
- **API prices** are Hive's starting prices, labelled with the date they were checked: edit any that are out of date, add a model, remove one, or **Reset to defaults**. Hive never fetches prices. Each table also lists the models the CLI has, so a model a CLI update adds appears on its own, marked **No price: enter one** until you do (its sessions' cost shows as unknown meanwhile, never $0). A model Hive has a price for that the CLI no longer lists stays, greyed and marked *not in <provider>'s list*, for older sessions. Codex doesn't report costs, so its table is the only source of its estimates, and the page links to OpenAI's pricing to check them against.
- **Session costs** (Claude Code and GitHub Copilot, which report each session's cost themselves): **<provider>'s reported cost** (the default) uses the CLI's own figure. It follows the CLI's own price table and updates with the CLI; Hive never recomputes it, even when that table lags a price change. **My prices (the table below)** uses Hive's estimate from the table instead, for your own rates (a discount, another tier). Either way the table is used for sessions with no reported cost. Haiku 5.5's starting price covers prompts up to 100,000 tokens and five-minute cache writes. Longer prompts cost five times as much, and one-hour cache writes ($0.20 per million) are not modelled, so Hive's estimate for such sessions is low. Where Claude Code reports a cost, that figure is the one to trust.

### Accounts and connections <a id="connections-preview"></a>

Every agent, and the Assistant, runs on a **connection**. They're in **Settings → Accounts & Connections**.

- **An account** is where an agent gets its model. It's either a sign-in that Hive keeps in a folder of its own (signed in through the CLI's own sign-in), or a server (below). GitHub Copilot's account is its one sign-in for the whole PC.
- **A connection** is a named setup on an account: its **model**, **effort**, **context**, **permission mode** and **extra arguments**, each left to the CLI when empty. An account can have as many as you like ("Claude · Medium" and "Claude · High" on one Claude sign-in).
- **Agents and the Assistant each choose a connection, and nothing else decides how they launch.** There's no agent, project or CLI setting on top. The CLI follows from the connection.
- **Each workspace chooses which accounts are on**, with **On here**; a connection is usable where its account is on.

**Hive doesn't use your own terminal's sign-in** (`~/.claude`, `~/.codex`), which stays yours as it is. Add an account and sign it in once. Until one is on in the workspace, its CLI's agents and the Assistant can't start, and the project, the Assistant panel and **Help → Provider Setup** each offer to add one. A notice says so once, until you add an account or dismiss it.

**The list.** Each account's row shows:

- its CLI's icon, its name and who it's signed in as (or its server);
- its plan and usage, once per account;
- **On here**, and **Needs set up** with a **Set Up** button when it needs it;
- a **⋯** menu with **Rename**, **Sign In** (or **Sign In Again**), **Set Up** and **Remove**.

Its connections are listed under it. Each shows its name, a summary of its values (**CLI default** for any it leaves to the CLI) and who uses it: agents and the Assistant, in the workspaces open now (hover for their names). Each has a **⋯** menu with **Edit**, **Duplicate** and **Remove**. A connection that something uses can't be removed: the refusal names who uses it. The account's first connection is marked **First**.

- **Add Account…** asks which CLI it's for, its name, and whether it signs in or reaches a server. It comes with its first connection, named after it, which you can edit or add to.
- **Add Connection** under an account, or **Edit** on a connection, opens its dialog: **Name**, **Model**, **Effort** (only the levels the model takes), **Context** (Claude Code: 1M or 200K), **Permission mode** and **Extra arguments**. A server's connection has Model, Permission mode and Extra arguments, but no Effort or Context.
- **Saving a connection changes every agent on it.** Running ones are offered **Restart session**, or **Switch Now** when only the permission mode changed.
- **An effort a model doesn't take** is refused when you save. One that a CLI update leaves is marked on the connection's row and isn't passed to the CLI: edit the connection to choose a level it takes.
- **GitHub Copilot's account** is made by Hive and can't be removed. Its row shows who Copilot is signed in as. Its **⋯** opens **Provider Setup**, where its sign-in is. Like any account, it can have many connections.
- **An agent whose connection is removed, or whose account is off here,** starts on the first connection of its CLI that's usable here, and its pane says so.
- **Updating to 1.0** moved every agent and the Assistant onto a connection holding exactly what they launched with before. Where none matched, Hive made one, named after its account and what differs ("Personal · High · Plan"), and agents set up alike share it. Nothing they launch with changed. A project's own permission mode and extra arguments went into its agents' connections. What a CLI's or the Assistant's settings held before stays, unused, for workspaces you open later.
- **Set Up after signing in.** The first time Claude Code runs on a new account, it asks a few setup questions (its look, its notes); Codex asks to set up its Windows sandbox, which its commands run in. Until that's done, the account's row says **Needs set up**, with a highlighted **Set Up** button, and once it's done it says **Set up**. An account that's on in a workspace also needs the CLI to trust the project folders it would be used in there: the row names those not trusted yet, and **Set Up** opens the CLI where one answer covers the most. For Claude Code that's the workspace's own folder, once, which covers every project in it. Codex never asks: Hive trusts each Codex agent's own folder for that launch, writing nothing to Codex's files (**Settings → Advanced → Codex → Trust Hive's folders on each launch**, on by default). With that off, Codex trusts each project separately, and Set Up opens each project's folder in turn. Set Up says where it opens. Each workspace has its own: the same account can be set up in one and not yet in another. Click **Set Up** (beside **Close** once a sign-in ends, or on the row) to answer them there once, rather than in your first agent's pane. Hive types nothing into it, and tells whether it's done from the CLI's own files.
- **Close the terminal any time.** The Sign In and Set Up terminal always has **Close**: while the CLI in it still runs, Hive asks first, then ends it.
- **Removing an account** deletes its folder and its login on this PC, with its connections; your conversations stay. One whose terminal is open asks to close its terminal first. An account with agents running on it can't be removed until they stop.
- **An account can get its model from a server** instead of a sign-in: in **Add Account…**, choose **Gets its model from: A server**, then a preset (DeepSeek, or Ollama on this PC) or your own base URL and key. Tick the Claude name (Opus, Sonnet, Haiku) each of its models stands in for, or **Read Models** to list what the server has. A conversation that ran only on a server on this PC costs **free (local)**; elsewhere it's priced only if you set a price for that model in Settings → Claude Code → API prices.
  - **Ollama's cloud models** (such as `gpt-oss:120b-cloud`) come through Ollama on this PC but run on ollama.com, so they're marked **cloud** and an account with one isn't free (local). **Read Models** asks Ollama which models these are. A model you type in counts as cloud by its name (a `cloud` tag), so a cloud model copied under another name isn't recognised: read the models from Ollama to be sure.
  - **Context length:** Ollama recommends a context length of 64,000 tokens or more for Claude Code. Set it in Ollama's own settings; Hive doesn't change them.

- **Claude Code saves its notes without asking on an account too.** Each account's conversations folder is a link to Claude Code's own, which made Claude Code ask before every memory it saved. Hive now gives each Claude Code agent on an account its project's memory folder by its real path: the same folder, nothing more allowed, and nothing written to Claude Code's files. **Settings → Advanced → Claude Code, Auto memory by its real path on connections** (on by default) turns it off. Where you name a memory folder yourself (`autoMemoryDirectory` in Claude Code's settings), yours is kept.

- **Codex accounts share their conversations, not their memory.** An agent can carry on a Codex conversation on another Codex account: its transcript is shared. Codex keeps its memories, thread names and prompt history per account. A key or token for Codex set in your environment is never used on an account: each runs on its own login.

#### Codex's sandbox on connections <a id="codex-sandbox-on-connections"></a>

A Codex connection runs its commands in the Windows sandbox you set up for Codex on this PC ([Codex's Windows sandbox](#codexs-windows-sandbox)), unless the connection's own Codex settings choose one. A few things are worth knowing:

- **Codex's sandbox protects writes, not reads of secrets.** A command Codex runs can't change files outside the project's folders, but it can read files: every connection's login file, and those of your own `~/.codex` and `~/.claude`, with either sandbox.
- **Several Codex connections on one PC.** With the default (admin) sandbox, each connection's sandbox setup resets the password of the sandbox's shared accounts, so the other connections ask for Administrator permission again (a known Codex issue, openai/codex #40627). To avoid it, turn on **Settings → Advanced → Codex → Connections: use Codex's non-admin sandbox**. Hive then passes Codex's non-admin sandbox to every Codex it runs on a connection (its agents, its Set Up and its checks), without changing Codex's config files; your own `~/.codex` stays as you set it up. It needs no Administrator permission, but commands run under your own account rather than separate sandbox accounts, and their internet access is blocked by environment settings only, not by the firewall. Writes outside the project's folders are still refused. A connection whose own config chooses a sandbox keeps its own.
- **The first time per project.** The first time a Codex connection works in a project, Codex grants its sandbox permissions over the project's files, once for each connection. On a large project (one with `node_modules`, say) this can take a while, and Codex may seem slow to start its first commands (openai/codex #48593). Hive runs one Set Up per connection at a time.

#### The order of accounts <a id="connection-order"></a>

Accounts have one **order**, for every CLI, the same in every workspace. A new agent gets the first connection of the first account in the order that's on here (whose CLI is on and installed), so turning accounts on and off never means choosing a new default.

- **Reorder** in **Settings → Accounts & Connections** by dragging an account by its handle, or press Alt+Up or Alt+Down on the handle. An account's connections keep their own order under it.
- **Agents already running keep their connection.** A change applies to new agents; the banner about agents running on another connection offers to switch them when their turn is over.
- **A project with no agents** says which CLI its first agent will run (its first connection's), and warns when no account is on here, or when none of the accounts on here has its CLI installed (**Help → Provider Setup** installs one).
- **Updating from an earlier version,** each CLI's order of connections became the order of its accounts, and the default of the workspace you used most recently came first. There's no default provider and no order of providers any more: lists of coding agents are in Hive's order (Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot), even if you reordered them in 0.9.

#### Moving agents at a usage threshold <a id="usage-moves"></a>

An account can move its agents to another account before its plan runs out. On the account's row in **Settings → Accounts & Connections**, tick **When it reaches [98]% of [its weekly limit], move its agents to [account]**, and choose the share, the limit (its weekly limit, its 5-hour limit, or either) and the account to move to (another of the same CLI's).

- **What moves:** every agent on that account in the workspaces open now. The Hive Assistant stays where it is: you choose its connection in **Settings → Assistant**. Each moves to the connection with the same name on the other account, or to its first connection if none has that name.
- **When:** an agent that isn't running moves at once. A running one moves once its turn is over (never in the middle of one) and resumes its conversation on the new connection.
- **Once per limit period, never back:** Hive moves them once each time the limit fills, and doesn't move them back after it resets. To move an agent back, choose its connection in **Agent Settings**; Hive leaves it there until the limit's next period. Choosing an agent's connection yourself while it waits to move also keeps it where you put it.
- **A notice** says who moved and why. If the other account can't take them (it's signed out, has no connections, isn't on in the workspace, or has used as much of its own limit), a notice says so once, and they move as soon as it can.
- **Turning it off for everyone:** **Settings → Advanced → Move agents at a usage threshold**. The rules stay as you set them.
- **The Assistant** sees each account's usage and rule in `hive_connection list`, and can wait for an account to reach a share (`hive_wait_for_tasks` with `usage`). Setting the rule is yours.

## Workspaces and projects <a id="workspaces-and-projects"></a>

A **workspace** is a folder of projects. When you open one, Hive creates a `.hive` folder in it:

```
MyWorkspace/
  .hive/
    shared/       notes, instructions and handovers for every project
    skills/       Hive skills (one folder per skill, Hive's own included)
    mcp/          MCP server definitions (one .json per server)
    tasks/        the task board (one .json per card)
    workspace.json
  ProjectA/
  ProjectB/
```

The workspace `.hive` folder is meant to be **committed** so a team can share skills, MCP servers and notes. Don't put secrets in it.

Each **project** (every subfolder except dot-folders) gets its own `.hive` folder for Hive's metadata: project settings, the session list and transcript backups. Hive adds it to the project's `.git/info/exclude`, so it's never committed and your `.gitignore` is left alone.

### What Hive keeps in a project <a id="what-hive-keeps-in-a-project"></a>

A project's `.hive` folder is this computer's, not the project's: its settings, the session list, transcript backups and archive, attachments (files pasted or dropped into sessions), and each agent's launch folder (the skills, MCP servers and hooks it started with). None of it belongs in commits or shared copies. What could hold a secret isn't kept there at all: the key the hooks use to report an agent's status, and each session's settings and MCP server configuration (which may carry your servers' keys or a `--settings` file's `env`), go in Hive's own data folder instead, for as long as that session runs.

Hive keeps `.hive` out of git by adding it to the repository's `.git/info/exclude`, never your `.gitignore`. It checks again whenever it refreshes the project and before every agent starts, so a repository you create later (`git init`), or one in a folder above the project (a workspace kept in git), is covered too.

Where Hive can't do that, the project's **Overview** and **Project Settings** say so:
- **No git repository**: nothing is excluded yet. If you use another version control system (Mercurial, Subversion and others are named), add `.hive` to its ignore list.
- **A sync service** (OneDrive, Dropbox): it copies the whole project folder, `.hive` included. Exclude `.hive` there if the service lets you, or keep the project in a folder it doesn't sync.
- **A git repository that still doesn't ignore `.hive`**: Hive couldn't write its exclude file, or a rule in a `.gitignore` (a line starting with `!`) brings `.hive` back; Hive asks git itself, so it notices. **Exclude** tries again and says which. Hive never edits your `.gitignore`.
- **Files in `.hive` already committed to git** (with `git add -A` before Hive excluded it, say): excluding only keeps new files out, so git goes on tracking these and committing their changes. The notice says how many, and the command that fixes it: `git rm -r --cached .hive` in the project folder, then commit; the files stay on disk. **Untrack…** does it for you after listing the files, and only for those: if git tracks other files there by the time you confirm, nothing changes and it says so (it stages their removal; you commit it). Hive checks again whenever the repository's index changes, so the notice goes once they're untracked, whoever did it.

The Overview's notice has ✕ to stop showing it for that project; it comes back if the situation changes, and Project Settings always shows it.

### Recent workspaces <a id="recent-workspaces"></a>

**File → Open Recent** and the welcome page list the 12 workspaces you opened last, the most recent first.

- **Remove one** with its **✕** (on hover) or by right-clicking it → **Remove from Recent**. Only the list forgets it: the folder is left alone.
- **File → Open Recent → Clear Recently Opened…** (also in the command palette) forgets them all, after asking; workspaces open in a window stay.
- **A folder that isn't there any more** (moved, deleted, or on a drive that isn't connected) is greyed, with *not found*. It stays in the list, since a drive may come back. Opening it says it can't be found and offers **Remove from Recent**.
- **A workspace open in another window** says *open in another window*; choosing it brings that window forward. The same goes for the folder by another name (through a junction or a link, or a short name such as `C:\Users\RUNNER~1\…`): Hive goes by where the folder really is, so a workspace is never open in two windows, and opening the one a window shows, by any name, keeps it as it is (while Hive is still opening it, once it has opened).

Every window's list follows a change made in another.

### Moving a workspace <a id="moving-a-workspace"></a>

Move the workspace folder (in Explorer, to another drive…) while Hive doesn't have it open, then open it from its new place. A few things are kept by full path and still point to the old folder: agents' worktrees (and git's links to them), the folders sessions ran in, and Claude Code's conversations and memory, which it files under each folder's path in `~/.claude/projects`. Hive notices and says so in a banner, **This workspace was moved from …** (the same folder opened by another name, through a junction or a short name, hasn't moved, and brings no banner). **Repair…** shows what it will do as a table, a group of rows a project: **What** (an agent's worktree or branch, the sessions, Claude Code's conversations and memory), **Was** and **Now** (the part of each path that differs, the full path when you point at it, and copied whole when you copy it), and **Will do**, with a missing worktree's choices at the row's right edge. Claude Code's folders are one row; its arrow lists them one by one. It does it all when you choose **Repair**:

- **Worktrees.** Hive keeps them in a folder beside the workspace (`<workspace>.worktrees`). Move that folder along, or leave it where it was: Repair finds each agent's worktree either way and links it to the moved project again (`git worktree repair`). One it can't find is listed: **Recreate worktree** makes it again on the agent's branch, which is kept in the repository, with its commits (**Create a new worktree** when the branch is gone too); or **Locate…** its folder; or **Remove the agent's worktree link** (the agent then works in the project folder; the worktree's folder, if it turns up, is left as it is). Locate or deal with every missing worktree you mean to keep before recreating another: recreating tidies git's list of missing worktrees, which would lose the links a moved one still needs, so Repair refuses and says which.
- **Slots, review copies and old folders.** Repair finds the project's slots (the working folders Hive lends to cards and lanes, including your agents' own worktrees from before 1.0), its review copies, and reviewers' old folders the same way, and names each in the plan: *slot-b (HI-B1, AM-12)*, *hb2 (carried, HB2)*, *copy-1*, *HR4's old folder*. Each one found is linked again, with its cards' work and its conversations following it. A slot lent to an agent that isn't found offers **Recreate** (made again on its branch, still the agent's), **Locate…** or **Release**; a free slot or a copy that isn't found is simply made again where it belongs the next time it's used. Until Repair has run, Hive doesn't prepare or hand out the moved slots.
- **Branches.** An agent's branch is named after where it was made (`hive/Scratch/juniper/jb1`): after a move or a rename, Repair renames each branch named after the old place (`hive/Tools/dialect/jb1`), and Hive's record of it, in the same step. The worktree follows its branch. A branch that tracks a remote is renamed here only: the remote keeps the old name until you push it under the new one. If a branch with the new name already exists, the agent's branch keeps its name, and the row says so. A card working on the branch follows it to its new name. If Repair can't finish a rename (a file it can't write, say), it says so, and the next Repair finishes it. A branch still named after an old place when nothing moved (renamed outside Hive, say) brings the banner too.
- **Sessions** point to the new folders, so they resume where they ran.
- **Claude Code's conversations and memory** are copied to the folder for the new path, so agents keep what they remembered. The old folders stay, and a file already there is never overwritten (Repair lists it).
- **Open Recent** lists the new folder instead of the old one, and the projects you had marked **Working on** are marked again.
- **How you left its views** follows too: each project's Skills tab (the provider and groups shown), Sessions tree, dismissed notices and its fold in the Hive Assistant's list, and the workspace's panels (whether the Hive Assistant and Progress were open, and their folds and sizes). If you changed one at the new folder in the meantime, yours stays. A project moved on its own into another workspace keeps its views the same way, as that workspace opens.
- **Always on Top** is on again for the workspace's window if you had pinned it, and the output Hive keeps of the workspace's [close command](#close-commands) runs is kept under the new folder. These follow as soon as the moved workspace opens, before Repair; a run still going when you open it follows once it has ended. One that can't be moved (a folder another program holds, say) keeps the banner up, and **Repair…** tries again. If the old folder is still there (you copied the workspace rather than moving it), it keeps both.
- **Microsoft Defender.** When Defender is the active antivirus, a **Microsoft Defender** group lists the old and new folders (the workspace and its worktrees), each with whether Defender scans it (**Excluded**, **Scanned** or **Unknown**, as in [Antivirus scanning](#antivirus-scanning)), and says where that comes from: Defender's list now, or Hive's last check with administrator rights and when it was made. **Repair** waits until Defender has been read. If Hive had excluded the old folders, **Move Hive's exclusions to the new folders** is ticked under the table: Repair then takes those exclusions away and excludes the same new folders instead, with one administrator prompt, after everything else; it reads Defender's list again first, so a new folder an old exclusion of Hive's covered (one moved inside the old folder) is excluded too. Exactly those folders, as **Add Exclusions…** would: never anything broader. An exclusion you made yourself of an old folder is named and left as it is (change it in Windows Security if you want). Untick the box to leave Hive's where they are; Antivirus scanning then lists them as exclusions of folders that are gone, to remove when you like (the same if you decline the administrator prompt). With nothing of Hive's to move and the new folders scanned, the table says so: Antivirus scanning can exclude them. If your organisation manages Defender and it ignores the change, the report says so.

Stop the workspace's agents first: Repair waits until none is running. It never moves or deletes anything at the old place, and running it again repeats nothing it has done. A file it couldn't copy (a folder it can't write to, say) is listed, and the banner stays: Repair tries it again, after a restart too. A file of Claude Code's that is already at the new place with other content is shown and left as it is. A project folder renamed or moved into the workspace on its own is repaired the same way. If the workspace was copied rather than moved, worktrees left beside the original stay with it.

Afterwards Repair says what it did as the same table, a group a project and then the workspace's, with **Result** in place of **Will do**: ✓ done, a problem in the warning colour with its reason, or ⓘ a choice still to make (a worktree not found). Problems come first in each group. Point at a row for its full account, paths whole.

The banner's **✕** hides it for now; **Repair Moved Workspace…** in the command palette opens Repair until it's done.

**Renaming a project.** Rename or move a project folder within the workspace (in Explorer, while no agent of it runs) and its cards go with it: on the board and in **Archived** they show under the new name, with their agents. So do lane records, agents' card and agent watches, and a merge left half done when Hive closed (the warning names the project as it is now), because Hive names a project by an id kept in its own `.hive` folder, not by the folder's name. Watches also come along when you move the whole workspace folder. The project also keeps its handovers (an agent picking up still finds its latest one), its Performance history, its kept comparisons, its Progress runs, and how you left its Skills and Sessions tabs, also when two projects swap names; a new project that takes the old name starts without them. If Hive couldn't move them at once (a file in use, say) and you rename the project again meanwhile, they follow it to its newest name. Hive keeps the projects' names in `.hive/project-names.json` and whose each handover is in `.hive/handover-owners.json`. That holds when two projects swap names, and when a new folder takes the old name: the cards stay with the project they were made for. A copy of a project folder gets an id of its own and starts with no cards. If a project leaves the workspace and another folder takes its name, its cards show as *name: gone*: they aren't the new project's, and you can give them to a project in the card. The first time Hive opens a workspace with ids, it keeps a copy of each card, of `lanes.json` and of `watches.json` it changes beside it (`….before-ids.bak`; since 0.9 these files are kept as they were when they move into the workspace's database, as `….imported-<time>`). Cards saved by Hive 0.5 and earlier, before projects had ids, still name their project's old folder. When a project like that was renamed, the banner says so (*"9 cards of hive-website belong to website"*), and **Repair** gives them to the project they belong to now: the project renamed from that name, or else the one whose agents worked on them. Each card's history says so, and an archived card keeps the date it was archived. Repair also gives that project the handovers and kept comparisons saved under its old name; if it can't, it says so and the next Repair finishes it. An older handover whose project name is now another project's goes to the project whose conversation wrote it; when nothing tells which, it is left out of both rather than guessed.

**A worktree folder that's gone.** If an agent's worktree folder was deleted (after its work was merged, say) or never came along (a workspace copied to another computer), starting the agent offers to **Recreate** the worktree on its branch, with its commits, at the same place; the project's worktree setup command runs first, as for a new worktree. When the branch is gone too, it offers a new worktree on it from the branch it started from, or to remove the agent's worktree link. If the old place can't be used (a drive that isn't connected), the worktree goes where Hive makes new ones, and the agent's sessions follow it.

### Several windows <a id="several-windows"></a>

Like VS Code, Hive can show several workspaces at once, each in its own window: **File → New Window** (Ctrl+K Ctrl+N) opens one on the welcome page, where you open or create a workspace. Each window is a full Hive with its own projects, agents, shared notes, skills and MCP servers; settings, the tray and updates are shared.

- **Open Workspace** replaces the workspace in the current window. If its agents are running, Hive asks first (*Switch workspace?*), then stops them; to keep them running, open the other workspace in a new window instead. If the workspace is already open in another window, that window comes forward instead, so two windows never run agents on the same project. A folder inside a workspace open in another window (or one containing it) can't be opened as a workspace: its projects would belong to both.
- **File → Close Workspace** stops every agent in every project of the workspace, asking first (*Close this workspace?*), and keeps the window open, on the welcome page.
- **Closing a window** (its **X**, or **File → Close Window**, Ctrl+Shift+W) closes its workspace and stops its agents, asking first as quitting does (*Close this window?*). Closing the last window keeps Hive in the tray, or quits Hive (asking first) when **Settings → General → Close to tray** is off.
- **Quitting** (**File → Exit**, Ctrl+Q, or the tray's Quit) stops the agents of every window. With several windows open, the menu says **Exit Hive (all windows)**, and its dialog says it closes them all and lists the agents under their workspaces (two workspaces with the same folder name also show the parent folder that tells them apart; hover a workspace for its full path); **Close this window only** there closes just the window it is shown in, as Close Window does. When Hive starts again, it reopens the windows that were open, each with its workspace, where they were. A window that wouldn't fit on its display (it was last on a larger one, or that display is gone) is made smaller and moved onto the display, as is a new window; only a display smaller than Hive's smallest window (900 × 560) leaves part of it off the display.
- Notifications and the tray menu take you to the window showing the project.
- The title bar names the window's project and workspace, centred. When the window is too narrow for it beside the menus (in a language with long menu names, say), it moves aside and is shortened with "…", or hides, so the menus always stay clear.

### Always on Top <a id="always-on-top"></a>

Keep a Hive window above your other apps, so a test run, an emulator or a browser can work underneath without covering it: click the **pin** in the title bar, just left of the window buttons, or use **View → Always on Top** (Ctrl+Alt+O), or the command palette. The pin is lit, and the menu item checked, while the window stays on top; click it again to turn it off.

- **Each window has its own.** Pinning one window leaves the others as they are.
- **It's remembered with the workspace.** Reopen the workspace, or start Hive again, and its window is on top again. A new window, and a workspace you never pinned, start off; so does the welcome page.
- It's your desk's preference, kept by Hive on this computer, not in the workspace's `.hive` folder: someone else opening the same workspace isn't affected.
- On top means above other apps' windows. Windows' own system screens (sign-in prompts, the Start menu) still come in front.

### Working on a project <a id="working-on-a-project"></a>

The switch next to a project marks it as one you're **working on**. Only active projects run sessions, show live status and send notifications, so a workspace with dozens of projects stays quiet. Turning a project on never starts a session by itself.

Status dots:

| Dot | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Hollow | Active, no session |
| Blue | Session starting / ready for a prompt |
| Pulsing orange | Agent is working |
| Pulsing yellow | Agent needs your input (e.g. a permission prompt) |
| Slow-pulsing, dimmer orange | Agent is waiting on background tasks it started (e.g. a test run) and carries on when they end |
| Blue ring | Agent is waiting for cards on the board to change, and carries on when one does (see [Card loops](#card-loops)) |
| Green | Agent finished its task |
| Violet | **Needs sign-in**: its CLI's sign-in has expired, so it stopped (see [When a sign-in expires](#when-a-sign-in-expires)) |
| Glow | Something happened you haven't looked at yet (it goes once the agent's pane has been on screen) |

A pulsing dot changes shade rather than fading, so it stays easy to see. Hive's grey text (timestamps, counts, hints) and its orange text and icons are drawn to be readable in both the light and dark themes, on hovered and selected rows too (they meet the WCAG AA contrast level).

The sidebar and the lists in the Sessions, Files, Version Control (Changes) and Memory tabs can be made wider or narrower by dragging their right-hand edge, as can the two halves of a split view in the Files tab. Double-click the edge to reset it. Hive remembers the sizes. When the Git tab's Changes has little room (a narrow window, with the sidebar and panels open), its list gets narrower to leave room for what you selected, and with less room still it moves above it, so the changes or the unused worktrees always show.

A project's tabs (Session, Overview, Performance, Tasks and the rest) shrink to **icons** when their names don't fit, as in a narrow window or with the sidebar and panels open; the tab you're on keeps its name. Hover a tab for its name and shortcut. They get their names back once there's room. In a very narrow window the tabs scroll sideways, keeping the one you're on in view.

### Removing a project <a id="removing-a-project"></a>

**Project → Remove Project…** (also in a project's right-click menu and the **⋯** at the top of a project) offers three ways. The dialog lists what each one touches as a table, **What** and **What happens** for the way you pick: running agents (stopped first; their conversations are kept), the folder (by its name; point at it for the full path), the project's handovers, its cards on the task board and its worktrees, each with any work not merged.

- **Hide**: Hive leaves the project out until you restore it. Nothing is moved or deleted; its cards are archived meanwhile.
- **Remove from Hive**: the folder stays on disk, and its handovers and cards are packed into it (`.hive/removed`), with its list of sessions, so you can move it somewhere else, or into another workspace. Wherever Hive sees the folder again, the project shows a banner offering to **Restore** them (or **Discard** them). A worktree can't go with the folder: if one has work that isn't merged yet, merge or discard it first.
- **Delete**: the folder, its worktrees and its handovers go to the Recycle Bin, and its cards are deleted. Type the project's name to confirm. If something has a file in the folder open for a moment (an antivirus scan, say), Hive waits a moment and tries again. If a program keeps it open (a terminal, an editor), Hive says so and deletes nothing: close it, then delete again. Every other delete to the Recycle Bin in Hive tries again the same way.
- **Slots and review copies.** With **Remove from Hive**, Hive removes the project's slots and copies that hold nothing to lose (clean, their work merged), the project's archive command running first, and keeps, listed in the dialog and afterwards with why, a slot with uncommitted files or unmerged commits, a reviewer's old folder, and any folder outside the project's worktrees folder; a slot with work never stops it. With **Delete**, they all go to the Recycle Bin with the project folder, whatever is in them (restore them together and they work again); a slot outside the project's worktrees folder stops Delete, since it would be left without its repository.

The coding agents' own transcripts (in `~/.claude` and `~/.codex`) are never touched. **Settings → Workspace** lists the hidden and removed projects, with **Restore**, which brings each back with its cards (and, for a removed one, its handovers). If a removed project's folder has left the workspace, **Forget** stops listing it.

**Compact project list.** To give the terminals more room while keeping an eye on every project, collapse the Projects sidebar to a narrow rail of status dots: click the **‹** chevron at the top of the list, press **Ctrl+Alt+B**, or drag the sidebar's edge almost all the way left. Each project shows as a tile with its initials and status dot; hover it for the name, status and a table of its agents (each one's state, its card and what it waits for, a builder beside its reviewer), click to open it, right-click for the usual menu. Click **›** at the top of the rail (or press Ctrl+Alt+B again, or drag the edge out) to get the full list back. Shared Notes, Skills and MCP Servers always open at full width.

## Sessions <a id="sessions"></a>

Each agent of a project runs one session at a time (most projects have just one agent; see [Several agents in one project](#several-agents-in-one-project)). A project without agents shows **No agents yet**: New Session and Resume add one for you.

**Where things are.** The project header is about the project, in one quiet row: its name and the state of its agents in one line (*1 waiting · 3 working · 7 idle · 2 stopped*, see [Agent states](#agent-states)), the **Active** switch, **Resume** while any stopped agent has a session to resume (running agents are left alone, except those an expired sign-in stopped, which it tells to carry on; if one can't resume, a notification says which and why, and the others still resume), **Stop** while any runs (it lists them and asks first; Resume and Stop are quiet buttons like Agents ▾, their colour on the icon, and Stop turns red as you point at it), **Agents ▾** for what acts on every agent at once (**Start New**, **Archive and Start New**, and **Remove All…** last), and **⋯** for **Explorer** (reveal the folder), **Terminal** (open an external terminal there), **Project Settings** and **Remove Project…**. Project Settings isn't one of the tabs: open it from **⋯**, **Project → Go to Project Settings** in the menu bar, or **Alt+0**; it shows with no tab chosen, and any tab takes you back.

**The project's tabs** are **Session**, **Overview**, **Performance**, **Tasks**, **Sessions**, **Files**, **Attachments** and **Git**, **Alt+1** to **Alt+8** in that order, then **More ▾**, a menu of **Memory**, **Skills**, **Templates** and **MCP**, and last, after a line, **Project Settings…** (with its shortcut, Alt+0 unless you change it): while one of those is open, More ▾ shows its name (*Skills ▾*). They have no shortcut of their own, but are in the menu bar's **Project** menu (which lists the tabs as the row does, then More ▾'s, then Project Settings) and the command palette, and you can give them one in Settings → Keyboard Shortcuts. In a narrow window the tabs show their icons, the open one keeping its name, with More ▾ always last.

With several agents, each of these says how many agents it acts on: **Stop (3)** stops the three running agents, **Resume (2)** resumes the two stopped ones (hover for the whole sentence: *Resume all 2 stopped agents*), **Start New (6)** and **Archive and Start New (5)** the agents their question lists. When the header runs short of room (a narrow window, the side panels open, or a language with longer words), it folds step by step as soon as the labels no longer fit beside the project's name: first Resume, Stop, Active and Agents ▾ become icons, then Agents ▾'s actions move into **⋯**. Tighter still, **Active** moves into ⋯ too, the agents' states shorten to the most important one (hover for all of them), and last **Resume** and **Stop** join them, so the header always stays one row: the name, its state and ⋯. The count stays beside Stop's and Resume's icons; hover for what each does. The project's name and status are cut with "…" only once the buttons have folded as far as they go. Each question lists its agents as a table: what each is doing, its card, its worktree, and what happens to it (stopped first when it's running, in amber when that interrupts something).

Each action has its own colour wherever it appears, in the project header, the agents' panes and menus, the Assistant and the questions that ask before it: **Stop** is red, **Remove** violet, **Start New** (and **New Session**) green, **Archive and Start New** teal and **Resume** amber.

**Fresh sessions for every agent.** **Start New** gives every agent of the project a new session at once: it lists them and asks once, saying which are in the middle of something (a working agent is interrupted). Running agents are stopped first; their conversations stay in the Sessions tab, to resume later. **Archive and Start New** does the same for every agent that has a session, archiving each one first (as an agent's own **Archive and Start New…** does), so the old transcripts move to the project's `.hive/archive`. If an agent can't start, one notice says which and why; the others still start. Both are also in the **Session** menu and the command palette. Agents are added from **+ Add ▾** in the session toolbar. Everything about one agent is on its own pane, whether a project has one agent or four:

- **The agent header** is kept quiet: its state mark, its name (hover it for its provider), its status and its card, then **Stop** (red) while it runs, or **Resume** (or **New Session** when there is nothing to resume) when it doesn't, and **⋯**. Everything else about the agent is in **⋯** (also a right-click on the header, or **Session → Agent Menu…** and the command palette for the focused agent): **Compact…**, **Archive and Start New…**, **Resume a Session…**, **Hand Over to…**, **Move Left/Right**, **Agent Settings…**, for a worktree agent **Review Changes** (its branch under it) and **Merge…** (what is left to merge under it), and **Remove Agent…**. When something needs a look, a dot on **⋯** says so (amber for a warning; hover for what) and it is at the top of the menu: processes the agent left running, messages it hasn't read yet or didn't ack in time, and the files it is editing this turn. In a narrow pane the status shortens first, then the name (hover for the full text), and in the narrowest the card goes, so Stop and ⋯ always fit. While the conversation is compacting, the status says so until it has finished.
- **The agent footer** is one quiet line, *Opus 5.5 · High · Auto* on the left and *2.4 MB · ctx 42% · $3.70* on the right (the same in the agents' panes, the focus view and the Assistant's panel): its model and effort (click for Agent Settings), its permission mode (click to switch), the transcript's size, the context it uses as a share of the model's context window once the CLI has said (*ctx 42%*; its tokens, *ctx 84k*, until then; hover for the tokens and the rest: the context is the last request's input plus its output, thinking included, which is what the CLI compacts on, so a turn that thinks a lot shows at once; hover for the two parts and where Claude Code compacts by itself: by default about 167K of a 200K window (967K of 1M), or as its settings change it, which the tooltip names: an auto-compact window set with `autoCompactWindow` (also per model), `--autocompact` or `CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW`, an earlier percentage, or auto-compaction turned off (`autoCompactEnabled`, `DISABLE_AUTO_COMPACT`, `DISABLE_COMPACT`), whether those variables come from the environment or from the `env` block of a settings file, and a `--settings` file or JSON in the agent's own arguments (only the settings files Claude Code reads: `--setting-sources` and `--restricted` in the agent's arguments leave some out); Hive reads them when the session starts and never changes them, says it's an estimate when it couldn't read one or can't tell which Claude Code reads (with `--safe-mode` or `--bare`), and calls Claude Code's default an estimate, since managed policies it can't read (Windows registry policies, your organisation's settings) could change it; click to see the session in the Overview, with its compaction history, as its tooltip says) and the session's cost (click it to see the session's details in the Overview: that agent's session, scrolled to). **Warnings appear only when they matter**, in amber: the context with a ⚠ once it passes your Compact threshold, the transcript's size (*⚠ 34 MB*) once it passes **Settings → Sessions → Warn when a transcript is over** (click for Hand Over to…), and *⚠ 1 background task*, just left of the size, while background tasks the agent started are running. In a narrow pane the transcript's size goes first while it isn't a warning (it's in the context's hover too), then the warnings show just their icon to make room, in turn: the background tasks', then the transcript's (hover for the figures); then the permission mode's name shortens, then the model's, and only in a very narrow pane is the line cut off at the right. The session it is running is in its **⋯** (see below).
- **Silent · state unknown**: an agent's status follows its CLI's turns. When Hive has heard nothing of a turn for five minutes and sees no sign that it ended (no word from the CLI, nothing in its own records, no idle prompt on its screen), the header says **Silent 23 min · state unknown** instead of guessing. Nothing is typed into it meanwhile, and the Assistant doesn't give it work. If its terminal shows it waiting at its prompt, click **Mark idle** beside it (also in its **⋯**, and only there in a narrow pane, where a dot on ⋯ says so); anything the agent does next puts it back to **Working…** by itself.
- **Rate-limited (429) · retrying at 15:05 (1 of 5)**: when a Codex agent's model request is refused (most often as too many requests, a rate limit on its account), Codex waits, sometimes for minutes, and tries again by itself, while its own screen still says only *Working*. Once such an agent has been quiet for 20 seconds, Hive looks in Codex's own log for that wait. While it lasts, the header says what was refused and when Codex tries again (**Request failed** for another refusal). Hover it for more, and, when the conversation is large (past your Compact threshold), for a hint that a new conversation sends less with each request. Nothing is needed from you: the next thing the agent does clears it. The Agent API's status message says it too. The log entry Hive reads, and how long it waits before looking, are **Retry wait** in Settings → Advanced. Claude Code and GitHub Copilot agents show the same: Hive reads Claude Code's tries from the end of its transcript (*Rate-limited (429) · retrying at 15:05 (3 of 10)*, or *Can't reach Claude Code* when its service doesn't answer), and Copilot's from the error it reports for each try (*Rate-limited (429) · retrying… (try 2)*: Copilot doesn't say when it tries again or how often). The tooltip says since when it has been retrying. Afterwards, hover the model in the agent's footer: it says how long this session has waited for retries in all, how many waits and when the latest began, so you can tell a slow round from a throttled one; the Hive Assistant reads the same, with the model and effort the agent runs with, from `hive_agent_activity`. If an agent keeps waiting for more than 5 minutes in one turn, Hive tells the Hive Assistant once (**Retry wait: tell the Assistant after** in Settings → Advanced; 0 turns it off). A rate limit (429) means too many requests for now, not that your plan's usage is used up; Hive restarts nothing. If the agent gives up, its turn ends as failed with its own message: after a dropped connection, Hive can resume it once the connection is back (see [When the connection drops](#when-the-connection-drops)), but never after a rate limit, which a resume would only add to.
- **Status unknown: no hooks from Claude Code**: Hive learns what an agent is doing from its CLI's hooks. When they stop arriving (a CLI update changed them, a policy turned them off), Hive says so rather than show a status that may be wrong: no hook within a minute of a Claude Code agent starting, after a prompt its screen shows it working on, or for a turn its CLI's own records show it took (a quick one too, over before Hive looked at its screen; Copilot agents this way). A long think after a prompt never counts, nor a local command. Not covered: a new Codex conversation whose hooks never arrived at all (Hive learns where Codex keeps its records from its hooks) until one of its turns is long enough to be seen working. The agent's mark turns to stalled, nothing is typed into it, a lane it is in pauses ("paused: B6's hooks aren't arriving"), Notifications has it once, and a banner over its pane says why when Hive can tell (its settings turn hooks off with `disableAllHooks`, its CLI is newer than Hive was tested with) with **Restart session**. If it comes back after a restart, update Hive, or check the CLI's settings. The wait is **Hooks missing: wait** in Settings → Advanced.

The status bar keeps what concerns the whole app: the workspace, branch, running agents (hover for a table of them: project, state, card and what each waits for), the agents that need you (see [Agents that need you](#agents-that-need-you)), plan limits, the Agent API, each CLI's version and Hive's updates.

An agent's header, its **⋯** and the **Session** menu let you:

- **New Session** — start fresh. If one is running, Hive stops it first (after asking).
- **Resume** — continue a previous conversation. From your click until the agent is running, the button you clicked shows a spinner and **Resuming…** (**Starting…** for New Session and Start), and the agent's other ways to start are greyed out, so a slow start never looks like a click that did nothing; this holds wherever you start or resume it: its pane, the Sessions tab, the Assistant's panel and the menus. If the prompt cache has expired, Hive shows how many tokens resuming will re-cache and offers **Archive and Start Fresh** instead. A new agent can resume sessions whose agent is gone (for example after the upgrade to 0.2, which starts every project without agents).
- **Stop** — end the session. The conversation is kept and can be resumed.
- **Compact…** (in the agent's **⋯**) — summarise the conversation so far so every later message is cheaper. Available once the agent has finished; the context in the agent's footer turns amber when it passes your threshold (**Settings → Sessions → Suggest compacting above**, 200,000 tokens by default, and overridable per project in Project Settings). The Hive Assistant has its own threshold, since it usually runs a bigger context: **Settings → Assistant → Highlight Compact over**, 500,000 tokens by default. You can add an optional focus ("keep the API decisions, drop the test output"); left empty, the agent decides what to keep. Nothing is lost: the full history stays in the session's transcript. Anything you had half-typed is cleared first; press Ctrl+Y in the session to get it back.
- **Hand Over to…** (agent menu) — hand this agent's work to another agent of the project, which may use another provider, or to the agent itself **in a new conversation** (see [Long conversations](#long-conversations)). Hive asks this agent to write a handover (if it's running and idle; untick to use the latest handover instead), then starts the other agent, or messages it if it's already running, and tells it to read that handover and carry on. Hive waits for a handover from that agent's own conversation, so one another agent writes meanwhile isn't picked up. Every handover an agent writes starts with a header Hive fills in: the project, the **author** (the agent and its CLI, e.g. *Claude (Claude Code)*), the **session** it was written in (to resume it with questions) and the date, in your time with UTC in brackets. The new session shows a **handed over** badge in the Sessions tab that links back. It needs **Settings → Agent API → Provide Hive tools to sessions**. If the other agent's CLI asks something first (for example whether to trust the folder), answer it in its terminal.
- **Archive and Start New…** (in the agent's **⋯** menu, or **Archive Session and Start New…** in the command palette) — archive the current conversation and start a clean one. This is the cheapest way to continue after a long session: the new session starts with an empty context instead of re-caching the old one. Write a handover first (see below) so the new session knows where to pick up.

### Cards in slots <a id="cards-in-slots"></a>

When a project's **Where cards are built** is **Each card in its own folder** (Version Control → Workflow), each card is built on a branch of its own, in a slot:

- **Starting a card** lends its builder a slot, a ready worktree (Hive makes and sets one up while the project has fewer than its builders), and Hive makes the card's branch there from the target's head: `hive/AM/AM-12-fix-the-login` (the project key, the card, and its title in lower case letters, digits and hyphens, at most 40 of them). The builder then works there: once its turn is over, Hive gives it the card in that slot, starting it there in a new conversation if it isn't running there already (its old conversation stays in the Sessions tab). An agent that starts the card itself is told so, and to end its turn.
- **A lane keeps its slot** from card to card. **Start Lane with These Cards…** lends the lane one before its agents start, and their brief names it (*B1 works in slot-b, a new branch per card*); a lane an agent registers gets one at its first card. A lane the Hive Assistant starts for you starts the same way when its agents are stopped: its slot first, then both agents with their brief. An agent already running, working or not, is left as it is: Hive gives a running builder the lane's first card once it is free, and the Assistant tells a running reviewer about the lane. Where cards aren't built in slots, an idle builder instead gets the lane in a new conversation, as **Start Lane** gives it; its last conversation stays in its **Sessions** history. Each next card's branch is made in the same slot, from the target as it is once the card before is Done.
- **A card from before 1.0** gets a slot the same way when you, the Hive Assistant or its agent start it. If a start ever gets no slot in a project that builds in slots, Hive undoes it and says so (*wasn't started: its project builds each card in a slot…*), with the card back where it was, rather than start its agent in the project folder. Start it again. A card already Active with no slot isn't started either: put it back to Todo, then start it.
- **A no-code card takes no slot.** It changes nothing, so it starts where its agent is, even when every slot is in use, and Hive makes no branch for it. In a lane, its builder stays in the lane's slot, and the lane keeps that slot for its next card. A lane whose cards are all no-code takes no slot either: it starts at once, even when every slot is in use, and its agents start where they are (the project folder, usually). If a code card comes up in it later, that card gets the lane a slot as it starts, or waits for one. A no-code card made into code while it's in Doing stays where it started: put it back to Todo and start it again to give it a slot.
- **Every slot in use:** starting a code card is refused with *waiting for a slot*, and a lane waits (*waiting for a slot* in its header in the Progress panel) and starts by itself once a slot is free, also after Hive restarts (with **Settings → Advanced → Workflow rules are enforced** on). If Hive closes while it gets a card's slot ready, it finishes that when it opens again.
- **Slots are brought up to date before each card** (a workflow option): Hive installs the slot's dependencies again when its lock files changed since its last install, as a run from **Hive (slot slot-b)** in the Progress panel, before the builder gets the card. Off, the branch is still made from the target, but nothing is installed.
- **When the card is Done, or put back,** however it got there (merged by Hive or its builder, moved by you or the Hive Assistant), its slot goes back to the project's slots (a lane's when the lane ends or is abandoned; while the lane runs it keeps the slot for its next card). The slot leaves the card's branch: Hive switches it to the target's latest commit, detached, so the branch isn't in use any more. Work committed on the branch stays there. Once its target contains the card's work (a squash merge too), Hive deletes the card's branch and notes it on the card; a branch with work that isn't merged, or checked out somewhere, is never deleted. A slot with **uncommitted changes** stays on the card's branch, and **For you** asks you on the card to commit or discard them; once the slot is clean, Hive detaches it and the question goes, and a lane's next card waits until then. **Settings → Advanced → Delete a card's branch once it's merged** turns that off.
- A project has as many slots as builders (agents whose role is Builder or Either), at most its agent limit.
- An agent with its own worktree from before slots keeps building cards there until its project is moved to slots.
- With **Settings → Advanced → Workflow rules are enforced** off, no slot is lent: a card starts where its agent is.

### Resuming in slots <a id="resuming-in-slots"></a>

A project's **slots** are worktrees Hive lends to a card or a lane, one builder at a time. A builder's new conversation starts in its card's slot (a reviewer's in the project folder, never a slot), with `HIVE_SLOT` and `HIVE_SLOT_PORT_BASE` (the slot's name and its block of ports) in its environment. A conversation always resumes in the folder it was made in, because the CLIs keep conversations by folder:

- **While its card's slot is still its own**, Resume, Resume All and a restart resume it there.
- **When Hive was quit just after a card started**, before the card reached its builder, the next start resumes the builder's last conversation in the card's slot and types the card into it. It starts a new conversation only when there is none there to resume. If that conversation can't resume just now, the card says why and Hive waits; if resuming fails three times, the card says so, and Hive types the card in once you resume the builder yourself. A Resume All that meets Hive resuming it counts it as resumed.
- **When the card ends** (Done, put back, or its lane ends), Hive lets the builder finish its turn, then stops its session in the slot. Its conversation stays in the Sessions tab.
