When its lane ends (its last card merged, or the lane abandoned), the builder is first told the lane is finished, with each card's merge commit, in the turn it is in; once that turn is over and it has read that, Hive ends its CLI the way you would (`/exit` in Claude Code, `/quit` in Codex), never typing over what you have begun to type there, and kills it only if it doesn't exit. After 10 minutes Hive ends it anyway (**Settings → Advanced → Lanes: longest a lane's end waits for its builder**); **Lanes: let the builder finish when its lane ends** turns this off. The lane's reviewer isn't stopped: it simply goes idle. The slot is lent again only once everything that session started has ended: processes it left running that you keep (**Keep Running**), or that Hive can't confirm have ended (after Hive itself closed unexpectedly), keep the slot out of use until they end or you release it.
- **When a slot is held by what a run left running there**, Hive doesn't try the builder again and again. It waits for the slot to be free. The card says once what holds it (*slot-a is held by WindowsSandboxServer, left running by MB1's last run*), the board shows the card as stalled with that reason (*MB1 waits to start: …*), and the agent's pane says **Waiting for its slot** (hover it to see what holds the slot). An agent switching connection when its turn is over waits in the same way, and a notice says so. Either one goes on by itself once those processes end, or once you end them or release the slot.
- **Windows Sandbox's broker** (`WindowsSandboxServer.exe`, Windows' own process, which stays after a Sandbox closes) doesn't hold an agent's slot once no Sandbox is running, nor after Hive quits or restarts. Hive recognises it only by where Windows installs it, and never ends it. After Hive closed unexpectedly, Hive asks the process helper that stayed behind what the session still has running, and frees the slot by itself when that is only the broker. When the helper can't answer (it has gone, or isn't sure), or another Hive held the slot, the slot stays held, and a notice says *slot-a is held by WindowsSandboxServer*. No Sandbox is running, so releasing it is safe: **Release Slot…** in the notice frees it, and leaves the broker running. While a Sandbox still runs, the slot stays held, because folders mapped into the Sandbox can still be written.
- **While that slot is free and unchanged**, only the agent's own **Resume** takes it again (under a new lease). **Resume All** and Hive's start leave it, and say so afterwards (*B1 — resume by hand*), so free slots stay for new work. The agent's next card or lane then carries on in that same slot, so it never holds two; its earlier card's work stays on that card's branch. Only work no branch would keep (changes not committed, or commits on no branch that aren't merged) keeps the slot for it, and the new card gets another slot; the slot is let go once that work is safe.
- **Once the slot holds other work** (*This conversation's slot (slot-a) now holds AM-14's work*), or it changed since, the conversation can't resume there. Hive says why and offers **Read Transcript** (the Sessions tab, on that conversation) and **Start Fresh with a Handover** (Hand Over to… for its agent). Resume All lists it as *slot reused*.
- **An agent that kept its own worktree** (from before slots) and is given a card in another slot builds the card there; its older conversation waits until that card ends (*B1 is on AM-15 in slot-b: resume this after AM-15*).

### Starting fresh <a id="starting-fresh"></a>

A long conversation gets slower and costs more with every message, so a finished piece of work is a good moment for a fresh one. **Start Fresh (Idle Agents)…**, in the project header's **Agents ▾** menu, gives each idle running agent a new conversation in one step. Its old conversation stays in the Sessions tab. First it lists every agent and what happens to it: an idle one starts fresh. One that is working, waiting for you, watching cards, has background tasks running, or still has a card or lane in flight is left as it is, and a stopped one stays stopped. Each is checked again as it starts, so one that started work meanwhile is left alone.

Hive also suggests it by itself: when a project's last lane ends, or (with no lane running) one of its cards is done, and none of its agents is busy and no card waits in Review or Passed, a notice says **A good moment to start fresh**, with **Start Fresh (Idle Agents)**. It comes once each time the work finishes, with or without the Assistant; when an agent marks its last card done while still finishing its turn, it comes as that turn ends. Its button opens the question for that project, whichever project you are looking at. Turn it off in **Settings → Notifications → Suggest a fresh start**, or for one project in Project Settings → Sessions.

### Agent states <a id="agent-states"></a>

Hive sums up what each agent is doing in one of four states, each drawn with its own shape as well as its own colour, so they read apart without colour too:

- **◆ Waiting for you** (a diamond): it asks you something (a permission, a question, a sign-in), or a card it has is blocked on you.
- **■ Stalled** (a square): nothing will move it on by itself: its coding agent failed or failed to start, it has gone silent with its state unknown, it stopped watching the cards it loops on, or it isn't running while its card is in Active.
- **● Working** (a dot that pulses, held still if Windows is set to show fewer animations): a turn, its start, a compaction or background tasks.
- **○ Idle** (a ring): ready, finished, waiting on cards, or stopped.

**A stopped agent's pane looks stopped.** When an agent's session ends, its last output stays in its pane, dimmed (still scrolled, selected and copied as ever), with a bar over its foot saying when it stopped and why: *Stopped · 20:46 · stopped by you* (or by the Assistant, by an agent, its lane ended, its workspace closed, Hive quit, its CLI exited, or exited with a code). **Resume** and **New Session** are on the bar. Its header and its tile in Tiles say *Stopped · 20:46* too. The moment you click Resume or New Session, the dimming and the label go. A pane Hive opens after a restart says only that the agent isn't running.

The project header's state line uses them (*1 waiting · 2 working*), with the mark of the most urgent.

### The session toolbar <a id="the-session-toolbar"></a>

Above the panes, the Session tab's toolbar holds what is about all of the project's agents; each agent's own controls are on its pane's header and footer.

- **Panes | Tiles** switches how the Session tab shows the agents: **Panes** shows each agent's terminal, **Tiles** a small tile per agent, so a whole team fits on one screen. The agents keep running either way. Each project remembers your choice (on this PC, with its layout: not in the project's files, so it is never shared and can't fail to save); **Settings → Sessions → Default session view** sets it for projects that haven't chosen yet (Panes unless you change it). **Switch Between Panes and Tiles** in the command palette does the same.
- **The summary chip** counts the agents in each [state](#agent-states), with the mark of the most urgent (*1 waiting · 2 working · 3 idle · 1 stopped*), the same count as the project header's. Its **▾** lists every agent, most urgent first, with its state, its status and its card: choose one to go to it, on another page too. It works from the keyboard (Enter opens it, ↑ and ↓ move, Enter chooses), and a screen reader announces each agent as you move.
- **The page buttons and the layouts** (Panes only) are on the right: see **Pages** in [Several agents in one project](#several-agents-in-one-project). In Tiles the page buttons are the tile pages: see [The Tiles view](#the-tiles-view).
- **+ Add ▾**: **Configure Agent and Add…** (the Add Agent dialog), **Add Agent** (straight away, with your defaults: Ctrl+Alt+Shift+N), **Add Agent from Template** and **Templates** (see [Agent templates](#agent-templates)).

To rename an agent, change its settings, move it or remove it, use its pane's **⋯** (or right-click its header); drag a pane by its header to rearrange the agents.

### The Tiles view <a id="the-tiles-view"></a>

**Tiles** (the session toolbar's **Panes | Tiles**) shows each agent as a small tile, so a whole team fits on one screen and you can see at a glance who is doing what and who needs you. A tile shows:

- the agent's [state](#agent-states) mark, its name and its provider;
- its status (*Working…*, *Waiting for AM-12 → Review*, *Not running*);
- the card it works on (or reviews);
- its progress, when it reports a long run (a test run, a build);
- the first two lines of its latest reply;
- small badges for background tasks still running, and for the warnings its pane's footer would show (a context past the Compact threshold, a long transcript).

A tile waiting for you has a red border and a diamond; a stalled one has a square and a border in its own colour. The tiles show what Hive already knows, so they cost nothing: no terminal is drawn for them, and no CLI is asked anything.

**Tiles keep one size.** Every tile has room for all of these, shown or not, so tiles don't grow and shrink as runs start and end or replies come in, and nothing moves while you read. Text too long for its line (a card's title, a reply, a status, a run's title) ends in *…*: hover it to read it in full. The badges share one line, the warnings first: when they don't all fit, each is cut short with *…* (the background tasks' badge first, down to its icon), and hovering them lists them all in full.

**A page holds as many tiles as fit the window.** All your agents show on one page when they fit; when they don't (a small window, many agents), a page holds as many as fit, and never fewer than six, as the Panes view's largest page (a window too small for six scrolls), and the page buttons on the toolbar's right show the other pages, and resizing the window pages again, keeping the tile you're on in view (or press Ctrl+Alt+PageDown and Ctrl+Alt+PageUp). **Settings → Sessions → Tile order** chooses the order:

- **Same as panes** (the default): the tiles are in the panes' order, so a builder stays beside its reviewer, and moving an agent moves its tile too. A tile waiting for you or stalled stays where it is, highlighted, and a page button shows how many such tiles another page has.
- **By urgency**: waiting for you and stalled first, then working, then idle, so anything that needs you is on page 1.

**Rearrange the tiles** (Same as panes): drag a tile onto another tile to **swap** them, or onto a page button to move it to that page's last place (hold it over another page's button for a moment and that page shows, to drop it on a tile there). From the keyboard, press **Ctrl+Alt+Shift+Left / Right** on a tile, or open its menu (right-click, or the menu key) for **Move Left** / **Move Right**. The order is the panes' order, so the panes change too, and it is saved with the project. By urgency, Hive sets the order, so tiles don't move.

**Open an agent**: click its tile, or press Enter on it. Its **focus view** shows the agent's own pane, large and fully working (type into it, scroll, select), with the other agents in a strip on the left; choose one there to switch. Each agent's card shows as a small chip at the right of its name, tinted with the colour of the board column the card is in (what it works on, else what it reviews, else a card of its waiting in Review or Approved): hover it for the agent's cards, click it to open the card. Drag the strip's right edge to make it wider or narrower (double-click the edge to reset it); Hive remembers the width. **Reorder the agents** there as on the tiles (with Tile order *Same as panes*): drag one up or down the strip, a line showing where it will go, or press **Alt+Up** or **Alt+Down** on it (its right-click menu has Move Up and Move Down too). The tiles and the panes follow the same order, and the agent you're looking at stays shown. On a narrow window the strip gives way so the agent's terminal keeps room: it takes at most two fifths of the width, narrower than you made it down to its least before the terminal narrows, then is hidden altogether (Esc and **Tiles** still go back), and as wide as you made it again once there is room. To go back to the tiles, press **Esc** (outside the terminal: in it, Esc goes to the agent, as always), **Ctrl+Alt+Esc** (from anywhere, the terminal too: **Back to Tiles**), click **← Tiles** at the top of the strip, or click **Tiles** in the toolbar again. Choosing an agent anywhere else (the summary chip's list, a notification, the board, Ctrl+1…9) opens its focus view too while the project shows Tiles.

From the keyboard: Tab to the tiles, move between them with the arrow keys (Home and End for the first and last), and press Enter to open one.

### Processes agents leave running <a id="processes-agents-leave-running"></a>

An agent can start programs that keep running in the background: a preview server (`npm run serve`), a `tail -f` on a log, a shell. Stopping the agent ends its terminal, but not always what it started, and those can go on holding the project's folder (Windows then won't rename or delete it: "in use") or a port long after the agent has stopped.

Hive keeps track of every process an agent's session starts, however it was started (also through the Microsoft Store's PowerShell 7, whose processes Windows starts outside the session's tracking: Hive takes them back in), and when the agent stops it looks at what is still running:

- **Ask** (the default): a notification lists them (*site left 2 processes running: node serve.js, tail -f capture-1.log*) with **End Them** and **Keep Running**, and the agent's **⋯** gets an amber dot and, at the top of its menu, **2 left running** until you decide or they end by themselves: its menu lists them, with **End All 2** and **Keep Running (Stop Listing)**. **Keep Running** leaves them running and stops listing them.
- **End them**: Hive ends them as the agent stops, without asking.

Choose in **Settings → Agents → Processes left running**. When you quit Hive with some still listed, it asks once whether to end them or leave them running (a quit with no window shown, or to install an update, leaves them running). Those Hive didn't ask about stay listed: when Hive next starts, it lists again the ones still running and offers to end them, checking that each is still the same process.

Agents are also told to stop what they start: to watch a test run with something that ends with it (`hive-progress`, or their CLI's notice that a background command ended) rather than a `tail -f`, and to end the watchers, servers and app copies they started before their card goes to Review, unless the card or you need them running.

Hive only ever ends processes the agent's session started: never one you started yourself in the same folder. If Windows doesn't let Hive end them, they stay listed in the agent's **⋯**, so you can try again or keep them. On a PC where Hive can't keep track of agents' processes (PowerShell blocked by a company policy, for example), agents start as usual, Hive tells you once, and Help → Copy Diagnostics says why; what they leave running then isn't listed. Processes the agent's CLI already ends with its terminal (those attached to it) are never listed. One gap remains with PowerShell 7 installed from the Microsoft Store (Codex runs its commands in PowerShell 7 when you have it): a program it starts and leaves running as it exits within about a second, before Hive has seen it (`Start-Process` returning at once), isn't tracked. Commands Codex leaves running in the background keep their PowerShell open, so they are.

### The Activity tab <a id="the-activity-tab"></a>

**Activity** (Alt+9) shows what the project's agents are running now. Each running agent has a tab across the top, with its state (a pulsing dot while it works) and the card it's on. A tab is a list you read top to bottom: the card it's working on (its key opens the card), or its session when it has no card, on one line: when the title doesn't fit, only it is cut (hover it for the whole title), never the key or the column; then **Now**, its prompt and what it's doing (the tool call under way and how long it has run, or *Thinking…* or *Writing…*); then its **subagents and background tasks**; then the **processes** it started (commands, test runs, dev servers), each with how long it has run. Past work isn't here: a card's comments and history are its story.

- **Frozen when it opens**: the tab is a snapshot of when you opened it, so nothing moves while you read it. A pill counts what changed since (*12 updates · Refresh*). **Refresh** takes a new snapshot. **Play** follows the agents live (a process that ends fades out); **Pause** freezes it again.
- **The agent's own processes** (its CLI and Hive's connection to it) are hidden: tick **Show the agent's own processes** beside **Processes** to see them.
- When a card's builder and reviewer are both on it, the tab shows both, each under its name.
- **Hide finished** (on unless you turn it off) hides the agents with nothing running. **Agents ▾** and **Cards ▾** (tick several, or **All**) narrow the tabs to those agents, or to the agents working on those cards. The tab remembers them for each project.

- Click a row to see its details: its status, its input exactly (a prompt, a tool call's input, a command line, with secrets hidden as in Hive's log) and what it did last.
- **Activity** in an agent's **⋯** menu opens that agent's tab. A card's **Activity** opens Activity across the workspace instead, on the agents working on it (below).
- A process an agent's session started has **End Process…** in its details. It ends only that process, after you confirm, and only while it is still the one listed. The agent's own CLI isn't ended here: use **Stop**.
- **Left running**: a process whose parent has exited while the agent runs (a server started with `&` from a shell that has ended, a search a command left behind) is marked once it has run ten minutes, with how long and its CPU time, and has **End It…** in its details (the same question). Hive says so once in a notice, which opens the agent, and tells the agent once at its next tool call or turn's end, so it can end it itself; Hive doesn't end it. **Settings → Advanced → Processes left running: tell me and the agent** turns off the notice and the agent's line (Activity still marks it). The agent's CLI and what it runs directly (its MCP servers, a command under way) have a living parent, so they never count.
- **Blocked edits** show under **Now** while they stand: *Edit blocked: app.ts is locked by HB3* when Hive's file lock stopped the agent editing a file another agent is editing (or *waiting for your OK* with **Ask me**). The row goes once the other agent finishes and releases the file, the agent tries again or does something else, or its turn ends. Its history is in the agent's **Hooks…** dialog.

### An agent's hooks <a id="agent-hooks"></a>

**Hooks…** in an agent's **⋯** menu (or **Agent Hooks…** in the command palette) shows the hooks its running session has and what Hive received from them. It's for finding out why an agent seems stuck or silent. Hive only shows hooks here: it never changes or turns off a hook, and never edits a coding agent's settings.

- **What's loaded** lists each hook: its event (SessionStart, PreToolUse…), where it comes from (**Hive**, **User**, **Project**, **Managed settings**…, with its file on hover), what it runs (whole on hover, Hive's sign-in details left out) and how sure Hive is that it's loaded:
  - **Passed by Hive**: Hive's own, given to this session when it started.
  - For Codex, the list is Codex's own (asked when the dialog opens), with Codex's trust: **Trusted**, **Not trusted** or **Changed since trusted** (the last two don't run until you review them in Codex).
  - For Claude Code, the list is its settings files as they were when the session started, **Configured · loading unverified** (Claude Code doesn't say which it loaded), or **Turned off** (`disableAllHooks`) or **Not loaded** (managed settings allowing only their own). A file changed since the session started is said so.
  - For Copilot, only Hive's are listed: Copilot may load others Hive doesn't read.
  - A file Hive couldn't read, or that isn't valid JSON, is said so: it never reads as "no hooks".
  - Every hook that isn't Hive's is marked **Not observed by Hive**: Hive never sees when it runs.
- **What fired** lists the last 50 hooks Hive received from the session, newest first, alike ones grouped (*34 × Stop*): when, the event and which of Hive's hooks it came through, **Hive's reply** (*Allowed*, *Blocked* with the reason, *Asked the user*, *Sent context*, *Error* and where) and what Hive **took it as** (*Applied*, or *Ignored* and why, such as a turn that is over). *Hive took* is Hive's own handling time, not how long any hook command ran. It's kept in memory only, and starts again when the session restarts.
- **Refresh** reads it again. If the agent restarted since, the dialog says so rather than showing the old session as current.

#### Activity across the workspace <a id="activity-across-the-workspace"></a>

**Activity** in the activity bar (**Show Activity** in the command palette) is the same view for the whole workspace: every project's running agents together, their tabs grouped by project, with the Hive Assistant as a group of its own while it runs. It works like a project's tab: it opens frozen, **Play** follows it live, and **Agents ▾** (each agent with its project) and **Cards ▾** narrow it. It keeps its own filters, apart from each project's. Its sidebar has **Whole workspace** and a row per project, with how many of its agents run: a project's row opens that project's own Activity tab, which stays as it is.

- **From a card**: a card in **Doing** or **Review**, a project's or the workspace's own, has **Activity** in its dialog and its right-click menu. It opens on the agents working on that card now (its builder, else its reviewer, and any agent whose running session worked on it), with *Card HI-12* above. When nobody works on it any more it says so: the card has its history. The **×** on *Card HI-12* shows every agent again.
- **From the Progress panel**: a run's details have **Show in Activity**, for the agent that ran it, and a lane's right-click menu has **Show in Activity**, for its builder and reviewer.
- It reads nothing while it isn't shown or the window is minimised, and only the projects with an agent running.

### Reading past sessions <a id="reading-past-sessions"></a>

The **Sessions** tab shows every session of the project on the left as a tree, like the Files tab: by **provider** (Claude Code, Codex), then by **agent**, then the sessions themselves, newest first, with when each was last used, its context size and the size of its transcript file (amber past **Settings → Sessions → Warn when a transcript is over**, with a tip to hand it over to a new conversation; **—** when the CLI no longer has the file). Sessions started outside Hive (in VS Code or a terminal) and adopted ones go under **Not from an agent**; started outside Hive, they show as *external*: **Adopt** them to manage them in Hive. A session keeps its own agent: if you remove an agent and add another with the same name, the old sessions stay under **Coder (removed)** (or **Removed agent**, for sessions from before Hive kept agents' names). Each session says where it ran, such as **Project folder** or **Worktree · hive/reviewer** (hover for the full folder), and the **Ran in** line over its transcript names the agent too. **Resume in …** says which agent would resume it now, and its ▾ menu shows where each agent works. Tick **Archived** to include archived sessions. On a busy machine with many sessions, the tab shows Hive's own sessions first, with *Looking for sessions started outside Hive…* above them until the rest are found.

A folder's sessions are found wherever its coding agent filed them, whatever the folder was called when they ran: a project opened through a junction or a link, or by its short 8.3 name (`C:\Users\RUNNER~1\…`), shows the same sessions as by its usual path, and the files its tools touched show by their path in the project.

Every branch shows how many sessions it holds. Branches start folded, except your focused agent's and the way down to the session you're reading; Hive remembers what you open and fold for each project. **Expand All** and **Collapse All** are in the header, and on every branch's menu.

**Sub-sessions.** Some sessions are started by the CLI for another one rather than by you: in **Approve for me**, Codex starts a short **guardian review** to judge each action, and saves each as its own session. They sit under the session that started them, which says how many it has (*3 guardian reviews*), so they don't crowd the list. They can be read like any session, but never resumed. One whose session isn't listed any more stays under its own agent.

**Keys** work as in the Files tab: ↑ and ↓ move (moving onto a session shows it), → opens a branch, ← folds it or goes up to the branch above, Enter folds or opens a branch, F2 renames and Delete deletes.

**Archive or delete a whole branch.** Right-click a provider, an agent or a session with sub-sessions (or use the **⋯** on its row) for **Archive All…**, **Unarchive All** and **Delete All…**. Hive asks once, naming how many sessions and which branch, then archives or deletes each of them. Sessions that are running, or whose files are in use (another program has the transcript or Hive's copy of it open, its CLI is still writing it, Hive is reading it for an export or search, or it's open in another Hive window), are left alone, and the result says which and why: *Deleted 2 sessions; 2 skipped: 1 running, 1 in use*. Try those again once they're free. A session is never half deleted: either all of Hive's copies of it go, or none. If the transcript you're reading is one of them, Hive closes it first and says so.

**Clean up old sessions.** **Clean Up Old Sessions…**, the clean-up button in the list's header beside Refresh, archives or deletes every session of the project that was last active more than **24 hours**, **7 days** or **30 days** ago. Pick the age and **Archive** (the default: Hive's own sessions are hidden and kept, and you can unarchive them) or **Delete** (archived ones too: Hive's copies go to the Recycle Bin, what they used stays in the totals, and each CLI keeps its own transcript). The dialog says how many will go (*2 sessions older than 7 days*) and which it leaves alone: running sessions, the one you're reading, the Hive Assistant's current conversation and any whose files are in use are never touched. While it works, the dialog and the list say how far it has got (*Archiving 120 of 300 sessions…*), as they do for a branch's **Archive All…** or **Delete All…**: archiving a few hundred sessions with large transcripts can take a minute, and the window stays usable meanwhile. The result says what was done and what was skipped, as for a branch. *Last active* is a session's last message, not when it started. The Assistant's **All Conversations** has the same button.

**Sessions you can't resume** show a muted *can't resume* marker, and their **Resume** button is greyed out: hover it to see why. A sub-session isn't a conversation; its provider is turned off or not installed; neither the CLI nor Hive has its transcript any more; or no agent can run it (it ran in a worktree no agent of its provider uses). A conversation always resumes in the provider it ran in, never in another CLI, whatever its agent's connection is now. If its agent was removed, hovering **Resume** says which agent resumes it now.

Select a session to read its transcript on the right, including everything before each compaction. It opens at the latest messages; scroll up to load earlier ones. A running session doesn't update on its own unless you switch on **Follow** (the Session tab always shows the agent working); otherwise click **Refresh**. **Settings → Sessions → Follow running sessions** sets the default.

- Your messages and Claude's replies are shown in full.
- Thinking and each tool call are collapsed to one line (for example "Bash · Run the login tests" or "Edit · src/app.ts"). Click one to see its input and result; very long output is shortened, with **Show all**. The expand button in the toolbar opens or closes them all.
- Codex in code mode runs its tools from a script. A **Script** line says what the script did (*3 hive calls, 1 command, 2 edits*, and how many failed); open it to see each call folded in turn, with its own input and result (and a command's exit code), then the script's code and what it printed. A command the script left running is shown under the script that started it.
- Each compaction is a divider showing whether it was manual or automatic, the context size before and after, and the size of the first request after it (which also carries Claude Code's instructions and tools). Click it to read the summary the agent continued from.
- Slash commands such as `/compact` appear with their output, and pasted images as thumbnails; click one to view it.

The transcript opens at the latest message. For the running session it updates as the agent works, and scrolls with it while you are at the bottom.

**Search** (Ctrl+F in the tab) looks through every session: names, dates, providers and agents, and the transcripts' messages, replies, tool calls and results, and compaction summaries. The tree keeps only the sessions that match, with their branches opened and each session's matches listed under it; the line under the search box counts them. Click a match to open the transcript at that point with the matches highlighted. Clear the search to get the whole tree back.

From the toolbar you can **Copy** a message or reply (hover it), **Export as Markdown** to save the whole conversation as a readable file, **Resume** a session that isn't running (with several agents, **▾** chooses which one continues it), **Show** the agent that is running it, and rename (the tag), archive, delete or adopt it. Rename, delete and **⋯** (the rest) also show when you hover a session in the tree, or right-click it.

**Deleting a session** removes it from Hive: Hive's copies of its transcript go to the Recycle Bin and it no longer shows in the tree. What it used (tokens, cost, prompts) stays in the Overview's totals. A running session has to be stopped first, and one whose files are in use waits until they're free. Claude Code and Codex keep their own copy, so their own resume lists still have it.

### Dates and times <a id="dates-and-times"></a>

Hive shows dates as **2026-10-04** and times as **14:05** by default: sessions named by when they started, the session lists and their tooltips, transcripts, exports and the rest. **Settings → General → Date format** chooses *yyyy-mm-dd*, *dd/mm/yyyy*, *mm/dd/yyyy* or *System* (as your Windows regional format shows them), and **Time format** *24-hour* or *12-hour* (2:05 PM). Names you give sessions stay as you typed them; only the automatic ones follow the format. How long ago a session was last active (*2 hours ago*) stays as it is: hover it for the date and time.

### Language <a id="language"></a>

Hive's window, menus and messages come in eight languages: English, Deutsch (German), Español (Spanish), Français (French), Polski (Polish), Português (Brasil) (Brazilian Portuguese), 日本語 (Japanese) and 简体中文 (Simplified Chinese). All but English were translated by AI.

Hive shows them in the language Windows displays, when Hive has it, else in English. **Settings → General → Language** chooses another: *Automatic* follows Windows (and says which language that gives), then each language by its own name. Languages translated by AI say so after their name, such as *Polski (AI)*, with a note in that language under the list: improvements are welcome. Anything a language hasn't translated yet shows in English, and *Automatic* only picks a language once Hive has translations for it. In any language but English, the setting's title also says *Language*, so you can always find your way back.

**Help → Improve This Translation** opens the guide for translators on GitHub: how to fix a translation, or add a language, with a pull request.

Numbers, sizes, percentages, money and the words in dates (weekdays, months, AM and PM) follow the language too: *1,5 MB* and *pon., 5 paź* in Polish. The date and time formats you chose above stay as they are.

A new language shows at once. A few parts that come from Chromium, the engine Hive is built on (the PDF viewer, date pickers), change when Hive next starts, and the setting says so.

What stays in English: this user guide and the other documents in the Docs view, for now (the Hive Assistant knows Hive well and can answer your questions in your language); what the developer console records (the events it shows and the recordings it saves, like Hive's log); and what Hive tells agents and the Hive Assistant (their instructions, skills, notices and the hive tools' replies), so they work the same whatever your language; they can still answer you in yours. Names you gave things, and what agents wrote, stay as they are. The installer follows Windows' display language too.

**Starting Hive in a language:** `--lang=<code>` (for example `Hive.exe --lang=pl`) starts Hive in that language for one run, whatever the setting says, which is how translators check their work. While Hive runs that way, the setting says it applies from the next start.

### Several agents in one project <a id="several-agents-in-one-project"></a>

A project can have up to 24 agents working at once (its workflow's **Maximum agents** says how many: see **Maximum agents** below), for example one building a feature while another reviews or writes tests. All agents are equal, and any can be removed once stopped. No agent has a worktree of its own: a builder works in one of the project's [slots](#cards-in-slots) while it builds a card, and in the project folder otherwise (see **Where each agent works** below). **+ Add ▾** in the session toolbar has **Configure Agent and Add…**, which opens **Add Agent…**, and **Add Agent from Template** (see [Agent templates](#agent-templates)). Its **Add Agent** adds one straight away, on the first connection that can be used here (the first account on in this workspace, in the order of **Settings → Accounts & Connections**), working in the project folder; so does **Ctrl+Alt+Shift+N** (**Add Agent** in the command palette, or right-click the project). In **Add Agent…** you choose:

- **Name** — "Agent 2" by default; rename it any time in **Agent Settings…** (in its pane's **⋯**). **Names are unique across the workspace**, so an agent is never confused with another project's on the board, in notifications or by the Assistant: a name another agent already has is refused, and Hive offers one with your project's initials in front (B1 in a project called *hive* becomes **HB1**; a word gets the project after it, *Builder (hive)*). Agents that shared a name from before keep working: Hive says so once, with a button to rename one. Its **Note** (Agent Settings) says what it is for in your own words; templates save it (see [Agent templates](#agent-templates)).
- **Role in the workflow** — **Builder**, **Reviewer**, **Either** or **Generalist** (see [Role in the workflow](#role-in-the-workflow)).
- **Where it works** — there is nothing to choose: an agent starts in the project folder, and a builder takes one of the project's slots for each card it builds and works there until the card is done (**Version Control → Branches & remotes** lists them). Agents from before 1.0 that had their own worktree keep it as their slot (*carried*) once the project is upgraded.
- **Connection** — the setup it runs on: a model, effort, context and permission mode on one of your accounts (see [Accounts and connections](#connections-preview)), such as *Claude · High* for a builder and *Work* (a Codex account) for a reviewer. The list groups them by account and offers only accounts that are on in this workspace; the first in the order is chosen to start with. Its CLI follows from it: there is no provider, model or effort to choose on the agent itself. **New Connection…** beside it makes another setup on the chosen connection's account (Name, Model, Effort, Context, Permission mode, Extra arguments) and chooses it. Under the list, a line sums up what the chosen connection sets (*Opus 5.5 · High · Auto*). With no account on in the workspace yet, the list says so with **Open Accounts & Connections**, and **Start a session now** is unticked and greyed out (*No account is on here yet*): the agent can still be added, to start once an account is on.
- **Note** and **MCP servers** — what it is for, in your words, and which of the project's MCP servers it gets (see [Giving a server to some agents only](#giving-a-server-to-some-agents-only)).

**Agent Settings…** (its pane's **⋯**) changes its name, note, role, connection and MCP servers. Choosing another connection on the same CLI applies when the agent next starts. While it runs, its footer names the connection it started on, and its rows mark it ⟳ when you've chosen another since, even one on the same account. A connection of another CLI can be chosen only while the agent is stopped, after asking: its conversations stay with the CLI they ran in (**Resume** then picks up its new CLI's last conversation; the earlier ones still resume from the Sessions tab). To change what a connection sets (say, High effort for every builder on it), edit the connection in Settings → Accounts & Connections: every agent on it follows. **If an agent's connection is removed, or its account is off in this workspace** (a project opened from another machine), it says so in its pane's footer (a ⚠ beside its connection; hover for why), a notice says which connection it started on instead, and it runs on its CLI's first usable connection until you choose another; it can't start only when no account of its CLI is on here.

**Layouts.** The layout follows as you add agents: two columns for two, three for three, a 2×2 grid for four, a 3×2 grid for five or six. The buttons at the right of the agent row choose one yourself: one at a time (click an agent to switch), two columns, three columns, a grid of four or a grid of six. A layout you choose stays as agents are added; choosing the one that shows all the agents makes it follow them again. Three columns and the grids work best on a wide window, or with the sidebar hidden (Ctrl+B). When the panes would be too narrow for a terminal (a narrow window with the sidebar and the Progress panel open, say), the layout shows fewer columns for now: three columns become two, a grid of six a grid of four, and two columns or a grid of four one at a time, with the pages to match. Your choice stays as it is (its button still shows it, and its tooltip says what is shown meanwhile), and the panes go back to it once there is room again (a little more than they need, so dragging the window's edge across the point doesn't make them flicker).

**Pages.** The project has one layout, and a page holds as many agents as it has panes, so every agent on a page has its own pane: with four agents in three columns, page 1 shows three and page 2 the fourth; six agents in a grid of four are two pages (4 and 2). Left automatic, the layout shows every agent up to a 3×2 grid, and a seventh opens page 2. **One at a time** has no pages: the summary chip's list chooses who is shown. The numbered buttons next to the layouts switch pages, or press Ctrl+Alt+PageDown / Ctrl+Alt+PageUp; a dot on a page's button shows its most urgent agent (one waiting for you, say), and its tooltip says which agents are there. With more than six pages (more than 12 agents in two columns), they become **‹ 3 / 12 ▾ ›**: ‹ and › go to the previous and next page, and the middle lists every page with its agents. Changing the layout re-pages at once. When the toolbar is short of room, the layout buttons become one button with a menu, then the page buttons become **3 / 6**, a menu of the pages; tighter still, **+ Add** shows only its **+** and **Panes | Tiles** become icons (point at one for its name), and the agents' counts are shortened last. Choosing an agent on another page in the summary chip's list goes there. Each agent runs its own copy of its CLI, so Hive notes the memory use when you add the seventh. Each pane has its own header with the agent's status and buttons; click a pane to make it the **focused** agent. The buttons in the project header, the Session menu, pasting screenshots and **Insert into Session** act on the focused agent. Ctrl+Alt+] and Ctrl+Alt+[ move between agents, across pages. With **One at a time**, choosing an agent in the summary chip's list shows it.

**Maximum agents.** Your team size is part of the project's workflow: **Maximum agents**, first in **Version Control → Workflow**, under **Team** (for every project in the [Hive and workspace defaults](#workflow-defaults)). The presets set it: **Solo** 1, **Pair** 2, **Team** and **All off** 12. Nobody adds agents past it, you included: at the maximum, **+ Add ▾** and the other ways to add one are greyed out, saying why, and the quick add (**Ctrl+Alt+Shift+N**) warns you with **Open Workflow Options**, which takes you there to raise it (up to 24). So asking the Hive Assistant (or a program using the Agent API) to "get this done fast" never brings in agents you didn't plan for: at the maximum it tells you, and you raise it if you want more. Change it as you change the other options: the form marks it and lights **Save…** as you type or step the number, and **Discard Changes** puts it back. A [template](#agent-templates) loaded with its workflow raises it by itself. Each agent runs its own copy of its CLI and they all share your plans' usage limits, so above 12 the option shows what that costs on your computer: the memory your running agents take now, and how much your computer has free. Lowering it removes no agents: the option says the ones you have stay. Before 1.0 this was **Agents per project** in Settings → Advanced: your value moved here.

**Arranging agents.** Any agent can go in any pane of the layout, and panes can be left empty. Drag an agent's pane by its header onto another agent's pane to **swap** them, running or not, or onto an empty pane to **move** it there (its own pane is left empty). To put it on another page, hold the dragged agent over that page's button for a moment: the page shows, and you drop it on a pane there; or drop it on the page button itself to move it to that page's first empty pane (a full page's button takes no drop). An empty pane says *Drop an agent here*, and its **Move Agent Here ▾** lists the agents on other pages to bring one in. From the keyboard, **Move Left** / **Move Right** in the agent's menu, or **Ctrl+Alt+Shift+Left / Right** for the focused agent, move its pane to the next pane (across pages at the ends), and in a grid **Move Up** / **Move Down** (**Ctrl+Alt+Shift+Up / Down**) to the pane above or below; a screen reader says where it went. Where you put the panes is kept **for each layout**, with the project, and survives a restart: switching from a grid of six to two columns and back puts them where you left them in the grid. A layout you haven't arranged shows the agents in order, and the order follows your arrangement as it reads (page by page, left to right, top to bottom), so the Tiles view's *Same as panes* order matches it. A new agent takes the first empty pane, else a new page. Arranging the panes of an automatic layout makes it yours: it no longer changes as agents are added. **One at a time** has no panes to arrange; there **Move Left** / **Move Right** change the order. In the Tiles view, drag tiles ([The Tiles view](#the-tiles-view)). Nothing is stopped or restarted.

**Where each agent works.** Beside each agent's name on its pane's header: **in slot-b** while it builds a card in a slot (click it for **Version Control → Branches & remotes**), **carried: hb2** in the slot kept for it (its own worktree from before 1.0), **home: hr4** for a reviewer resumed in its retired home, or **0.9 worktree: hb1** for an agent whose worktree from before 1.0 hasn't been moved to a slot yet; nothing in the project folder. In a narrow pane it shortens with "…" once the status has given way, before the name does. A stopped agent's pane says the same in words (*Works in hb2, the slot kept for it.*); a reviewer whose last conversation is in its retired home says that a new conversation starts in the project folder and **Resume** opens the last one there. Its name's hover gives the folder's path, the project's hover lists it in a **Slot** column, and **Agent Settings** shows it under **Works in**. The **Files** tab's folder picker opens the folder an agent works in, and the **Sessions** tab names each session's folder (**Slot · slot-b**, **Home · hr4**, a worktree, or **Project folder**).

**Which conversation each agent has.** Each agent's **⋯** has **Open in Sessions Tab**, with the name of the session it is running and when it started under it, and **Rename Session…**. Until a session has a name, it is named by when it started, e.g. *2026-10-04 14:05*, and keeps that name when you resume it (in your date and time format: **Settings → General → Date format** and **Time format**). You can rename a session in Hive (the agent's ⋯ or the Sessions tab) or in Claude Code with `/rename`: whichever you did last is the name Hive shows, and resuming the session keeps it. A conversation can only be open in one agent at a time. **Resume** reopens the agent's own last session and is greyed out when it has none. Its **▾** lists the recent sessions from the agent's folder: pick one to continue it in this agent, including a paused conversation another agent in the same folder started. Sessions that are open in another agent are greyed out; clicking one takes you to that agent. Agents in their own worktree only see that worktree's sessions.

The sidebar keeps one status dot per project, showing the most urgent agent (needs input, then working…), with the number of running agents next to the name. The project header's state line counts the agents in each state, most urgent first, with the most urgent one's mark (for example *1 waiting · 2 working · 3 idle · 2 stopped*: the working and idle agents are the ones **Stop** counts, and agents with no session running are counted as stopped); hover it for each agent's state. Notifications name the agent, e.g. "hive · Agent 2 finished".

**Background tasks.** An agent can start something and end its turn while it runs, such as a long test run, or subagents working in the background (Claude Code's Agent tool, Codex's spawned agents). Claude Code is told when the task ends and carries on by itself, so Hive shows the agent as **waiting on background tasks** ("Waiting on 1 background task") rather than finished, and only says it has finished (chime and notification) once it really has. A task the agent stops itself doesn't wake it: if that was the last one, the agent is finished then. Meanwhile the Assistant waits for it and won't give it a new task, and quitting asks first, since the task would stop. Codex isn't told when its background terminals or subagents end, so a Codex agent shows as finished with the count next to it ("Finished · 1 background task running"). Hive can't tell a test run from something that never ends, such as a dev server, so it stops counting a task after an hour; change that in **Settings → Agents → Count background tasks for up to**.

**File locks.** Agents sharing the project folder could otherwise edit the same file at the same time. When an agent edits a file, Hive notes that it is working on it; if another agent then tries to edit that file, Hive tells it to wait or work on something else, and it carries on with other work. The claim ends when the first agent finishes its task. The files an agent holds are listed in its pane's **⋯**, named by their path in the agent's folder, even when the folder is reached by another name (a junction, or a short name such as `C:\Users\RUNNER~1\…`). The workflow option **File locks** chooses what happens (in the project's **Version Control → Workflow**, under **Where cards are built**, or **The work** without version control; inherited from the workspace's and Hive's [Hive and workspace defaults](#workflow-defaults)): **Block** (default), **Ask me** (you approve the edit; Codex can't show its own approval for this, so Hive holds the edit back and shows a notification with **Allow**, and Codex waits until you allow it; Hive tells it to go ahead as soon as it has finished replying and you aren't typing in its terminal. **Allow** stays on the notification after Codex stops to wait, until you answer or the other agent finishes with the file; then Hive tells Codex to try again itself), **Warn** (the edit goes ahead, the agent is told), or **Off**. Locks cover the agents' file edits (a Codex patch counts even when Codex runs it as a shell command), not other shell commands (formatters, `npm install`, `git checkout`), and not files agents share outside the project such as the CLIs' own settings or memory — keep that in mind when several agents work in one folder. Agents in their own worktrees never get in each other's way.

If git can't read the changes, the **Git** tab's **Changes** says why, with **Retry**. The same goes for every list and search: if one can't be read, Hive says so with **Retry** rather than showing nothing, and if a refresh fails the last results stay, with a note saying when they're from. They are always the same project's (or workspace's): switching to another project never shows the last one's.

**Reviewing and merging a worktree agent's work.** This is for an agent still in its own worktree from before 1.0 (until the project is moved to slots); a card's work in a slot is merged through the card. In the **Git** tab's **Changes** and the **Files** tab, the selector at the top switches between the project folder and each such agent's worktree. For a worktree, Changes lists everything the agent changed since its branch started (its commits and uncommitted edits). **Open file**, above a diff, opens that folder's copy of the file: the worktree's, not the project's. When it is done, choose **Merge** (pane header, agent menu or Changes tab):

- Only committed work is merged. If the worktree has uncommitted files, the dialog says how many and **Merge** stays off: commit or stash them in the worktree (or ask the agent to), then merge.
- The branch is merged into the branch checked out in the project folder: **Merge** (keeps the agent's commits and their messages, plus a merge commit) or **Squash** (one commit, with the message you enter). The dialog starts from the project's own merge style (Version Control → Workflow): Squash when it squashes, a merge commit otherwise.
- If the same files changed on both sides, nothing is merged. Hive lists the files and can send an agent in the project folder instructions to do the merge and resolve the conflicts, or copy them for you.
- **Remove the worktree and branch afterwards** (and the agent) is off by default: an agent can keep its worktree and work on task after task, merging as it goes. Tick it when its work is finished (stop the agent first).
- **Squash** with the worktree kept offers **Move the branch to main afterwards** (ticked). The squash commit holds all the branch's work, so the branch is moved there and the agent's next merge brings only what is new. Without it, the branch keeps its old commits and its next merge may conflict with its own earlier work. Hive only moves it when nothing would be lost, and tells you if it couldn't.

**Working with one agent for a while.** With **Merge** and the worktree kept, an agent can do a task and merge it, then the next task and merge again, or several tasks and one merge. When the project folder's branch moves on (another agent's work was merged), ask the agent to merge it into its branch before its next task, so it works on current code.

**One merge at a time: the merge slot.** When several agents finish close together, each merging main into its branch, checking it and merging, one agent's merge can land while another is still checking, and that one has to merge and check again. So agents take turns: an agent claims the project's **merge slot** for the branch it merges into, merges main in, runs its checks, merges, and releases it. The others wait in order, and their status reads *Waiting for the merge slot (B4 is merging #305)*. While an agent holds the slot, nothing else merges into that branch through Hive, so its checks run once. Agents do this by themselves when they merge (the **merge-ready** skill).
- **Who holds it and who waits** shows in the project's **Overview**, on each agent's status, and as a card at the top of the **Progress panel** with the branch going in and the step its checks are on (see [Merges and lanes](#merges-and-lanes)).
- **It is never stuck.** The slot is released when its agent's session ends, and a hold lasts 60 minutes (longer while the agent keeps reporting progress, or when it claims again). A hold that runs out goes to the next in line, and Hive tells you, since that merge may not have finished. **Release** beside a holder (in the Overview or the Progress panel) frees it yourself, after a question; if the slot changed hands while you answered, nothing is released and Hive says so. If Hive closed while an agent held the slot, it tells you when the workspace opens again, naming the folder the merge was in (the agent's worktree, or the project's main checkout), so you can check it for a merge left half done. After an unexpected close, that is part of the notice that Hive closed unexpectedly (see [When Hive closed unexpectedly](#when-hive-closed-unexpectedly)).
- **Your own merges** take the slot too: while an agent holds it or waits for it, the **Merge** dialog says who is merging and waits until the slot is free. Merges made outside Hive (in a terminal) aren't controlled; agents still check that main hasn't moved before they merge.
- **Two Hives, one repository.** When another Hive on this computer (a dev or test copy beside the installed one) is merging into the same repository, a merge, pull or branch switch waits for it, and after a moment Hive says so: *Another Hive is merging into this repository (Hive-Dev)*. If it is still merging after 30 seconds, nothing is changed and Hive says which Hive holds it: try again once it has finished. A Hive that closed or crashed while merging doesn't hold the repository up. Change the wait in **Settings → Advanced → Merges: wait for another Hive**.

**A worktree agent**'s branch, folder, the branch it started from and what's left to merge are in its name's hover, in its pane's header. Its **⋯** has its branch under **Review Changes**.

**Work not merged yet** shows on the agent: **Merge…** in its **⋯** says what is left ("2 commits not merged into main · 1 uncommitted file"), and so does its name's hover. Hive checks when the agent finishes a turn or stops, when you come back to Hive's window, after a merge, and every minute while something is left to merge. **Merge…** is greyed out while the agent is in the middle of a task (working, asking you something, or waiting on background tasks it started), because it may still be committing to its branch; the menu says why under it: merge once it has finished. When the agent's branch is a card's that Hive merges through its merge queue (the card's own rules, with the workflow rules on), the Merge dialog says so and where the card stands (not queued yet, waiting with its place, being tested), and **Merge** is off: the queue merges it after its checks. Merge also stays off while Hive is still reading this, and if it can't (the dialog says so, with **Retry**). **Merge anyway…** merges it here all the same, after asking; it is recorded on the card as an override, and the queue takes the card out once it sees the merge.

**Remove Agent** asks whether to keep a worktree agent's worktree and branch or delete them; **Discard** deletes them straight away. Their sessions stay in the Sessions tab, labelled with the agent and branch.

**Remove every agent at once.** **Remove All (4)…** in the project header's **Agents ▾** (also **Session → Remove All Agents (4)…** and the command palette) removes all of the project's agents after one question. It lists them in a table, says which are working (running agents are stopped first) and, for each worktree agent, whether its worktree is *merged and clean* or *always kept* (with the reason, such as "2 commits not merged into main"). Worktrees and branches are kept unless you tick **Also delete the worktrees and branches fully merged into main with no uncommitted changes**: then only those are deleted; anything not merged or not committed is always kept. If the agents have open cards, you're asked once what to do with them. Their sessions stay in the Sessions tab. If an agent can't be removed, one notice says which and why, and the others still go. In a narrow window it moves into the header's **⋯** with the rest of Agents ▾. Two dev servers from different agents can clash on the same port; give them different ports.

### Role in the workflow <a id="role-in-the-workflow"></a>

Each agent takes a side in the workflow, chosen in **Add Agent…** and **Agent Settings…** under **Role in the workflow**:

- **Builder** works on cards and sends them to Review.
- **Reviewer** reviews the builders' cards and passes or fails them.
- **Either** does both, but never reviews its own work.
- **Generalist** does whatever you ask in its pane and takes no side in the workflow: it is never a lane's builder or reviewer, never in the reviewer pool, and doesn't add to the project's slots. A card it takes follows the project's workflow like any agent's: where it's built, its review, checks and merge are the project's options (**Version Control → Workflow**), never the role's. A single agent working for you in one pane is the case it's for, with a workflow such as **Solo** that never stops you to review. Every agent has the **generalist** skill, which says how to do such work well: understand first, change carefully, prove it works, report honestly.

**Start Lane** offers builders and either-role agents as the lane's builder, and reviewers and either-role agents as its reviewer: never a generalist. The project's agents table (point at the project's status in the sidebar or its header) has a **Role** column. The role is separate from the agent's **Note**, your own words for what it is for.

**Agents from before 1.0** have no role saved. Hive reads one from the agent's note or name: a note or name that says *review*, or a name like **HR1** or **HI-R2**, makes it a reviewer; anything else makes it a builder. A project with HB1–HB6 and HR1–HR6 therefore reads as six builders and six reviewers, and a single agent reads as a builder. Nothing is written at the upgrade: the role is saved the first time you save the agent's settings. The Assistant can change an agent's role (within **Settings → Assistant → Control**), but project agents can't change their own or anyone else's.

### Unused worktrees <a id="unused-worktrees"></a>

A worktree you keep when you remove its agent (**Keep worktree and branch**, or **Remove All** without its tick box) stays on disk with its branch: Hive says so in a notice with **Review unused worktrees**. They live in the **Git** tab's **Changes**, with every other worktree. Its picker starts with **Unused worktrees (n)**, for worktrees no agent of the project uses right now (for example ones whose agents a template's agents replaced, or ones an agent made itself with `git worktree add`; loading a template gives none of them to its agents), then lists the worktrees agents work in, then the project folder. **Unused worktrees** lists them as a table, a line each: its branch, whether it is **merged into main and clean** or what it holds that is nowhere else ("3 commits not on main · 2 changed files") and who made it (**was B4**, the Hive agent that last worked there; **made by Hive**; or **not made by Hive**), its folder (its name; the full path when you point at it, copied whole), its last commit and its size, with its buttons at the right. In a narrower pane each takes two lines, or stacks; the ⓘ beside the heading says where unused worktrees come from:

- **Remove** deletes a merged, clean worktree and its branch. **Remove all merged (n)…** does them all after one question. Hive checks each again first: one that changed since (a new commit, a file written) is kept, and the notice says why.
- **Open** (or picking it by name in the folder picker) shows its changes since main, file by file, as an agent's worktree does. If it holds work, **Merge** opens the usual Merge dialog, which waits its turn like an agent's merge; tick **Remove the worktree and branch afterwards** to tidy it once its work is on main.
- A worktree that holds work has no plain Remove. To keep its work, **Open** it and merge its branch from there. Agents aren't given worktrees in 1.0: cards and lanes are lent slots. **Remove anyway…** checks the worktree and asks first, naming exactly what goes (its unmerged commits, or for a worktree not on a branch the commits only it has, its uncommitted files and the files git ignores in it, such as build output). If anything in it changes before you confirm (a file added, renamed or edited, ignored ones included, or another branch checked out), nothing is removed: Hive checks it again and asks you again, saying what changed and showing what it holds now, so you confirm what would really go. The same goes for a question left open more than 15 minutes. If Hive can't check what a worktree holds, it doesn't offer to remove it anyway.

**Slots and old folders.** Hive's slots (the worktrees cards are built in) are listed here only when they need you, each named by its slot (**slot-c**) with what it is beside it:
- a **slot with changes, not lent**: a card's work left uncommitted, lent to nothing. **Open** shows the changes;
- a **free slot beyond the pool**: more slots than the project's builders need now, the least recently used first;
- an **agent's old worktree, given up**: one Hive carried into the slots for its agent, whose lease has ended;
- a **retired home, no conversation left**: an agent's old folder kept so its conversations could resume (a reviewer's old worktree, say), once none of them can;
- a **merge queue's old folder, not needed now**: where the merge queue tested merges, kept after its last one, once no merge waits and no card in progress will be merged by it (the queue makes a new folder when it needs one).

A slot lent to a card or a lane, an old worktree still carried for its agent, a review or check copy, and a merge queue folder in use (merging now, or kept for the next merge while cards that Hive merges are in progress) are never listed. **Remove** on a slot goes through Hive's guarded clean-up: the project's archive command runs first, and the slot is kept, saying why, while a session or a process is in it, it has uncommitted files or commits that aren't on its target branch, or a card it held is still being merged. A slot has no **Remove anyway…** or **Give to an agent…**: Hive lends its slots itself. Whatever you remove is checked again first: a folder that stopped being unused since the list was read (a retired home whose conversation can resume again, say) is kept, and so is one with a session running in it.

The project's **Overview** shows one line, such as "7 unused worktrees (7 merged) — Review in Changes". **Changes**' project folder says when an unused worktree has work that isn't on main, with **Review**. **Project Settings → Storage** has a line for unused worktrees with their total size, linking to Changes. Deleting a template never touches worktrees, but if its worktree agents left worktrees that no agent uses now, Hive says how many, once. The Hive Assistant can see how many unused worktrees a project has and mention them; only you can remove them. Without git (see [Git](#git)), nothing is listed or removed.

### Agent templates <a id="agent-templates"></a>

Set a project's agents up once, see them work, and reuse the setup anywhere: a **template** saves each agent's name, **role in the workflow** (builder, reviewer or either), note, MCP servers and the **coding agent it suggests**, the project's layout, and its workflow (see [A template's workflow](#template-workflow)). Builders paired with reviewers stay paired. A template never keeps a **connection**, nor a model, effort, mode or context: those are the connection's, a setup of this PC's accounts, and you choose them when you load it (see **Its connections**, below). Conversations, folders and branches aren't saved either: they belong to the project. Where an agent works is its card's or lane's, never the template's, so a template's agents all start in the project folder. Hive also comes with templates of its own (below).

**+ Add ▾** in the session toolbar holds everything about templates: **Templates**, which saves and loads them, and **Add Agent from Template**. Its menus work from the keyboard too: ↑ and ↓ to move, Enter to choose, → to open **Templates** or **Add Agent from Template**.

- **+ Add ▾ → Templates → Save Template…** names the template and saves it for this project only (kept in its private `.hive` folder) or, ticked, for every project in the workspace. Saving with a name that's already there asks before replacing it; saving again is how you change a template. When agents' names start with the project's key (**HI-B1**, **HI-R2**), a second box, **Use the project key in names**, saves them as patterns instead (`{key}-B{n}`), so the template names its agents after whichever project it's loaded into; names that don't follow the key are kept as they are, and so are the numbers of a group that isn't numbered 1, 2, 3… in order (`{key}-B4`). **Include this project's workflow**, ticked at first, saves the project's workflow too (see [A template's workflow](#template-workflow)).
- **Templates** then lists Hive's templates, the workspace's and this project's (the same name can be in more than one): choose one to load it. Loading **replaces every agent** of the project: Hive first lists who goes and who comes. The new agents are a table, in the template's order: each one's role in the workflow (with its note, and the agent it's paired with) and the connection it gets, with what that runs under it (*Opus · High · Auto*, or the coding agent's defaults). Its **Change** column says what happens to each: **replaced** (a new agent takes the place of the one with its name), **new**, or **removed** (the project's agents the template has none of, at the end of the same table). A removed agent that had its own worktree keeps it, with its branch, to merge or reuse.

  **Its connections.** Under the table, **Connections** chooses what each agent runs on, and the table follows as you choose. There's a choice for each role the template has (**Builders**, **Reviewers**, **Either**, **Generalists**), each starting on the first connection of the coding agent the template suggests for it (*suggests Codex*), else the first you have, in the order of your accounts (Settings → Accounts & Connections). Tick **Same for all** for one choice for everyone. **Per agent** opens a choice for each agent, over its role's (*Same as its role* until you change it). Only connections usable in this workspace are offered: their account turned on here, and their coding agent on and installed. With none usable yet, the agents are made without one, on the coding agent each suggests, and get one once you add an account.

  **Naming the new agents.** Beside each agent's name in the template (kept as it is, for reference) is a box for the name the new agent gets: change any of them before you load. Agent names are unique across the workspace, so when another project's agent already has one of the names, Hive fills in a free one instead and says so above the table: another project's key becomes this project's (**AM-B1** becomes **AP-B1**), and other names get the project's name after them ("Builder (apiary)"). A name you type that another project's agent has, or that two agents share, is marked, saying whose it is, and **Load template** stays greyed out until it's changed. The agents being replaced don't count, so loading a template into the project it came from works without changes. Names in a template can hold `{key}` (the project's key) and `{n}` (a number, counting the agents with that name in turn): Hive's own templates name their agents `{key}-B{n}` and `{key}-R{n}`, so **HI-B1**, **HI-B2**, **HI-R1** in a project whose key is HI. If Hive can't read the project's key, it says so rather than guess one, and the template doesn't load.

  **Its workflow.** Under the agents, the dialog shows what the template's workflow would change in this project, each option with its value now and with it; **Also apply its workflow** (ticked) then opens **Version Control → Workflow** with it filled in, to save there. See [A template's workflow](#template-workflow). A template with no workflow of its own can still suggest a preset (Solo, Pair or Team): **Also switch this project to Pair…** opens Version Control → Workflow with that preset chosen but not saved. A template's workflow carries its team size (**Maximum agents**: Hive's own, their number of agents). Loaded with its workflow, that one option is saved with the agents, never fewer than they are (the dialog says *Maximum agents goes from 2 to 8 for this project, saved with its agents*); the others wait for you to save them in Version Control → Workflow. Unticked, a template with more agents than the project's Maximum agents can't be loaded: **Load template** is greyed out, saying so, and **Open Workflow Options** takes you there to raise it first.

  It won't load while an agent runs or is starting (stop it first), or while a worktree agent has uncommitted work (commit or discard it first) or Hive can't check its worktree. A coding agent the template suggests that's off or not installed doesn't stop it: its agents get another connection. The removed agents' conversations stay in the Sessions tab, their open cards go back (Active ones to Todo), and their card loops (lanes) end, as when you remove an agent. No agent of the project can start while a template loads. If an agent can't be created, the agents change meanwhile, or a connection chosen is removed or turned off meanwhile, nothing changes.
- **+ Add ▾ → Add Agent from Template** lists each template's agents: choose one to add it and leave the others alone. Hive asks for its name, with a free one filled in (its name from the template, or a free one as when loading); **Add Agent** adds it under the name you give, on the first connection of the coding agent it suggests (change it in its Agent Settings, or with **Choose Connections…**).

**Saving from a project.** Each agent's **Role in the workflow** and **Note** (Agent Settings) are saved in the template; an agent from before 1.0 with no role saved is saved with the role its name reads as. **Save Agents as Template…**, **Load Template…** and **Add Agent from Template…** are in the command palette too (the last two open + Add ▾'s menus).

**Templates from Hive 0.9** held whether each agent had a worktree of its own, which 1.0 doesn't do, so 1.0 doesn't use them: they aren't listed or loaded, and importing one is refused, saying so. Your saved 0.9 templates (the workspace's and each project's) stay where they were, untouched, and at the upgrade to 1.0 Hive also keeps a copy of each in `.hive/backups/templates-0.9`, beside the 0.9 board backup, and says so once. Hive's own templates take their place; make your own again with **Save Template…**.

### Choosing connections for a project's agents <a id="choose-connections"></a>

**Project Settings → Agents → Choose Connections…** opens the same **Connections** choice over the project's agents as they are: a choice for each role they have, **Same for all**, and **Per agent**. It's how you give every builder here a higher effort, say: choose *Claude · High* for **Builders** and **Apply**. Each choice starts at **Keep each one's**, and a table shows each agent's connection now and after. Only their connections change: no agent is replaced and their conversations stay (another coding agent's connection starts a fresh one, as in Agent Settings).

- A **running** agent keeps its connection until it restarts. After **Apply**, Hive offers **Switch Them When Their Turn Is Over** (each ends once its turn is over and carries on, on the connection chosen, another connection on the same sign-in too), or **Later**.
- A running agent can't take **another coding agent's** connection (stop it first): those are greyed out for it, and a role's choice skips it, saying so.

### A template's workflow <a id="template-workflow"></a>

A template can keep a project's **workflow** too: the rules its Version Control → Workflow options set, one section for each kind of version control (None and Git), since their options differ. It keeps the rules, never the project's own facts:

- **Kept:** who reviews and how many reviewers, one per lane or a shared pool, nobody reviewing their own work; the checks before review and on the merged result, **by name**; whether each card's focused tests run on the merged result; whether a pass survives a clean rebase; who merges, the merge style, up to date before merging, installing from scratch; your OK before merging and its labels; where cards are built and slots kept up to date; whether commits use the project's identity (on or off); agents stopping what they started; dependencies merged first; the shared-folder warning and file locks; card loops (rounds, wait, wakes); and the stage names.
- **Never kept:** the branch work merges into and pushing it, the identity's name and email, the checks' commands (the focused tests' too), the merge commit's message (set for the workspace) and excluded folders.

**Saving.** **Save Template…** has **Include this project's workflow**, ticked at first: the template keeps the project's options as its section for the project's version control. Saving again over a template keeps its other section (a Git section stays when you save from a project with no version control), and unticked, keeps the sections it had; the notice says which.

**Loading.** The load dialog shows the section for this project's version control: each option it changes, with its value now and with it, a check this project has no command for (add it under **Checks** before cards need it), the command that runs each card's focused tests when the template turns them on and this project has none (the workflow can't be saved until it is set), and an identity it can't turn on (this project's git config has no name and email). **Also apply its workflow** (ticked) opens **Version Control → Workflow** after loading, with the template's options laid over the project's own, which keep their target branch, commands and identity. Its **Maximum agents** is saved with its agents (the template's own, or as many as its agents when the project allowed fewer), and the note atop the form says so; the other options aren't saved until you **Save…** there, which says what changes and applies from the next card (cards in flight keep their rules); **Discard Changes** drops them. Untick it to load only the agents.

- A template with only a **Git** workflow, in a project with no version control: its workflow needs Git first, so **Load template** waits. **Set Up Git…** closes the dialog and opens Version Control on Git's page; once **Initialise repository…** has made the repository and the project uses Git, the dialog opens again. If the project isn't switched to Git, a notice says the template's Git workflow wasn't applied. Or untick **Also apply its workflow** to load only its agents.
- A template with **both**, in a project with no version control, uses its None workflow, and says it has a Git one too.
- A template with no section for the project's version control leaves its options as they are. A section that can't be used (made by another version of Hive, or changed by hand) is shown as such and never applied.

**Hive's own templates** carry their preset as their workflow, for None and Git, made with this project's own suggested checks when loaded. In the **Templates** view, a template's workflow shows under **None | Git** tabs; **Edit…** fills a section from a preset (Solo, Pair, Team or All off, keeping its check names) or removes it. To set options one by one, set them in a project's Version Control → Workflow, then save its agents as a template under the same name.

### Hive's own templates <a id="hives-own-templates"></a>

The **Hive** group of the Templates view, and of + Add ▾ → **Templates**, holds ready-made teams. Each carries its preset as its workflow (see [A template's workflow](#template-workflow)), and its builders are paired with their reviewers:

| Template | Agents | Suggests |
|---|---|---|
| **Solo** | 1 builder (Claude Code) | Solo |
| **Pair** | 1 builder (Claude Code) and 1 reviewer (Codex) | Pair |
| **Pair: one provider** | 1 builder and 1 reviewer, both Claude Code | Pair |
| **Team 3×3** | 3 builders (Claude Code), each with a reviewer (Codex) | Team |
| **Team 6×6** | 6 builders (Claude Code), each with a reviewer (Codex), on two pages | Team |
| **Team 6 + pool** | 6 builders (Claude Code) and 2 reviewers (Codex) shared by all of them | Team |

The coding agents in brackets are only what each role suggests: loading one offers, for each role, the first connection you have on that coding agent, else your first one (so Pair runs both on Claude Code where you have no Codex account), and you choose (see **Its connections** in [Agent templates](#agent-templates)). Their layout fits their count (one pane, two columns, or a grid of six). They're read-only: **Duplicate…** copies one into the workspace or a project, where you can change it.

### Managing and sharing templates <a id="managing-and-sharing-templates"></a>

The **Templates** view (its icon is next to Skills on the activity bar) lists Hive's templates, the workspace's and each project's, grouped by where they're kept; the drop-down at the top shows all of them, Hive's, the workspace's, or one project's. A project's **Templates** tab lists its own templates, the workspace's and Hive's, each marked with where it's kept.

Select a template to see what it holds: each agent's name, role in the workflow (with its note and the agent it's paired with), the coding agent it **suggests** (and its MCP servers), the layout, and its workflow under **None | Git** tabs (or the preset it suggests). In a narrow pane (a small window, or the side panels open) its settings wrap rather than run off the side, the editor puts each field's name above its box, and a project's **Templates** tab shows its list above the template; when even that doesn't fit, scroll the agents table sideways. With a template selected:
