- **Load into Project…** (in the view, you choose the project) or **Load into This Project…** (in a project's tab) replaces the project's agents and layout, with the same list of who goes and who comes, and the same checks, as **+ Add ▾ → Templates**.
- **Edit…** changes it where it's kept. Give it a new name, a short description and a layout, and change its agents. **Add Agent…** adds one, and each row has buttons to edit, copy, move up or down, and remove that agent. Editing an agent opens an **Agent Settings** dialog: name, note, role in the workflow, the coding agent it suggests and its MCP servers (no connection, model or effort: those are chosen when it's loaded). A name can hold `{key}` and `{n}` (as in Hive's own templates); copying an agent whose name has `{n}` gives the copy the same name, the next number. Pairs follow the agents as you move, copy, rename or remove them. Its **Workflow** section fills a version control's workflow from a preset or removes it (see [A template's workflow](#template-workflow)). Saving an agent in that dialog saves the template too, with your other changes, and the editor stays open. **Save Template** checks it (1 agent up to as many as a project can have, each with its own name) and saves it with changes made in the editor itself (name, description, layout, order, removed agents). Changes not saved yet are marked: *Changes not saved* in the editor, and *Not saved* beside the template in the list. They stay if you leave the editor for another template or view, and it opens with them again; **Cancel** asks before discarding them. Quitting Hive, closing the window and **Reload Window** ask about them too, as they do for unsaved files. While a save is in progress, the editor can't be changed. If the template was saved again meanwhile (from a project, or another window), Hive tells you instead of overwriting it. Editing never changes a project you loaded the template into earlier. Saving a project's agents again under the same name (**Save Template…**) still works too.
- **Rename…** renames it where it's kept. Another template there can't have the same name.
- **Duplicate…** copies it into the workspace or any project. A name that's already taken there gets a number: "Pair (2)".
- **Export…** saves it as one file, such as `Build and review.hive-template.json`, wherever you choose. Templates live in `.hive`, which git ignores, so exporting is how you share one. The file holds only the template: no folder paths, sessions or names of people.
- **Delete** sends it to the Recycle Bin.

**Import…** (the download icon at the top of the list) opens a template file and asks where to keep it: the workspace, or a project. Hive checks the file first and refuses one it can't use, saying why: not a template, made by Hive 0.9 or a newer Hive, or more than 24 agents. A template with more agents than a project's **Maximum agents** still loads when you load it: its Load dialog offers to raise the maximum (see [Agent templates](#agent-templates)); the Assistant can't load it there. Nothing is half imported. If a template of that name is already there, choose **Replace** or **Keep Both** (the imported one gets a number). A template suggesting a coding agent this Hive doesn't know, from a newer Hive say, is imported and marked; loading it offers your connections as for any other. A template saved before connections (1.0's release candidates) loads without its models, efforts, modes and connections: you choose connections as you load it, and nothing else of it is lost.

### Loading a template through the Assistant <a id="assistant-templates"></a>

With **Settings → Assistant → Control** at **Control agents** or above, ask the Hive Assistant to set a project's team up from a template: *"load Team 3×3 into web, and call the first builder Coder"*. It lists the templates the project can use, shows you what loading one would do (who goes and who comes, names another project's agent already has, and what its workflow would change), and loads it when you ask. At **Look and advise** it can list templates and show what a load would do, but not load one.

- It loads the template as **Load template** does, with the same checks: it won't load while an agent of the project runs or is starting, and agent names stay unique across the workspace. A name another project's agent has is never changed for you: the Assistant is told a free one to use, and asks or uses it.
- It chooses connections as the dialog does, by role or agent (*"reviewers on Work"*), each otherwise on the first connection of the coding agent its role suggests; what a load would give each agent is in what it shows you first. It can also do **Choose Connections…** on a project's agents (*"high effort for every builder in web"*), changing nothing else; **Undo** puts their connections back.
- It applies the template's workflow as the project's Version Control → Workflow options unless you ask it not to. Lanes and cards already under way keep their rules. A template whose workflow is for Git only, in a project with no version control, isn't loaded with its workflow: setting up Git is yours, in Version Control.
- **Undo** puts back the project's agents (with their conversations), its layout and its workflow options, each only while it is still as the load left it. The removed agents' cards stay put back and their lanes stay ended.

### Archiving and backups <a id="archiving-and-backups"></a>

Claude Code and Codex delete old transcripts after a while. Hive keeps a copy of every session transcript in the project's `.hive/sessions` folder, and archived sessions in `.hive/archive`. Once a session ends or is archived, Hive compresses its copy (`<id>.jsonl.gz`, usually under a third of the size); you can still open, search, export and resume it as before. Files you paste or drop into a session are kept in `.hive/attachments`. Hive only deletes them when you ask (deleting a session sends its transcript copies to the Recycle Bin; its attachments stay), or as **Keep transcript backups** says (below). If the CLI has removed a transcript, Hive restores it from the backup when you resume.

**Keep transcript backups.** **Settings → Sessions → Keep transcript backups** is **Always** unless you change it. Set it to 30, 90 or 180 days or a year, and Hive's copies of sessions that have had no activity for that long go to the Recycle Bin, but only for sessions the coding agent still has: the session stays in the Sessions tab, can still be opened and resumed from the agent's own transcript, and what it used stays in your usage totals. A session whose only copy is Hive's is never removed this way; delete it yourself, or use **Clean Up…** in Storage.

**Storage.** **Project Settings → Storage** (or **Project Storage and Clean Up…** in the command palette) shows how much space Hive keeps for the project: transcript backups (`.hive/sessions`), the archive (`.hive/archive`), attachments (`.hive/attachments`) and each agent's worktree. Under the backups' and the archive's sizes it says what they hold before compression. It's measured in the background, so a large worktree never holds up the window, and leaving the page stops measuring; **Refresh** measures again. The coding agents' own transcripts (in `~/.claude`, `~/.codex` and `~/.copilot`) aren't counted, and neither **Clean Up…** nor **Purge…** (below) ever touches them. **Settings → Workspace → Storage** shows the workspace's total and every project, the Hive Assistant included, biggest first; **Storage** next to one opens its page, and **Purge…** there removes what Hive keeps for the whole workspace.

**Clean Up…** on that page moves old files to the Recycle Bin. Nothing is cleaned up automatically. Choose what goes:

- **Attachments of archived sessions** last active more than 90 days ago (or the number of days you set). This is the only option on at first.
- **Attachments of deleted sessions**, and of launches that never got a session (folders named `run-…`).
- **Hive's backups of archived sessions** last active more than a number of days ago, only where Claude Code or Codex still has the transcript. The session stays: you can still resume and read it, from the CLI's copy, for as long as the CLI keeps it.
- **Backups of archived sessions the CLI no longer has.** Hive's copy is the last one, so these sessions are deleted, as **Delete Session** does: they can't be resumed or read afterwards. The dialog warns you when this is on.

The dialog lists every file that will go, with its size and the total, before anything happens; **Move … to the Recycle Bin** removes exactly those. Running sessions and sessions that aren't archived are never touched, also when one is resumed or unarchived while the dialog is open: it's skipped and listed in the result. What the cleaned-up sessions used still counts in the Overview and the Workspace Overview.

**Purge…** removes everything Hive keeps for sessions, not only old files: Hive's backups of their transcripts (the archive's too), their attachments, and what is left of sessions you deleted. It's on the same page, for the project, and in **Settings → Workspace → Storage** for the whole workspace; for one session, right-click it in the Sessions tab and choose **Purge Hive's Files…**. Before anything goes, the dialog lists every file with its size and the total, and **Move … to the Recycle Bin** removes exactly those; tick **Delete permanently** to skip the Recycle Bin. A few things to know:

- The coding agents' own files (in `~/.claude`, `~/.codex` and `~/.copilot`) are never touched, and running sessions are left alone (the dialog says how many). A session the coding agent still has stays in the Sessions tab: you can still open and resume it from the agent's own transcript.
- A session whose only copy is Hive's backup (its coding agent no longer has the transcript) is deleted, as **Delete Session** does; the dialog tags those backups *last copy* and warns you.
- What the sessions used always stays in your usage totals: Purge never clears the usage history.
- Purge removes exactly what the dialog listed: anything that changes while it is open (a session resumed, a file added or written to, a coding agent's transcript gone or back) is left alone and listed in the result. A dialog left open for over an hour removes nothing: Hive says its list is out of date, and you open Purge again to see what goes now.
- If Hive ever stopped while deleting or moving a session's files, the files it left may be that session's only copy: Purge keeps them, and the dialog says how many.

### Antivirus scanning <a id="antivirus-scanning"></a>

On Windows, Microsoft Defender's real-time protection scans every file as it is created or opened, and Hive's work creates and opens a great many: installs, builds, tests, git, worktrees, transcripts. With several agents at once that adds up. **Settings → Workspace → Antivirus scanning** (or **Antivirus Scanning of the Workspace** in the command palette) shows how Defender treats the workspace:

- whether Defender is the active antivirus and real-time protection is on (if another antivirus is active, Hive names it and leaves it alone);
- the folders Hive's work goes through: the workspace (every project is in it), its worktrees folder once an agent has one, and, if you develop Hive itself, its test area. For each, whether Defender scans it: **Excluded**, **Scanned**, or **Unknown**, because Defender shows its exclusions only to administrators. **Check with Administrator Rights…** reads them (Windows asks once);
- whether each is on a **Dev Drive**: a trusted Dev Drive, with Defender's performance mode on, is scanned much more lightly. Without administrator rights Hive can only see that a drive is ReFS (*maybe*); **Check with Administrator Rights…** confirms it. Microsoft recommends a Dev Drive for code: **About Dev Drive** explains how to set one up (Hive doesn't create drives).

**Add Exclusions…** asks Defender to stop scanning exactly those folders, never anything broader: Hive never offers a whole drive, your home folder or a system folder, even if the workspace is one. It lists them first, one folder per row (a long path wraps, its next lines indented), with a button beside the list, **Copy** (**Copy all** when there are several), that copies every path, one per line. Then it asks for administrator rights once; if the folders change before you answer (an agent makes a worktrees folder), it asks you again rather than add more. A folder you had already excluded is left as it is. Think about the trade-off: Defender won't scan anything in an excluded folder, including `node_modules` and whatever agents download there, so only exclude folders whose contents you trust. **Remove Hive's Exclusions…** lists the folders it will take away the same way, and takes away only the ones Hive added; exclusions you added yourself stay. If your organisation manages Defender, it may refuse the change; Hive says so.

Hive's exclusions don't follow a workspace you move by themselves: **Repair a Moved Workspace** offers to move them to the new folders, in one administrator prompt (see [Moving a workspace](#moving-a-workspace)). If you leave them, decline that prompt, or delete a folder Hive excluded, the panel lists **Hive's exclusions of folders that are gone**: those Defender still lists (or, while it hides its list, every one Hive added, as **Added by Hive**). **Remove Hive's Exclusions…** takes them away with the workspace's, in the same administrator prompt. Hive forgets one that Defender no longer lists once it reads the list.

On the machine Hive is developed on, with several agents at work, Hive's full test run took about 3 to 5% less time with these folders excluded (for example 12.0 minutes before, 11.6 after); a single heavy install or build may gain more or less.

When Defender scans the workspace and it matters (two or more agents running at once, or a command in the Progress panel that took two minutes or more), Hive suggests this, with **Review…** and **Don't Ask Again** (for this workspace). If you close it, or review without adding exclusions, Hive reminds you later: at most once a day, and only when scanning matters again. It stops when you pick **Don't Ask Again**, or once the folders are excluded or on a trusted Dev Drive, or Defender isn't the active antivirus. A folder added since, such as the worktrees folder, is suggested straight away. The Performance view says so too.

### Token use, cache and compaction <a id="token-use-cache-and-compaction"></a>

The **Overview** tab updates as sessions change (at most every 15 seconds; **Settings → Sessions → Overview updates** can make it every minute or only when you click **Refresh**). An update starts once the one before has finished, so a project with hundreds of sessions still fills in; one that gets no answer within a minute says so, with **Retry**. It starts with the project's **cards at a glance**, as on the Workspace Overview but for this project only: how many are On Hold, in Todo, Active, Waiting for review, Approved and Done, and how many are stalled or blocked. They update as cards change, and clicking a number opens the project's **Tasks** tab. A project without cards says so, with **Open Tasks**. Then a **project summary** for the period you choose (All time at first, or Today, 7 days, 30 days), across every provider: tokens, API-equivalent cost, sessions and prompts. Periods are calendar days (Today is since midnight; 7 days is today and the six days before), and they count only what happened in them: a session that has been running since Monday adds only today's work to Today. For 7 and 30 days a small chart shows the tokens of each day; hover a day for its cost and prompts. Below it are the agents **running now** (with how full each one's context is; each one's status, then the chip of its card, which opens it: the card it works on or reviews, or, for an agent waiting on cards, those cards, as *Waiting → Review* with a chip each, or *2 cards* when they don't fit), a section per provider with its totals and plan limits, a table **by agent**, and one agent's session: its running session, else its most recent one. It follows the focused agent; with several agents, a picker next to the heading shows another's.

- **Context** — how many tokens the conversation occupies right now: the last request's input plus its output (thinking included), which stays in the conversation. That is what the agent compacts on, so a turn that thinks for 70K tokens can take a context showing 116K to 189K and set off a compaction; the compaction history says so ("turn added 72.4k output").
- **Cache** — whether Anthropic's prompt cache is still warm (5-minute or 1-hour lifetime) and how long it has left (Claude Code).
- **Re-cache on resume** — an estimate of the tokens written to cache on the first message after resuming, once the cache has expired.
- **Compactions** — each time the agent summarised the conversation to free space, with before/after sizes, newest first, ten to a page (**Rows per page** changes that, and is remembered). Filter by trigger (auto or manual) or by date and time, and click a column's header to sort by it (again for the other way, a third time for newest first). Click a compaction, or press Enter on it, to read it in the **Sessions** tab, at its divider with the summary the agent continued from. Clicking the context in an agent's footer brings you here.

**Tables.** Hive's longer tables work the same way: the compactions, archived cards, keyboard shortcuts, Performance's and Compare's tables, Storage and the Overviews' tables by project and by agent. Click a column's header to sort by it (again for the other way, a third time for the table's own order); Hive remembers each table's sort and rows per page. A table with many rows has filters under its headers and pages under it.
- **API-equivalent cost** — what the session would have cost at API prices. On a subscription you are not charged this; it is a measure of how heavy the session is. Claude Code calculates it itself; for Codex, Hive **estimates** it from a price table (shown with **≈**). Hive ships the published prices as starting values, and you can change, add and remove them in each provider's settings page (**API prices**) if they change or you have different rates. For a model Hive has no price for, the cost shows as unknown (**$?** in the agent's footer, **Unknown** on the Overview; a total that includes such sessions shows what is known with **+ ?**); hover it to see which model, and add its price under **API prices** to see the estimate.

### Long conversations <a id="long-conversations"></a>

A conversation's transcript keeps everything that happened in it, and compacting doesn't make it smaller: it only shortens what the agent has in its context. A very long one (100 MB and more) slows down the CLI and Hive, and can make typing in the terminals stutter. The size shows on the right of each agent's footer (and when you hover its context), and beside each session in the Sessions tab (the Assistant's conversations too); past **Settings → Sessions → Warn when a transcript is over** (20 MB; projects can set their own) it turns amber, with a ⚠. Hive also tells you once when a conversation passes it, when the agent's turn is over (a conversation grows during a turn, so you hear of it once the agent can act on it).

To start afresh without losing the thread, click the size (or choose **Hand Over to…** in the agent's menu) and pick the agent itself, **in a new conversation**: the agent writes a handover, Hive ends its conversation (it stays in the Sessions tab) and starts a new one that reads the handover and carries on.

So that a hand-over never cuts into an agent's work, the notice offers **Hand Over to…** only while the agent is idle, isn't merging and isn't on a card of a running card loop. Otherwise it just says the agent can hand over when it has finished, and **Hand Over to…** doesn't offer the agent itself. Hive checks again when you click (a notice can be old by then), and once more just before the conversation ends: if the agent took the merge slot or started other work while it wrote the handover, its conversation isn't ended and the handover is kept. The Assistant sees each agent's transcript size too, and can suggest it. For new work that doesn't need the thread, ask the Assistant to give the agent the task **in a new conversation**: the same, without the handover (see **Card loops**).

**Usage history.** Hive records what each session used as it goes, in the workspace's `.hive/usage.db`, so the Overviews open at once without reading any transcript, and a session's usage stays in the totals after you delete it or its transcripts are gone. A running session is recorded at the end of each turn, about every minute, and when it ends, so the Overviews can be a turn behind. The first time a workspace opens in this version, Hive imports what its existing sessions used, in the background; the Workspace Overview says how far it is, and its figures are partial until it's done. Sessions started outside Hive aren't counted. A running agent's footer and context gauge still follow each turn; they're read in the background, so a long session doesn't hold Hive up, and a session you resume after restarting Hive picks up from what its history already has instead of reading its whole transcript again. **Settings → Sessions → Usage cache size** only sets how many transcripts Hive keeps in memory while it runs (5,000), and **Clear the usage cache** forgets those; the usage history stays. Once a session ended more than two weeks ago, the history keeps when it used what in 1-hour steps rather than to the second, so it stays small; its totals and costs stay exact, and only a card's or lane's share of it that began or ended inside an hour is less exact. **Settings → Advanced → Usage history: keep full detail for** sets how many days (14).

### Workspace Overview <a id="workspace-overview"></a>

The **Workspace Overview** (in the activity bar, or **Ctrl+Shift+O**) adds up the whole workspace: every project and the Hive Assistant. It opens on the last **7 days** (or Today, 30 days, All time) and shows:

- the task board at a glance: cards per column, stalled and blocked, each opening the board;
- tokens, API-equivalent cost, sessions and prompts for the period;
- for 7 and 30 days, a chart of tokens per day, stacked by project (the six busiest, the rest as Other); each day is a tile of its own, as in the Performance chart, so quiet days still show; hover a day for each project's share;
- every agent running now, in any project (click one to go to it);
- a table **by project**, with the Assistant as its own row: click a column to sort, or a row to open that project's Overview;
- each provider's totals and plan limits.

Hidden and removed projects aren't counted. It updates like the project Overview, and reads no transcript: its figures come from the usage history (see **Usage history** above).

### Plan usage <a id="plan-usage"></a>

If you use a subscription (a Claude plan for Claude Code, a ChatGPT plan for Codex), the status bar shows how much of each plan's limits is used, one item per provider, for example **5h 34% · Week 12%**: the rolling 5-hour allowance and the weekly one. Hover it to see when each resets; the **Overview** tab shows them with meters in each provider's section. The numbers are for your whole account (every session, not just Hive's) and come from the CLI while a session is running, so after a quiet spell they show when they were last updated, and which agent reported each. Each session passes on the figures from its own latest request, so one that last asked a while ago can report a little less than another: within a reset period Hive keeps the highest it has been told, and once a limit resets it shows the new figure (or nothing, until a session reports again). The status bar darkens at 80% and turns red at 95%, and Hive notifies you once when you pass 80% and 95% of each limit in each reset period.

Each agent's footer shows its model and **effort**, e.g. *Opus 5.5 (default) · High*: the model that actually runs, as the running session reports it, otherwise the model new sessions will use. An alias such as **Opus** shows as the model it stands for (*Opus 5.5*), with *chosen as Opus* in the tooltip; the model pickers list it as *Opus (Opus 5.5)*. *(default)* means nothing chose the model, so the CLI runs its own default; a model set on the agent's connection shows without it. With no effort chosen, the effort is the model's own default once the CLI has said it (*Medium (default)*).

### Performance metrics <a id="performance-metrics"></a>

Hive counts what its own parts cost, for each workspace: its Agent API's requests (how many, how long, how big), its tools' replies as the agents got them, what each session was given at launch (Hive's guidance, the Assistant's mode, the skills), and its skill service's work, alongside the providers' own reported token usage. Only totals are kept, never prompts, replies, tokens or paths, for 30 days, in the workspace's database, `.hive/hive.db` (kept out of git; before 0.9, `.hive/metrics/metrics.json`, which moves in the first time 0.9 opens the workspace, with the same numbers). Hive doesn't guess token counts: what it measures is exact bytes and characters, and token usage is what the providers report.

It's on by default: **Settings → Sessions → Record performance metrics** turns it off (what was recorded stays), and **Reset performance metrics** clears the workspace's. Scripts can read them with the Agent API (`GET /v1/metrics`).

### Performance view <a id="performance-view"></a>

**Performance** in the activity bar shows these metrics for the whole workspace; each project's **Performance** tab (next to Overview) shows only that project's. Pick the last **24 hours**, **7 days** or **30 days**; in the activity bar view, the sidebar (or **Scope**) picks the whole workspace, its own work (the Assistant, scripts) or one project. Each filter sits with what it changes: **Who** (project agents, the Assistant, scripts) heads a box holding the cards, the chart and the tables, and **Provider**, inside it, heads the two parts recorded per provider, the guidance given at launch and the providers' usage (Hive doesn't record API requests or tool calls per provider). Provider always lists every provider with data in the range, so you can switch from one to another directly. **Export** (and Compare's **Keep current view**) saves the page's scope, range and **Who**, with every provider's rows, each naming its provider, so its totals match the cards whatever provider is picked. You see:

- how long Hive was recording in the range: metrics are only kept while the workspace is open in Hive with recording on, so time before that, while Hive was closed or recording was off, or before a reset is **not recorded** (no data, not zero), and so is history Hive removed to keep its metrics file small, which the page says;
- cards: Agent API requests (per hour recorded) and how many failed or were cancelled (an isolated check asking again while it waits for a check copy is a **wait**, counted but never a failure; before 1.0's HI-1831 those showed as failures, as client errors, and older hours still do), their latency (p50 and p95), the data sent and received, the characters Hive's tools gave the models, and what each session got at launch;
- a chart of requests per hour (24 hours) or per day, failures in red, waits in grey and hours not recorded hatched; hover a bar for its tool calls and launches;
- tables of Hive's tools (sorted by the characters they cost a model's context), Agent API routes, the guidance given at launch in parts (Hive's core instructions, what it adds for the project, the Assistant's role and mode, the skills' catalog), by the launched agent's **workflow role** (builder, reviewer, either, or none for generalists and the Assistant, as it was when it launched: its part in a lane counts over its own role), and the providers' own usage for project agents and the Assistant apart: tokens, requests, context (each session's latest, not added up), compactions, **safeguard stops** (responses a CLI's safety classifier stopped; *not detected* for Copilot) and API-equivalent cost (≈ where Hive estimated it, **unknown** where a session reported nothing; not a bill), and, when there were any, the safeguard stops per agent and model. If a project's session history couldn't be read, the page says the totals are partial;
- the **Hive tool list** each session was given, a start, by who and workflow role: how many tools, its characters, and of them the tools' descriptions, their parameters' descriptions and the schemas (the rest: names, types and punctuation), and its bytes. A dash means Hive hadn't measured it yet. Help → Copy Diagnostics includes the same for the last 24 hours, so a bug report shows if the list has grown;
- for the whole workspace, the skill service's scans and cache, Hive's [main process stalls](#main-process-stalls), and Hive-wide event streams.

It refreshes every minute while you look at it (and **Refresh** at once); reading it doesn't add to the numbers. A note says when measurements were dropped because a limit was full, and **What isn't measured** lists what it can't see. **Export** saves what the page shows (its scope, range and filters) as a JSON file with its units, coverage and Hive's version; **Shift+click** Export to replace project names with project-1, project-2… and leave out the workspace's path, before sharing it.

### Main process stalls <a id="main-process-stalls"></a>

Every pane's typing goes through Hive's main process, so when it is busy for more than a quarter of a second, you feel it as lag. The workspace's **Performance** page lists those times under **Main process stalls**, newest first: the last 50 since Hive started (kept in memory only, so a restart clears them; no range or filter applies). Each row says:

- **When** it ended, and its **Length**;
- **Main thread CPU**: how much of it the main process spent working. **mostly waiting** (under half its length) means it was waiting on the disk, a lock or another program rather than working. It's measured from half a second before, so it can be a little more than the length;
- **Step**: the database or file step that held the main process, where Hive knows one;
- **During it**: what else was under way, such as a window's call (`ipc …`), an Agent API request (`api …`) or a hook (`hook …`), with how long each took. Some of these may only have waited behind the stall rather than caused it.

In **Step** and **During it**, work that happened several times shows once, with how many times and how long in all (`db transaction … ×52 · 81 ms`), the longest first.

The names are Hive's own for its work, never your text. Hive's log (**Help → Open Logs Folder**, `hive.log`) notes the same stalls, at most one line a minute. If Hive feels slow, a screenshot of this table with **Help → Copy Diagnostics…** helps find why.

### Cost per card <a id="cost-per-card"></a>

Hive works out what each card cost from who held it when and what their sessions used then:

- **What counts.** A card's **builder** (its agent) while it is in **Active**, and its **reviewer** from the start of each review to its verdict. Usage is each provider's own report: Hive's estimates (where a provider reports no cost, or you chose your own prices) are marked **≈**, and what Hive can't know, such as an agent with no session Hive knows of or a model with no price, shows as **Unknown** or **+ ?**, never as $0.
- **On the card.** The bottom of a card's panel says, for example, **cost: ≈ $1.84 · 2 rounds**. Hover it for the builder's and the reviewer's parts and their tokens (input, output, cache writes and reads, requests).
- **By round.** The card's **Work** section ends with **Cost by round**: a row for each review round, with the builder's and reviewer's parts and the round's total, and a **Total** row that matches the card's cost line. It shows whether a card's cost went on extra rounds or on one long read. Each part's tokens are on hover, and each round's verdict on its name. Each role's cost is shared between its rounds by when its agents used it, so the parts are **≈**. Every row and column adds up to what it shows, so a part under a cent shows as **< $0.01**. A card taken on at the upgrade has a **Before its rounds** row for the time its agents held it before its work began under the new rules. A card from before Hive 1.0 has no rounds, only its cost line. Hive keeps older sessions' use in 1-hour steps (**Settings → Advanced → Usage history: keep full detail for**, two weeks by default), so a round from longer ago than that may be split only to the hour, as a note under the table says.
- **In Performance.** A **Cards** table lists the cards worked on in the range, with their cost, the builder's and reviewer's parts, their review rounds and when they were last held. Sort by any column (unknown costs go last); click a row to open the card. In a project's Performance tab, only that project's cards.
- **On a lane.** In the Progress panel, hover a lane's top line (the pair and its count) for what the lane cost since it started, split into **on cards** and **overhead**: what its builder and reviewer used while holding no card, such as merging, watching for a verdict and compacting. It also says the cost per finished card.

Time an agent spends between cards, such as reading a card it was just woken for before taking it, is overhead, not the card's. While an agent holds several cards at once, what it uses then is shared between them equally. A reported charge is split only between the requests it covered, so a card's cost never changes because of work done later; where a card took only some of a charge's requests, its share is marked ≈. Copilot reports its tokens only when a session ends: until then a Copilot card's tokens show as unknown, and then Hive spreads them over that session's requests, marked ≈ (its cost comes from Copilot's own charges). Cards worked on before Hive kept this have it worked out from their history, by the agents' names.

### Comparing before and after <a id="comparing-before-and-after"></a>

**Compare** (at the top of the Performance page) puts a baseline next to a run, to see whether a change made Hive's
traffic and guidance smaller while the work still got done:

- **Keep current view** saves what the page shows now (its scope, range and filters). Keep one before a change and
  another after it, then pick them as **Baseline** and **Run**. They are compared per hour Hive was recording, with what
  makes either partial noted. Real use isn't a controlled workload, so a difference can be the work that was done.
  Guidance and the hive tools' list (in characters) are compared a launch, for all launches and for each workflow role's
  (builders, reviewers…), so a change to what reviewers get shows apart from the builders'. Launches from before Hive
  recorded roles show as *role not recorded*, and a view kept before then shows these rows as **unknown**.
- **Import…** reads a Performance export or a **scenario benchmark**: the `benchmark.json` that Hive's development
  scenarios write (tests/scenarios in Hive's source). A benchmark compares scenario by scenario, **correctness first**:
  *smaller, still correct* only if every check that passed still passes and still runs, and no more calls, failures or
  retries were made. A smaller result that lost a check is *smaller but failing*, not an improvement. The table shows the
  checks passed ✓, failed ✗ and skipped – on each side; click a scenario for every measure, its samples and spread, and
  which checks changed. Something that wasn't measured shows as **unknown**, never as zero, and a scenario that wasn't
  fully measured isn't judged. A scenario's details show its measures for the session it tests (the Assistant, or the
  agent under test) first, then for the other agents (a lane's builder and reviewer, agents it or Hive started) and for
  all of them together, which is what its result is judged on, with each agent's hive calls under them. A benchmark from
  before Hive split them has totals only, and Compare says so. With the fake Claude Code or fake Codex, API requests
  and tool-list starts count per hive call, since those fakes start a hive server for each one (fake-copilot runs the
  real Copilot CLI, which keeps one a session).
- If the two measured different things (other scenarios, provider, model or filters, or a fake against a real model), the
  page says **Not comparable** and why.
- The scope is the page's: the whole workspace, **Workspace's own work** or one project each keep their own list. A
  project's Performance tab compares only that project's own kept views and exports; given a workspace export, it asks
  before using that project's part, and an export that holds other projects' data is refused. Scenario benchmarks are
  compared with the whole workspace selected.
- While comparing, the range and filters of **Now** are hidden: each file keeps the ones it was made with (shown above the
  table). They only shape what **Keep current view** saves.
- A fake provider's token counts are simulated, so they're never compared; a model trial's are, when both report them.
- Up to 20 are kept in the workspace's `.hive/metrics/benchmarks` folder (this machine's); when it's full, the oldest
  unpinned one goes. **Pin** one to keep it. If the folder's list is damaged, Hive rebuilds it from the files it finds
  (keeping the old list aside) and says so: nothing kept is deleted for it. Files Hive can't account for go to the
  folder's `quarantine` subfolder instead of being deleted. A removal that can't finish (a file in use) is retried
  later, and the comparison doesn't come back to the list.

### Progress panel <a id="progress-panel"></a>

Long runs, such as test suites and builds, can report their progress to Hive, and the **Progress panel** shows them: which agent is running what, how far along it is and about how long is left. It sits on the right, beside the Hive Assistant's panel. Show or fold it with **Ctrl+Alt+P** (Toggle Progress Panel); each workspace remembers whether it's open, and dragging its left edge makes it wider.

- **Four tabs** across its header: **Pulse**, what is going on now (the merge going on, a line for each merge queue testing or paused, a line when runs failed, above the runs going on so it stays in sight, then the runs going on, and the lanes at the bottom of the panel: all of them up to six, then they scroll; drag the line above them for up to four more, and double-click it to go back to six); **Merges**, the merge queues and the merge going on; **Runs**, every run; and **Lanes**, every lane. Below about 360 px wide the tabs show only their icons (hover one for its name). Each has a number when it has something: cards waiting in the merge queues and agents waiting to merge on **Merges**, the lanes on **Lanes**, and in red on **Runs** the runs that failed with nothing passing since. A run counts there until a later run of the same thing (the same card's check, or the same command) passes; Pulse has a line for them too, such as **2 runs failed**, which opens Runs on the failures. With the keyboard, Tab to the tabs and use the arrow keys, Home and End. Each workspace remembers the tab you chose.
- **Folded**, it is a narrow **Progress** strip down the right edge with a small bar for each run, so you can see something is going without giving it room. It has bars for the newest six; more runs show as **+N** under them (red when one of those failed, amber when one stopped reporting), and hovering it lists them. It never opens by itself; click the strip to open it.
- **Each run** shows the agent and its project, what is running ("e2e: 12 suites"), a bar, the step ("4 of 12: carddialog"), how long it has taken and, once Hive has an estimate, about how long is left. A run that doesn't report steps shows a moving bar and the time. Long titles end in "…" (hover for the whole command). A run for a card shows the card and the card's revision it ran for under the agent, such as **#1061 · a3f2c1d**: click the card to open it, or the revision to copy the whole commit id. An agent's run is for the card it is working on (its card in Active) and that card's revision (what is checked out in its folder; in a folder agents share, the card's newest commit; in a project without version control, its files' contents), unless it names others (`hive-progress --card`, `--revision`). Click the agent's name to show that agent: its pane flashes and its terminal takes the keyboard, also when it was already on screen (from the keyboard, **Tab** to the name and press **Enter** or **Space**). If you press a key or click somewhere else before the terminal appears, the keyboard stays where you put it.
- **Details:** click anywhere else on a run, or on one in **Runs** (or the chevron at the end of its row, which points down while the details are closed and up while they're open), to see everything Hive has for it: the command (with **Copy command**), the card and the whole revision it ran for (and **Tested on**, the commit that was checked out where it ran, when that isn't the card's revision: in a folder agents share, or a reviewer's checkout), the agent, project and provider, when it started and ended (how long ago; hover for the date and time in your format), how long it took against what was expected, its steps, its state and why, the exit code, a log or run record when the command named one, and a failure's summary. **Copy details** copies all of it as text, a line per field, ready to paste to the Assistant. **Open log** opens the log in its app and **Show in folder** shows it in Explorer, wherever it is; if the log has gone, Hive says so. **Show the agent** is there too; for a script's run, or one whose agent has since been removed, it says why there's no agent to show. **Escape** closes the details.
- **A failed check that holds up Review:** when a check the card must pass before Review fails, its row says so, **Blocks review for AL-12**, for as long as the failed run shows.
- **Runs as checks:** a run for a card that finishes is recorded in the card's work as a check of its revision, and its details say whether it counts: **Counts as check: typecheck**, or why it doesn't. It counts only if it was started as that check (`hive-progress --check typecheck`, which runs the command the project has for it) or its command is exactly that one; what was checked out contains the card's revision (in a folder several cards share, later commits of other cards may be in it too); the folder had no uncommitted changes when it started and ended, with the same commit checked out; and nothing changed in the folder while it ran: no file Git doesn't ignore was written (an edit undone before the end still counts as one), nothing was checked out and back, and no agent edited a file there. A build writing ignored outputs (`out/`, `node_modules`) doesn't count against it. In a project without version control, it counts when the card's declared paths are the same at the start and the end and none of the project's files was written meanwhile. Nothing waits on checks yet: they are recorded for the workflow's rules to come.
- **Hive's own runs:** when Hive merges a card itself (the merge queue), the merge checks it runs on the merged result are runs too, from **Hive (merge queue)** rather than an agent, in the card's project, named for the card, the check and the merged result's revision (*AL-12 · typecheck · a3f2c1d*). Each runs in the queue's own folder with the command the project's options have for it, one after another, stopping at the first that fails; the install before them (when the project's package files changed since the last one, or before every merge if the options say so) is a run too, named for its command. It starts from scratch: the queue's `node_modules` is removed first, then your worktree setup command runs in its clean form (`npm install` becomes `npm ci`, with the same flags; pnpm and yarn keep to their lock file). A setup command Hive has no clean form of (several commands, packages named, a flag such as `--dry-run` or `--prefix`, another tool) runs as written, and its details say so. Their details have the log (**Open log**), and say whether the check counts for the merged result: a check passes only if it counts, so one that changed the folder while it ran (an edit undone, a checkout and back) doesn't pass, and the card is tested again; after three times, its merge pauses. A check that couldn't start, or ran past **Settings → Advanced → Merge queue: longest a merge check runs** (90 minutes), counts as a failure of the machine rather than of the work: the card comes back marked so, and its builder can send the same revision back into the queue (at most twice). So does a check that failed for the network, with one of Node's and npm's network errors (`ENOTFOUND`, `EAI_AGAIN`, `ECONNRESET`, `ECONNREFUSED`, `ETIMEDOUT`) in its last 40 lines (**Settings → Advanced → Merge queue: network errors that count as temporary**), and one that exits with code **75**: a project can make its check scripts exit 75 for a failure of their own environment (a test server that didn't come up, say). A temporary failure still returns the card to its builder, marked temporary: nothing is retried by itself, but the builder may submit the same revision again, straight into the queue (at most twice in a row), instead of making a new one. The check's details say why it counted as temporary. With no setup command and no lock file, nothing is installed, and the first check's details say so. **Show the agent** says there is no agent for these runs.
- **A slot's set-up:** a project's *slots* are worktrees Hive keeps ready to lend to a card or a lane. Setting one up is a run too, from **Hive (slot slot-b)** (the slot's name), named for its command: your worktree setup command in a new slot, again when the slot's lock files (`package-lock.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `yarn.lock`…) changed since its last install, and the slot's archive command before Hive removes one. Each gets the slot's name in `HIVE_SLOT` and the first of its 10 ports in `HIVE_SLOT_PORT_BASE`, and runs at most **Settings → Advanced → Slots: longest a setup command runs** (60 minutes). A slot whose setup failed isn't lent; its run's log says why. A free slot beyond the project's builders, unused for a day, is removed (**Settings → Advanced → Remove idle slots after a day**), never one with an agent, a process, uncommitted work or work not merged in it, and not if its archive command fails.
- **Filter by project:** when the runs come from more than one project, a dropdown at the top shows **All projects**, or one project's runs (in every tab), or **Assistant and scripts** for runs that aren't a project's. Choose a project with several agents and a second dropdown narrows it to one agent. The merges and lanes on top follow it too: one project's merge, or the merge its agent holds or waits for. Each workspace remembers its choice, and the folded strip shows only those runs too, with a filter mark and how many there are in all.
- **When a run ends:** a passed one shows ✓ and the time it took for a few seconds, then leaves Pulse (it stays in **Runs**). A failed one shows ✗ for a few seconds too, then leaves Pulse, marked red in Runs, where Pulse's line counts it until a later run of it passes; the strip's and the taskbar's red stay until you've seen it (the panel open while you use Hive). A run that stops reporting for longer than expected says **Stopped reporting** (one whose agent stops says so), and goes the same way; a command run with `hive-progress` that keeps printing output counts as reporting. **Dismiss** (✕) takes a failed or stopped-reporting one off Pulse at once (it stays in Runs). A run that was interrupted (Ctrl+C) says **Stopped**, and one whose command was ended before it could say how it went (an agent stopping a preview server it ran with `hive-progress`) says **Ended without a report**: neither is a failure, so they show muted, and leave Pulse like a passed run.
- **Waiting runs:** a run that is queued, such as a test run waiting for a test slot while other heavy runs finish, says what it waits for and how long it has waited, with a still, hatched bar; it shows no time left until it starts, and the wait doesn't count against its usual time. A run that takes longer than its estimate says **finishing**, then **longer than expected**, never "0 s left".
- **Runs** shows what is running at the top, and every finished run Hive keeps (the last 200 in each workspace) docked at the bottom behind a line, as Pulse's lanes are: by day (**Today**, **Yesterday**, then the date), newest first, scrolling in their own height. They start about six and a half lanes high: drag the line above them to make them taller (up to ten lanes) or shorter, smoothly, and double-click it to go back. Each workspace remembers the height; a short window shows what fits and gives it back when it grows, while what's running keeps some room. **Today** always heads them: *No runs today* when nothing has ended today, *No runs match.* when your filters match only older runs, and *No finished runs* when there are none. Each day is grouped by card: a heading per card, with its key (click it to open the card), its title, how many runs it had and how they went (*7 runs · 1 failed*, *all passed*), and when the last one ended; the card with the newest run comes first, and runs for no card (Hive's own, your scripts) are under **Other** at the end. Every run shows, one line each: whether it passed, who ran it, what it was, how long it took and when it ended; hover it for its card, commit and copy, and click it for everything. Several can be open at once; they close again when you leave **Runs** (another tab, or folding the panel). A run tried several times the same day is one line, such as *B1 cardwork ×4 · 2 failed, then passed*, marked ⚠ when one failed before it passed and ✗ when the last failed: click it for each run. The merge queue's checks for a card are one line too, *Hive (merge queue) lint, typecheck*. **Find runs** finds them by their title, command, agent or card; **Failed** shows only failures; and you can show only a lane's runs (its cards') or an agent's; **Clear** shows them all again. The filters show the matching runs under their cards, and hide a card with none. The project filter applies too. The runs are kept when the workspace closes and when Hive restarts; runs still going at that moment aren't (their commands can't report to the new Hive), and turning the Progress panel off in Settings forgets them.
- **The taskbar:** while runs are going, Hive's taskbar button fills with their combined progress (a moving bar for runs without steps), and turns red when one fails, until you look at the panel. So you can keep an eye on a long run from another app or with Hive minimised.
- **The Assistant** can see an agent's progress too, so you can ask it how long until an agent's tests finish.

Runs come from what agents run: a tool or script reports through the Agent API (`/v1/progress`, see the Agent API reference), as the agent that started it. **Settings → General → Progress panel** turns it all off; agents can still report, and Hive ignores it.

#### Merges and lanes <a id="merges-and-lanes"></a>

When agents work in pairs, a builder and a reviewer taking cards in turn (the **card-loop** skill), the Progress panel shows how they are getting on, around the runs:

- **The merge going on**, at the top of Pulse and in **Merges**. While an agent is merging (it holds a project's merge slot), a card in the merge colour, teal, says who is merging which cards (*B1 is merging #484*), which branch goes where (`hive/b1 → main`), the step its checks are on (the agent's running progress, such as *Full test set · 113 of 146*), how long it has had the slot and has left, and who is waiting. **Release** frees a holder that is stuck, after a question. The chevron folds the card to one line; each workspace remembers it. The card goes when the merge is done, and a line under it parts it from the runs.
- **The lanes**, in Pulse right after the runs, and every one of them in **Lanes**. Each lane is a row: the pair (*B1 / R1*), what the lane is about, the card being worked with a dot for its latest run (a ring while it runs, green when it passed, a red cross when it failed; hover for which run) and how many of its cards are finished (*2/8*), then a bar with a segment for each card, in the order they are worked. Each segment shows where its card is, by colour **and** pattern, so it reads without colour too:
  - **To do:** an empty segment.
  - **Active:** blue stripes, moving.
  - **In review:** an amber outline.
  - **Approved** (reviewed, waiting to be merged): green, with a light edge.
  - **Done** (merged): a dimmer green.
  - **Blocked**, or **On Hold** waiting for you: red cross-hatching.
  - **A failed review** underlines the segment in red until the card goes back for its next round.
  - **A refused request** (workflow rules on): a small red notch in the segment's corner, when the card's latest request was refused (an agent submitting before its checks passed, say, or starting a lane's card before the one before it is Done). Its hover says why and what to do next, as the agent was told, and how many times in a row; it clears when the card next moves.
  - **A stale pass:** amber dashed edges on a passed card whose work changed after its review into something that isn't the same change. Its hover says *Pass was for a3f2c1d; now at b71e09a*; the mark goes when it is submitted again, or when its branch is put back at the revision that passed.
  - **A move past its rules** (yours or the Assistant's): a small dot in the corner; its hover says who moved it, past which rules, and why.
  - **Old rules:** a small hollow square in the bottom corner, on a card started before its project's workflow options changed. It keeps the options it started with until it's Done, unless you apply the new ones to it when you save them.

  **Stages and the legend.** A segment's colour and pattern is its card's category, as above. Its hover and what a screen reader says name the card's **stage**, as the project's options make them and by its own names if you renamed them: *Merging* for a card in Hive's merge queue, which sits in Approved. Where a category has two stages (Approved and Merging), the hover lists both with a thin line between them and the card's own in bold. Hover over the **Lanes** bar, or Tab to it, for the legend: each category with its colour and pattern and the stages in it of the lanes' projects (Esc closes it). The **Lanes** tab has a **Legend** button that shows the same.

  **The project and old rules.** A lane's header names its project: click it to open that project's **Version Control** tab at its workflow options. When you change a project's options while a lane runs, the lane keeps the ones it started with until it ends (its next cards too), and its row shows **old rules**. Hover over it for what that means: new lanes and cards use the new options. The mark goes when the lane ends. Renaming a stage changes no rules, so it never shows old rules. With **Workflow rules are enforced** off (**Settings → Advanced**), the marks still show, and say that the rules aren't enforced now.

  **Solo projects.** A project whose workflow preset is Solo (or options changed from Solo) works without card loops, so Pulse shows no lanes for it unless one of its lanes is running. Its merged and abandoned lanes are still in the **Lanes** tab, with **Start Lane…**.

  **The Lanes tab.** Every lane, stacked from the bottom and growing upwards until they fill the tab, then scrolling. The chevron before a lane's name opens its cards in order: each with its state (✓ done, ● being worked on, an eye in review, ○ to do, ⏸ waiting for you), its key (click it to open the card), its round (*r2* after one failed review), how long it has taken so far or took, and a done card's merge commit (click it to copy the whole commit). A change to a running lane's list that waits for its card being worked shows under the lane's bar, saying what it will do once that card is Done: *Next, once HI-12 is Done: HI-14 added, HI-9 moves to HB6's lane* (cards added, taken off, moved to another running lane, or a new order); it goes once it applies. An ended lane shows its cards as it left them, with only what happened before it ended. They fold again when you leave the tab.

  **How long a lane has waited.** After why a lane waits, its header says for how long, from when Hive saw the wait begin (a new reason or card starts it again; a comment doesn't). *23 min+* means it was already waiting when Hive started, so it has waited at least that long. The time turns amber when a lane has waited for you over an hour, or for a reviewer over 15 minutes: **Settings → Advanced → Lanes: a long wait for you** and **a long wait for a reviewer** change those.

  Hover a segment for the card, where it is and its round (*#405 Built-in loop wakes · Review, round 2*), and what its marks mean: a red underline the review that failed and the round it waits for, with the reviewer's one-line gist (*#650: review failed (round 4): waiting for round 5*), red hatching why the card is blocked or On Hold. Under that, what the card's work says, in a few lines: the revision under review (*Under review: a3f2c1d*), the last verdict and the revision it was given on (*Last verdict: failed on a3f2c1d*, and *which no longer counts* once there is a newer change or decision), how many checks passed and failed on that revision, and for a Done card the commit that merged it. A merged or abandoned lane's cards show only the commit the lane merged, never what was done to them later in another lane. Click it to open the card for the rest. When a lane has more cards than fit, its finished ones fold into one **✓ n** segment at the start. A paused lane (a card waiting for you, and nothing else being worked), a merged one and an abandoned one (an agent of it removed) say so. With workflow rules on, a lane also says **why it waits**, in its header (where its name was; hover for all of it): *waiting for you (approval): AL-3*, *waiting: AL-1 not merged* (one card at a time: the next starts once this one is Done), *waiting for a reviewer on AL-2*, *stale: AL-2 must be submitted again*, *list changes after AL-2*, a round limit, or a card blocked or On Hold; the **Lanes** bar counts the lanes waiting for you. A no-code card's hover says so, with no commits to show. A merged or abandoned lane is greyed and still, and shows its cards as they were when it stopped (a lane that stopped before Hive kept that shows dashed outlines: not recorded), so a card another lane has taken on never shows as worked in two lanes. Running lanes come first, newest first, then paused ones; merged and abandoned lanes go to the bottom in the order they stopped, so the one that stopped last is always last. **To put the running lanes in your own order, drag one up or down** by its row: a line shows where it will go, and near the top or bottom of the list it scrolls. With the keyboard, Tab to a lane and press **Alt+Up** or **Alt+Down**; a lane's right-click menu has **Move Up**, **Move Down** and **Usual Order**, which goes back to newest first. Each workspace remembers your order, after a restart too. A lane started later comes in on top, above the ones you moved, and a lane that stops leaves your order for the bottom. The lanes sit at the bottom of the panel and grow upward; up to four show, then they scroll. To see more, **drag the line above Lanes** up (down for fewer): it moves a whole lane at a time, up to all your lanes, and stops where the runs above still have room. In a smaller window it shows the whole lanes that fit, and goes back to your choice when there is room. Double-click the line to go back to four. With the keyboard, Tab to the line and press Up or Down. Each workspace remembers how many you chose, after a restart too. Click **Lanes** to fold them down to that bar (each workspace remembers it); opening them again keeps the height you chose. The project filter applies to lanes too.

- **Start a lane yourself**, without the Assistant: right-click the **Todo** column and choose **Start Lane with These Cards…** (or **Start Lane with It…** on a Todo card, **Start Lane…** in the board's ⋯ menu, or the button in the Progress panel's **Lanes** tab). Choose the **builder** and the **reviewer** (a project whose rules have a reviewer agent review the work needs one), put the cards in the order they are worked (up and down, take one out, or add one from Todo), and give it a few words and a note for the agents if you like. **Start Lane** records the lane and starts both agents, each in a new conversation with a brief: the cards in order, where the builder works, what the project's rules ask (the checks before review, who merges, your OK), how to move the cards, and your note. Their earlier conversations are kept in the Sessions tab. An agent busy now (working, waiting for you) can't start a new conversation: the dialog says so, and **Wait and Start** waits until both are idle, then starts. One that turns busy just as the lane starts (reading a message Hive had just given it, say) starts in its new conversation as soon as it is free: the lane's row says *waiting: B1 starts in a new conversation once it is free*, and its cards wait for it. If it isn't free within 10 minutes (Settings → Advanced → *Lanes: longest Hive waits to start a busy agent afresh*), or Hive closed while starting it, Hive stops trying and tells you: choose **Retry New Conversation** on the lane's row, or end the lane. Hive never interrupts its turn for it, and never gives the lane's cards to its old conversation.
- **End or abandon a lane** from its row: right-click it for **End Lane** (the loop is over; the cards stay where they are) and **Abandon Lane…** (it stops here, unmerged), which asks first.

With no lanes and no merge going on, the panel is just the runs, as before.

#### Slots, pools and copies in the Progress panel <a id="slots-pools-and-copies-in-the-progress-panel"></a>

- **A lane's slot:** its row names the slot its builder works in, *HI-B1 / HI-R1 · slot-b* (or the builder's own worktree from before 1.0, *· hb2*). Click it for Version Control → **Branches & remotes**, where the project's slots are listed. Point at the row: its hover says whether the slot is ready or being prepared, since when the lane has had it, and the branch of the card being built there.
- **A pool lane:** *HI-B1 / pool*. Its hover says who reviews the current card now and who gave its last verdict.
- **A reviewer's or isolated check in a copy:** the run reads *AM-12 · a3f2c1d · in copy-2* under it while it runs (in **Runs**, in its hover): a check of that card's revision, not of the builder's slot. Its details name the copy and the folder it ran in.
- **Hive setting up a slot** (its setup command, an install) is a run of **Hive (slot slot-b)**, each slot its own entry in the Runs tab's **Agent** filter. **Setting up a review copy** is a run of the reviewer whose review it is for (*HR6*, its details and hover saying *in copy-1*), so the filter lists each reviewer once; with no reviewer yet, it is a run of **Hive (review copies)**.
- **Waiting:** *waiting for a slot* and *waiting for a check copy* pause a lane; *waiting: preparing slot-b* doesn't. Click the reason to see what it waits for: the slots, or the runs.
- A run's folder is where it ran, kept as it was, even after its agent moves to another slot.

#### A reviewer pool <a id="a-reviewer-pool"></a>

Lanes can share their reviewers instead of each having its own. A project whose workflow option **Reviewers** is **A shared pool** (set it in **Version Control → Workflow → Review**; the **Team 6 + pool** template suggests it, and the Hive Assistant sets it as `project.workflow.reviewerMode`) offers **The reviewer pool (HI-R1 and HI-R2)** in **Start Lane**'s reviewer list. Its agents are the project's reviewers and either-role agents. A lane started with it has no reviewer of its own, so only its builder starts, and its brief says the pool reviews its cards. The Hive Assistant can start a pool lane too, when you ask it to.

- **Each card submitted** in a pool lane goes to the next free reviewer, oldest submission first, across all the pool's lanes. Free means: its role reviews, it isn't in a turn, waiting for you or running something, it isn't reviewing another card or checking a design, and it isn't building in a slot (an either-role agent mid-card is left alone until its card is Done). A reviewer that ended its turn watching a card still counts as free: Hive tells it of the card it's given, and its watch carries on. A reviewer never gets a card it built.
- **Hive tells it**: *#12 (HI-B1's card) is in Review: you review it from the pool*, typed into its conversation, or it is started for the review in the project folder (it reviews in a review copy).
- **Nobody free:** the card waits, and its lane says *waiting for a reviewer*. A verdict frees the reviewer for the next card.
- **A design checked before building:** a pool lane's builder can post its design for a check before it builds (a card loop's design step), as in a lane with its own reviewer. The design goes to the next free reviewer the way a submission does (its lane says *waiting for a reviewer* until one takes it), and the reviewer who answers it then reviews the card when it is submitted, so the same eyes see the design and the build. If that reviewer is busy then, the next free one takes the card.
- **Two reviewers per card** (**Reviewers per card: 2**): a second reviewer, another agent, gets the card once the first has passed it.
- **A card outside a lane** (one started on its builder alone, or whose lane has ended) that reaches Review in a project whose work an agent reviews is given out the same way, whichever **Reviewers** option the project has: a free running reviewer is told *#12 (HI-B1's card) is in Review, outside a lane: you review it*, or a stopped one is started for it. Nobody, the Hive Assistant included, needs to start a reviewer for it.
- With **Workflow rules are enforced** off (Settings → Advanced), nothing is given out: name a reviewer for each lane yourself.

#### The merge queue in the Progress panel <a id="the-merge-queue-in-the-progress-panel"></a>

When Hive merges a project's cards itself (**Who merges: Hive merges**, the merge queue), the **Merges** tab has a card for each target the queue merges into (*Merge queue · main*), beside any merge slot an agent holds, by project and then target. It says, each with its own icon and words:

- **Testing now:** the card, the target at the commit it is tested on, and the step: *AL-61 + main @ 1cd2592 → check: unit* (the test merge, the merge checks and which one runs, or merging into the target), and how long so far against how long the target's merge checks usually take (the middle of its last five merges' times, from Hive's own runs; none until it has merged once). The check itself is a run in **Runs**, from **Hive (merge queue)**, with its log.
- **Waiting:** the cards queued, in order (*AL-107 and AL-108 waiting*), each with the revision that was approved (click it to copy the whole commit id), a link to the card, and **Remove**, which asks first, then takes the card out of the queue: back to Approved, not merged, and its builder is told.
- **Merged:** *✓ AL-61 merged as 9e1f0aa* for a few seconds. The merge stays in the card's work.
- **Returned:** *✗ AL-108 returned to B1: conflict in tasks.ts* (or *checks failed: unit*, *a hook failed*), with **Open card** and **Show log** (the failed check's log, or what git or the hook said). Hive also says so in a comment on the card (*Came back from the merge queue: a conflict with main (in tasks.ts)*), and its builder is told why and what to do next, ahead of anything else about the card. It stays until you've seen it, the **Merges** tab showing it (its project in the filter, no dialog over it) while you use Hive, as a failed run does, then goes a few seconds later. When many are waiting, the card shows them a page at a time and says how many more there are (*12 more returned cards*): they come up as you see the ones shown.
- **Paused:** why, in a sentence (paused by you or by the Assistant; files with changes in the folder the target is checked out in; signing that waits for your passphrase; the workflow's rules off; *Needs you*, with **Open card**…), and **Resume queue**. **Pause queue** stops the queue before its next card: a card in its merge checks finishes them and waits, unmerged. **Resume queue** looks again at what Hive can see of the cause first, and says so if it still holds. A git command that fails for a moment (another program holding a lock, a busy PC) doesn't pause the queue: before anything is merged, Hive tries the card again a few times, a moment apart, and pauses only if git keeps failing, saying what git said. If you pause the queue, or change the card, meanwhile, that stands.
- **Run by** another Hive, when the dev and the installed Hive share the workspace and the other one runs this queue. If that Hive ends unexpectedly (a crash, say), this one takes the queue over within about half a minute: what the other was checking is ended and tested again here, in a folder of its own, and merged once.
- **Queue in Version Control** opens the project's Version Control tab at its **Merge queue**.

##### Recent merges <a id="recent-merges"></a>

Under each queue's card (and under a merge slot an agent holds for that project, so what merges now comes first), **Recent merges** lists what the queue did lately, newest first, up to 20 rows:

- **✓ Merged**, with the merge commit (click it to copy the whole commit id).
- **✗ Returned**, with why in a few words: *conflict in tasks.ts*, *checks failed: unit*, its focused tests.
- **⊘ Taken out**, with why: *Remove from queue*, put On Hold, merged outside Hive…
- **⏸ Paused**, with why (*by you*, *the push failed*, *needs you*…; point at it for the whole sentence), and how long it lasted, or *still paused*.

Each row has the card's key and title, the time (point at it for how long ago), and how long the queue took over the card: from its first merge check to the outcome, or the merge alone when the target has no merge checks. A card returned before anything ran (a conflict at its test merge) shows no time. When the project pushes the target after merging (**Push the target branch after merging**), a merge also says **Pushed**, **Not pushed yet**, or **Not pushed** and why (pushing was turned off, or the branch no longer had it); an older merge that Hive has no record of says **Push not recorded**. Click a row to open its card. The list follows the panel's project and agent filters (an agent's cards; a pause of the whole queue always shows), updates as the queue works, and says *No merges yet.* when there's nothing to show. **More in Version Control** opens the full history.

The queue's card shows while the queue has something: a card testing, waiting or paused, a return you haven't seen, a merge of the last few seconds, a Hive running it, or a card still in flight under rules that have Hive merge into that target. With nothing going on, it shows, *Nothing queued.*, while the project's options have Hive merge. So when you switch the next cards to **The agent merges** (or a project to no version control), the cards already going through the queue keep going, and its card stays, with Pause, Resume and Remove, until they are merged or out of the queue and you've seen how they ended. Pulse has a line for a queue testing or paused, *main: testing AL-61 · 2 queued*, which opens **Merges**. Each change is said to a screen reader once, politely: *AL-61 merged*, *AL-108 returned: conflict in tasks.ts*, the queue paused or resumed. The project filter applies to the queues too: under **All projects**, a project you aren't working on shows its queue only while it's doing something (a card testing, waiting or returned, a pause, or a merge in the last hour); choose the project in the filter to see its queue anyway (the filter shows whenever a queue is left out, even for one project). **Runs**' agent filter has **Hive (merge queue)** for the queue's checks.

#### Show progress for any command <a id="show-progress-for-any-command"></a>

Any command can show in the panel by running it with **`hive-progress`** in front, for example `hive-progress -- npm test`. Agents do this by themselves for tests, builds and other commands they expect to take more than about 30 seconds, in the background too, so you see them without asking. To have them do it only when you ask, turn off **Settings → General → Agents show long commands in the Progress panel** (agents started or restarted afterwards follow it; with the Progress panel off, they don't use it either). The panel shows the command under the agent's name with the time it has taken and, from the second time, about how long is left (from how long the same command took before in that folder; Hive remembers up to 1,000 commands, forgetting those run only once first). The command's output and result are unchanged, and outside Hive it simply runs the command.

A command shows real steps when it prints lines like `##hive-progress step=4 total=12 name=carddialog`; any project's test runner can print them (see the Agent API reference). A total printed after a slow start still counts: the bar moves without steps until then, and shows them from then on. `hive-progress` is on the path of every session Hive starts, in Claude Code's and Codex's shells. A command that reports its own progress, such as Hive's own test runners, still shows as one row when an agent wraps it.

Stopping a `hive-progress` command (Ctrl+C where it gets one) gives it 5 seconds to end by itself, then ends it with everything it started, such as the server under an `npm` or `.cmd` command. Hive checks when each of those processes started, so a process that only looks like the command's (an older one whose own parent had the same process number before it ended) is left running. If Windows won't list the processes, only the command itself is ended.

**A check that doesn't count says why.** When a `hive-progress --check` run passes but doesn't count for its card, it says so in one line as it ends, with the reason and what to do: for example *not counted as check typecheck: files changed in the folder while it ran (src/a.ts). Run it again with nothing else writing in the folder, or with --isolated*. The exit code stays the command's. A submit refused because that check hasn't passed gives the same reason, so an agent isn't left running the same command again.

**A check when Hive is slow.** `hive-progress --check` asks Hive for the check's command before running it, and waits up to 15 seconds for the answer (**Settings → Advanced → hive-progress: Hive's reply time**, for sessions started afterwards, up to 2 minutes). With no answer it asks once more, then runs nothing and says *Hive is slow to answer* (try again, or give it longer); a refused connection says *Hive couldn't be reached* at once, adding that a sandbox blocking local network access, as Claude Code's Bash sandbox does, has the same effect. If Hive doesn't confirm the check's result as it ends, hive-progress sends it once more, and says when the check may not have been recorded, so an agent runs it again rather than finding out at submit.

**A check in a copy of its own.** In a folder several agents share, another agent's edit while a check runs makes it not count (its details say files changed while it ran). Run it isolated instead: `hive-progress --check typecheck --isolated` runs the project's command for that check in a **check copy**, a folder Hive keeps at exactly the card's revision (`<workspace>.worktrees/<project>/copies/copy-1`), which nothing else writes, so it counts whatever happens in the shared folder. Its run's details name the copy. When every copy is in use, it says *waiting for a check copy* and runs once one is free (it asks every 2 seconds, less often after a minute, up to every 10 seconds; Performance counts these asks as waits, not failures); a copy is used again only once nothing is left running in it. **Stop** (the square on its row in the Progress panel) ends the check and everything it started, also processes it left running in the background, and the run shows as stopped. A project keeps the larger of two copies and its reviewers plus one; **Settings → Advanced → Copies: wait for one to be free** (10 minutes) is how long a check waits before it says which copy is still in use, and by what. Copies set themselves up with your setup command, shown as **Hive (copy copy-1)** runs. A project with no version control has no check copies: run the check again once nobody is changing the card's files.

#### Review copies <a id="review-copies"></a>

A reviewer reviews a card's work in a **review copy**: a folder from the same pool as check copies (`<workspace>.worktrees/<project>/copies/copy-2`), at exactly the revision the builder submitted, which nothing else writes. When it starts its review, Hive leases it a copy and its reply says where: *Review in …\copies\copy-2 at a3f2c1d: read and run commands there, with it as their working directory; your session stays where it is.* So the builder can carry on in its own folder while the review reads and tests what was submitted, and a check the reviewer runs there (`hive-progress --check typecheck`) counts for that submission. Its verdict returns the copy, and so does its review ending otherwise (the card moving on, or the reviewer's session ending); a copy is used again only once nothing is left running in it. When every copy is in use, the card's history says *R1 is waiting for a review copy*, the reviewer is told which copy is likely to come free first and what still holds it, and Hive tells it once one is free. A copy an isolated check used is free again within seconds of the check ending, even while its builder carries on working. A review that waits more than five minutes for a copy shows as stalled to whoever watches the lane.

The reviewer's session stays in the project folder, so its conversation always resumes there. Its launch is given the project's copies folder, one fixed path, as each coding agent allows on its command line, never in its own settings: **Claude Code** and **GitHub Copilot** `--add-dir` (Copilot's Accept edits approves its edits there as in its own folder), **Codex** `--add-dir`, which adds the folder to the writable roots of your own Codex settings rather than replacing them. Reviewers and agents that do either get it whenever they start in the project folder; builders, and an agent that does either while it builds in its slot, don't. Builds and tests in the copy run within the agent's own permission mode. **Settings → Advanced → Reviewers review in review copies** turns this off: reviewers then review the builder's folder, as before, and sessions started afterwards aren't given the folder. With **Workflow rules are enforced** off, no review copy is leased either; a copy a reviewer already has is handed back as usual when its review ends.

## Task board <a id="task-board"></a>
