The **Task Board** (in the activity bar, or **Ctrl+Shift+J**) is the workspace's list of work, as cards in six columns: **On Hold**, **Todo**, **Active**, **Review**, **Approved** and **Done**. (Before 1.0, Active and Approved were called Doing and Passed; agents and the Agent API still name them by their ids, `doing` and `passed`, and read a card's history in those words: "Moved to Doing" where the card dialog says "Moved to Active".) You, the Hive Assistant and the agents all use it, so it's where you can see at a glance what is planned, what is being worked on and what is waiting for you.

- **Card keys.** Each project has a short key of 2 to 4 letters (HI, AM), and its cards are numbered in it: **HI-12**. See [Project keys](#project-keys).
- **Cards.** Each has a name (HI-12; #12 for a card about the whole workspace), a title, a description (Markdown: what to do and how to tell it's done), a project, and optionally the agent working on it. Add labels, mark it **blocked** with a reason (it shows in red; a long one shows its first three lines on the card, and all of it when you hover it or open the card), list the cards it **depends on** and related ones, and comment. With workflow rules on, a card can't start until the cards it depends on are Done with their work in its target; one you (or the Hive Assistant, with your reason) move to Done by an override counts as done, and Hive notes on each waiting card that it starts without that card's work. Its history shows who changed what and when: you, the Assistant, or an agent. **New Card** (or **Ctrl+Alt+T**) adds one. An open card can be moved out of the way, as any dialog can: drag it by its header (Escape while dragging puts it back; see [Moving dialogs](#moving-dialogs)).
- **Short titles from agents.** Agents and the Assistant keep a card's title short (about 70 characters, what the card is for) and put the detail in its description, as a long title is cut in git's history (it becomes the merge commit's subject) and on the board. Hive never refuses a long one: it tells the agent a shorter one would be better, past **Settings → Advanced → Suggest a shorter title past** (80 characters; 0 turns it off). Titles you write yourself are never questioned.
- **Decisions.** What you decide about a card (scope, wording, a default, "option B") goes in its **Decisions**, pinned above the comments, not in a comment where it gets buried. Add one at the bottom of the list (**Add Decision**, or Ctrl+Enter); change or remove one with its pencil and bin. Each says who wrote it down: you, or the Assistant or an agent you told (it is still your decision; only you change or remove one), and **new since start** when it came after work on the card began. Agents read a card's decisions when they start and again before they move it to Review, and follow them where the description says otherwise; reviewers fail work that ignores one. Recording one while an agent works on or reviews the card doesn't interrupt it: its next reply from a Hive tool says the card has a new decision, and it checks at its next step; a reviewer reads the card again at each round. With review, or with Hive or the agent merging, Hive refuses an agent's submit or verdict while the card has a decision it hasn't read (with every option off, and Solo, a submit stays free). An agent waiting for your answer (it asked you something and its turn ended) hears of it too, so you don't have to paste your answer into its pane (agents take a card, moving it to Active, before they ask you about it, so a question about a card in Todo counts too): once nothing is in the way (it isn't working, showing a question or permission prompt, or watching cards, and you aren't typing in its terminal), Hive rings its doorbell, and it reads the line in its inbox (*"[Hive] #392 has 2 new decisions from the user: read them (hive_read_task) and carry on from where you asked."*). Decisions recorded together come in one line, about 15 seconds after the last; each is told once, and the card's history says whom Hive told. A card with decisions shows how many on its tile (hover for them) and on the agent's card chip. An open card can be moved out of the way, as any dialog can: drag it by its header (Escape while dragging puts it back; see [Moving dialogs](#moving-dialogs)).
- **The columns.** **On Hold** is for work you've parked: nobody picks it up until you move it to **Todo**, and only you and the Assistant put cards there (an agent that tries is told to ask you). **Todo** is ready to start, **Active** is being worked on and **Review** is waiting for its review. A card that passes its review goes to **Approved**: reviewed, but not merged yet. **Done** means merged: the agent that built it moves it there once its branch is merged, or you do. A **blocked** card stays where it is, marked in red, in any column. Cards On Hold or in Approved are never called stalled.
- **Stages and their names.** From 1.0 each column is a *stage* of the board, in one of six fixed categories (On Hold, Todo, Active, Review, Approved, Done). On Hold, Todo, Active and Done always show; Review and Approved show only when a shown project's options use them, or while a card is still in them after you changed the options (with **Workflow rules are enforced** off in Settings → Advanced, all six show). With several projects, the board shows every stage any of them uses. **Merging** has a column of its own under the Approved heading: cards come into it through Hive's merge queue, never by dragging (it takes no drop), and **Remove from queue** takes one out. The toolbar shows the project's **Workflow: Git · Team** (with all projects, **Workflows** lists each one's), which opens its Version Control tab at Workflow. Your project's workflow options decide which stages exist: **Review** only when work is reviewed, **Approved** when a review or your OK before merging approves the work and something follows it (a merge, or in a project without version control your OK after the reviewer agent's), and **Merging**, inside Approved, when Hive merges. A project can give its stages names of its own (**Stage names**; for now the Assistant sets them: *"call Active Building in web"*), each at most 24 characters and different from every other stage's name, in English and in the language Hive shows. A name is only a name: renaming a stage changes no rule, applies at once to every card, and agents keep using the ids. The board shows a project's names when it shows that project alone, or when every project shown names the stage alike; otherwise the default name, and hovering a card's project says what its project calls the stage.
- **Tidying the board.** Ask the Assistant to *"tidy the board"*: it links cards that report the same problem and marks the extras for you to archive (it never archives or deletes), records answers you gave in chat or in an agent's pane as **Decisions** on their cards, and points out blocked reasons that are out of date (what they wait for has shipped or been answered). It leaves On Hold and Done alone.
- **Fold what you don't need.** The **«** in a column's header collapses it sideways to a narrow strip (its **»** opens it again) with its name and how many cards it has; the other columns widen to fill the board, and a click on the strip (or Enter) opens it again. Drop a card on a strip and it goes to the top of that column, even while a search hides some of its cards. Each card has an arrow too, which folds it to one line: its number, its title, its agent's dot and any blocked or stalled marker; a folded card still opens with a click and drags like any other. A column's **⋯** (or right-click its header) has **Collapse All Cards** and **Expand All Cards**. Hive remembers which columns and cards you folded, for each workspace, after a restart too; a new card starts open.
- **Moving cards.** Drag a card to another column, or within one to reorder it, or right-click it for **Move to**. In a long column, hold the card near the top or bottom of the column and it scrolls until you can drop the card where you want, even at the very top or bottom. Moving a card into **Active** from another column (dragging it, **Move to → Active**, or changing **Column** in the card dialog and saving) asks who works on it: **Nobody yet** (it goes to Active with no agent, also taking it from the agent it had), **Assign an agent** (any agent of its project, busy ones too; nothing is sent to it and nothing starts), or **Assign and start**, which starts the card as **Start…** does. The card's agent is the default. **Cancel** leaves the card where it was, with its agent; in the card dialog your other changes stay unsaved until you choose. Reordering within Active doesn't ask. Ask the Assistant to move a card to Active and it asks the same, unless you've said (*"have Claude start #12"*, *"assign #12 to Claude without starting it"*). A column's order is its priority, top first. Agents and the Assistant can put cards in order too: ask *"put the bugs at the top of Todo"* or *"prioritise Todo"* and the board changes as you watch; each card they move says so in its history. The order of Done stays yours. A card in Active shows what its agent is doing right now (working, waiting for you, finished…); a green edge means its agent has finished and the card is waiting for you to look. Nothing moves by itself: agents move their cards to Review when they're done.
- **What each agent is working on.** An agent with a card in Active shows it as a small chip: `HI-4` (+1 when it has more, an eye when it is reviewing one) in its pane header, in the summary chip's list and in the Assistant's workspace list, so it takes little room; hover it (or the agent's name) for each card's number and title. The Overviews' running lists show the title too. Click the chip to open the card. Hover the project in the sidebar to see each agent's card. The Sessions tab shows which cards a session **worked on**.
- **Colours.** Each column has its own colour (On Hold grey, Todo slate blue, Active blue, Review purple, Approved teal, Done green) on its heading and as a light tint on its cards, so a card's colour shows where it is. Change them in **Settings → Board → Column colours**, or turn them off with **Colour columns**.
- **Each agent works on its project's cards.** An agent sees and changes only its own project's cards: another project's are for that project's agents, the Assistant and you. A card it adds is its project's, and a card linked to another project's shows that card's number only. The Assistant and your board see everything. Moving a card to another project takes it from its agent (its history says whose it was), so give it to one of the new project's agents afterwards; the card dialog does that for you when you change both. Ask an agent to *"check the latest comment on #65"* and it reads just that comment, not the whole card.
- **Review, Approved, then Done.** An agent you give a card to in its conversation (*"task #59 please"*) moves it to Active when it starts, also when the card is back from Review, Approved or Done with more to do; when that work is done it goes to Review, even if it was in Done before. Agents move a card to Review when they finish its work; a reviewer moves it to Approved when it passes. Done means merged: an agent moves its cards there after merging its work, or when you ask (*"merged, move #65 to done"*); the Assistant too. Every move is in the card's history with who made it, and you can move a card back out of Done at any time, or ask an agent to. Putting Done in order, archiving and deleting stay yours. Archive a card when you no longer need to see it; deleting sends it to the Recycle Bin. **Archived (n)** in the board's toolbar shows the archived cards as a table, the most recently archived first: sort by any heading, filter by number, title, agent, project, label or the column a card was archived from, and choose how many rows a page shows. The board's search narrows it too. Click a row to open the card. **Unarchive** brings a card back to the end of the column it came from; tick several (or the box in the heading, for every card the filters show) and **Unarchive Selected** brings them all back. **Archive a whole column, or the whole board.** A column's **⋯** has **Archive All in Done (23)…** (every column, with its count), and the **⋯** beside **New Card** has **Archive All Cards…**, which counts the cards per column before you confirm. Cards an agent is on (working on in Active, reviewing, or waiting on, unless the card is in Done: nothing more comes of it, and Hive ends the wait) are left where they are and listed; tick **Also archive cards agents are working on** to take them too (their agents lose the card). If every card leaves the column while the dialog is open (an agent moved it on, say), it says none is left to archive. While a search or the sidebar's project hides some cards, it reads **Archive the 5 Shown (of 23)…** and archives only the cards you can see. Afterwards **Archived 23 cards** offers **Undo** for a few seconds, which puts them back in their columns, in their order; later, pick the batch in **Archived** → **Batch** and **Unarchive this batch** does the same (a card whose neighbours have gone goes to the top of its column). A card an agent picks up while you confirm stays on the board, and the toast says so; a card Hive can't bring back (its file open in another program, say) stays archived with its batch, so you can try again.A card in Active that nobody is working on (no agent has it, or its agent was removed or isn't running) shows as **stalled**, in amber (an agent still starting, its slot being prepared, isn't; one whose start failed shows why on the card: *HB5's start failed: …*), and the board's sidebar lists these under **Stalled**; start it again or move it back. An agent that moves a card into Active takes it, so its card doesn't show as stalled: also one another agent had in Todo, Review, Approved or Done (the history says so), but not one already in Active with someone else. When you remove an agent that still has open cards, Hive asks whether to move them back (Active ones to Todo, with nobody) or leave them. The Assistant points out stalled cards when it looks at the board and suggests who could take them, but asks before changing anything. Cards in Done are archived for you after 14 days there; change that, or turn it off with 0, in **Settings → Board → Archive Done cards after**.
- **An agent reviews a card.** Ask an agent to review one (*"review #87"*): the card stays in **Review** with the agent that did the work, and shows **Reviewing: Codex** while it does (in amber if the reviewer has stopped or been removed). The reviewer's tab shows the card with an eye. Its verdict goes on the card as a comment. A card that passes goes to **Approved**; one that fails stays in Review for its fixes. One agent reviews a card at a time, and the review ends by itself if the reviewer stops or the card is moved (one that stopped as Hive or its window closed the workspace: as the workspace opens again). If the review finds things to fix, ask an agent to fix them: that is work on the card, which moves it to Active.
- **Start.** **Start…** on a card gives it to an agent of its project, with the card as its prompt, and moves it to Active. If you've changed the card, the button reads **Save and Start…**: your changes are saved first, so the agent gets the card as you see it. Choose an agent that is stopped (it starts a new conversation on the card) or idle (it gets the card as its next message), a new agent, or a new agent in its own worktree. A new agent runs on the **Connection** you choose for it (grouped by account, as in Add Agent); the first usable one in your order of accounts is chosen to begin with, and its CLI follows from it. The agent gets the card and Hive's **work-on-card** skill, which tells it to keep the card up to date and move it to Review when it's done. You can start a card in Review or Done again for more work: it moves to Active, the agent is told it's back for more work and gets the card's latest comment (the feedback it came back with), and it goes to Review when that's done. Asked to have an agent act on review feedback, the Assistant does the same, with a note saying what to do.
- **Nothing is lost by closing.** Closing a card with unsaved changes, a half-written comment or decision, or a change to a decision asks **Keep Editing** or **Discard**. **Save** keeps everything you've typed in the card: it records the decision you're writing and posts the comment you're writing too, each once. While a box has text, its **Add Decision** or **Comment** button is highlighted and says so (*Save adds it too*); use the button, or Ctrl+Enter in the box, to add just that one and keep the card open.
- **Read first.** A card can point its agent at the parts of the docs it needs, instead of a whole long plan: in the card's **Read first**, one a line, a Markdown file and a heading, `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md#core-contracts`, or a shared note, `shared:plans/design.md#extraction-lanes` (the heading in lower case with hyphens, as an agent's **hive_doc_outline** lists it; a file alone means all of it; never a line number, which breaks as the doc changes). Each shows as a link: click it to read that section in Hive, then **Open in Files** (at the heading) or **Open in Shared Notes**. When the card starts, the agent's prompt lists them with each section's size under **Read these first**, not the sections themselves, and the agent reads each with **hive_read_section**. Agents and the Assistant set them too (`context` in their card tools). Up to ten a card. Hive checks them when you save them and when the card starts: one whose file is gone, whose heading was renamed, or whose heading text now appears twice shows on the card (**Read first no longer resolves**, why on hover), in the dialog with the closest headings, and in the agent's prompt, so it is never silently left out.
- **One project.** The sidebar filters the board by project and lists the cards waiting for review; the badge on the activity bar counts them. Each project also has a **Tasks** tab with its own cards.
- **With the Assistant.** Ask it to plan work as cards, start them on agents and follow them: *"split the login rework into cards for web and start the first two"*. Asked only to move a card to Active, it asks whether you want nobody on it, an agent on it, or an agent started on it; nobody on it is kept on the card with your reason. Agents can add cards for follow-up work they find instead of doing it unasked.

### The card's work <a id="the-cards-work"></a>

From Hive 1.0, a card records its **work** as it goes: what its builder submitted for review, the verdicts and checks on it, and how it was merged. Open a card to see it in its **Work** section, below the decisions:

- **Where it works:** its **Branch** (in a Git project), the **Base** revision it started from, its **Folder** (the project folder, or a folder of its own) and its **Target** (where it goes once approved, such as main).
- **Revisions, newest first:** each revision submitted for review, with its round, the agent who submitted it and when. Click a revision to copy it whole; **1 file changed** lists its files, and a click on one opens it in the project's Files tab. A file opens in the folder the work was done in; if no agent has that folder any more, its files are listed as text, saying so. What Hive couldn't read when the work was submitted (its revision, its files) says so, with why: never *No files changed*. Under it, each reviewer's verdict (*passed* or *failed*, and *no longer counts* once a newer revision or decision came since) and each check run on it (*not counted* when it doesn't count for that revision: hover it for why).
- **A card worked in the project folder** (no slots) shares its branch with the folder's other cards, so its work is the commits made on that branch for it: those whose message names it (`Hive-Card: HI-12`), and, while it is the folder's only unfinished card, those that name no card. A branch merged into the folder's branch keeps its commits with their own cards; only the merge commit is noted, by the same rule.
- **Marks:** **Stale** when the pass was for an earlier revision that isn't the same change as the card's now, or a decision came after it; a pass that **carries over** to a later revision with the same changes; **merged outside the rules**, **merged in part**, **no longer in** its target, or moved on by an override.
- **How it was merged:** merged, squashed or already in its target, with the target's revision; *merged outside Hive* when that happened in a terminal or another tool. A new commit on the target is looked at straight away; a change only to git's config or `.gitattributes` (a merge driver, say), with the same commits, can take up to 30 seconds to change whether the card's work counts as in its target, there and for deleting its branch or freeing its slot. A project without version control shows when its work was accepted.
- **The merge queue**, for a card Hive merges (its own rules decide, whatever the project's options are now): **queued** for its target, with its place in line and the approved revision; **testing now**, with the step (the test merge, the check running) and **Show the run**; **merging** into the target. A card the queue **returned** says why: the files of a conflict, the check that failed with the last lines of its output and **Show log**, a hook's output. Each file of a conflict says how many conflicts git left in it; click it to see them, read-only, as the queue's test merge had them: a few lines around each, the target's side and the card's under their names (and the common ancestor's, if your git writes it). A file Hive didn't read says why (a link, not text, too large to read whole…), and one it read only in part says there may be more. The agent building the card is told where each file's conflicts are, so it needn't merge again to find them. **Retry merge…** puts it back in the queue, unchanged, once (it asks first). A card taken out of the queue says why. While the queue is paused, it says why, with **Resume queue**. When the queue waits for your OK to update a branch checked out in a folder (**Ask each time**), **Approve** (with an optional note, your comment on the card) and **Decline…** (asking why) are here too, the same answer as under For you. If the update **stopped part way** (Hive or the computer stopped in the middle), it shows what Hive found: the folder, the branch it is on, where the target branch points, the commits before and after the merge, each file the merge touched and whether it is as before, as merged or neither, and the PowerShell commands that finish or undo it, each with **Copy**. Nothing runs them for you: Hive changes nothing more there on its own. Run one, or put the files right by hand, then answer the card's For you request, whose **Show the files and commands** brings this part into view.
- A card from **before 1.0** says it has no work record: Hive invents none. A card Hive took on at the upgrade says it was **adopted**, from the column it was in. If its work can't be read, the section says why, with **Retry**.

On the board, a card shows its work in one quiet line: the revision under review, the last verdict (an icon, dimmed when it no longer counts) and how many of its checks passed and failed, with a mark when its pass is stale or it was merged outside the rules. Hover any of them for what it says.

### Project keys <a id="project-keys"></a>

Each project has a **key** of 2 to 4 letters (HI, AM, WEB), and its cards are named by it: **HI-12** is the hive project's twelfth card. Everything in Hive names a card this way, and only this way: the board, the card dialog, agents' card chips, the Progress panel, Performance, notifications, and what Hive tells agents and the Assistant. A card about the whole workspace, with no project, keeps its number (#12).

- **Where keys come from.** **New Project…** suggests a key from the name you type, and checks the one you choose as you type it: 2 to 4 letters A to Z, not another project's key (now or before), and not the start of another's (AM and AMB can't both be keys). Leave it empty and Hive suggests one. While the key can't be used, **Create** is off. The key you choose is kept for the new project while it is made: another project can't take it meanwhile. A folder you open or add gets its key from its name, with no step. After the upgrade to 1.0, every project with cards got a suggested key and a quiet notice listed them; cards you had before keep their number in their project (#1061 is HI-1061).
- **Changing a key.** **Project Settings → General → Project key**: type the new key and press **Change** (or Enter). The old key stays the project's for good and still finds its cards (AM-12 opens what is now AMB-12); the field lists the old keys under it. No other project can take an old key.
- **Finding a card.** The board's search and the command palette (**Ctrl+Shift+P**) take a card's name: **AM-12** (any case), **AM-** for all of that project's cards, an old key, or just the number in its name: **12** finds AM-12, HI-12 and any other project's twelfth card. **#1061** finds a card by its old number from before keys, which keeps working everywhere. In the palette, a card's name lists the cards it finds first; choose one to open it. **Depends on** and **Related** in the card dialog take names too.
- **Links.** In a card's description and comments, a card's name (**HI-12**) or old number (**#1061**) links to that card: click it to open the card (not inside code). In an agent's terminal it is a link too: Ctrl+click opens the card, and hovering it shows its title and column.
- **The sidebar's tiles.** Folded to tiles, the Projects sidebar shows each project's key (3- and 4-letter keys in smaller letters, so the tiles stay the same size); hover a tile for the project's name.
- **Moving a card** to another project gives it that project's next number; its old name keeps finding it, in the search, the palette, Depends on, Related and links.
- **Agents** take a card's name wherever they take a card, and Hive names cards to them by key alone (**HI-12**), so the name an agent or the Assistant quotes is the one you search for.

### Moves and your project's rules <a id="moves-and-your-projects-rules"></a>

From 1.0 a project has workflow rules (its version control's options): for example, that work is reviewed before it reaches Done. When you move a card to a column its rules have, Hive does what the move stands for: into **Active** it starts the card's work (in its agent's worktree, if it has one), into **Review** it submits what the card's folder has now. A move that skips one of the rules, such as a card dragged to **Done** that was never reviewed or merged, asks first: *Move HI-12 to Done anyway?*, with the rules it skips by name and a **Reason (optional)**. **Move Anyway** makes the move and records it on the card as an exception (an override), naming the rules it skipped, with your reason; **Cancel** leaves the card where it was. A card moved past its rules shows an open lock on its tile, and in its lane's segment in the Progress panel: hover it to see who moved it, past which rules and why. Some steps no exception can make true, such as a card dragged to **Review** with nothing to submit: the move stops there (the card's work started, say) and a message says why and what to do (*HI-12 can't move to Review: Nothing is committed to review yet. Commit the card's work, then move it again.*). While you drag a card, a column its project's rules don't have (Review in a project with no review) isn't highlighted and doesn't take it. The same goes for the card's right-click **Move to**, the **Move to Active** dialog and the card dialog's **Column**.

The Hive Assistant moves cards the same way, but a move of its that skips a rule is refused unless you asked for it and it gives the reason, which is recorded on the card. Agents can't make an exception.

Agents can also say what they did instead of moving the card: they **start** a card, **submit** it for review, **start a review** and give a **verdict**, **report a merge**, **ask you** something or say a card **can't go on within a rule** (both under For you), and a reviewer can **ask the builder** for something. Hive moves the card by the project's rules and records each step in the card's work. A card whose review fails goes back to its builder, not to the reviewer. Agents name cards by their key (**HI-12**) or **#12**; Hive refuses a bare number from an agent, so an old card number can't reach another project's card.

Agents whose instructions still say to move cards (to Doing, to Review, to Passed with a verdict, to Done) keep working while those instructions catch up: Hive takes each such move as the step it stands for, by the same rules. Moving a card to Review submits it, so a card with uncommitted changes, say, stays in Doing and the agent is told why. A card whose review fails goes back to Doing, with its builder.

Moving a **Done** card back to Active (or further) reopens it: its work starts again as a new round of the card, with what was done before kept in its Work section. On Hold keeps a card as it was, and moving it back where it was carries on from there.

Turn every rule off at once with **Settings → Advanced → Workflow rules are enforced** (see [Advanced settings](#advanced-settings)): moves are then as before 1.0.

### No-code cards <a id="no-code-cards"></a>

Some cards have no code to write: a decision, a write-up, a trial. Make one with **No code (a decision, a write-up, a trial…)** in the New Card dialog: its tile and its dialog say **No code**, and when its work is done it is submitted with a summary instead of commits, reviewed if your project reviews work, and goes to Done with nothing to merge. An agent that adds a card can only propose it as no code: the card shows **No code?** and its dialog asks you to **Confirm No Code** or **Keep as Code**. To change a card's kind after it was made, use **Make It Code…** or **Make It No Code…** beside its **Kind** in the dialog: as it is an exception to its rules, Hive asks first and records it on the card with your reason. The Hive Assistant changes a kind only with your reason; agents never change it.

### Skipping review <a id="skipping-review"></a>

Some cards need no reviewer even where your project reviews work: building an installer once its fixes have merged, say, or a quick trial. Tick **Skip review** in the New Card dialog (with a note on why, if you like): submitting the card then finishes it with no review. A no-code card goes to Done; a code card goes on to its merge as your project merges work, with its merge checks and any OK before merging you asked for, so nothing unchecked reaches its target. The card shows **Review skipped** (point at it for who set it and why) and its history says so. Until the card is submitted you can change your mind beside **Review** in its dialog (**Skip Review…** or **Review It**); once it is in Review, the way past its review is moving it on, which records an override with your reason. The box only shows where the card's project reviews work.

The Hive Assistant can make a card skip review only with your reason, in your words. An agent can only propose it, with its reason, when it makes the card: the card shows **Skip review?** and its dialog asks you to **Skip Review** or **Keep Review**.

### For you <a id="for-you"></a>

Some cards wait for you: a review or an OK that is yours to give, a question an agent asked (*"OK to push the release tag?"*), a rule an agent couldn't keep, or a card loop that reached its round limit. Each such card shows a **For you** chip with what it asks (Approve, Decide, Test, Sign in or Other; hover it to see who asks and the start of the request, and open the card to read it in full and answer), the **Task Board** icon on the activity bar counts them, and the **For you** box in the board's toolbar shows only those cards (with the search box, both apply).

Open the card to answer in its **For you** section, where an agent's question shows formatted, as card comments do (lists, bold, code; a long one folded, with **Show all**): **Approve** or **Decline…** for an approval (declining asks why, and the agent reads your reason; declining your own review sends the card back to its builder as a round; the box beside Approve takes an optional note, for "approved, but change X": on an agent's question it is recorded as your decision on the card as well as your comment, so the agent works to it and its reviewer checks it, and on Hive's own requests, such as your review or the OK before merging, it is your comment, since the card moves on at once; the box says which, and left empty Approve is one click), or for a question, type your answer and **Send Reply** (the agent gets your reply in its inbox, and the card keeps it as your comment), or **Record as Decision** to keep it as one of the card's decisions (its agents follow it from then on). When the answer is something only you can do on the card, the agent can offer it as a button under its question: **Move to Done**, **Make It No Code** or **Unblock**, with its suggested reason beside it, which you can change. One click does it as you, just as the board would (moving past the card's rules is recorded with your reason), and answers the question, so the agent knows what you chose. **Skip Review** is one of them too. With one action offered, **… With This Reply** beside Send Reply does it with what you typed as the reason; approving such an approval does it too. Hive never takes a yes from a reply's words: a reply you send to an agent's question also goes to the Hive Assistant, which can do what the agent can't (move the card to Done, make it no code, skip its review) when your answer agrees. If the Assistant can't be told (its Control doesn't let it change cards), the card and a notice say so, so you know only the agent has your answer. A code card that passes review with nothing left to merge (its work is already in its target) is proposed as no code, for you to confirm. Only the actions the card allows now are shown. At a card loop's round limit you choose how it goes on: carry on with more rounds, split it, accept it with follow-ups, or take it over. A Test request lists what to try. Answering clears the chip at once and wakes the agent waiting on it. A question settled another way leaves on its own: when its card reaches Done, or when the agent that asked carries on with the card (say you answered it in the agent's pane), and the card's history says so. Only you answer: agents and the Assistant can ask, never answer for you. If the request was answered or retired meanwhile (a new submission, the card moved on), your answer isn't taken and Hive says why.

**Answering in a chat with the Assistant.** When you tell the Assistant your answer to a card's questions (say you answered three of them at once), it records your words as a decision on the card, naming the questions they answer, and Hive closes those: the agents that asked are told, the card stops waiting for you, and a card loop paused on them goes on. A question your words don't answer stays open. Only a question can be closed this way: an approval (an OK to push, your review) and a card loop's round limit stay yours to approve, decline or choose in For you. The card's **For you** section lists what your decisions answered under **Answered by your decisions**, each with the decision and who recorded it (*recorded by the Assistant*), and its history has a line for each. No agent can close its own question.

### Exporting the board <a id="exporting-the-board"></a>

The board is kept in the workspace's database, `.hive/hive.db`, and so are the card loops' lanes, agents' card watches and every project's inbox (what Hive has for its agents to read): their `lanes.json`, `watches.json` and `deliveries.json` files are moved in the first time Hive 0.9 opens the workspace, and kept beside it as `….imported-<time>`. Every change to the board is saved whole or not at all, also when many cards change at once (Archive All, a batch move), and a board of a thousand cards stays quick. The database is this computer's: a workspace you commit to git leaves it out.

- **Export Board to JSON…**, in the board's **⋯** menu or the command palette, saves every card, archived ones too, as a JSON file in a new folder inside the one you choose: a file per card (`12.json`), as Hive kept them before 0.9.
- **The first time 0.9 opens a workspace**, it moves the cards' files from `.hive/tasks` into the database and keeps the folder as `.hive/tasks.imported-<date>`. A card file that couldn't be read stays out, and a notice names it.
- **Going back to an older version.** An older Hive doesn't know the database, so it shows an empty board: nothing is lost. Cards you add there aren't added to the board when 0.9 opens the workspace again: they are kept, unchanged, in `.hive/tasks.set-aside`, and a notice asks you to review them. To use the whole board in an older version, export it and copy the files into `.hive/tasks` with Hive closed.
- **Reviewing cards made by an older Hive.** **Review…** on that notice (later, **Set-Aside Cards…** in the board's **⋯** menu while any are left, or **Review Cards Made by an Older Hive…** in the command palette) lists each card with the number the older Hive gave it, who made it and when, its project, column and comments, and the start of its description. **Add to Board** adds it with everything it had, last in its column: under its old number when no card here has it, else under the next free number; its history says *Added from an older Hive, where it was made as #n*, by Repair. **Discard…** asks first, then moves its file to the Recycle Bin. This is the only way such cards reach the board, and nothing else is rewritten: links to an old number stay as they are, also when the card gets a new one.

### Upgrading to 1.0 <a id="upgrading-to-10"></a>

Hive 1.0 keeps the board in a new format. The first time it opens a workspace whose board Hive 0.9 saved, it asks: **Upgrade this workspace's board to 1.0?** A new workspace has nothing to upgrade, so it isn't asked.

- **Upgrade** converts the board in one go: it all happens, or none of it does, so Hive closing part way leaves the board as 0.9 had it. While a workspace opens, and while it upgrades, the window shows what Hive is doing (updating the workspace's skills, upgrading its board, adopting its cards and its agents' worktrees), and opens no other workspace until it is done. Before it starts, Hive keeps a copy of the 0.9 board in `.hive/backups` (`hive-before-1.0-v<n>-<time>.db`). Hive never removes that copy, unlike the three it keeps of the board before other changes.
- **Close every other Hive first.** The upgrade needs the board to itself. It is refused while another Hive has the workspace open, such as an installed 0.9 beside a 1.0 test build, and says *Another Hive has this workspace open: close it first*. Nothing has changed then: close the other Hive and choose **Upgrade…** again. While the upgrade runs, a Hive that tries to open the workspace can't read its board. Once it is done, a 0.9 Hive can no longer open the board: the cards are kept in a new way that 0.9 can't read. To use the workspace in 0.9 again, restore the 0.9 board (below).
- **Not Now** leaves the workspace as it is, for reading. Until it is upgraded, its board is read-only (**New Card**, dragging cards and the card dialog's changes are off) and its agents and the Assistant can't start (**New Session** and **Resume** are off): Hive couldn't record their sessions. A banner at the top of the window says so, and its **Upgrade…** asks again; Hive asks once more when it next starts. Once you upgrade, Hive does then what it left for the upgrade (the merge queues start, projects get their keys), with no restart.
- **What happens to your cards.** Nothing moves on the board, and agents in a card loop carry on as before. Hive 1.0 records how each card's work goes, so the upgrade notes where each card in **Active**, **Review** or **Approved** stands, from what its folder shows: its agent, its branch and how far it has got. A card in Review is reviewed as usual, and a card in Approved is taken as passed before 1.0. Cards in **Todo**, **Done** and **On Hold**, archived cards, and cards of projects no longer in the workspace are marked as **from before 1.0**: they keep everything they had, with no record of how their work went. Each project gets settings matching how it works today: its version control, a reviewer agent if it has one or runs card loops, and its agents' folders as they are. This runs in the background just after the upgrade, and again when Hive next starts if it was interrupted; it never adds anything twice.
- **A card in Done that its workflow still has in progress.** A card from before 1.0 that an agent started again since is a 1.0 card from then on, and moving it to Done goes through its workflow like any other. On 1.0 test builds before rc.4, such a card could reach Done on the board while its workflow still had it in Active, leaving its agent's question in **For you**. Each time a workspace opens, Hive brings such cards into line: Done in the workflow too, their open questions closed, and a line in the card's history (*Recorded as Done, as the board had it: its workflow still had it in Active*). A card moved again meanwhile is left as it is.
- **Going back to 0.9.** **Help → Restore the 0.9 Board Backup…** puts back the copy kept at the upgrade. First it keeps a copy of the board as it is now (`hive-before-restore-v<n>-<time>.db`, in the same folder), so changes since the upgrade aren't lost. Then quit this Hive and open the workspace in Hive 0.9, where the board can be changed again. It brings back the board's cards and their history only, not your agents' worktrees, sessions or lanes once slots have moved them (see **Back up first**, below). The command is refused while another Hive has the workspace open, or while any of its agents or the Assistant runs (stop them first), and nothing is deleted. Afterwards this Hive treats the workspace as one waiting for its upgrade again.
- **Which Hives have a workspace open.** From 1.0, every Hive with a workspace open writes a small file in `.hive/open` (one per Hive profile: its process and a time it renews every 30 seconds) and removes it as it closes. The upgrade checks it, as well as the board's own lock. A file left behind by a Hive that stopped suddenly is ignored once its time is 90 seconds old.
- **Back up first.** Before upgrading, back up the workspace's folders (its `.hive` folder and your agents' worktrees). There's no going back to 0.9.3 once your agents' worktrees become slots: the board backup above puts the cards back, but not the worktrees, sessions or lanes as slots have moved them.
- **Your agents' worktrees become slots.** Nothing moves at the upgrade: each builder's own worktree (`hb2`) becomes one of the project's slots under its folder's name, kept for that agent, and its conversations there resume there as before. A card loop running at the upgrade finishes in it, and a card in flight is finished there; then the slot is no longer kept for the agent. An agent with nothing in flight keeps working there until its next card or lane, which takes the slot over if its work is committed and merged (its conversation carries on there), or gets another slot otherwise. To give a slot up yourself, choose **Release Slot…** in the agent's **⋯** menu. Once nothing holds a slot named after an agent, Hive renames its folder to a slot name (`slot-c`) the way **Repair** moves a worktree, taking its cards' work and its conversations with it; turn this off in **Settings → Advanced → Rename adopted worktrees to slot names**. Hive never deletes the agent's branch. A reviewer's own worktree doesn't become a slot: its conversations still resume there, and its new ones start in the project folder. A worktree that's missing is left as it is (**Repair** or **Recreate**, as before), and becomes a slot once it's there again; so does one you give an agent later.
- **A slot held after Hive closed.** Nothing else uses a slot while an agent's session runs there, nor afterwards while Hive doesn't know that everything the session started has ended: when Hive closed unexpectedly with the session running, when you chose **Keep Running** for what it left running, or when Hive couldn't follow its processes. When the Hive that held it quit (say to install an update) after seeing everything the session started end, the next Hive frees the slot by itself, so builders resume into their own slots after a restart. The same goes for a workspace you closed first, or one in another window, whenever you open it again. If the session's processes were still running when Hive closed (a test run you kept, or Hive closed unexpectedly), Hive's process helper stays after it, hidden, watching only those processes; once they have all ended it notes it in Hive's profile and exits, and the next Hive frees the slot by itself then, logging *slot-d … settled: HB5's ended run left nothing running*. While some still run, the slot stays held and **Release Slot…** lists them. The helper ends nothing itself, and shows in Task Manager as a **Windows PowerShell** process while it waits. Otherwise Hive never guesses, and such a slot stays held until you release it. **Release Slot…** in the agent's **⋯** menu, or **Release…** beside the slot in the **Version Control** tab's list of slots or its Worktrees list (*Held by B1's ended session*), says which Hive held it and what Hive lists of the processes left running, offers to end those first, and frees the slot once you confirm. It refuses while the session still runs in this Hive, or while the other Hive holding it is still running. The Hive Assistant can do the same when you ask (with **Control agents**): it tells you which slot, session and processes first, and releases the slot only once you agree. A slot kept for an agent can be released this way too. Neither can be undone.
- **How Hive merges, asked once.** Hive doesn't decide for you how an upgraded Git project's cards are merged. The first time one of its cards reaches the merge queue, the queue waits and asks under **For you** on that card: the **Merge style** (a merge commit, as until now, Squash, or Rebase) and the **Commit message** (the branch's own, chosen to start with, or Hive's, as until now). **Save and Carry On Merging** saves both as the project's options, and the queue merges the card that way. Changing either in **Version Control → Workflow** answers it too.
- **Choosing a preset afterwards.** Once the upgrade is done, a banner names the projects that kept working as before and offers the presets: **Choose…** opens the first one's **Version Control** tab at **Workflow**, **Not Now** dismisses it. Either way it isn't shown again in that workspace. A workspace made with 1.0 never shows it.

### Workflow options <a id="workflow-options"></a>

A project's workflow is how its cards move from Todo to Done: who reviews the work, which checks pass first, who merges it and where cards are built. Set it in the project's **Version Control** tab, in its first section, **Workflow** (until 1.0 it was in Project Settings, which now has an **Open Version Control** button there). Agents and the Hive Assistant still read and change each option as a setting (`project.workflow.<option>`).

- **Presets** come first: **Solo**, **Pair (the agent merges)**, **Pair** and **Team** (for a project without version control, Solo, Pair and Team), and **All off**, each with what it sets up in a sentence. Click one to preview it: each option it sets, with its value and why, and the ones it would change here marked with their value now. **Use** fills the form with it; nothing is saved until **Save…**. The line beside **Preset** names the one the options started from: **Custom, started from** it once an option differs (the tab's header says *Custom (from Team)*), or **No preset chosen**. Your stage names stay through a preset: they aren't rules. While Team is chosen, a note says that running each card's own focused tests on the merged result comes in a later version.
- **The options come in groups**, each a heading:
  - **Team**: **Maximum agents**, your team size, the most agents the project has, whoever adds them (see [Maximum agents](#several-agents-in-one-project)). Each preset sets it, but changing it never makes your options Custom, and it applies at once, once saved;
  - **Review**: who reviews the work, how many reviewers per card, whether lanes share their reviewers (**Reviewers**: one per lane, or a shared pool), nobody reviews their own work, whether a pass survives a clean rebase, and your OK before merging;
  - **Checks**: the checks before review, and, while Hive merges, the checks on the merged result and when dependencies are installed from scratch;
  - **Merging**: who merges, the merge style, the branch work merges into, whether work is brought up to date with it first (while Hive merges), and whether cards wait for the cards they depend on;
  - **Where cards are built**: each card in its own folder (a **slot**, a folder Hive lends to the card or its lane, with a branch per card) or everyone in the project folder; with slots, whether **slots are brought up to date before each card**; the project's commit identity (on, agents' commits must be authored with git's identity for the project, read from git at each submission, so a change in Branches & remotes → Commit identity or git config applies at once), agents stopping what they started before review, and, with everyone in the project folder, the warning when a commit includes another agent's files. While agents still finish their current work in their worktrees from before 1.0, the option says so, naming them;
  - **Slots and copies** (Git), right after it: the project's slot and copy settings, saved as you change them rather than with **Save…**. With slots: the **Setup script** (run in a new slot, and again when its lock files changed, e.g. `npm ci`), the **Archive script** (run before Hive removes a slot; a failure keeps it), the **Files copied into new slots** (git-ignored files such as `.env`), each showing, when you haven't set it, the value it uses and where it comes from (the setup script: the project's setup command from before 1.0; the copied files: **From the workspace**, **From Hive defaults** or **Default**, `.env*`, as set in [Hive and workspace defaults](#workflow-defaults)); **Set to none** (or emptying a value you set) turns the setup script or the copied files off for this project, and **Inherit** goes back to the inherited value; and **Slots in use and ready**: each slot, its state and what it's lent to, with a link to **Branches & remotes**. Always: how many **Review and check copies** the project keeps (empty keeps the larger of two and its reviewers plus one), and whether **Reviewers review in review copies** (Settings → Advanced's switch, with a link);
  - **Stages**: your names for the board's stages (below);
  - **Card loops**: whether Hive wakes reviewers and builders, the rounds and the wait a loop gets when it doesn't say, and the design check wait (see [Card loops](#card-loops)). With **File locks** (under **Where cards are built**, or **The work**), they aren't rules: no preset sets them, they never make your options Custom, and a change applies at once, once saved, without making anything *old rules*;
  A project without version control has **Review** (*Who checks the work before it's done*, and nobody reviews their own work), **Checks** (before review), **The work** (what the work is, and whether cards wait for the cards they depend on), **Stages** and **Shown here, set elsewhere**.
- **Each option** is worded as what happens to the work, with one sentence on what it does, and where its value comes from: the preset, this project, the workspace, Hive's settings, or the default (off). An option set for this project has **Inherit**, which goes back to the inherited value. With everyone in the project folder, the first option reads *Work is reviewed before it's done*, since the work is on the target branch meanwhile. **Slots are brought up to date before each card** shows only with each card in its own folder (set with everyone in the project folder, it contradicts). **Reviewers** shows whenever a reviewer agent reviews, wherever cards are built: with everyone in the project folder, a shared pool's reviewers read the work in review copies. When some reviewers' coding agent can't be given review copies, the option says they review the builder's folder read-only.
- **A pass survives a clean rebase**: with **Same change** (every preset's), a reviewer's pass (or your OK) still counts when the builder rebases or squashes the work onto a newer target without changing it; with **Exact revision only**, any new revision makes it stale, and the card goes back for review.
- **Your OK before merging** (every card, or cards with the labels you list): once a card's work is approved, Hive asks you under **For you** before it merges, whoever merges it. The merge queue waits for your OK, and so does an agent's report of its merge, also when merges aren't checked (*Not checked*).
- **Who merges → Hive merges** adds two rows to **Checks**. **Checks pass on the merged result** lists the named commands the merge queue runs on each card's work merged with the target branch (as **Checks pass before review**, they share one list of commands); the target moves only once each one has passed. With checks there, **Install dependencies from scratch** says when the queue installs the project's dependencies afresh before them: when the lock file or manifests change (the default), or before every merge. A change to the lock file or the manifests always installs from scratch. Another **Who merges**, or no merge checks, is their off switch.
- **Options that contradict each other** say so as you choose them: the reason shows under each option it's about at once (for example *"Committed is done" needs everyone in the project folder*, under **Who merges** and **Where cards are built**), and **Save…** stays off until it's resolved. A save is checked again when it's made, with the same reasons.
- **Stage names** has a box for each stage your options produce, its default name as the placeholder: type a name to rename it, clear it to go back to the default. A name is at most 24 characters and differs from the other stages'. Names aren't rules: a rename alone applies at once, to every card, and Save… says so.
- **All off** sets no review, *Not checked*, everyone in the project folder and no checks: cards move freely, and Hive still records what happens. The section then lists what never turns off, to keep your work safe: file locks (the **File locks** option decides), Hive never deleting git's `index.lock`, no branch switch or pull in a folder while an agent works there, and the merge queue moving a target only if nothing else moved it meanwhile.
- **Save…** shows what changes, each option's old and new value. The change applies from the next card that starts: running lanes and cards already started keep the rules they started with (*old rules*), and the dialog lists them. Tick a card in flight to apply the new options to it now. If the options changed meanwhile (the Hive Assistant changed one, or you saved in another window), nothing is saved: Hive says so and shows them as they are now, never writing over them.

- **Copy as JSON** copies the project's options; **Paste JSON…** in another project with the same version control fills the form with them (checked as a save would be), to save there.
- With **Settings → Advanced → Workflow rules are enforced** off, the section says so: the options are saved, and apply once the rules are enforced again.
- Agents read the options in force (`hive_project_status`). The Hive Assistant can change them one at a time with its settings tool (`project.workflow.<option>`), within Settings → Assistant → Control, and the same checks apply. Project agents can't change them.

**The presets, and why.** What each sets for Git:

| Option | Solo | Pair (the agent merges) | Pair | Team | Why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Work is reviewed before it's merged | No review | A reviewer agent | A reviewer agent | A reviewer agent | A pair exists to review: a second agent catches what the builder missed, while working solo you read the card's work yourself. |
| Reviewers per card | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | One reviewer is enough for most work; a second doubles the cost. |
| Reviewers | — | One per lane | One per lane | One per lane | One reviewer per lane keeps the work with a reviewer who has seen its earlier rounds; a shared pool suits a large team. |
| Nobody reviews their own work | Off | On | On | On | A builder never passes its own work. |
| Checks pass before review | None | Quick checks | Quick checks | Quick checks and the tests | Cheap checks before review catch most of the rounds that would be lost, and a team adds its tests. |
| A pass survives a clean rebase | Same change | Same change | Same change | Same change | A clean rebase doesn't change what was reviewed, so the pass still counts. |
| Who merges | Committed is done | The agent merges, Hive checks | Hive merges | Hive merges | Merged through Hive's queue, red work never reaches the target branch; working solo, a card is done once it's committed. |
| Merge style | Merge commit | Merge commit | Merge commit | Merge commit | A merge commit keeps each card's own commits, as Hive has always merged. |
| Work merges into | The branch the project is on | The repository's default branch | The repository's default branch | The repository's default branch | Finished work goes to the repository's default branch; working solo, to the branch the project is on. |
| Work is up to date with its target branch before it merges | — | — | Off | Off | The merge queue tests the merged result anyway, so this mostly changes the history's shape. |
| Push the target branch after merging | — | — | Off | Off | Pushing reaches your remote and its sign-in, so it stays yours unless you turn it on. |
| Commit message | — | — | The branch's own | The branch's own | Card titles can name people and details a public history shouldn't keep; choose Hive's for the card's key, linking each merge to its card. |
| Checks pass on the merged result | — | — | Quick checks | Quick checks and the tests | The checks run on the work as it will be once merged, not only as it was built. |
| Install dependencies from scratch | — | — | When the lock file or manifests change | Before every merge | A clean install before every merge catches a dependency that only worked on the builder's machine. |
| Your OK before merging | Never | Never | Never | Cards labelled `ui` or `release` | Some work needs your eyes before it ships, such as screenshots and release steps. |
| Where cards are built | Everyone in the project folder | Each card in its own folder | Each card in its own folder | Each card in its own folder | Pairs and teams build in parallel, each card apart from the others. |
| Slots are brought up to date before each card | — | On | On | On | Each card starts from the latest work, so no card is built on an old copy of the target branch. |
| Commits use the project's identity | Off | On | On | On | Commits name the project's identity, whoever's computer made them. |
| Agents stop what they started before review | Off | On | On | On | A run left going would keep changing or testing the work under review. |
| Cards start only when the cards they wait for are merged | On | On | On | On | It's cheap, and no card is built on work that isn't in yet. |
| Warn when a commit includes another agent's files | On | — | — | — | In a shared folder, one agent's commit can take in another's half-done files. Hive knows an agent's files from its file locks while it works a card, so with file locks Off it can't warn. |

Without version control, **Solo** has no review (a card is done when its builder submits it), **Pair** has a reviewer agent check each card after the quick checks pass, and **Team** has a reviewer agent and then you check each card, after the quick checks and the tests; all three leave `node_modules` and `.venv` out of what the work is, and have cards wait for the cards they depend on. A card's paths (the files its work covers) can only narrow that: Hive refuses a path inside `node_modules`, `.venv`, `.git`, `.hive` or another folder the project leaves out, and a folder's files inside them never count. A card's paths are set from its agents' edits at its first submit, and after that only when its agent declares them again: when a later round adds a file, or something else changed a file since the card started (your editor, a formatter), the agent is told which files its paths leave out when it submits, and includes them or leaves them. Nothing is added to a card's paths by itself. When you submit such a card yourself, by dragging it from Doing to Review (or **Move to → Review**), Hive first asks **Include files AM-12's paths leave out?**, listing them: **Include Them** adds them to the card's paths and submits it with them, **Leave Them Out** submits it as it is, and **Cancel** leaves it in Doing. A file changed since the card started may be another card's work or your own, so include only what belongs to it. Moving a card to Done (Solo's way) isn't a submission, so it doesn't ask. To find what changed outside a card's files, Hive reads the project's files when the card starts, when it is submitted, and when a check of it starts and ends, in the background, so typing in your panes doesn't pause while it does, however large the project. A read that takes longer than **Settings → Advanced → No version control: longest read of the project** (two minutes) is stopped, and the card goes on without that list (a check read so doesn't count). A no-code card reads nothing of the project.

### Hive and workspace defaults <a id="workflow-defaults"></a>

Each project's workflow options start from defaults, which you set in **Settings → Version Control**, on two tabs:

- **Hive defaults**: for every project in every workspace.
- **Workspace defaults** (named after the open workspace): for the open workspace's projects, over Hive defaults. Shown while a workspace is open.

(Before 1.0's last settings tidy these were Settings → Workflow defaults and Settings → Workspace's workflow defaults; nothing you set there changed.) Each tab shows only its own form, with its own None · Git choice, presets, draft and **Save…**: saving one leaves the other's unsaved changes as they are. Switching tabs keeps each draft, and a tab with unsaved changes has a dot. Settings opens on the tab you last chose; a search shows the tabs it finds. In a project's options, **From Hive defaults** and **From the workspace** are links to their tab. The arrow keys, Home and End move between tabs.

The order they win in: a project's own option (its Version Control tab), then the workspace's default, then Hive's, then the option off. Each level has its own defaults for **None** and for **Git**: choose which at the top.

The editor is the Workflow section's: the same groups, presets (with what each sets and why), All off, and options that contradict each other refused as you choose. Each option says where its value comes from: **Set here**, from the level below (**From Hive defaults**, for the workspace's), or **Default** (off). **Inherit** goes back to the level below. Options that belong to one project (its commit identity, and the settings shown elsewhere) aren't at these levels.

**Save…** lists each open project that inherits what changes (Hive's level: those of every open workspace), what changes for it, and its running lanes and cards in flight, which keep their rules until their next card. A project that sets an option itself isn't affected by it. A change that would make an inheriting project's options contradict each other is refused, naming the project and the reason: change that project's options first. As in a project's section, a save made after the defaults changed elsewhere is refused, and the editor shows them as they are now.

For Git, the same place has **Files copied into new slots**: the git-ignored files (such as `.env`) copied from the project folder into each new slot and review copy. It is saved as you change it. Set it at a level, set it to none there, or **Inherit** (the workspace's inherits Hive's, Hive's the default, `.env*`); a project's own, in Version Control → Slots and copies, wins.

The Hive Assistant can set one at a time with its settings tool, within Settings → Assistant → Control: `workflow.<git|none>.<option>` for Hive's and `workspace.workflow.<git|none>.<option>` for the workspace's, with the same checks, and `workflow.git.slotCopy`, `workspace.workflow.git.slotCopy` and `project.slotCopy` for the copied files.

### The merge queue <a id="the-merge-queue"></a>

When a project's workflow says Hive merges, each approved card goes into its target branch's **merge queue**, and Hive merges the cards one at a time: a test merge in its own worktree, the merge checks on exactly that result, and the target moved only when they pass. A card that conflicts or fails a check goes back to its builder.

**Work is up to date with its target branch before it merges** (Version Control → Workflow, shown when Hive merges; off in every preset): when the target branch moved on after a card's review, the queue first brings the card's work up to date in its own folder: it merges the target branch into the work, or with the **Squash** merge style rebases the work onto it. It never touches the builder's branch or worktree. If the result is the same change as was reviewed, the review still counts and the queue merges it; the merge commit names both the reviewed revision and the updated one (`Hive-Revision`, `Hive-Updated`). If it conflicts, or isn't the same change (part of it is in the target already, say), the card goes back to its builder (*not up to date*), who brings the branch up to date and submits it again.

**Merge style: Rebase and fast-forward** (Version Control → Workflow; no preset sets it) keeps the target branch's history linear. The queue rebases the card's commits onto the target in its own folder. Each commit keeps its message, author and date, and nothing on the builder's branch changes. The merge checks run on exactly that result, and the target then moves forward to it. Every commit it adds has a single parent: merge commits on the card's branch are flattened, even when the card is already on top of the target. There is no merge commit, so the history doesn't carry the card and reviewer lines a merge commit would. If the rebase conflicts, or flattening would change the work (a merge commit that made changes of its own, say), the card goes back to its builder, who rebases it and submits it again. With it, **Work is up to date with its target branch before it merges** needs no separate step.

**Push the target branch after merging** (Version Control → Workflow, shown when Hive merges; off in every preset): after each merge, the queue pushes the target branch to the remote it tracks, or, if it tracks none, to the repository's only remote or `origin`. It never forces a push, never changes what the branch tracks, and never pulls. It runs with no prompt, as the Version Control tab's Push does, for up to 5 minutes. If the remote refuses (it has commits the branch lacks), the push fails (a sign-in, the network) or there is no remote, the queue pauses and says why. The card it merged stays Done, and the next card waits. Pull or fix it, then **Resume**: the queue pushes first, then carries on. Or turn the option off and Resume to keep merging locally. Turning it off takes effect at once, also for cards already in a lane. If Hive stops between a merge and its push, it pushes when it starts again.

**Commit message** (Version Control → Workflow, shown when Hive merges with Merge commit or Squash; the branch's own in every preset since 1.0, so card titles never reach your history unless you choose Hive's). Projects set up before then switch once to the branch's own when Hive first opens their workspace, unless you chose Hive's (set it yourself, or answered the queue's question with it), which they keep. It is the message of the commit the queue makes when it merges a card. **Hive's** is the card's key and title, `HI-12: Fix the login`, followed by Hive's trailers (`Hive-Card`, `Hive-Revision`, `Hive-Reviewed-By`). **The branch's own** uses the messages of the branch's own commits instead: one commit's message as it is, or with several, each one's in turn, the first one's subject first. It has no card key and no trailers, for a project that keeps card numbers out of its history (with Squash, each card lands as one commit with its builder's message). Either way Hive knows which card each merge was, so the board, the card's Work section and the queue's history are the same. A change applies to the next merge, also for cards already approved.

You stay in charge of each queue:

- **Pause queue** stops a target's queue before its next card; a card whose checks are running finishes them and waits, unmerged. The pause is saved, so it holds after a restart and in every Hive on the workspace, with who paused it.
- **Resume** runs it again, starting the waiting card from a fresh test merge. When Hive paused the queue itself (workflow rules are off, you have changes in a file the merge changes, a merge was interrupted with files changed since), Resume first looks again, and if the cause is still there the queue stays paused and says why.
- **Remove from queue** takes a card out: it goes back to Approved, is never merged (after a restart too), and its builder is told. Once its merge has begun it can't be taken out; it finishes.
- **Retry merge** puts a card the queue sent back (a failed check you know is flaky, say) into the queue again, as it was approved, once: after that, the card's Work section says a new revision is the way back. It also queues again a passed card that was taken out of the queue and is still approved, as often as it is taken out, though not while a follow-up waits for its reviewer (the card goes back into the queue once the reviewer has checked it).
- **A follow-up on a queued card** (a change its reviewer is asked to check, such as a screenshot's) takes it out of the queue first, so the queue can't merge it while the follow-up changes it; the reviewer's OK queues it again. Once its merge has begun, a follow-up waits for a new card.
- **A stale pass** (the card's work changed after its review into something that isn't the same change) takes it out of the queue: its builder submits it again, or puts its branch back at the revision that passed, which makes the pass count again and queues it again by itself.

The Hive Assistant can do these for you within **Settings → Assistant → Control** (it gives your reason when it takes a card out or retries one), and lists them among its actions. Agents can see a queue but never change it. You see each queue in the Progress panel's **Merges** tab, in the project's Version Control tab (**Merge queue**, with its history), and on each card in its **Work** section.

### Each card's focused tests <a id="each-cards-focused-tests"></a>

**Each card's focused tests pass on the merged result** (Version Control → Workflow, shown when Hive merges; on in Team when the project has the command that runs them) runs the tests a card's change needs on the merged result too, not only the project's merge checks. The project gives two commands under the option:

- **Command that chooses the tests** (optional): given the paths a change touches, it prints the tests the change needs. When a card is submitted, Hive runs it in the card's folder at exactly the revision submitted; if the folder changes while it runs, its answer doesn't count. Without it, the builder names the tests.
- **Command that runs them**: given a list of tests, it runs them, writes each one's result, and exits 0 unless one failed (a test skipped for its environment isn't a failure). Hive passes the list and where to write the results in a file, and **Merge queue: focused tests run at once** (Settings → Advanced, 2) as how many to run in parallel.

The tests a card's change needs are kept with its submission, and its reviewer sees them. A choice never passes by default: if the command fails, takes longer than **Focused tests: longest the choice of tests runs** (Settings → Advanced, 180 s), or says the change needs every test, the submit is refused until the builder names the tests itself (`tests` on its submit), for its reviewer to check.

The merge queue runs the list after the merge checks, once, and moves the target only when every test passed. For any file the merge changed differently from the reviewed work (a target that changed the same file, say), the tests are chosen again there. Tests that need a real CLI or the installed app aren't run by the queue: they count from the card's reviewer's own run at the revision submitted (`hive-progress --check "focused:<test>" -- <its command>`), never the builder's, shown as review evidence, and a card without that run goes back to its builder. A test skipped for its environment (a usage limit, a sign-in, the network) pauses the queue: the card's reviewer decides whether it merges with it skipped, and you can answer first under **For you**. The decision holds for that merged result only: if the target moves on, you are asked again. A test that failed, or one the command gave no result for, returns the card with the output.

### When the merge queue pauses <a id="when-the-merge-queue-pauses"></a>

When Hive merges cards itself, it commits each merge with your repository's own setup. Its commits run the repository's hooks and are signed as the repository says; Hive never skips or overrides either.

Only a conflict, a failing merge check or a failing hook sends a card back to its builder. A hook that fails (`pre-merge-commit`, `commit-msg`, or `pre-commit` for a squash) sends the card back with the hook's output, as a failed check does.

Anything else that stops a merge is something to fix on this computer, not in the card's work. The queue for that target pauses, the card stays where it is, the target doesn't move, and the queue's own folder is left clean. Fix the cause, then resume the queue (**Resume queue**, on its card in the Progress panel's Merges tab or the Version Control tab's Merge queue section):
- **Set a commit identity for this repository**: git has no `user.name` and `user.email` for the project (Hive's merges are made as git's identity, the one Version Control → Branches & remotes → Commit identity shows). Set them in the project folder (`git config user.name "…"` and `git config user.email "…"`), or globally with `--global`.
- **Signing waits for you**: the repository signs its commits (`commit.gpgsign`) and its signer wanted a passphrase or a PIN: it stopped saying so (it needed a passphrase, a PIN entry window or a terminal), or it was still waiting after **Settings → Advanced → Merge queue: longest a signed merge commit takes** (60 seconds, hooks included), and Hive ended it. Hive never waits on a prompt. Unlock your key so it signs without asking: start your GPG agent with the passphrase cached, or add your SSH key to `ssh-agent`. If signing or your hooks are only slow, raise that setting.
- **A hook didn't finish**: the repository signs its commits, and one of its commit hooks was still running at that same limit. Hive ended it. Make the hook quicker, or raise the setting.
- **Signing failed**: the signer refused, with its message (no secret key, a key that can't be loaded). Fix the signing setup (`user.signingkey`, `gpg.program`, `gpg.format`), or turn signing off for the repository.
- **Needs you**: git couldn't merge for another reason, with git's message: another git command holding the repository's index (`index.lock`), a damaged repository, a folder it couldn't check out. Let the other command finish, or fix what git says.

### Merging into a branch checked out in a folder <a id="merging-into-a-branch-checked-out-in-a-folder"></a>

The branch a card merges into is often checked out somewhere, usually `main` in the project folder. Merging then changes that folder's files as well as the branch, so Hive takes more care:

- **It merges there by itself, or asks first.** **Settings → Advanced → Merge queue: merge into a checked-out branch** is **Automatically**: once a card is tested and its merge checks pass, Hive updates the folder. Choose **Ask each time** to approve each one: the card then waits under **For you** with *"AL-12 is tested and ready to merge: update main in D:\work\alpha now?"*. **Approve** merges it at once. If `main` moved meanwhile, the card is tested again and asked again. **Decline** takes it out of the queue, back to Approved, as Remove from queue does. Switching back to **Automatically** lets a card that waits go ahead. A branch that isn't checked out anywhere is always merged at once.
- **It never merges over your work.** Before it touches the folder, Hive checks that no agent is working there (one editing files in it, or running a command there; an idle agent, or a reviewer reviewing in its own review copy, doesn't count), that the folder is on that branch, that nothing is staged, and that you have no changes in the files the merge changes. If any of these fails, the queue pauses, saying why and naming the files, and the card stays queued. Your other edits are left alone. Put it right (commit or stash the change, say) and resume the queue. While it updates the folder, Hive types nothing into the agents working there.
- **Your work isn't held up.** Hive tests the merge and runs its checks in its own folder, without locking anything. If you commit in the project folder while the checks run, the merge is tested again on your commit.
- **If something interrupts it.** Hive keeps a record of each step of the update, so if Hive or the computer stops halfway, the next start finishes the update or undoes it, and never merges a card twice. If it finds the folder in a state it can't be sure about (a file in the merge edited since, or the folder switched to another branch), it stops, changes nothing more, and pauses the queue with **Needs you**. The card asks you under **For you** to settle it, and says what to run in PowerShell: a `git update-ref` that finishes the merge, a `git read-tree` that undoes it, or, when the files are neither as they were nor as merged, to put them right by hand. Answer it once that's done, and Hive looks again. Until you do, Hive leaves that branch alone, even when another card is queued for it or Hive restarts; **Resume** looks again too.
- **One rare case.** If another program switches the folder's branch in the very instant Hive updates it, the merged files can end up as uncommitted changes in that other branch. Hive notices at once, records the card as merged (it is, into its branch), pauses the queue and lists the files, with the `git read-tree` command that puts them back.

### Merging outside cards <a id="merging-outside-cards"></a>

**Merge a branch** merges any branch by hand: **Merge…** (⑂) beside a branch in Version Control → **Branches & remotes** opens it on that branch.
