Pick the **Branch** (cards' branches first, each with the slot it's checked out in; then agents' old worktrees' branches; then the rest) and the branch to merge it **Into** (the project folder's, unless you choose another). It merges as an agent's merge does: committed work only (uncommitted files where the branch is checked out stay there, and the dialog says how many), one merge at a time into each branch (it waits while the merge slot is held), as a **Merge** or a **Squash** with your commit message. A merge that would conflict changes nothing and lists the files.

A branch whose card isn't Done is that card's work: the dialog says so, with a link to the card, and merges it only after you confirm. The Merge dialog of an agent's own worktree, and of an unused worktree's **Open**, asks the same; a slot's **Open** → **Merge** opens **Merge a branch**. Hive then sees the merge and marks the card **merged outside the rules**, as for any merge done by hand. A card Hive's merge queue merges offers **Merge anyway…** instead, recorded on the card as an override, as for an agent's merge.

### Card loops <a id="card-loops"></a>

Two agents can take a list of cards in turn, one building and one reviewing, without you passing each card between them. Tell one agent *"work through #111 to #117 as the builder"* and another *"review #111 to #117 as they come in"*. Both use Hive's **card-loop** skill. The builder does a card and moves it to Review. The reviewer reviews it and passes it, or sends it back with findings; the builder fixes them and moves it to Review again. When a card is Done, the lane goes on to the next one: with workflow rules on, Hive starts it for the builder itself (below); with them off, the builder starts it. The builder says which checks it ran, on exactly which code; the reviewer checks that record rather than running everything again, and runs the tests the change affects and its own probes.

A card sent back with findings goes back to **Active** with the agent that built it, which works on the fixes there and moves it to Review again for the next round. The same happens whatever the project's workflow rules, and however the reviewer gave its verdict. A card that failed review in an earlier Hive may still sit in Review: if its builder moves it to Review without it leaving, the card is **returned for review** all the same (*Returned for review, round 2* in its history) and the reviewer is woken. You can return such a card yourself: right-click it → **Move to → Review (next round)**.

- **Waiting costs nothing.** While one agent waits for the other, it isn't running: it ends its turn and Hive wakes it with a short line when the card changes (*"[Hive] New in your inbox: 1 card event (#112) (m7). Call hive_inbox to read it."*), and the agent reads what changed in its inbox (*"#112 is in Review; latest comment by Codex…"*). The agent shows a blue ring in the sidebar and **Waiting for AM-112 → Review** in its pane header.
- **Hive wakes them itself, too.** When a card arrives in Review, Hive wakes its reviewer, and when the review gives its verdict, Hive wakes the card's builder, even if that agent didn't set a wait. The builder is also woken when you move its card into Approved or Done yourself, and at once when its reviewer asks it something mid-review; a decision you record on the card wakes its builder, and its reviewer while the card is in Review or Approved, even if they are waiting on the card rather than for you (in its inbox: *"#112 (B1's card) is in Review. You review this card in its loop: carry on with it…"*). Only for cards with a reviewer (the reviewer of its lane, or the agent that last reviewed it), so a card nobody reviews never wakes anyone; only while the agent is idle (never typed over its work or your typing); never for the agent's own move; and not when the agent's own wait already tells it, so it gets one line, not two. A builder's wait for its verdict also hears its reviewer's question and your decisions, and its line says so (*"#112 is in Review (its reviewer asks you something); latest comment by R1 (hive): "Which commit?""*); the card's history keeps the question (*"Asked the builder: "Which commit?""*). A comment from the lane's reviewer on a card in Review that it hasn't started reviewing counts as such a question too (*"Keeping the lane's order: #344 first"*), so the builder hears it at once. It works the other way too: when the builder comments on its card in Review after the reviewer asked or said something in that round, Hive wakes the reviewer with the reply (the history keeps *Replied to the reviewer: "Logs attached."*), and a reviewer that starts waiting on the card after the reply still hears it. The card's history says whom Hive woke (*"Woke R1 (hive) to review it (a loop wake)"*). A builder asked for a design before it builds posts it on the card, and Hive wakes the reviewer to check it, even one that waits only for the card to reach Review; the reviewer's answer (*OK*, or the gaps it finds) wakes the builder, and with no answer within the project's **Design check wait** (30 minutes unless you change it), the builder goes ahead as posted and says so (not before: the deadline is set when the design is posted, and a later change of the setting doesn't move it). Only the card's reviewer answers, and only the design it was shown: an answer to an earlier design doesn't count for a newer one. A card nobody reviews (no lane with a reviewer, no review yet) can't have a design posted for a check. A **follow-up** works the same way once the work is in Review or Approved: when a change is asked of it for its reviewer to check (a screenshot OK with changes, say), the builder posts it as a follow-up, and Hive wakes the reviewer, even one waiting for the card to come back to Review, which a follow-up never does; the reviewer's OK or gaps wakes the builder. A follow-up is never gone ahead with unchecked: one left unanswered for 15 minutes while its builder waits shows its card as stalled (*"B1 posted a follow-up on #333 for its reviewer to check and has waited 20 min: nobody has answered it"*). The card's history keeps each step (*"Posted a design for the reviewer to check: …"*, *"Posted a follow-up for the reviewer to check: …"*). To drive reviews by hand, turn off **Wake reviewers and builders** under **Card loops** in the project's **Version Control → Workflow** (or for every project, in the [Hive and workspace defaults](#workflow-defaults)).
- **Hive sets the wait after a handoff.** When a lane's builder sends its card to Review, Hive starts its wait for the review itself (the verdict, a move, a comment), as long as the lane's wait, and tells it so; when the lane's reviewer fails a card, Hive starts its wait for the card's next round in Review. So an agent that hands a card on and stops is still waiting, and its lane doesn't show as stalled. An agent already waiting on something else keeps its own wait.
- **A new conversation starts with no old wait.** An agent's wait on its cards carries on when it's resumed, but when Hive starts it in a new conversation (a lane's next card, Start Fresh, a handover from another agent) any wait from its last conversation ends first, so it never shows as *watching* a card it no longer works on, and Hive can give it its new card.
- **A loop that stops waiting shows.** If an agent in a card loop ends its turn without waiting (it said it was watching but isn't), or Hive's wake line sits in its prompt without being sent, nothing would wake it again. Hive puts its lines into Claude Code (agents and the Assistant) and GitHub Copilot as a paste, so a line that arrives while the agent is busy for a moment is still sent at its Enter (a long one shows as *[Pasted text #1]* in Claude Code's prompt until then); into Codex, Hive presses Enter only once Codex shows the line in its prompt (up to 5 seconds; **Settings → Advanced**, Codex → **Typed line: wait to be drawn** changes it). Hive presses Enter again when a wake line isn't taken; if it still isn't, the agent's pane says **A line Hive typed wasn't taken** and you're told once (click its terminal and press Enter: Hive types nothing more into it until then), and its card shows as stalled for that (*"Stalled: Codex hasn't taken the wake Hive typed for #333: it waits in its prompt"*). If the agent sits idle with no wait at all, its card shows as stalled (*"Stalled: Codex isn't watching #333: its turn ended with no card watch"*) and you're told once. Show the agent and ask it to carry on. In a lane, an agent waiting on the other side isn't stalled for having no watch, as Hive wakes it when the other side moves: a builder whose card is in Review, or whose design waits for its answer, and a reviewer waiting for the next submission. Nor is an agent waiting on its own question to you on the card (under **For you**): your answer wakes it, and its card shows as waiting for you. A card is stalled only for its own builder and reviewer: an agent watching a card it neither builds nor reviews (one its own next card depends on, say) never makes that card show as stalled, and when its wait runs out it's told to keep waiting rather than ask you (its lane shows what its next card waits on). Once the move is its own again, such as a failed review sending the card back, it is stalled if it still sits idle. This applies while the project's loop wakes are on and the agent has its hive tools. An agent that ended its turn while its background tasks run (a long test run) isn't flagged when its CLI wakes it as they end (Claude Code does; Codex doesn't). A card that has waited in Review for 15 minutes since it arrived or came back, while its reviewer is waiting for it, without its review starting, shows as stalled too (*"R1 is watching #333 but hasn't started reviewing it: in Review for 40 min"*), on the board and in its lane's row in the **Progress** panel: show the reviewer and ask it to review the card. So does a card whose reviewer started its review and then went back to waiting on it for 15 minutes without a verdict or a question blocking the card (*"R1 started reviewing #333 but has given no verdict, and has only been watching it for 20 min"*): read its latest comment, which may ask you something, then show the reviewer and ask it to finish its review. A reviewer that asked you on the card (under **For you**) is waiting for your answer, so it isn't stalled. A reviewer waiting on a job the card's builder runs for it (a long test run through `hive-progress`, or a background task) isn't stalled while the job runs, up to the background task limit (**Settings → Agents**); once it ends, the 15 minutes count from then. And so does a lane where each agent waits for the other: the builder sent a card to Review and waits for its verdict, while the reviewer waits for a card that comes before it in the lane, still in Todo or Active, and neither has moved for 15 minutes (*"R1 is waiting for #344, before #364 in the lane, while B1 waits on the review of #364: each has waited on the other for 20 min"*): ask the builder to send the card the reviewer waits for, or the reviewer to review this one. A card whose builder posted a design and has waited past its design check wait for an answer, without going ahead, shows as stalled too (*"B1 posted a design on #364 for its reviewer to check and has waited 40 min: nobody has answered it, and it hasn't gone ahead as posted"*): ask the reviewer to answer it, or tell the builder to go ahead. A lane's builder that waits on nobody is caught too: one that starts watching its own card while nothing on it waits on anyone (it's in Active and not submitted, with no question for you under **For you** and no design out for its reviewer) is told at once to carry on with the work or ask someone, and if it keeps only watching for 5 minutes its lane reports the card as stalled (*"B1 only watches #364 (6 min), and nothing waits on it: no question for the user, no design step, not submitted"*), to the Assistant and in the lane's notices: show the builder and ask it to carry on.
- **Hive tells you how a lane goes, with or without the Assistant.** When a lane's review fails, one of its cards is blocked (with why) or stalls, every card has passed so its merge is due, or that merge stalls, Hive tells you: one notice per lane, as a pop-up in the window you're using or a Windows notification, as **Settings → Notifications** says, kept under the bell either way. A card that needs you (an approval, a question) is told the same way. Each one is also kept as a line in the card's history, with what it said (*Notified you that it's blocked: Which logo?*; a lane's own news on its current card), so you can read it there later, whatever happened to the notification itself, which can come once more after Hive closed unexpectedly. Turning off the project's **Settings → Card loops → Wake reviewers and builders** stops these notices too, with the wakes.
- **Hive keeps the lane's list, and one card at a time.** While workflow rules are enforced (Settings → Advanced), a lane takes its cards one at a time: the next card can't start until the one before it is Done (merged, or accepted), and meanwhile the lane is paused with why (*waiting: HI-11 not merged*). Adding, removing or reordering cards while one is being worked is never refused: the change waits until that card is Done, the lane shows *list changes after HI-12*, and the builder is told when it applies. Only taking cards out (keeping the one being worked) applies at once. **To change the order now**, tick **Apply now** in **Start Lane** for the lane's builder (or ask the Assistant to apply it now): the card being worked goes back to Todo (its work stays on its branch) and the new order applies at once; the builder is told and takes its next card. It isn't offered while that card is in Review or Approved (its review or merge would be lost), and it is refused while the builder is in a turn or the card's folder has changes not committed, so nothing is moved under work in progress. If the lane ends or changes in the meantime, the new order isn't applied and nothing is started again. Undo of the Assistant's reorder puts back only the lane's order: the card stays in Todo. A card in the lane's list that another agent has, such as one moved to another lane, is that lane's: this lane never takes it, waits on it or holds a change for it, and starting a lane with a card another lane lists says so. A lane's cards keep the workflow options in force when the lane started, even if you change them meanwhile (*old rules*). The builder asks Hive for its next card (`hive_lane next`). **Hive starts the lane's next card itself** once the card before it is Done: it gives it to the lane's builder as a Start from the board would, in the lane's slot (or typed into the builder while it's idle, or starting it if it isn't running), so the lane never sits still waiting for a prompt from you or the Assistant. A builder still in a turn (reading that its card is Done, finishing something on it) is waited for until the turn ends; one waiting on something else, or whose last start failed, is left for you to see (the card shows as stalled after 15 minutes). Its first card too, so a lane started over a running agent doesn't sit waiting; while a card waits to start, the lane's row says why (*HI-12 waits to start: B1 is in a turn*). Where the project's rules start a card only once the cards it depends on are Done (Version Control → Workflow → Merging), a lane card that depends on one not merged yet waits for it (*HI-12 waits to start: it waits for HI-11 to merge*) and starts once it is, never shown as stalled meanwhile. A blocked card is never started. The builder's wake when its card is Done names what comes next (*"Your lane's next card is HI-12 ("Show progress"): Hive starts it for you once your turn ends"*, or that it was the lane's last card). A card that was started ahead of its turn (you dragged it to Active, or it was started before the lane listed it) moves before the lane's earlier cards nobody has started yet when it goes to Review, and its reviewer is told to review it now, so the two agents never wait on each other. A lane ends by itself once every card is Done, its merge taken from what Hive recorded of each card, not from the agent's word; an agent can't abandon a lane: that's yours, or the Assistant's.
- **When Hive merges the cards (the merge queue), builders don't.** A builder that tries to report its card's merge, or take the merge slot for it, is refused with where the card is in the queue (*in the merge queue for main, position 2*) and told to wait for Done or for the card to come back; the refusal shows on the card too. If the queue sends the card back, the builder's wake says what came back (a conflict's files, the merge check that failed with its log, a hook) and what to do next; a card that failed for the network or another temporary reason can be submitted again unchanged. What counts is the rules a card started with: changing who merges in Project Settings doesn't change cards already started. With **Settings → Advanced → Workflow rules are enforced** off, agents merge and report their merges as before.
- **An agent's branch after a squash merge.** When an agent builds in its own worktree and its card is merged as a squash, its branch still has the card's commits, so its next card would include them. Before the lane's next card starts, Hive brings the branch up to date with the target: once the agent is idle, its runs have ended and its worktree is clean, and only when everything on the branch is in the target. Meanwhile the next card waits (*"waiting: bringing the builder's branch up to date"*). If the branch has work that isn't in the target (a commit made after the review passed), or git won't move it, Hive asks you under **For you** and moves nothing. Turn off **Settings → Advanced → Bring an agent's branch up to date after a squash merge** to be asked every time: approve and Hive does it once, or decline and do it yourself. A merge commit leaves the branch as it is.
- **A question for you blocks its card.** When the builder or the reviewer needs your decision (a scope question, a finding the builder disputes, the last round failing), or the builder's merge at the lane's end needs your OK (its CLI refused a commit, say), it blocks the card with its question and notifies you, so the card shows as blocked on the board and the Assistant's lane watch picks it up. Answer it (to the Assistant, or as a decision on the card): Hive tells the agent, which clears the block and carries on.
- **The Assistant can still reach a waiting or busy agent.** It can't give a new task to an agent that is waiting, working or busy with a test run, but it can leave it a **message** (to pass on your answer, or add a card to its lane). An agent waiting on cards gets it at once and keeps waiting afterwards; a busy one hears of it at its next step, or before its turn ends. Until it has read it, its pane header shows **✉ 1 unread**: hover it to read it, click it to cancel it.
- **Messages are kept, and you can see where they got to.** Each message has an id (m4) and is saved in the project, so it survives the agent stopping and Hive restarting. Hive types only a short line into an idle agent (*"[Hive] New in your inbox: 1 message (m4). Call hive_inbox to read it, act on it, then ack it."*), never the message itself: the agent reads it from its inbox (`hive_inbox`), which is when it counts as received, and **acks** it when it has acted on it. An agent just started with a task (a card's **Start**, a new conversation) isn't idle until its coding agent has taken that task, for up to 30 seconds after it's ready: a message left in that moment waits, so its line never goes in ahead of the task. Nor is an agent told anything before Hive's tools have connected in it: a resumed session is ready a moment before they are (usually about a second), and a line typed then would be a turn in which the agent can't read its inbox. A message, or a task given in that moment, waits until they connect, then goes in. If they never connect (Hive's server failed to start in that CLI), Hive tells the agent anyway 30 seconds after it became ready; **Settings → Advanced**, Hive → **hive tools: wait to connect** changes that. Until the agent has read it you can still cancel it. Agents read their inbox with Hive's tools, so with **Settings → Agent API** or its built-in hive server off they have none: nothing the Assistant or Hive leaves them reaches them, and the sender is told why (*"B1 has no inbox: turn on the Agent API"*). A card you **Start** yourself still goes in, typed as your own input. One not acked within 30 minutes shows as **✉ 1 overdue** in its pane. The Assistant sees each one's state and times in the agent's activity. A message still waiting when the Assistant that left it stops (or Hive restarts) is refused, not sent later, and the Assistant's activity list says so.
- **Everything Hive tells an agent is kept the same way**: a card watch's wake, a loop's handoff, your decisions, a mode change, a lock's go-ahead, a task. Each is saved, so a restart can't lose it, and each reaches the agent the same way: Hive types the short line, and the agent reads what it says in its inbox. None counts as delivered just because Hive typed it: a card watch ends once the agent has read its wake. An agent that let the line pass and went idle again without reading it hears it once more. One Hive was typing when it stopped, or that the agent's session lost before reading it, is offered once more after the restart, saying it may repeat (*"m12 again: Hive restarted while offering it, so it may repeat"*).
- **A busy agent hears of a message between its steps.** A message for an agent that is working doesn't wait for its turn to end, and isn't typed over its work: at its next tool call Hive adds a short notice to what its CLI tells it (*"[Hive] New in your inbox: 1 message (m4). Call hive_inbox now to read it; act on it and ack it, then carry on."*), and if its turn would end with the message unread, Hive asks it to read it first. The agent reads the message from its inbox, labelled with who sent it, and acks it. Hive's notices only ever say how many items there are and their ids, never what they say, so text in a card or a message that claims to be from you ("the user says…") reaches the agent as a quote, not as your instruction. Agents act on what the Assistant relays of your decisions, and ask you before deleting work or doing anything outside their project, whoever asks. This works the same in Claude Code, Codex and GitHub Copilot. An agent that has ended its turn while a background command of its own runs (a sleep, a timer) counts as idle for this: its messages and card events reach it within seconds, not when the command ends.
- **A message about a round goes stale, not late.** When the Assistant relays something about a card's current round (a finding to fix, a change to the round), it ties the message to that card. If the card moves on before the agent reads it (it passes, leaves Active and Review, or another review fails), Hive drops the message as **stale** instead of delivering it late, so an agent never reopens a passed card for an old finding. The agent's activity shows why (*"stale: #699 passed review"*), and so does the Assistant's activity list.
- **Cancel Card Watch** in the agent's **⋯** menu ends the wait. Typing to the agent doesn't, and Hive doesn't wake it while you're typing there. A waiting agent takes no other work: the Assistant won't prompt it and **Start…** can't choose it.
- **A card taken out of a loop.** If you give a card another agent, or another agent reviews it, the agent that was waiting on it is told once (*"#354 was reassigned to B4 (your watch on it ended)"*) and moves on to its next card, instead of waiting for a card that won't come back to it. The same goes for a card that is merged (in **Done**) while an agent still waits for it to come to Review, and for the cards of a lane that ended or that you abandoned: the agent is told its wait is over and goes idle, ready for its next lane (off with the workflow option **Wake reviewers and builders**, under **Card loops**). A **blocked** card (waiting for your decision, say) stays in its loop, paused: its reviewer keeps waiting for it, and once you've answered and it's back in Review, it is reviewed as usual. On a card in Review, recording your answer, or clearing its block, doesn't wake the loop by itself (an agent working on the card, or waiting for your answer, hears of it anyway), so when the Assistant does it for you it also leaves the card's builder a message saying what you decided (and its reviewer, when it changes what they check). A wait on several cards keeps the others. The card's history says whose wait ended. Agents still on the card, and the Assistant, keep their waits, until the card reaches **Done**: then every wait on it ends, whoever waits and with the option on or off, and an agent that starts waiting on a card already in Done is told at once.
- **How long it waits.** With no change for six hours, Hive wakes the agent, which tells you. Say *"wait: 2h"* for another wait, up to a day.
- **Your defaults for loops.** **Rounds** and **Wait (hours)**, under **Card loops** in the workflow options, set the rounds and the wait for every loop that doesn't say otherwise (10 and 6 hours to start with): Hive's and the workspace's in the [Hive and workspace defaults](#workflow-defaults), a project's own in its **Version Control → Workflow**. What you say when you start a loop (*"rounds: 3, wait: 1h"*) always wins. Agents read them when they register the loop, so a change applies to loops started afterwards; a loop already running keeps its own. **Design check wait (minutes)** sits beside them. Before 1.0 these were in Settings → Workspace and the project's Settings → Card loops: your values moved into the options.
- **Rounds.** A round is one build and its review: a card that passes its first review passed in round 1, and each fix with its review is another round; when you review a card yourself and decline it, that is a round too. When the tenth round's review fails, Hive asks you what to do (*"HI-12 failed 10 reviews: carry on with more rounds, split it, accept it with follow-ups, or take it over?"*, as a notice and on the card) and the lane pauses on the card until you answer; carrying on adds the rounds you choose, and your answer wakes its builder and reviewer. The agents also stop and ask you, with a line per round. With **Settings → Advanced → Workflow rules are enforced** off, Hive doesn't ask and the lane doesn't pause for it. Say *"rounds: 15"* to allow more, or *"rounds: 3"* for fewer. A finding that comes back because a fix missed part of it doesn't stop them sooner: the reviewer marks it *recurring* and it is another round. A finding the builder disputes does: they stop and ask you.
- **A change asked for during a review.** When a reviewer asks for a commit while it reviews a card, the builder submits it as a *follow-up*: the card stays in **Review** and the builder's, no round is counted, its history says *Follow-up submitted for review at 1a2b3c4*, and Hive wakes the reviewer, whose next review is of that commit (its review copy moves to it). If a reviewer moves a card it reviews back to **Doing** itself, the card stays its builder's (HI-1832).
- **Approved, then Done.** The reviewer moves a card that passes to **Approved**. Once the builder has merged its branch (when you ask it to, or have said it may), it moves its Approved cards to **Done**, which means merged.
- **Clear wake lines.** The line names the reviewer when a review passed or failed (*"#112 is in Review: Codex (hive) failed it"*). An agent waiting on **another agent's card** (one its own card depends on) is told whose card it is, and that Passed isn't merged: *"#111 (Claude's card) is in Passed: Codex (hive) passed it (Passed isn't merged)"*. Passed means the review passed; the work is merged when it reaches Done.
- **Nothing waits for what has already happened.** A wait for a card to reach a column it is already in ends at once: a builder that starts waiting for *"→ Passed"* after its card has passed carries on straight away. A reviewer waiting for a failed card to come back asks for its next round explicitly, so it keeps waiting while the card is still in Review.
- **Cards reviewed together.** When two watched cards change at about the same moment (one passed, the other failed), the line names both. A change that lands just after an agent was woken still reaches it: its next wait counts what others changed since its last wake.
- **Closing Hive or stopping an agent** keeps its wait: resume the agent and it is woken if its card changed meanwhile. Hive asks first, as it does for a working agent, and says what it waits for. **Quit when agents finish** waits for a waiting agent only while the other agent is working on its card.
- **While it waits** you can still **Compact** it (it is woken once that's done). **Merge…** waits until it has finished, or until you cancel its wait.
- **Hive knows its loops.** Each loop is kept as a **lane**: its builder and reviewer, its cards in order, its rounds and its wait. The builder registers it as it starts and the reviewer joins it; you don't set anything up. Hive follows the board from there: the lane is on the card being worked, **paused** while it waits for your answer (its next card is blocked and no other is being worked; a builder that moves on to another card meanwhile keeps it running), and **ended** once its cards are all Done. A lane whose builder or reviewer you remove is **abandoned**. Ask the Assistant *"which lanes are running?"*, or read them through the Agent API. One agent working on a card on its own has no lane, and a loop works the same if a lane can't be registered.
- **Reports start with the gist.** An agent sending a card to Review starts its comment with one line saying what is ready, and a reviewer starts its verdict with one line (*"Round 1: FAILED: 2 findings: …"*), the details below. Agents and the Assistant read just those lines when the gist is enough, so long reports cost less to follow.
- **The Assistant watches whole lanes.** Running several lanes, the Assistant waits on all of them in one go and is woken only when something needs it: a review fails, a card is blocked (a question for you), unblocked again (you OK'd what it waited for), still blocked but with news (its builder commented, say with new screenshots for your OK, or changed why it's blocked) or stalled, a lane's cards have all passed so its merge is due (and again if that merge then stalls: its builder idle for 10 minutes without taking its turn to merge), a card the merge queue sent back sits for 15 minutes with its builder idle or only watching it, or a lane ends (with its merge commit, *"ended: merged at dd4ed990c0ff"*). What you type to a lane's agent in its pane reaches the Assistant too, with its first line, while it keeps watching (**When you answer an agent yourself**, below). A card simply moving on, or passing, doesn't wake it, and a lane started later, or a card added to one, is followed too. Its status says **Waiting on all running lanes**. The same watch follows cards in no lane that it keeps an eye on, such as a release card (**Waiting on all running lanes and #720**): a comment, a move, a verdict or a block on one wakes it too. And when any agent starts waiting for you (a permission prompt or a question in its pane), the Assistant is woken once, told which agent, its card and what it asks, so it can tell you; it never answers for you. So does a question an agent asks you on a lane's card (under **For you**): the Assistant is woken naming the card and the question's first line, however it narrowed what wakes it (HI-1991). Asked to tell you when an agent's work on no card is done (a build, say) while lanes run, it follows that agent in the same watch (**Waiting on all running lanes and B2**), rather than swapping its lane watch for an agent watch and losing the lanes meanwhile; if it swaps anyway, Hive's reply tells it which lanes it just stopped following (HI-1987). A lane pausing for a card's merge only wakes it when it asked for that. An agent that needs something only the Assistant or you can do on a card (parking a card On Hold, moving one to Done on your answer) asks the Assistant with a tool rather than in a comment: the request is the agent's comment on the card, with *Asked the Hive Assistant: …* in its history, and it wakes the Assistant, which does it only within its Control and what you decided, and tells the agent. If the Assistant can't take it (its Control doesn't let it change cards), the agent is told to ask you instead; one the Assistant never got says so on the card and in a notice.
- **Each lane in a new conversation.** When the Assistant starts an agent on its next set of cards (a new *lane*), it gives the agent a new conversation in the same step: the old one ends and stays in the **Sessions** tab, and the new one starts on the task, with no handover. Within a lane, the agent keeps its conversation. Every request re-reads the whole conversation, so short ones cost much less. The Assistant doesn't do it while the agent is busy, waiting for you or watching a card, or just after you typed in it.

#### Messages and the inbox <a id="messages-and-the-inbox"></a>

Hive gets an agent to read its inbox in three ways, each with its own switch in **Settings → Advanced** (under Hive), on by default. A project can set its own of each in **Project Settings → Sessions** (Inherit follows Settings'); the Hive Assistant follows Settings'.

- **Tell a working agent at the end of a tool** (`hive.inbox.checkpoints`): what arrives while an agent works is named in the reply to its next tool call, so it reads it without stopping. Off, it isn't told until its turn ends or it is idle.
- **Keep an agent going when it stops with unread items** (`hive.inbox.stopContinuation`): an agent about to end its turn with items unread is asked to read them first, once a turn. Codex and Copilot have their stop held back; Claude Code gets a line added to its stop. Off, the turn ends as the CLI ends it, and the items wait for an idle wake.
- **Lanes: remind the Assistant to watch them** (`hive.lanes.unwatchedNotice`): when the Hive Assistant may control agents (**Settings → Assistant → Control** at **Control agents** or above) and is about to end its turn while lanes are running and no watch of its covers them, Hive keeps it going once to say so, naming the lanes' builders, so it watches them before it finishes. At **Look and advise** the lanes are yours to run, so it isn't told. Off, nothing wakes it about those lanes until it watches them again. It needs **Keep an agent going when it stops with unread items** on.
- **Wake an idle agent** (`hive.inbox.idleWakes`): Hive types one short line into an idle agent's terminal saying what has arrived (*"[Hive] New in your inbox: 1 message (m4). Call hive_inbox to read it, act on it, then ack it."*). A card loop's wakes (the workflow option **Wake reviewers and builders**) and a card watch's are such lines. Off, nothing is typed for anything waiting, of any kind: the agent's pane shows **✉ 2 unread**, and its menu's **Deliver Now** types the line once. A card you start on an agent, or a task given to it now, is still typed: that is no wake.

None of them drops anything: with any of them off, an item stays in the inbox, unread, and counts as overdue after 30 minutes as before. With the first two off, an idle wake still tells the agent once its turn ends; with all three off, it reads its inbox when it next calls `hive_inbox`, or when you press **Deliver Now**. Whether agents can read an inbox at all is **Settings → Agent API → Provide Hive tools to sessions**: the Advanced list links to it beside these switches.

## Hive Assistant <a id="hive-assistant"></a>

Each workspace has a **Hive Assistant**, its overseer, in a panel on the right. Show or hide it with **Ctrl+Alt+I**; hidden, it folds into a narrow **Hive Assistant** strip down the right edge, and clicking the strip opens it. Each workspace remembers whether it's open, and dragging its left edge makes it wider (double-click the edge for the default width). Hiding the panel doesn't stop the Assistant.

**In a narrow or zoomed window**, your work keeps its room and nothing covers it. What is beside it gives way in turn: the Assistant's panel narrows, then the Progress panel, then the sidebar; then the Projects list folds to its rail of status dots, then the Progress panel folds to its strip, and last the Assistant's panel narrows a little more. Each comes back on its own once there's room, and your saved widths, your choice of compact list and the Progress panel's open or folded don't change. Click **›** on the folded rail, or the Progress strip, to see it anyway; the Progress panel you fold yourself stays folded.

**On the left instead.** If Windows notifications or other apps cover the right of the screen, put the panel on the left: **View → Move Assistant Panel to the Left** (also in the command palette and the panel's **⋯** menu), or **Settings → Assistant → Panel side**. There it sits between the project list and your work, folds into its strip on that side and widens from its right edge. The choice is the same in every workspace and window, and Hive keeps it when it restarts.

Ask it anything about the workspace: what the agents are doing, what a project is, what changed, what things cost, or for a plan or a review. It reads any project's files and uses Hive's own tools to see projects, agents, usage, shared notes and handovers. It doesn't edit project files itself: the agents do the work. What else it may do, from only advising to running agents and creating projects, is up to you (see **What the Assistant may do** below).

**The panel.** Its header works like an agent pane's: the Assistant's state mark (a ring when it's idle or not running, a pulsing dot while it works, a diamond when it waits for you, a square when it's stuck), **Assistant** (hover it for its coding agent), its mode as a small pill (click it to switch), then **Stop** while it runs; when it doesn't, **Resume** (|▷) to go back to its last conversation, or **Start** if there is nothing to resume, then **⋯** and the fold button (the chevron, as on the Progress panel: it folds the panel to its strip, Ctrl+Alt+I, and the Assistant keeps running). Everything else is in **⋯**: **Compact…** while it runs (recommended there once its context passes **Highlight Compact over**), **New Conversation**, **Resume** and **Resume a Conversation…**, **All Conversations…**, **Assistant Settings…**, **Manage Modes…**, **Move Panel to the Left** and **Fold the Assistant panel**. Under it, one line says what the Assistant is doing: **Status: Working**, **Idle**, **Waiting for you** (in the attention colour, when it asks you something), **Waiting for your approval** (when one of its actions needs your answer on a card under the line, such as stopping a busy agent; one that is only watching cards it stops without asking, and a question you don't answer within about 4½ minutes is withdrawn, the agent left running), **Not running**, or the cards it is watching and until when ("Waiting for #12 #13 → Review (watch until 22:22)"), each card the same chip as elsewhere in Hive, in its column's colour: click one to open the card; or the agents it is watching ("Waiting for B6 to finish (watch until 22:22)"). Hover the line when a narrow panel cuts it short. Once the Assistant has changed something, the line ends in **Done by the Assistant (n)**: click it to open the list under the line. It lists its changes by your message they were for (**Your message at 08:14: 61 changes**), newest first: the newest message is open, the others folded (click one to open it). A message shows its last 3 changes, newest first, each with **Undo** (hover one for when it was and, if it wasn't done, why); see **Undo** below. **Show all** lists every one in the same space, scrolling, so the terminal keeps its size; click **Done by the Assistant** again to close it (each workspace remembers which). Then the workspace at a glance: agents waiting for you (or in trouble) first (click one to go to it; going to an agent that has finished also removes its row, and its **×**, shown as you point at the row or Tab to it, removes the row without going there, until that agent finishes again), then one line per active project: its name (click it to open the project), a state mark for each of its agents that is running (or not running but stuck on the board: a card of its in Active, or one blocked on you) and how many run. Hover a mark, or Tab to it, for the agent, its state in words, what it's doing (how far its tests or build have got, "e2e: 12 suites 4/12"), its cards, its progress and its worktree's branch; click it to go to that agent, switching project if needed. The tag button beside the counts shows each agent's name beside its mark (each workspace remembers it). Inactive projects fold into one row at the end; click it to show them. Drag the line under the workspace to give it more or less room. Then the Assistant's terminal, and a footer with its model and effort, permission mode, context and cost, as agents have. While it isn't running, the panel offers **Start Assistant**, **Resume** and its past conversations. While the panel is hidden, the strip shows the Assistant's icon and status dot. The same icon (a hive cell with a crown, the queen's) marks the Assistant wherever it's listed: the activity bar, **Done by the Assistant**, its runs in the Progress panel, the inbox and the Workspace Overview.

**Waiting on agents.** Ask the Assistant to tell you when an agent is done (a build, a test run, a release step, a push and its CI) and it doesn't keep checking on it: it starts an **agent watch** and stops, and Hive wakes it with one line when the agent finishes, needs you, or stops (*"[Hive] B6 (hive) finished: "The installer is built…""*), and it tells you. Meanwhile its status line says **Waiting for B6 to finish**. An agent just given a task counts as busy until its CLI takes it, so a watch started straight after doesn't answer at once that it is idle before the work has begun: a task typed into it, or one it was started with (a card's **Start**, a new agent's prompt, a task in a new conversation), which its CLI takes only once it is ready. Hive waits for that at most 30 seconds (after typing it, or after the agent became ready). A question or sign-in its CLI shows as it starts still ends the wait, as needing you. As with a card watch, Hive doesn't wake it while you're typing to it, and after two hours with nothing done it is woken to say so (it checks on the agent, and tells you if it seems stuck). Agents can watch another agent the same way.

**When you answer an agent yourself.** If you reply to an agent by typing in its pane (picking an option it asked about, say), the Assistant doesn't otherwise know. When it is watching that agent, or a card the agent has or reviews, Hive tells it with the first line of what you sent (*"User replied to B1 on #399: "C, the hive cell""*, or by the name you set in **Settings → General → Your name**: *"Darren replied to B1…"*), so it doesn't ask you again and can record a decision on the card. It hears it within seconds also between two of its watches, after it checked an agent that was already idle, and while it watches lanes the agent builds or reviews: the reply comes as a wake of its own. Only the Assistant of the same workspace is told, and only the first line. Lines Hive types into the agent itself don't count. Turn it off with **Settings → Assistant → Tell the Assistant when I reply to an agent**.

It doesn't use one of a project's agent slots, and it runs in the workspace folder. Closing the workspace or the window, or quitting, stops it like any agent (the dialogs call it "Assistant"), and its conversations can be resumed.

### What the Assistant may do <a id="what-the-assistant-may-do"></a>

**Settings → Assistant → Control** decides, for every workspace:

- **Look and advise**: it reads and suggests; you act. It can still tell you which card loops (lanes) are running.
- **Control agents**: when you ask it, it also adds agents, changes their settings, loads a template into a project, starts and stops them, gives idle ones tasks, hands one agent's work over to another, adds, changes and starts cards on the task board, and releases a stuck slot once you agree (see **A slot held after Hive closed**). Ask *"add two agents to web: one to fix the login tests, one to update the docs"*, or *"ask Codex to review hive, then hand its findings over to Claude to fix"*, and it does.
- **Control agents and create projects** (the default): also new projects.

It never removes agents (but replacing a project's agents with a template's when you ask it to load one, see [Loading a template through the Assistant](#assistant-templates)), discards worktrees, archives or deletes cards (but one it made itself, when it undoes it), or removes projects, and never edits project files itself: the agents do the work. It won't type into an agent that is working, asking you something, or that you have just typed in: for 15 seconds after your last key, unless that key was Enter (**Settings → Assistant → Pause after you type** and **Enter ends the pause**). Before it stops an agent in the middle of something, or moves a card to Done, a card at the top of its panel asks you (**Stop** or **Don't stop**, **Move it** or **Leave it**). Everything it does is listed under **Done by the Assistant** in its panel, where you can undo its changes (below). There is no limit on how many changes it makes for a message: it moves or labels up to 100 cards in one step. Your view never moves: an agent it adds on the project you're looking at shows a dot on its page's button, and another project opens on the new agent's page the next time you go to it.

**Undo.** Hive keeps what the Assistant changes for 7 days, grouped by your message it was for, with what each change replaced. That covers a card's title, description, labels, block, links, agent, column and project, the cards it made, a lane it started or changed, an agent's settings, a Hive setting, an agent it started, and a template it loaded (the project's agents, layout and workflow options). It is kept across restarts. Ask it to undo (*"undo what you just did"*, or one change) and it puts back each thing that is still as it left it. Anything you or an agent changed since stays as it is, and it says so. A card it made is archived, a lane it started is abandoned, and an agent it started is stopped (the work it did stays). Prompts, messages and comments can't be taken back. What it undoes is listed in **Done by the Assistant**. It undoes only its own changes, never yours or an agent's.

**Undo it yourself**, in **Done by the Assistant**. Each change has **Undo** on its row, and each message **Undo** on its heading, for every change made for it. A message's Undo asks first (*Undo 61 changes? Changes made since by you or an agent are kept.*), then says what it did under the heading: how many changes it undid, and each one it kept as it is with why (*#12 Fix the login: labels changed since*), and those it can't undo with why. An undone change stays in the list, struck through, with when (**Undone 2 minutes ago**), and isn't offered again; one undo had to leave as it was says **Kept**, or **Partly undone** when only some of it went back (hover it for why), whether you or the Assistant undid it, and after a restart too. What can't be undone says so on its row (**Can't be undone**; hover it for why): a prompt or message that was sent, a comment, a decision it recorded, a card's place in its column. Undoes the Assistant made with its own tool are listed with the message they were for.

When an agent's CLI asks whether to trust a new folder before it starts, the agent shows as **waiting** for you: answer in its terminal.

### The Assistant's journal <a id="the-assistants-journal"></a>

The Assistant keeps a **journal** in each workspace: its memory across sessions. Instead of reading a long handover after a restart or a compaction, it recalls a few lines at a time: what was decided and why, plans, what happened, incidents, what it learned. Each entry is a one-line summary with optional detail, a project, an importance, and links to cards, commits, notes and other entries; newer entries can supersede older ones. Hive adds its own facts as they happen: a lane started and ended (with its merge), a card moved to Done, Hive closing unexpectedly, and, when you build Hive itself from its source code, each successful build (`npm run dist` or `npm run release`) with its version, commit, kind (beta, baseline, release candidate or release) and where its installer is. A build made while Hive is closed is recorded when the workspace next opens.

It is in **Settings → Assistant → Journal**, for the workspace you have open:

- **Keep a journal** (on by default). Off, nothing is written and the Assistant's journal tools go at its next start; the entries are kept for when you turn it on again.
- **What it may store**: decisions, plans, events, incidents and insights by default. **Your preferences** (how you like things done) and **Moments** (memorable moments of working together) are off until you turn them on. The Assistant can't store a kind you leave out, and it is told when you change them.
- **Keep everyday entries for** 90 days by default (7 to 3650). Important entries and the weekly digests the Assistant writes are kept longer. A number out of range is refused, saying so, and a box left empty keeps what it had: neither changes anything.
- **Size cap**, 50 MB by default, or **No limit**. Past it, the oldest everyday entries go first. The journal's size and number of entries show below.
- **Delete Journal…** asks first, then moves the journal to the Recycle Bin; the Assistant starts again with an empty one.

**What it does for you.** Each time the Assistant starts, and again whenever its conversation is compacted, Hive gives it a short view of where things stand: what waits on you, the cards in Active and Review, lanes and agents running, and the few journal entries that matter now (recent decisions and plans, incidents, entries about the cards in hand). It does so whichever provider the Assistant runs on (with Copilot, after a compaction it comes with your next message). It is kept small, about two thousand tokens at most, and doesn't grow with your board: when more than ten things wait on you, it shows the ten that matter most (agents asking now, cards blocked on a question, then On Hold cards waiting on a decision of yours, before the ones waiting for a later version) and counts the rest, and each other section shows a few lines too, so the Assistant's context stays low; it recalls anything else from its journal when it needs it. It writes your decisions in your own words as you make them, and once a week a digest of the week before. Asked for a handover, it tells you one isn't needed (its journal carries on), and writes one only if you still want it, for another agent or a person. With the journal off, it writes handovers as before, and suggests the journal once.

The journal is a file in the workspace, `.hive/assistant/journal.db`, kept on this computer only and never sent anywhere (it stays out of git with the Assistant's other files). Long entries are kept compressed, and a search finds words in summaries, details and reasons.

### Reading and correcting the journal <a id="reading-and-correcting-the-journal"></a>

The **Journal** section of the Assistant view (or **Show Assistant Journal** in the command palette) shows the journal itself. **Timeline** lists every entry newest first, a line each under its day: the time, a mark for its kind, the summary, its project, a star for an important one, who wrote it (the Assistant's mark, or Hive's for its own facts) and the cards it links to as the board's chips, which open the card. A superseded entry is struck through. **Weekly Digests** lists only the Assistant's weekly summaries.

- **Search** finds entries with every word you type (the start of a word is enough) in their summary, detail or reason. The filters narrow it by **kind**, **project** (or the workspace's own entries), who wrote it (**Hive** or **the Assistant**) and a **From** and **To** day. **Clear Filters** shows everything again.
- **Click an entry** (or **Enter** on it; the arrow keys move along the timeline, **Ctrl+F** goes to the search) to open it beside the timeline: its detail, why it mattered, when, project, importance, links (cards, commits, notes, the entries it supersedes) and tags.
- **Edit** corrects its summary, kind, importance, detail or reason. The kinds offered are those the journal may store (Settings → Assistant → Journal) and its own.
- **Delete…** deletes it after asking. **Forget These…** deletes everything the search and filters found, after saying how many: "forget everything about X" is a search for X, then Forget. If an entry is written, changed or deleted so that the search finds something else while you're being asked, nothing is deleted and Hive says how many match now, so you can look and ask again. Deleted entries are gone for good (they don't go to the Recycle Bin), and the Assistant can no longer recall them. To delete the whole journal, use **Delete Journal…** in Settings.

The footer shows the journal's size, how many entries it has, how long everyday entries are kept and its size cap, with **Journal Settings** to change them. While the journal is off, the browser says so with **Turn On**; its entries can still be read, corrected and deleted. The browser follows changes as they happen: an entry the Assistant or Hive writes shows at once.

### The Assistant and Hive's settings <a id="the-assistant-and-hives-settings"></a>

Hive has a lot of settings. Ask the Assistant what one does (*"what does Warn when a transcript is over do?"*) or how to make Hive behave differently (*"the chime is driving me mad"*), and it finds the setting, explains it and says where it is. It reads the same descriptions Settings shows. When it notices something a setting would help with, such as a transcript growing very large or several agents editing the same files, it suggests that setting in a line, and changes nothing unless you agree.

To let it make the change for you, turn on **Settings → Assistant → Control → Change settings** (off by default; a running Assistant takes it after a restart). With it on, the Assistant changes a setting when you ask it to or agree to its suggestion, in Settings or in a project's Project Settings. Each change shows under **Done by the Assistant** with the old and new value and an **Undo** button that puts the old value back. Some settings stay yours whatever you allow: its own Control and the other Settings → Assistant permissions, permission modes, the Agent API, and what Hive runs (CLI paths, extra arguments, a project's setup command). For those it tells you where to change them. Which connection an agent runs on is part of running agents (Control agents); making or changing a connection (its model, effort, mode) is under this switch.

### The Assistant view <a id="the-assistant-view"></a>

The **Hive Assistant** button in the activity bar (the crown on a hive cell) opens everything else about it. At the top, **Used so far**: its conversations, prompts, tokens and API-equivalent cost, for Today, 7 days, 30 days or All time. These aren't counted in any project's Overview. Below that:

- **Modes** lists its modes (below).
- **All Conversations** shows every conversation you've had with it, in the same tree as a project's **Sessions** tab (by provider, with any sessions a conversation started under it). Search them, read any in full, export, rename, archive, delete or resume one, or archive or delete a whole branch. **Show** on the running conversation opens the panel.
- **All Attachments** shows the screenshots and files you've pasted or dropped into its conversations, grouped by conversation, newest first, like a project's [Attachments](#attachments) tab. Click one to view it, or right-click it to copy or reveal it. Click a conversation's name to read it. The bin next to it moves all of that conversation's attachments to the Recycle Bin (none if another program has one open). Attachments of a conversation that is running can't be deleted until it stops.

The panel's **⋯** menu opens the same view with **All Conversations…** or **Manage Modes…**; the command palette has **Show Assistant Conversations**, **Show Assistant Modes** and **Show Assistant Attachments**.

### Modes <a id="modes"></a>

A **mode** is how the Assistant works: what it puts first, its habits, and the shape of what it hands back. It is written as instructions in a Markdown file in the workspace's `.hive/modes` folder (`.hive/personas` before Hive 0.9.3: Hive moves it, with your edits, when the workspace opens). Hive comes with four:

- 🧭 **Coordinator** (the default): runs the work across your agents. It checks the board and the agents before every request, looks a fact up (the card, the project's docs, then the card's builder) before asking you for it, takes what you ask for as your go-ahead, reports in short status tables, starts agents on settled cards, follows the work until it ends, checks what agents report, and asks you only for real decisions, not what Hive's rules already decide. With a team (three or more agents, or several builder and reviewer pairs) it plans lanes by the files they touch, briefs each builder and reviewer in full and watches every lane to its end; a builder and reviewer pair's cards run as a lane, even one card, where an agent reviews; with one agent it stays light, and at most suggests more structure in a line. Either way you can still use card loops, reviews and everything else.
- 🗺️ **Planner**: shapes work before anyone starts it. It leads with the open design questions (a recommendation each), compares options, splits the work into lanes by the files it touches, and writes complete cards.
- 🔍 **QA triager**: turns a report (what you did, what you saw) into a card. It reproduces the problem or reads the code to find the likely cause, says what it checked, and files the card with the evidence and a proposed fix in the right lane. Before filing, it checks for a card that already covers it, including ones reviewed but not merged yet (the **triage-report** skill).
- 📦 **Release manager**: takes a release through the project's release path (its RELEASING.md) one stage at a time, such as a build for daily use, a release candidate and the release, checking the release notes, licences and the packaged build. Each step that builds an installer, changes the version, merges into the main branch, tags, uploads or publishes waits for you to ask for it: an OK for one isn't an OK for the next.

A mode never changes what the Assistant may do: that is **Settings → Assistant → Control**, whatever a mode says, and how it runs agents and cards comes from Hive's own skills. When a request clearly fits another mode, the Assistant may suggest switching in a line; it never switches by itself.

**Switching mode** (the mode menu in the panel's header) takes effect at once and keeps the conversation: Hive tells the running Assistant the new mode and its habits in a short line (`[Hive] Mode: Planner …`), or tells it as soon as it has finished what it is doing. Its next conversation starts in that mode, and if you switch while it isn't running, Hive tells it the mode the same way when you resume its conversation (once). **Restart in This Mode…** (in the same menu) resumes the same conversation with the mode's full instructions; it caches the whole conversation again, so it costs more than switching.

The **Modes** section of the Assistant view lists them. Click one to read or edit it, **+** to write your own, and the bin (on hover, or at the top of an open one) to delete one. Hive's own come back with **Restore** or **Revert to Default**, and Hive keeps them up to date in each workspace as it does its skills: one you've edited stays as you wrote it. **Use in This Workspace** makes one the Assistant's. A mode of your own can have a `summary:` in its header (its habits in a few lines), which is what Hive tells the Assistant when you switch to it; without one, Hive uses the first lines of its instructions.

**From personas to modes.** Up to 0.3, Hive shipped character personas (Overseer, Planner, Reviewer, Orchestrator). The modes replace them: a workspace's unedited copies go, and an Assistant that used one moves to the closest mode (Overseer and Orchestrator → Coordinator, Reviewer → QA triager), which Hive says once. Personas you wrote or edited stay as they are.

### Assistant settings <a id="assistant-settings"></a>

**Assistant Settings** (in the Assistant panel's **⋯**) chooses, for this workspace:

- **Connection** — the setup it runs on (its model, effort, context and permission mode, on one of your accounts; its CLI follows from it), as an agent's [connection](#several-agents-in-one-project) is chosen. A connection whose permission mode is *The CLI's own setting* is greyed out, saying why: the Assistant's Control relies on knowing its mode. Choosing another connection restarts a running Assistant after asking: on the same CLI it resumes its conversation; on another CLI it starts a new one (conversations can't move between CLIs; the earlier one still resumes from its own CLI).
- **Mode** — Coordinator, Planner, QA triager, Release manager or your own; a new mode is told to a running Assistant at once.

A new workspace's Assistant runs on the first connection that can be used there, until you choose one.

**Settings → Assistant** holds what applies to every workspace: the default mode (Coordinator unless you choose another), **Control** and **Change settings** (above), **Pause after you type** and **Enter ends the pause**, **Tell the Assistant when I reply to an agent** (on: whether its watches hear the first line of what you send an agent from its pane), **Panel side**, **Highlight Compact over** (the context size at which the Assistant's **Compact…** is recommended and the context in its footer turns orange, as an agent's does past **Settings → Sessions → Suggest compacting above**; 500,000 tokens by default, higher than your agents' 200,000, since the Assistant usually works with a bigger context; **Never** turns it off) and the [journal](#the-assistants-journal). It has no provider, model or effort of its own: its connection holds them.

Keep the Assistant on a connection with your agents' model and effort: a lighter model or low effort makes it careless (it may say it will check on an agent later and never do). Its mode, like your agents', is best one that approves safe actions itself and only asks about risky ones: **Auto** for Claude Code and **Approve for me** for Codex. Claude Code says which models it runs in Auto, for your version and account (currently not **Haiku**), and the connection dialog warns you when you pick a model without it: the session then runs in Manual instead (asking before edits and commands), and Hive shows that mode. With **Change settings** on, the Assistant can make a connection on one of your accounts or change one when you agree (`hive_connection`), for example High effort for a project's builders; adding an account (a sign-in, a server, a key) is always yours.

#### DeepSeek as the Assistant <a id="deepseek-as-the-assistant"></a>

The Assistant can run on a model from a server, such as DeepSeek, through Claude Code: **Settings → Accounts & Connections → Add Account… → Claude Code → Gets its model from: A server**, the **DeepSeek** preset and your key. Its first connection, *DeepSeek*, is made with it: **Edit** it and set **Permission mode** to **Manual** (or **Accept edits**), then choose it in **Assistant Settings → Connection**. Before you rely on it:

- **Its mode**: Claude Code runs Auto only on models it lists, so on a server it would run in Manual anyway, asking before edits and commands; setting the connection's mode says so in the badge.
- **Hive's tools are untested there**: the Assistant makes many `hive` tool calls a turn, and a model that calls tools poorly makes it unreliable (it says it will check and doesn't). Try it on a workspace you can watch.
- **No effort or context choice**: a server's connections have none. Its cost shows only once you set a price for its model in **Settings → Claude Code → API prices**.
- **What DeepSeek sees**: the key stays in Hive's key store, but DeepSeek receives the Assistant's prompts, which include card text and the contents of files in the workspace.

## Skills <a id="skills"></a>

Skills are instructions an agent loads when they're relevant. Every skill an agent can see is available to it; Hive's own skills can also be switched off for agents (below, **Choosing the skills agents get**). Hive shows where each one comes from:

| Level | Where | Who gets it | In Hive |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hive** | `Workspace/.hive/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` | Its audience: the project agents (every agent in every project, of every provider; the default), the Hive Assistant, or both | Add, edit and delete in the **Skills** view |
| **Local (User Managed)** | `Project/.claude/skills` (Claude Code), `Project/.agents/skills` (Codex) | That provider's agents, in that project | Add, edit and delete in the project's **Skills** tab |
| **User** | `~/.claude/skills` (Claude Code), `~/.codex/skills` (Codex) | That provider's agents, everywhere | View only |
| **Plugin** | Claude Code plugins you've installed | Claude Code agents, everywhere | View only |

**The Skills view** (the sparkle in the activity bar) lists the workspace's Hive skills. **+** creates one from a starter `SKILL.md`; **Add Skill from File** adds a `.md` (it becomes the skill's `SKILL.md`) or a `.zip` (unpacked as the skill's folder: use a zip for a skill with scripts or other files). Select a skill to read it; **Edit** changes it, and the bin deletes it (to the Recycle Bin). A warning over the list, and over a Hive skill while you edit it, reminds you that editing these skills may change how agents behave in Hive: they're instructions every agent (or the Assistant) follows.

**A project's Skills tab** lists everything its agents get: the Hive skills first (those for the Hive Assistant alone aren't listed; a note under them says how many there are), then one provider's local skills, your user skills and plugin skills, folded under its name: click it to open them. Pick the provider from the dropdown above them: it starts on the CLI of your first usable connection, the one a new agent would get. Hive remembers, for each project, the provider you last picked and which groups you left open or folded. Hive skills are shared by every project, so their **Edit in workspace** button takes you to the Skills view to edit them there. Local skills belong to the project: add (with **+** or from a `.md` or `.zip`), edit and delete them right there. When both Claude Code and Codex are on, adding a local skill offers to add it for the other provider too, since each reads its own folder. **Copy to workspace** turns a local or user skill into a Hive skill: the project agents in every project get it, unless its header names another audience (the notification says who).

**Skills that come with Hive.** Hive gives every workspace its own skills for working with Hive:

- for agents: `work-on-card` (carry a card from Active to Review; tests it writes wait for what they expect to see, never for a length of time, so they pass on a slower or busier machine too), `review-agent-work` (review another agent's work without changing it, marking the card; a new test that sleeps and then checks fails the review; it checks the builder's record of the tests it ran rather than running them all again), `merge-ready` (get a branch ready to review and merge, and merge a card's work where your project's options make that the agent's job: where Hive merges, it leaves that to the merge queue), `licence-audit` (check the licences of everything a project ships, files vendored inside packages and what the installer adds included, and flag anything that isn't plainly permissive for you to decide) and `use-hive-api` (scripts that call Hive's Agent API);
- for the Hive Assistant: `coordinate-agents` (plan work as cards, brief and start agents, follow them) and `run-lanes` (run a team's card loops: plan lanes, brief builders and reviewers, watch them to the end), `triage-report` (turn a problem you report into a card with its cause and evidence) and `board-hygiene` (tidy the board);
- for both: `handover` (wrap up for a later session), `pick-up` (carry on from a handover, checking it against the current state), `split-work` (plan how several agents can share a task) and `workspace-note` (record a decision or convention in the shared notes).

They're ordinary Hive skills: edit or delete them as you like. **Hive keeps them up to date**: when a new version of Hive improves one, a copy you haven't changed is updated when the workspace next opens, and a skill new in that version is added. If you edit, delete or replace one while Hive is updating it, your version stays and the update waits for the next time (if Hive was interrupted mid-update, anything of yours it had set aside is kept beside the skill as `<name>-conflict-<date>`). A copy you've edited is left as you wrote it: its page offers **Revert to default** (with **Update available** when Hive has a newer version than the one you edited). A skill you've deleted stays deleted: it's listed greyed out, with **Restore**. Revert and Restore send your copy to the Recycle Bin.

**A skill's files.** An agent reads a skill's `SKILL.md` whenever it uses the skill, so a long one costs every time. A skill can keep `SKILL.md` short, as a contents page, and put what is needed only sometimes (a role's steps, edge cases, rare paths) in other `.md` files beside it, each linked from `SKILL.md` (`[builder.md](builder.md)`): the agent reads one only when its link applies. Hive's own long skills are split this way (HI-2011): `card-loop`, for example, keeps the lane and waiting in `SKILL.md`, and the builder's and the reviewer's steps in `builder.md` and `reviewer.md`. A skill with more than its `SKILL.md` shows its files as tabs above its page, `SKILL.md` first: click one to read it, and edit it where you'd edit the skill, with the same check when it changed on disk meanwhile. A link to one of them in the preview opens its tab. Hive copies the whole folder to agents, and **Restore** and **Revert to Default** put back the whole folder. Add a file by putting it in the skill's folder (the folder button).

**Who gets a skill.** Each Hive skill's page shows who gets it: **Project agents**, **Assistant** or **Agents + Assistant** (in the list, the last two are marked). Your own Hive skills go to the project agents. If a skill's header can't be read, or its audience is misspelt, nobody gets it until you fix it: it's marked **Not given**, with the reason on hover. To write one for the Assistant, add `audience: assistant` (or `all`, for both) under `metadata:` in its `SKILL.md`'s header:

```yaml
---
name: weekly-report
description: Summarise the week's cards and agents for the user. Use when asked for a weekly report.
metadata:
  audience: assistant
---
```

When a session starts, Hive copies the Hive skills into the agent's own launch folder in the project's `.hive` and points Claude Code at it (Claude Code shows them as `hive:<name>`), so a running Claude Code agent keeps the version it started with. Codex only reads skills from the folder it works in (`.agents/skills`), so for Codex agents Hive copies them there as `hive-<name>` folders (and keeps them out of git); those copies aren't listed as local skills. Codex agents sharing a folder share those copies, so after one starts with a changed skill, the others use the new version too. Either way, a restarted session has the current skills. When a workspace skill is edited, added or removed (in the Skills view, in an editor, by an agent or by a git pull), each running agent that started with the older version shows the **Restart session** banner, as an MCP server change does, and the sidebar marks its project.

### Choosing the skills agents get <a id="choosing-the-skills-agents-get"></a>

Every Hive skill for agents is delivered to them, unless you switch it off. **Delivered to agents** is the switch beside each Hive skill: in the **Skills** view it is the workspace's (off there, no project's agents get the skill), and in a project's **Skills** tab the project's own (off there, that project's agents don't; a skill the workspace has off shows off and can't be turned on there). A change reaches each agent's next session: Claude Code's launch folder and the Codex and Copilot copies both follow it, Hive removes its copy of a skill switched off at the agent's next launch (while another of Hive's agents works in the same folder, the copy stays until it stops: it may be reading it), and Hive's guidance stops naming it. The Hive Assistant's own skills aren't chosen here.

**Where Codex and Copilot copies go, and when they're removed.** Codex and Copilot read Hive's skills from copies in the folder an agent works in (its worktree, a slot, or the project folder), and those copies stay after the session, so a CLI you start there yourself would still see them. Hive records each copy it makes, and once the last of Hive's agents in a folder stops, it removes its copies there as **Settings → Advanced → Remove Hive's skill copies when no agent works in the folder** says: **Slots only** (the default) removes them in every folder but the project folder, **Everywhere** in the project folder too, **Never** nowhere (as before). Hive also tidies them as a workspace opens, for folders whose agents have gone. It only removes a copy its record and the copy's own marker both say it made, and that nobody has changed since: never a skill of yours, and never a copy you've edited. Reviewers' sessions run in the project folder, so the review copies they read get none. Claude Code's launch folder leaves nothing in your folders.

## MCP servers <a id="mcp-servers"></a>

MCP servers give agents extra tools. Each server is a JSON file in `Workspace/.hive/mcp`:

```json
{
  "description": "GitHub issues and pull requests",
  "command": "npx",
  "args": ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-github"],
  "env": { "GITHUB_TOKEN": "${GITHUB_TOKEN}" }
}
```

Reference secrets with environment variables (`${NAME}`), never literal values — Hive warns when a definition looks like it contains one. Sessions get the environment Hive was started with, so after setting a variable (for example in Windows' *Edit environment variables for your account*), quit Hive completely, including from the tray, and open it again.

A server can be a program Hive starts (`command`, `args`, `env`, as above) or one reached over HTTP (`"type": "http"`, `"url"` and optional `"headers"`), the same fields as in Claude Code's `.mcp.json`. To keep a server's own code with the workspace, put it in a folder next to its definition, for example `.hive/mcp/notes/server.js`, and refer to it as `${HIVE_MCP_DIR}`, which Hive replaces with the path of the `.hive/mcp` folder: `"args": ["${HIVE_MCP_DIR}/notes/server.js"]`. Keep build output and `node_modules` out of git there.

A server you add or change is used by sessions started afterwards; restart a running session to give it the change.

Hive approves only its own `hive` tools in advance, for every CLI (for the Assistant, only those its Control setting allows; [Hive's changes to the CLIs](#hives-changes-to-the-clis) can turn it off). A server you add is treated like the agent's other tools: whether the agent asks before using one depends on its CLI and its [permission mode](#permission-modes). Codex follows its own approval rules for them. In **Read only** and **Ask for approval** it asks before calling such a tool, except where the server marks the tool as read-only, or you chose **Allow for this session** or **Always allow** when it asked before. In **Approve for me**, its automatic reviewer decides, and asks you only about a call it thinks is risky. In **Full access**, it calls them without asking.

### Adding servers where they're needed <a id="adding-servers-where-theyre-needed"></a>

A server in the workspace is **available** to every project and to the Hive Assistant, and runs only where it is **added**. That way a big server you need in one project doesn't fill every other project's agents' context with its tools. A server you create, or add from a file, starts added nowhere. There are no switches to turn on: a server is either added somewhere or it isn't.

- **In a project:** its **MCP** tab lists only the servers added to that project. Each has **Remove**, which takes it off that project only (the server stays in the workspace). **Add Server…** opens a picker of the workspace's servers not added there yet. Choosing one shows what it runs and anything to know about its definition, and **Add** adds it.
- **In the MCP view:** a server's own view has **Add to…**. It shows what the server runs and ticks the projects it is added to, with **Hive Assistant** last. Tick or untick projects and **Save**: it is added where ticked and removed where unticked. **All projects** and **No projects** tick or untick every project and leave the Assistant as it is. If a project can't be written to partway through, Hive says which places changed and which didn't.
- **Search and filter:** the MCP view and the picker find servers by name, description and tags (a definition's optional `"tags": ["godot", "game"]` list, which Hive doesn't pass to the CLIs). They can also show only the servers **Added somewhere** or **Added nowhere**, and in the MCP view only those **With problems**.

Each server in the MCP view says where it is added: "Added in Ambrosia", "Added in Ambrosia and for the Hive Assistant", "Added for the Hive Assistant only" or "Not added anywhere". Sessions of every provider use **only** the servers added to their project, from their next session: a running agent keeps what it started with until it restarts.

**Deleting a server** asks first, saying where it is added. Deleting removes it from those places too, and a server added later under the same name starts added nowhere. If a project's settings can't be read at that moment, Hive changes nothing and says so (a replaced or deleted server stays as it was): try again once the project can be read.

**Upgrading changes nothing that runs.** A server that ran in a project (on in the workspace and on in that project) is added there, and one that ran for the Assistant is added for it. A choice a project kept for a server the workspace had switched off is dropped, since that server wasn't running there; a notice says how many were dropped, once. Add those servers where you need them. Upgrading from 0.9.0 or earlier still keeps everything that ran: a server that was on for all projects is added to every project that hadn't turned it off.
