If a project has its own `.mcp.json` (or, for Codex, `.codex/config.toml` servers), Hive tells you which of its servers aren't in the workspace and offers to copy them there; until then they stay disabled. **Copy to workspace** shows what each runs, then copies it and adds it to that project straight away. Other projects can add it in their **MCP** tab.

### Servers for the Hive Assistant <a id="servers-for-the-hive-assistant"></a>

The Hive Assistant gets a workspace server the way a project does: only once it is **added for the Assistant**. It starts with none, so a server with personal notes, say, can reach the Assistant and no project's agents.

- **In the Assistant's view** (the Hive Assistant on the activity bar), **MCP Servers** lists the servers added for it, each with a button to remove it. **+** opens the same picker as a project's **Add Server…**.
- **In the MCP view,** **Add to…** ends with **Hive Assistant**.
- **Settings → Assistant → MCP servers (this workspace)** links to the Assistant's view, where they are chosen now.
- **It applies from the Assistant's next session;** a running Assistant shows that it needs a restart.

What is added for the Assistant is separate from every project's: adding a server for it never adds it to a project, and the other way round.

Upgrading: if you had turned on **The Assistant uses the workspace's other MCP servers** in Settings → Advanced, each workspace you open gives the Assistant the servers that were on in it, so nothing it used goes away. That setting is gone; a new workspace's Assistant starts with none.
### Giving a server to some agents only <a id="giving-a-server-to-some-agents-only"></a>

By default every agent in a project gets all the servers added to its project. To keep a server's tools away from agents that don't need it (each server's tools take up room in an agent's context), open the agent's settings (its pane's **⋯** → **Agent Settings…**) and under **MCP servers** choose **Only these**, then untick the servers it shouldn't have. For example, a release-testing agent can have a sandbox server that the builders don't. Choosing **Only these** starts with every server ticked, so nothing changes until you untick one; **All of the project's** puts it back.

- Hive's own `hive` server is always given, whatever you choose.
- An agent with **Only these** doesn't get a server added to the project later: tick it in its settings. One with **All** gets it.
- A running agent takes the change when it restarts.
- Templates keep the choice, so an agent loaded from a template gets the same servers, where they're added.
- The agent's tooltip shows its choice, and once any agent has one, the project's **MCP** tab says under each server which agents get it.

### Adding a server from a file <a id="adding-a-server-from-a-file"></a>

**Add MCP Server from File** (the file icon in the MCP view's header) adds a server someone gave you, as **Add Skill from File** does for skills:

- a **`.json`** definition, in the format above: it becomes `.hive/mcp/<name>.json`;
- a **`.zip`** of a server's folder: its code is unpacked to `.hive/mcp/<name>/` and its definition becomes `.hive/mcp/<name>.json`. The definition is a file named `hive-mcp.json` (or `hive-mcp.example.json`) at the top of the zip, or inside one folder at its top. It refers to the server's code through `${HIVE_MCP_DIR}`, for example `"args": ["${HIVE_MCP_DIR}/notes/server.js"]` for a zip of a `notes` folder holding `hive-mcp.json` and `server.js`. Added under another name, Hive changes those references to the new folder.

Before adding it, Hive shows the name it will have (the file's or the folder's, which you can change) and **what it runs**: the command and its arguments, or the address it connects to, and the names of its environment variables and headers (a `${NAME}` reference shown as itself, never a value). A name already in use asks whether to **Replace** that server (the old one goes to the Recycle Bin) or choose another name. It is added to the workspace **added nowhere**, so nothing runs until you add it to a project (its **MCP** tab's **Add Server…**, or **Add to…** in its view), which shows what it runs again (see [Adding servers where they're needed](#adding-servers-where-theyre-needed)). Replacing a server adds the new one added nowhere too, even where the old one was added. A server's view shows what it runs above its definition, and deleting a server added from a `.zip` also moves its folder to the Recycle Bin.

Nothing in the file runs while it's added. Hive refuses a zip with files that would land outside the server's folder (`..`, a drive or a leading `/`), names Windows can't hold, links, more than 5,000 files or more than 200 MB unpacked, and a definition without a `command` or `url`.

### Installing servers from a catalogue <a id="installing-servers-from-a-catalogue"></a>

The MCP view's **Catalogue** tab lists servers you can install from **catalogues**. A catalogue is a list of servers with a zip for each, published as a `catalog.json`. Hive's own catalogue (Suremise/hive-mcp, with servers such as Windows Sandbox) comes first. You can add others, such as a company's internal servers or a community list, in **Settings → MCP Catalogues**.

- **Each catalogue is its own category.** Search finds servers by name, description and tags. The filter shows **Not installed**, **Installed**, **Updates available** or **Runs on this PC**, and the source picker narrows the list to one catalogue. A long catalogue loads a page at a time (**Show more**).
- **A server's requirements** are checked on this PC: its system, its Windows edition and build, Node.js and Docker. Hive can't check every Windows feature without administrator rights; Windows Sandbox it can. A requirement Hive can't check says so. A server this PC can't run shows **Can't run here**, and its **Install** is greyed out.
- **Install…** downloads the server's zip and checks its size and checksum against the catalogue. Anything that doesn't match is refused and nothing is written. Hive then shows what the server runs, as **Add MCP Server from File** does. Installed, it is in the workspace and added nowhere: use **Add to…** in its view, or **Add Server…** in a project's **MCP** tab, to add it where it should run.
- **Updates are never automatic.** When a catalogue has a newer version of a server you installed from it, the server shows **Update to x.y.z**. **Update…** downloads and checks the new version, says where the server is added (those places keep it), keeps your own values for its environment variables and headers, and shows which lines of what it runs change. If where it is added changes while the dialog is open, Hive writes nothing and shows the dialog again.
- **Hive reads catalogues only when the Catalogue opens or you refresh it** (↻), never in the background. A catalogue Hive can't read shows why in its category.
- **A catalogue you add isn't checked by Hive.** The first time its servers are listed, a note says so. Look at what a server runs before you install it.

In **Settings → MCP Catalogues**, turn each catalogue on or off, put yours in order, remove one (servers installed from it stay), or add one by its `https://` or `file://` address (a `file://` catalogue on a share can point at `file://` zips). Removing a server is **Delete** in the MCP view, as for any other.

### The built-in `hive` server <a id="the-built-in-hive-server"></a>

Every session also gets Hive's own MCP server, which lets agents list projects, read and write shared notes, write handovers, read and update the task board, and notify you. Try: *"Write a handover for the next session using the hive tools."* A new session is told about these tools and about the project's latest handover, so *"read the handover"* is enough to pick up where the last session stopped. See the [Agent API reference](AGENT_API.md).

## Shared notes and handovers <a id="shared-notes-and-handovers"></a>

The **Shared Notes** view edits the markdown files in `.hive/shared`. Use it for team conventions, instructions every agent should follow, and **handovers** — notes a session writes before it ends so the next session (or another project) can continue. Handovers go in `shared/handovers`, named by date and project (`2026-09-29-api-auth-refactor.md`), with the project on a `**Project:**` line at the top. When project names overlap, such as `hive` and `hive-website`, that line decides whose handover it is.

Several agents can update the same note without losing each other's changes. An agent rewriting a note says which version it read, and if someone changed the note meanwhile, its write is refused, so it reads the note again and merges its change in. Notes that many agents add to, such as status logs, get one appended entry each, dated and signed. Saving a note in the Shared Notes view that an agent changed since you opened it asks before overwriting it.

**Long documents by section.** An agent that needs one part of a long plan, design note or spec doesn't have to read all of it: its **hive_doc_outline** tool lists a Markdown file's headings with each section's size, and **hive_read_section** reads just one section (with its subsections), by the heading's text or its slug, the heading in lower case with hyphens (`#core-contracts`; two headings called Setup are `#setup` and `#setup-1`). They work on the project's files (in the agent's worktree when it has one) and on shared notes (`shared:plans/design.md`), for Claude Code, Codex and Copilot agents and the Hive Assistant alike. A heading that isn't there gets the closest ones in reply, never an empty section. An agent reads its own project's documents only.

### Workspace notes <a id="workspace-notes"></a>

**Workspace notes**, pinned first in Shared Notes, is for what is true of this machine: a full path a command needs, the version of a tool you have installed, a folder that lives somewhere unusual. Such notes don't belong in a committed `AGENTS.md`, and `CLAUDE.local.md` doesn't reach every agent: worktrees don't have it, and Codex and Copilot never read it.

Hive adds the note to every agent's instructions when it starts, on every coding agent and in whichever folder it works (its worktree or slot, or the project folder), and to the Hive Assistant's. A section headed with a project's name (`## alpha`) goes only to that project's agents; everything else goes to all of them, and the Assistant gets the whole note. A change reaches each agent at its next launch or new conversation: running agents keep what they started with.

Agents get at most 4 KB of it (Settings → Advanced → **Workspace notes: most an agent gets**). The editor warns as you type when the note is longer for any project, and a longer note is cut, with a line telling agents to read the note for the rest. You and the Hive Assistant edit the note; project agents can read it but not change it. To stop delivering it, empty the note or turn off Settings → Advanced → **Deliver workspace notes to agents**.

## Memory <a id="memory"></a>

A project's **Memory** tab shows what each provider its agents use reads about it, grouped by provider:

- **Claude Code:** `CLAUDE.md` (project instructions read at the start of every session, usually committed), `CLAUDE.local.md` (your personal project instructions, not committed), the **User CLAUDE.md** (for all your projects) and **Auto memory** (facts Claude Code chose to remember about this project).
- **Codex:** `AGENTS.md` (project instructions) and the **User AGENTS.md** (for all your projects).

All of them can be edited here.

**One set of instructions for both.** When a project has Claude Code and Codex agents, each reads its own file. **Share one AGENTS.md…** at the top of the list makes `AGENTS.md` the shared file: `CLAUDE.md` gets an `@AGENTS.md` line, which makes Claude Code read it too. Anything else in `CLAUDE.md` stays and applies to Claude Code only. If there's no `AGENTS.md` yet, what's in `CLAUDE.md` moves into it, so nothing is lost.

## Files <a id="files"></a>

The **Files** tab is a file browser for the project folder.

- **Create:** the New File and New Folder buttons (or right-click a folder) add an entry inside the selected folder; type the name in place. A name like `docs/notes.md` creates the folders in between.
- **Rename** with F2, **delete** with Del. Deleted files go to the **Recycle Bin**, so they can be restored.
- **Cut, copy and paste** with Ctrl+X, Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V, or from the right-click menu, which also has **Duplicate**, **Copy Path** and **Copy Relative Path**. Pasting into another project always copies. A paste, a rename or a new file selects what it made once it's done; if you click another row before then (in a big folder or on a slow disk), your row stays selected, so the next key acts on it.
- **Drag and drop:** drag entries onto a folder to move them (hold Ctrl to copy). Drag files in from Explorer to copy them into the project. Drag files onto the **Session** tab to paste their paths into the running session.
- **Open elsewhere:** the buttons above the editor (and the right-click menu) open a file in its default app or reveal it in File Explorer.
- **Find:** the box at the top searches the whole project by name or path (git-ignored files are left out).
- Ctrl+click and Shift+click select several entries; the arrow keys move around the tree.

Names are coloured by git status like in VS Code (modified, new), folders containing changes get a dot, and git-ignored files and the `.hive` folder are dimmed. `.git` is hidden. The tree updates by itself when files change on disk.

### Viewing and editing files <a id="viewing-and-editing-files"></a>

Select a file and it opens on the right, ready to edit, with syntax highlighting for code, SQL, XML, JSON, YAML and many more. **Ctrl+S** (or **Save**) saves it. Some file types also get a nicer view, and the buttons above the file switch between them:

| File | Views |
|---|---|
| Markdown (`.md`) | **Preview** (default) with coloured code blocks, images and clickable links to other files · **Split** (edit on the left, live preview on the right) · **Edit** |
| CSV / TSV | **Table** (default, first 5,000 rows) · **Edit** |
| HTML | **Edit** (default) · **Preview**, sandboxed so scripts don't run · **Split** |
| SVG | **Image** (default) · **Edit** |
| Images, PDF | Viewer (click an image to toggle actual size) |

When the file's pane is narrow (or the labels are long in your language), these buttons and **Save** show only their icons, with their names in tooltips, and the file's size and age are left out; narrower still, the buttons move to a second row under the file's name. **Save** is always in view.

Files with unsaved changes get a ● in the tree. The edits are kept while you look at other files or tabs, and follow a file you rename or move in the Files tab. Deleting a file with unsaved changes warns you first. When you quit, Hive lists the files with unsaved changes and saves them or discards them, as you choose; reloading the window or switching workspace offers to save them first. Unsaved changes are not kept if Hive crashes, so save often. If a file changes on disk while you have unsaved edits, for example because the agent edited it, Hive tells you and lets you **Reload** it or **Overwrite with mine**. A file without edits simply updates. Binary files, text that isn't UTF-8 (a Windows-1252 file, say, which saving would change) and files over 5 MB aren't opened; use **Open in Default App** for those.

## Attachments <a id="attachments"></a>

You can attach any file to an agent's or the Assistant's prompt: paste a screenshot with **Ctrl+V**, or drag files from Explorer (or from the Files or Attachments tab) onto its terminal. Each file reaches the agent as its path in the prompt:

- A file from **outside the agent's folder** (its project or worktree; for the Assistant, the workspace) is copied into the session's attachments folder, `.hive/attachments`, and the copy's path is pasted. So is every **image**, wherever it comes from, so the Attachments tab keeps what you showed the agent.
- A file **inside the agent's folder** goes as it is, by its path, so the agent works on the real file. So does a folder.
- Files over **20 MB** are asked about first: an agent may not manage a file that large (its CLI can refuse it, or reading it can fill much of its context).
- Hive never runs what you attach: it only copies it and pastes its path.

While you write the prompt, a **chip** for each attachment shows over the top right of the terminal, with the kind of file. The agent's CLI reads some kinds itself and the rest **by path**: the agent opens them with its own tools (a zip it might unpack, a PDF it might convert). Hover a chip for its path and which. **×** dismisses a chip (the path stays in the prompt); sending the prompt clears them.

| | Images | PDFs | Text, logs, code | Other files |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Claude Code | read itself | read itself | read itself | by path |
| Codex | read itself | by path | read itself | by path |
| GitHub Copilot | read itself | by path | read itself | by path |

The **Attachments** tab has two tabs, each with how many files it has: **Pane attachments** and **Card attachments** (see [Card attachments](#card-attachments)). It opens on the one you last chose, or on Pane attachments while the project's cards keep no files. Each tab has its own filters by kind, counting only its own files. **Show Session's Attachments**, in an agent's **⋯** menu, opens Pane attachments at its running session's files. **Pane attachments** shows everything pasted or dropped into the project's sessions, grouped by session (newest first), with archived sessions at the bottom. Each session's heading says how many attachments it has and the days they were added ("71 attachments · Oct 9 – today"), and when a session spans days, a thin line marks where each day starts. Images show as thumbnails, other files by their type's icon (Markdown, JSON, PDF, zip, code, text) and name; every tile shows its size at the bottom left and its time at the bottom right. When there's more than one kind, the filters above (**All**, **Images**, **PDFs**, **Text**, **Other**) show just one kind. Click one to view it large and use ← / → to move between them: images, PDFs, Markdown, CSV tables and text are previewed as in the Files tab, and other files say there's no preview. From the viewer or the right-click menu you can **Insert into Session**, **Copy Path**, **Copy Image** (images), or **Delete** it (to the Recycle Bin). An image or a PDF has **Open in Its App** in the viewer, so Windows' own viewer gives you zoom, printing and the rest; any other kind has **Show in Folder** instead, because Hive never opens an attached file that could run (the right-click menu has **Open** for images and PDFs, and **Reveal in File Explorer**). Click a session's name to read its transcript, or its bin to move all of its attachments to the Recycle Bin at once. A running session's attachments can't be deleted until it stops: **Delete** is greyed out (in the viewer too; hover it to see why). You can also drag one onto the **Session** tab.

This tab was called **Images**, and the folder `.hive/images`, before attachments could be any file. Hive moves the old folder's files into `.hive/attachments` the first time it needs them, and links to the old paths (in a transcript, or printed in a terminal) still open the moved files in Hive. An agent given an old path in an earlier conversation won't find it there any more: give it the file again.

### Card attachments <a id="card-attachments"></a>

A card can keep files too: a screenshot of the bug, a log, a mock-up. They stay with the card, whatever sessions it has had or not had yet, until the card is deleted, or some days after it is archived (see below).

- **In the card dialog**, paste (**Ctrl+V**) or drop files into the description or a comment. Each shows as a chip under the box, with a link in the text where the caret was: an image is shown inline, any other file as a link. They are kept only when you **Save** (the description) or **Post** (the comment); **Cancel** drops them. The card's **Attachments** list, under its description, shows every file it keeps, with its size and who added it, and **Open** (images and PDFs), **Show in Folder** and **Remove** (to the Recycle Bin, after asking; links to it then say it was removed).
- **From an agent's pane**, the pin on an attachment chip copies that file to a card: **Attach to HI-12** for the card the agent works on or reviews, or another of the project's open cards. In the **Attachments** tab, right-click a session's file for **Attach to a card**. The card keeps its own copy, so cleaning up the session can't break it.
- **Agents and the Hive Assistant** attach files with `hive_create_task` and `hive_update_task` (`attachments`: file paths). An agent can attach only files of its own project (its folder and slots) or the temp folder, where its screenshots go; the Assistant can attach files of the workspace, such as one you pasted to it. When an agent starts a card, its prompt lists the card's files by path, and `hive_read_task` lists them all.

Each file is at most **25 MB** (**Settings → Advanced → Card attachments: largest file**), and one change takes at most 10 files and 100 MB together. The same file added to a card twice is kept once. Card files are kept in the project's `.hive/attachments/cards`, named by the card's number, and the **Attachments** tab shows them on its **Card attachments** tab, by card: **Show in Attachments**, beside the card dialog's Attachments heading, opens it at that card's files. Click one to open it in the same viewer as a pane's file, and use ← / → to move through the card files shown (the filter applies). The viewer has **Remove from Card** where a pane's file has Delete, asking first, and a file that is gone from disk says so there. **Clean Up** and **Purge** never remove them; deleting the card sends them to the Recycle Bin, and moving it to another project moves them with it. **Settings → Storage** counts them on a row of their own.

- **Archived cards clean up after themselves.** A card's files go to the Recycle Bin **30 days** after the card is archived. Choose **3**, **7** or **90 days**, or **Never**, in **Project Settings → Storage → Remove archived cards' attachments**, beside the size of what Hive keeps. Hive looks after the workspace opens and once a day, while you're away from the PC (it pauses as soon as you type or move the mouse); **Clean Up Now…** beside the choice lists the cards due now, with their files and sizes, and removes them at once. A file changed since the list was made stays, and the notice says so. Only archived cards lose theirs: open and **Done** cards keep them, and so does a card you bring back from the archive in time, or one archived because its project was hidden or removed. The card then gets a comment from Hive saying how many files went and which, and its links to them show as removed.

## Version Control tab <a id="version-control-tab"></a>

A project's **Version Control** tab (**Alt+8**; before 1.0 it was the **Git** tab) starts with the project's version control, **None** or **Git**, as Hive found it when it first saw the project. Hive never switches it by itself: you switch it by clicking the other one, and Hive offers a switch when it finds another version control in the folder (see [Switching version control](#switching-version-control)). Beside it, a line says what Hive finds in the folder now: *This folder is a Git repository at …*; a folder inside a larger repository, whose branch and merge actions affect that whole repository; a folder with no version control; one kept in Mercurial or Jujutsu, not supported yet, so the project uses None; or a Git repository that is gone. **Workflow: Git · Team** names the project's preset (**Custom (from Team)** once an option differs from it, **no preset chosen** when there is none): click it to see the options.

Its sections start with **Workflow**, the project's workflow options (see [Workflow options](#workflow-options)). A Git project then has **Changes**, where it opens, **History**, **Branches & remotes** and **Merge queue**. In a narrow tab the sections show their icons, the one you're on keeping its name: point at an icon to see its name. A project without version control (**None**) has only **Workflow**, where it opens. While its folder has no repository of its own, you can still choose **Git**: its page offers only **Initialise repository…** (see [Initialise repository](#initialise-repository)), and choosing it saves nothing: leave the tab, or choose None, and the project is None as before. If it used Git before and a merge queue from those options still has something going on (a card testing, queued or paused, or a return you haven't seen), its **Merge queue** section stays, with **Pause queue**, **Resume queue** and **Remove**, until that's clear. The tab remembers each project's section while Hive runs.

The status bar shows the selected project's git in one item, such as **main · origin · ↑2 ↓0**: its branch, the remote its branch tracks, and how many commits are to push (↑) and to pull (↓). Click it for **Branches & remotes**. It says **no upstream** for a branch that tracks no remote branch yet, and **detached at abc1234** or **rebasing** when git is in that state.

Everything here is yours: agents have no tool for any of it, and only the project folder changes here. Agents' worktrees are shown, read-only: their work comes into the project folder through **Merge**.

### How do you work? <a id="how-do-you-work"></a>

A project added since 1.0 starts with one step, at the top of its Version Control tab: **How do you work?** It offers **Solo**, **Pair** and **Team** (for Git, Pair has Hive merge the work), with the one that fits the project's agents marked **Suggested**: Solo for one agent or none, Pair for a builder and a reviewer (an agent whose name says *review*), Team for more. It follows the agents as you add them. Under the presets, the one you picked shows what it sets and why.

- **Choose** saves it, after one question (in a new project nothing is in flight, so the options apply from its first card).
- **Customise…** opens the **Workflow** section with that preset filled in, for you to change and save.
- **Not Now** applies nothing: the project's options stay those of the workspace and of Hive (Settings → Version Control), else every rule off, and the header says **no preset chosen**. You can choose a preset in **Workflow** at any time.

Nothing is applied before you choose. **New Project** opens the new project's tab at this step. A project Hive finds in the workspace folder, or one the Assistant adds, gets a notice instead (*alpha was added: how do you work?*), with **Choose…** and **Not Now**. Each version control asks once: a project you switch to one it never had asks again for that one, and your answer for each comes back when you switch back. Projects from before 1.0 don't ask: they kept how they worked, with the one-time presets notice.

### Switching version control <a id="switching-version-control"></a>

Click the version control the project doesn't use, **None** or **Git**, atop the tab to switch it. A dialog says first what changes:

- the switch applies **from the project's next card**: cards already started finish under the rules they started with (*old rules*), and a running lane keeps its rules until it ends. They are listed. Unlike a change of options, a switch can't be applied to cards in flight: a Git card can't finish under None's rules. A Git card in the merge queue carries on there after a switch to None, and the **Merge queue** section and the Progress panel's queue card stay until the queue is clear;
- each version control keeps **its own workflow options**: switching back brings back the preset, the options and your answer to *How do you work?* the project had for it, and one it never had starts with none of its own (it inherits the workspace's and Hive's defaults);
- **None** can always be chosen, also for a folder that is a Git repository: Hive then records no commits or merges for its cards, and the dialog says so. **Git** switches the project where Hive finds a Git repository and no other version control. Where the folder has no repository of its own, Git shows its page with **Initialise repository…**, and the project switches to Git once that's done. Where another version control is found, Git is greyed out, and hovering it says why (*Mercurial found: not supported yet*).

If the version control changed while the dialog was open (switched in another window, or by the Assistant), the switch is refused: look again. The Assistant can switch it too, within Settings → Assistant → Control → Change settings (`project.workflow.platform`), with the same checks; its reply names the lanes and cards that keep the old rules.

**When the folder changes**, Hive notices on its next look at the project (opening it, a refresh, switching back to Hive) and offers the switch in the tab, under the header, with a notice once while Hive runs (**Open Version Control** shows the tab):

- **Git found** (someone ran `git init`, or you used **Initialise repository…**): *Git found at …: switch to Git?* with **Switch…** and **Not Now**.
- **The Git repository is gone** (`.git` removed or moved): Git's rules hold until you switch, because what they check can't be read: a card's start or submit is refused, saying so, with the switch to None as the next step. **Switch to None…**, or put the repository back. The tab notices as soon as you come back to Hive's window, even while it's open on the project.
- **Mercurial or Jujutsu found** in a Git project (a colocated `.jj` beside `.git` counts as Jujutsu): not supported yet, so **Switch to None…** is offered and Git can't be chosen.

While an offer shows, it says what Hive found; **Not Now** hides it until Hive starts again, and the line beside the choice says it instead. A project has one notice at a time: a later change replaces the one before.

**A project inside a larger repository** (its folder is a subfolder of a Git repository) is Git, and the tab says that branch and merge actions affect that whole repository. So when Hive or the agent merges, the project must name the branch its work merges into itself (**Target branch** in Workflow): options that would merge into the whole repository's default branch are refused when you save them, saying so. If a card's options name none anyway (from before), the merge queue pauses at it, saying how to go on.

### Changes <a id="changes"></a>

**Changes** lists files changed in the project's git working tree and shows each one as a side-by-side diff against the last commit — a quick way to review what the agent did.

Its picker chooses whose changes you see, in this order: **Branches**, two branches compared (see [Comparing two branches](#comparing-two-branches)), **Unused worktrees** (worktrees no agent works in now, and each of them), the **worktrees in use** by agents (the folder each one works in: a slot it builds a card in, *B1 · in slot-b · its card's branch*, the slot kept for it, *HB2 · carried: hb2 · hive/hb2*, or its worktree from before 1.0; a slot's page shows what its branch has changed since it left the branch it merges into, with **Merge** for that branch), and the **project folder** last (where agents without a worktree, and you, make changes). A project whose work merges into a branch other than main (its **Target branch** in Workflow, such as 1.0.0) opens on that branch compared with main; otherwise, a project with worktrees opens on Unused worktrees, and one without on its folder. A sentence at the top of each page says what it shows. Listing unused worktrees asks git about each of them, which can take a few seconds: the first time, the page shows a spinner while Hive looks; after that, the last list shows at once while Hive checks it again. **Refresh** (↻) looks again.

Hive checks your projects' git status in the background (this tab, branch status, the Files tab's dimmed entries) without writing to the repository's index, so your own `git add` or `git commit`, or an agent's, never fails with *"index.lock: File exists"* because of it. Hive's own git actions, such as **Merge**, still update it as git does. If git is busy when the Files tab lists a folder, ignored entries keep their dimming and the folder is listed again a moment later.

#### Comparing two branches <a id="comparing-two-branches"></a>

The picker's **Branches** group compares any two local branches, whether or not a folder has them checked out: first your **Target branch** against main (*1.0.0 ↔ main*), then each local branch against the target (the target itself against main). Its **Slots** group lists every slot that has a branch checked out, with the card it's lent to, or *free* (*slot-c · HI-1758 · hive/HI/HI-1758-…*), a slot whose card is blocked included; each opens its branch against the branch it merges into. A branch is listed once: under Slots when a slot has it. On the page, **Branch** and **Against** choose the two, and ⇄ swaps them.

It shows what the branch changes since it left the other: how many commits each has that the other hasn't, and where they parted. **Files** lists only the files the branch changes since then (swap the two to see the other side's); there are 200 at a time, and **Show more** lists the next. Click one to see its diff, side by side or inline, as it was where the branches parted against the branch's version. The diff is of the commits the page counted, so if a branch moves meanwhile, it still matches what's listed until you refresh (↻). If a refresh or another page can't be read (a branch deleted, say), the last result stays with *Could not refresh the comparison* and **Retry**. **Commits** lists each side's commits, 50 a page. It only reads git: nothing is checked out, no worktree is made and nothing in the repository changes, and a file's diff is read only when you open it, so even a comparison of thousands of commits stays quick.

#### Leftover branches <a id="leftover-branches"></a>

Branches outlive their work: a card that was superseded or archived keeps its branch, and so do agents' branches from before slots. **Leftover branches (N)**, at the end of the picker's Branches group, lists every local branch nothing uses now: not your Target branch or the default branch, not checked out in a worktree or slot, and no open card's. They're in two groups. **Merged into** your target lose nothing when deleted. **Not in** your target say how many commits only they have. Each row shows its card, when the board knows one. **Compare** opens the branch against the target (see [Comparing two branches](#comparing-two-branches)) so you can look first, and **Delete…** deletes it as Branches & remotes does: one confirmation for a merged branch, or for one with commits of its own, a confirmation that lists those commits. Hive checks again as it deletes: a branch checked out somewhere, taken by an open card or moved since you were shown it is kept, and the dialog says why. A card that is open again keeps every branch it has worked on, even after starting again somewhere else. Only the branch goes, never a worktree or a remote's copy. ↻ reads the branches again after a change made outside Hive.

### History <a id="history"></a>

**History** lists a branch's commits, newest first, 20 a page (the arrows under them go back through older ones), with their branches and tags. It opens on the branch your project's work merges into (its **Target branch** in Workflow, such as 1.0.0), or, with none set, the branch the project folder is on; the branch named in its heading is a picker for any other, yours or a remote's. Below the commits come the repository's tags. **Create tag…** tags the commit the folder has checked out: give it a name, and a message for an annotated tag, and choose a remote to push it to if you like.

### Branches & remotes <a id="branches--remotes"></a>

- **Branch**: the branch the project folder is on (or its detached HEAD, and a merge or rebase git has in progress, which you finish in a terminal), the branch it tracks, and ↑ / ↓. These counts are as of the last fetch, whose time is shown: **Hive never fetches by itself**. **Fetch** fetches every remote. **Pull** fetches the branch it tracks and brings yours up to it when that needs no merge; when both have new commits, it says so and changes nothing (merge or rebase in a terminal, or ask an agent). **Push** pushes to the branch it tracks, or, for a branch that tracks none, to the remote you choose, which it then tracks. A push is never forced: when the remote has commits yours hasn't, it says to pull first. **Push to…** pushes to any remote, a backup on your NAS say, without changing what your branch tracks (**Push** still goes where it went): **This branch**, or **All branches and tags**. Push to… pushes the branch it names, even if you switch branch in a terminal before you click. With all of them, it lists each branch by what happened to it (pushed, already up to date, refused) and the tags it pushed; a branch the remote has newer commits on is refused, and the rest still go. **New branch…** makes a branch at the folder's commit and switches to it.
- **Branches**: each local branch, with what it tracks and ↑ / ↓, its last commit, and **Switch**. **Track…** (🔗) chooses the remote branch it tracks, which **Pull** and **Push** then use: a remote, and the branch's name there (its own name by default). If that remote has no such branch yet (as of the last fetch), the form says so, and **Track** pushes yours there first, never forced. **Stop tracking** (beside it) leaves the branch tracking nothing; nothing changes on the remote. Uncommitted changes come along when they don't clash with the other branch; when they do, git refuses and nothing changes. A branch checked out in an agent's worktree can't be switched to (a branch is in one folder at a time). A branch checked out in a slot shows the slot's name; point at it for whose it is (*slot-a: HB1 on AL-12*). **Merge…** (⑂) opens [Merging outside cards](#merging-outside-cards) on the branch. **A branch next to its card:** a branch a card works on shows that card's key (click it to open the card) and its stage, in its category's colour and by your name for it if you renamed it. In Review it adds *under review: a3f2c1d*, the revision the review is on (the button beside it copies it); once Done, *merged* and the commit that merged it. An agent's branch carries one card after another: it shows the card being worked, else the latest, and how many earlier cards worked on it (hover for which). A no-code card has no branch, and a branch no card works on shows as before. **Remote branches** lists those you have no local branch for: **Switch** makes one that tracks it.
- **Update from the target**: a branch that lacks some of the target branch's commits (*2 commits of 1.0.0 it hasn't*) has an **Update** button (⇣) when it has work of its own, or when it has none but is in use (a card's line, a slot's or a worktree's branch). Its dialog says what will happen: where it runs (the slot or folder the branch is checked out in, or, when it isn't checked out anywhere, a temporary folder Hive removes after), whose card it is, which agents work there (their next turn starts from the updated branch), and whether a card that passed review keeps its pass. Choose **Merge** (the default: one merge commit) or **Rebase** (its commits replayed on top of the target, which rewrites them; a copy you pushed then differs, and Hive never forces a push). A branch with nothing of its own is simply moved forward to the target: **Fast-forward** is its only choice. It waits while an agent there is in a turn, the folder has uncommitted changes, or the card is in the merge queue, which brings it up to date itself. If git stops at a conflict, Hive undoes it at once and names the files: the branch is left as it was, for you or its builder to update by hand. If Hive can't undo it (another git program holding the repository, say), it says so instead: which folder is still in the middle of the merge or rebase, and to finish or abort it there yourself (`git status` says how). In a temporary folder, that folder is then kept as git left it, the branch itself unchanged, and the message names it: look at it, then remove it in **Unused worktrees**. A temporary folder Hive couldn't remove after an update is named the same way.
- **A branch's row** keeps its time and its buttons (Track…, Update, Merge…, Delete, Switch) whole and in the same place on every row, however much the row says: when it's short of room, what it says about the branch (its worktree, card, where it stands, its last commit) is cut with **…**, the last commit first, then the branch's name; hover it for the whole of it.
- **Branches by state, and cleaning up**: measured against the branch the project's work merges into (its **Target branch** in Workflow, else the default branch), the branches show in three groups. **In use**: that branch, the default branch, the project folder's, and those a slot, a worktree or an open card uses. **Merged into** it: every commit of theirs is in it and nothing uses them. **Not merged into** it, each saying how many of its commits aren't in it. **Remote branches** are grouped the same way. **Clean Up Merged Branches…** lists the merged branches nothing uses, each ticked, to delete the ones you leave ticked; it never offers the target, the default branch, the project folder's, a slot's or worktree's, or an open card's. Below them, apart and under a warning, the merged branches on your remotes: ticking one deletes it on the remote for everyone, which Hive can't undo, so none is ticked for you (one whose local branch you ticked says so). Unmerged branches are never offered together: the bin (🗑) beside a branch nothing uses deletes it alone, and for an unmerged one it first lists the commits that would go. Each branch is deleted only at the commit you were shown: one that moved meanwhile is kept, saying so. A card's own branch is deleted by Hive once the card is Done and its slot has moved on (**Settings → Advanced → Delete a card's branch once it's merged**); until then its slot has it, so it's in use.
- **Switching branch or pulling waits for your agents.** It moves the ground under an agent working in the project folder and under a merge into its branch, so while an agent there is working, asking you something or waiting on its background tasks, or the project's merge slot is held or waited for, **Switch**, **New branch…**'s switch and **Pull** are greyed out, and hovering one says why ("B1 is working in the project folder: switch branches once it has finished"). An agent whose session is idle in the folder finds the new branch on its next turn: the confirmation says so. While git switches or pulls, Hive holds what it would send agents in that folder itself (a message, a wake, a card's task) and doesn't start or resume an agent there; they go on as soon as git is done. What you type into an agent's terminal yourself isn't held, so don't type there while a switch or pull runs.
- **Remotes**: each with its URL (a password or token in a URL shows as •••, in git's error messages too; to change such a URL, type it whole), **Add remote…** and ✎ to change a URL. **Rename** (ab) renames a remote; the branches tracking it follow the new name, and a name another remote has is refused. **Remove** (🗑) asks first, naming the branches that will stop tracking it: Hive forgets the remote and its remote branches here, and those branches track nothing. Nothing on the remote itself changes, and you can add it again.
- **Worktrees**: the repository's other worktrees, whose agent works in each, the card that agent is on (shown as on a branch, whatever the worktree has checked out), and **Changes** to review it. A slot is named with its lease: *slot-a: HB1 on AL-12*, *slot-b: HB2, lane P7 C*, *slot-c: carried for HB3* (an agent's old worktree) or *slot-d: not lent*. Read-only. Hive's own worktrees are listed apart, with nothing to remove: **Review copies (Hive's)**, each saying who uses it (*copy-1: HR6 reviewing HI-12 at a3f2c1d*) or that it is idle in the pool, and **Merge queue (Hive's)**, the folder where the merge queue tests merges.
- **Commit identity**: the name and email commits are made with, agents' too: this repository's own setting, your global one, and the one git uses. It warns when commits would fail (no name or email), and when a repository with a GitHub remote commits with a personal address, which anyone can read on GitHub; GitHub's no-reply address (*…@users.noreply.github.com*, in GitHub's email settings) keeps it private. **Set for this repository…** sets this repository's own (`git config --local`); leave both empty to use your global one.

### Merge queue <a id="merge-queue"></a>

**Merge queue** shows each of the project's merge queues, a part per target branch: the queue now, as its card in the Progress panel shows it (what it tests, what waits, a card it returned, a pause, with **Pause queue** and **Resume queue**), and its **history**, the latest 50 outcomes, a line each in a table like the archived cards' (sort by a heading, filter under it, click a row to open the card): the card and its title, its revision, **Merged**, **Returned** or **Taken out** (point at it for the target's new commit or why), the merge commit (in a narrow window, only in that tooltip), and when (point at it for the exact time). Click a revision to copy its whole commit id. A queue shows here while it has anything going on, including after you switch the project to **The agent merges** while cards still go through it, and once it has a history. The Progress panel's **Queue in Version Control** opens this section.

A project inside another repository (say, a workspace you keep in git) shows that repository here, read-only: Hive changes only a project's own repository.

Fetch, pull, push and Push to… use your git's own sign-in (Git Credential Manager may open its window); they never wait on a prompt in a terminal you can't see, and give up after five minutes.

### Without git <a id="without-git"></a>

A project that isn't a git repository keeps working in its project folder, without worktrees or merging: its **Version Control** tab says so; choose **Git** there for **Initialise repository…** to make it one. Without git itself, the tab says so, with the fix (see [Git](#git)).

Git isn't required: each project has a **version control**, found when Hive first sees it. A Git repository (the project's own, or one in a folder above) is **Git**; any other folder, or one kept in Mercurial or Jujutsu (not supported yet), works **without version control**. Hive never switches it by itself: when a project's folder changes (you run `git init` in it, or its repository goes), a notice says so, the project keeps the version control it had, and its Version Control tab offers the switch (see [Switching version control](#switching-version-control)).

### Initialise repository <a id="initialise-repository"></a>

**Initialise repository…** makes a project without git a repository in one click, ready for Git's workflow (merging, and each card in a slot). Choose **Git** in the project's Version Control tab: until the folder is a repository, Git's page has only this button (*This folder isn't a Git repository yet*). Once the repository is made with its first commit, the project switches to Git, with its sections, and Hive offers its presets (*how do you work?*) as for any Git project; if lanes or cards in flight keep None's rules, Hive asks first, as any switch does. If it fails, Git's page says why and nothing changes. A folder inside another repository offers that one (**Use that repository…**, which asks first) or **Initialise its own repository…**. Its dialog:

- offers a **starter .gitignore**: none, **Minimal** (OS files and logs), or one for **Node**, **Python**, **.NET** or **Godot** projects, each with the minimal lines too. If the project already has a .gitignore, it is kept and none is offered;
- asks for **your name and email** when git has none (no global identity, counting files your git config includes), and saves them to this repository's config only, never your global one. Otherwise it says who the commits are made as. If your git config has conditional includes (`includeIf`), which may set an identity for the new repository, the fields are optional: what you enter is used only where they set none. If the new repository turns out to have no identity and you entered none, Hive makes the repository and stops before the first commit: the dialog stays open as **Make the first commit**, asking for what's missing. On GitHub, its no-reply address keeps your email private.

**Initialise** then runs `git init` on a branch called **main**, adds the project's .hive to the repository's `.git/info/exclude` before anything is staged (so it is never committed: see [What Hive keeps in a project](#what-hive-keeps-in-a-project)), writes the .gitignore you chose, and commits everything in the folder as **Initial commit** (an empty commit if there's nothing yet). The tab then offers to switch the project to Git (*Git found at …: switch to Git?*): accept it with **Switch…** (see [Switching version control](#switching-version-control)), and the tab shows Git's sections, Changes, History, Branches & remotes and Merge queue, its cards using Git's workflow from the next one. Add a remote under **Branches & remotes** to push it. There is no undo: if a step fails, the message says what was done and where it stopped (say, a .gitignore rule that brings .hive back stops it before anything is staged, with nothing committed).

- A project **inside another repository** (a workspace you keep in git) shows that repository's **Branches & remotes** read-only, with **Initialise its own repository…**: a repository inside another is usually a mistake, so the dialog names the repository around it and asks you to confirm.
- A repository with **no commits yet** (one that stopped short, or one made with `git init` by hand) says so on **Branches & remotes**, with **Make first commit…**: the same dialog, which keeps .hive out of git and makes the first commit on the branch it has.
- A folder another version control system keeps (Mercurial, Subversion, Jujutsu, Bazaar, Fossil), in it or in a folder above, isn't initialised, even a Jujutsu repository that has git beside it: the dialog says which.

## Project settings <a id="project-settings"></a>

Each project can override the global defaults in its **Settings** tab, which has the same categories and search as Settings. In a narrow window (or with the side panels open), Settings and Project Settings make room: a setting's control moves under its description, the list of categories narrows, and when even that doesn't fit, the categories become a row across the top that scrolls sideways.

How its agents launch isn't here: each agent chooses a connection (see [Accounts and connections](#connections-preview)).

| Category | Setting | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| General | Project key | The letters its cards are named by (see [Project keys](#project-keys)) |
| Sessions | Suggest compacting above | When an agent's context turns orange in its footer (see [Sessions](#sessions)) |
| | Warn when a transcript is over | When a conversation's transcript size turns amber |
| | Completion chime | On, off or inherit |
| Agents | Agents | See [Several agents in one project](#several-agents-in-one-project). The setup script and copied files are in the project's Version Control tab, under **Slots and copies**; file locks and card loops are workflow options there (see [Workflow options](#workflow-options)) |
| Keyboard Shortcuts | | Shortcuts for project and session commands in this project (see [Keyboard shortcuts](#keyboard-shortcuts)) |

**Number settings**, here and in Settings, save when you press Enter or click away. An empty box inherits the global value here; in Settings it just puts the saved value back. A number out of range, or text that isn't a number, isn't saved: the range shows under the box. Settings that can be turned off have a **Never** (or **No pause**) checkbox beside the number; untick it to get your last number back.

Changes apply to new sessions.

### Permission modes <a id="permission-modes"></a>

| Mode | Behaviour |
|---|---|
| Manual | Asks before edits and commands |
| Accept edits | Edits files without asking; asks before commands |
| Plan | Read-only planning until you approve |
| Auto | A safety classifier approves low-risk actions and asks about risky ones (default) |
| Don't ask | Never asks; anything not pre-approved is refused |
| Bypass permissions | Never asks; allows everything |

**Bypass permissions** only appears after you tick **Settings → Claude Code → Enable the Bypass permissions option**. Connections using it show a red warning. Use it only for disposable work.

Codex has its own presets:

| Preset | Behaviour |
|---|---|
| Read only | Reads files; asks before any edit or internet access |
| Ask for approval | Edits files and runs commands in its sandbox; asks before internet access or edits outside the project |
| Approve for me | An automatic reviewer approves safe actions and asks only about risky ones (default) |
| Full access | No sandbox and no approvals |

When you switch a running Codex agent's preset, the badge says **Switching to …** until Codex confirms it; if Codex doesn't, the badge goes back and Hive tells you. If Codex is busy at that moment, Hive waits until it's free; if Codex kept `/permissions` in its input instead of running it, Hive presses Enter again (the same goes for **Set up**, below). If Codex stays busy for 30 seconds, asks you something meanwhile, or its session stops, Hive types nothing more and tells you the preset wasn't switched. Hive chooses the preset by the name Codex shows in its `/permissions` menu, whatever order a Codex version lists them in. If a Codex version doesn't show that name (renamed or dropped), Hive closes the menu without choosing another preset and says it couldn't find it: switch in the agent's terminal with `/permissions` instead, and update Hive when a new version is out.

In **Approve for me**, Codex's reviewer decides on risky actions itself: while it does, the agent stays **Working…** with a small shield beside its status (hover it to see what's being checked), and Hive doesn't ask for you. Hive tells you an agent needs you only when Codex actually puts a question or an approval to you, and once for each, even when a new session is slow to start on a busy computer.

**The CLI's own setting.** Every CLI's modes end with **The CLI's own setting**, for an agent, a project or a CLI's default like any mode: Hive then passes no permission mode at all, and the CLI starts in the one its own config sets (Claude Code's `defaultMode`, Codex's `sandbox_mode` and `approval_policy`, Copilot's own settings), or its default. Hive doesn't know which mode that is, so the agent's badge says **CLI's own** (the CLI's own screen shows the mode), and Hive switches no mode in such a session while it runs: choosing a mode from its menu restarts the session in that mode, the conversation continuing. A mode your CLI config sets is yours to choose, so Hive doesn't ask first for one without guardrails, as it does for **Bypass permissions** or **Full access**. The Hive Assistant isn't offered it: what it may do (**Control**) relies on knowing the mode it runs in.

### Codex's Windows sandbox <a id="codexs-windows-sandbox"></a>

On Windows, Codex runs commands in a sandbox, which it sets up once. **Help → Provider Setup… → Codex → Set up** opens Codex and, once it has finished loading and is idle (on a busy computer this can take a little while), brings up its setup, which offers two choices. With either, Codex agents in **Ask for approval** or **Approve for me** edit files in their own folder (the project, or the agent's worktree) and run commands there without asking, and ask before going online or writing anywhere else. Hive's own features (status, file locks, handovers, shared notes) work the same with both.

- **Set up default sandbox** (recommended): commands run under two local Windows accounts that Codex creates for them (`CodexSandboxOffline` and `CodexSandboxOnline`), with a firewall rule that keeps them offline unless you allow it. It isolates commands best. Windows asks for Administrator permission once.
- **Use non-admin sandbox**: needs no Administrator permission. Commands run under your own account with restricted rights. It protects your files and blocks internet access in most cases, but Codex warns it carries more risk if the agent is tricked by instructions hidden in a file or web page it reads (prompt injection).

If **Set up** can't find what it needs in Codex (a Codex version that names its `/permissions` presets differently), Hive shows a warning saying what it couldn't find and leaves Codex's terminal open: choose **Ask for approval** in its `/permissions` menu yourself to bring up the sandbox setup, or update Hive.

**A stray `.codex` folder.** Codex treats a `.codex` folder in a project as its own (for the project's Codex config). If an agent leaves scratch files or logs in one, Codex's sandbox can fail to protect it, and then every Codex agent's commands in that project fail with *"setup refresh had errors"*. Hive warns when a `.codex` folder in a folder an agent works in holds something that isn't Codex's configuration (its `config.toml`, custom agents in `agents`, `rules`, and files they name): move those files out, and leave Codex's configuration where it is.

Once Codex has set the sandbox up, Hive closes it and Provider Setup says so. With the non-admin sandbox, Provider Setup shows **Upgrade** to switch to the default one later. Codex agents that are running keep their sandbox until they restart. **Full access** doesn't use the sandbox at all.

**PowerShell 7 from the Microsoft Store.** Codex's sandbox can't start the Store's PowerShell 7 while another copy of it is running, in a terminal of yours, another agent's command or one of Hive's terminals. A Codex agent's commands then fail at once with *"CreateProcessAsUserW failed: 5"*, and work again once every PowerShell 7 is closed, so it can look random. When the Store's PowerShell 7 is the one Codex would use, Provider Setup warns about it (*PowerShell 7 from the Microsoft Store is installed*, with the explanation under it). To avoid it, replace it with the installer's PowerShell 7, in this order: first uninstall the Store one (Settings → Apps → Installed apps → PowerShell → Uninstall, or `Get-AppxPackage Microsoft.PowerShell | Remove-AppxPackage`), since winget takes it for the same app and installs nothing while it is there; then install PowerShell 7 (`winget install --id Microsoft.PowerShell --source winget --installer-type wix`); then restart Hive and its Codex agents, which keep their old PATH until they restart. Hive doesn't change your shell itself.

**Full access** is like Bypass: it only appears after you tick **Settings → Codex → Enable the Full access option**, and connections using it show a red warning. Codex's **Plan** mode is separate from the preset: choose **Plan** in the mode menu (or press Shift+Tab in the terminal), and the badge shows "· Plan". In Codex's sandbox the `.git` folder is read-only, so Codex asks before committing.

GitHub Copilot's modes:

| Mode | Behaviour |
|---|---|
| Accept edits | Edits files in the agent's folder without asking (not where its path has parentheses: there it asks, as in Ask); asks before other commands, other MCP tools, web access and changes outside its folder (default) |
| Ask | Reads files and runs harmless commands without asking; asks before edits, other commands, other MCP tools and web access |
| Plan | Plans the work and asks before changing anything |
| Allow all | Approves every tool, path and web request without asking, in its folder and outside it |
| Autopilot | Allow all, and carries on by itself until the task is done without handing back to you; once a turn has run in it, the session approves everything even after you switch out of it |

**Autopilot** only appears after you tick **Settings → GitHub Copilot → Enable the Autopilot option**, and connections using it show a red warning. **Allow all** needs no opt-in: it is Copilot's mode for working with few interruptions, and its description says what it approves. In every mode, Copilot agents use Hive's own `hive` tools without asking.

**Switching mode while an agent runs.** The mode badge in each agent's footer shows the mode its session is really in. Click it, or press **Ctrl+Alt+M**, and choose another: Hive switches it straight away, without a restart, the same way pressing **Shift+Tab** in the terminal does (which Hive also notices). Don't ask and Bypass permissions can't be reached that way, so choosing them restarts the session in that mode and carries on the same conversation; the menu says *Restarts the session* under the modes that will. For Codex, Hive picks the preset from Codex's `/permissions` menu, Full access included: after Hive's confirmation, Codex shows its own warning in the terminal (*Enable full access?*); answer it there, and the session switches without a restart (choose Cancel and it stays as it was). The switch applies to that session only; the settings decide what new sessions start in. When you change the setting while agents are running, Hive offers to **Switch Now**: the agents switch side by side, so the others don't wait while one waits for your answer to Codex's warning, and Hive lists which switched once they all have. This works with `NO_COLOR` set too (Claude Code then shows its footer without colours): Hive reads the mode as it appears on the terminal's screen and leaves your `NO_COLOR` preference as it is.

**A stopped agent's mode** is its connection's: every agent on a connection starts in its mode. Click a stopped agent's badge (or press **Ctrl+Alt+M**) and the menu lists the connections on its account, each with its mode, the one it uses ticked: choose another to move the agent onto it (Hive asks first for a mode without guardrails). The last item, **Edit "*connection*"…**, opens that connection's dialog, where a change of mode applies to every agent on it. An agent whose CLI has no account here yet has no connection to show: choose one in Agent Settings.

## Agent API <a id="agent-api"></a>

Hive has a local API that agents, scripts and other apps use to talk to it: it listens on your computer only (`127.0.0.1`), and every call needs a token. **Settings → Agent API** turns it on or off, sets its port and shows its access token, which can do anything you can. Agents get tokens of their own automatically. See the [Agent API reference](AGENT_API.md). If its port is already in use when Hive starts, the API stays off and the status bar's **API** says so; after Hive closed unexpectedly, Hive tries the port again for up to 5 seconds first, so that a copy of Hive that has only just ended doesn't leave you without it.

### Viewer tokens <a id="viewer-tokens"></a>

An app that only shows what Hive is doing, such as **Apiary** (the office view), should get a **viewer token** rather than the access token: it can read everything in your workspaces (projects, agents, the board, lanes, merge slots, the Progress panel) and follow what happens as it happens, but it can't change anything. Every attempt to change something is refused.

To make one, open **Settings → Agent API → Viewer tokens**, type a name for the app (say *Apiary*) and click **Make viewer token**. The new token shows in its row: copy it and paste it into the app once. **Revoke** cuts that app off at once (it is refused from its next call, and stops receiving events), without touching anything else that uses the API. Hive keeps viewer tokens in its own folder (`%APPDATA%\Hive\agent-api-viewers.json`), never in a project.

The app finds a running Hive by itself: while the Agent API is on, Hive keeps its address (never a token) in `%APPDATA%\Hive\agent-api-discovery.json`, and removes it when the API is turned off or Hive quits.

### The developer console <a id="the-developer-console"></a>

The developer console shows what each coding agent's CLI sends Hive (its hooks, status line, transcript records, the terminal signs Hive reacts to, its launch and exit) next to what Hive made of each: the status it set, the details it read. It also shows Hive's own work in the workspace: Agent API calls, the merge slot, watches waking agents and Progress runs. It's for power users and for developing Hive, so it is hidden until you turn on **Settings → Advanced → Show developer features**. Then open it from **Help → Developer Console** or with **Ctrl+Alt+J**.

- It opens in a **window of its own**, like a browser's developer tools, so you can put it on a second screen. Hive remembers its size and position, and opened on a smaller display it fits that display. Each Hive window has its own console, on that window's workspace. It closes with that window, or when the window opens another workspace or closes this one; open it again for the new workspace.
- **Pick a source** at the top: **Hive** or the **Hive Assistant**, or one of a project's agents. Hive starts capturing that source when you pick it and stops when you pick another or close the console. An agent you pick before it starts shows its launch too.
- Each row is one event: its time, its type, its name and a one-line summary. **Click a row** to see the event as it came in (**Raw**) beside **What Hive made of it**. Large payloads start folded: click a node to open it.
- **Filter** by event type (click a type to hide or show it) or by provider, and **search** (Ctrl+F) the events' names, summaries and payloads. **Pause** stops the list moving while you read (Hive goes on capturing, and the indicator counts what came); **Resume** fills it in. **Clear** empties the list, including anything that came while paused.
- What it shows is **redacted**: no prompt or reply text, commands, tokens or sign-in details. Hive keeps the newest 500 events of each source in memory only, and nothing once the console closes.
- **To see the text** while you debug, turn on **Settings → Advanced → Developer console: show prompt, reply and command text** (off by default). While it's on, the console shows the prompts, replies, commands and tools' arguments of what comes from then on, under an **Unredacted** banner (what came before stays redacted), and **Record** starts with its text box ticked. **Tokens, API keys and sign-in details stay masked whatever it says**, in the text too, wherever it is in an event: a key or token, an option such as `--api-key` or `--password` and its value, a password written out, cookies, and a sign-in or device code (`ABCD-EFGH`, or one in a sign-in link). Hive errs towards masking: after a credential's name (`--token`, `token=`, `password:`, `Authorization:`, a JSON key such as `"password":`) it hides everything to the end of that text, the options after it too, and now and then something that only looks like a code is masked too. It changes only what you see in the console: agents reading it with `hive_console` still get it redacted, and a recording with text is still never attached to Copy Diagnostics. Turn it off and the banner goes, and the console shows its events redacted again. Copy Diagnostics lists it among the Advanced settings you changed (by its name, `hive.console.showText`).
- **Record** (the red dot) writes the source's events from then on to a file, until you click **Stop recording**, pick another source or close the console. Recordings are redacted like the console; tick **Include prompt and reply text in this recording** to keep the text in that one recording (tokens and sign-ins are still masked; the box starts unticked each time, or ticked while the console shows text). Each file starts with the versions of Hive and of your coding agents' CLIs (and the versions this Hive was tested with). The bar at the bottom names the file; **Show in folder** opens Hive's `console-recordings` folder. If the file can't be written (a full disk, say), the bar says **Recording failed** and why, and keeps what was written.
- **Help → Copy Diagnostics…** offers to attach the newest recording to a bug report: tick it to add its newest events, with names taken out as from the log and every file path shown as `<path>`. A recording that includes text is never attached.

#### The flight recorder <a id="the-flight-recorder"></a>

Some things happen too rarely to catch with the console open: a model's safeguards stopping a response, a CLI update sending something new. The **flight recorder** saves them for you. Turn on any of its triggers in **Settings → Advanced** (they are off by default):

- **Flight recorder: capture safeguard stops**: a model's safeguards stopped a response or switched the model.
- **Flight recorder: capture something unknown from a CLI**: a hook arrived that Hive doesn't know, or a hook or status line with a field Hive doesn't know, often the first sign that a CLI update changed something. What Hive knows each CLI sends is listed under that CLI in Settings → Advanced (**Hooks: known events**, **Hooks: known fields**, and Claude Code's **Status line: known fields**): add a harmless newcomer there and it is no longer captured.
- **Flight recorder: capture a CLI version change**: an agent started on a different version of its CLI than the last agent of that CLI did.
- **Flight recorder: capture an agent gone silent**: an agent working on a turn went silent past its limit, so Hive can't tell what it is doing.
- **Flight recorder: capture events matching**: a regular expression of your own, matched against each event's name and summary (its default, `(?!)`, never matches).

While any trigger is on, Hive keeps each agent's newest developer console events in memory (redacted, at most 200 or 256 KB an agent), whether the console is open or not. When a trigger fires, Hive waits 10 seconds for what follows (**Flight recorder: wait after a trigger**), then saves that agent's events, from before it to just after, as a **capture**: a recording like those you make, redacted, in the same `console-recordings` folder. A notice says what was captured and on which agent, with **Show in Folder**. The console's **Captures** button (the clock) lists them, newest first, and **Help → Copy Diagnostics…** offers the newest one like a recording. Each trigger captures an agent at most once every 10 minutes (**Flight recorder: one capture per trigger and agent every**), and Hive keeps the newest 20 captures (**Flight recorder: captures kept**), removing older ones; recordings you made yourself are never removed. Captures stay on your computer: nothing is sent anywhere.

### Agents reading the developer console <a id="agents-reading-the-developer-console"></a>

Hive's developer console shows what each coding agent's CLI sends Hive (its hooks, status line, transcript records, the terminal signs Hive reacts to) next to what Hive made of each: the status it set, the details it read. **Settings → Agent API → Let agents read the developer console** gives agents and the Hive Assistant a read-only tool for it (`hive_console`), so they can see what their CLI does while they build and debug. It is off unless you turn it on, and running sessions get the tool when they next start.

- Each agent reads **only its own sessions**. The Assistant reads its workspace's agents, its own session, and Hive's own events there (Agent API calls, merge slots, watches waking agents, Progress runs).
- Hive captures only while you have the developer console open on an agent: an agent reading starts nothing, and is told when nothing is captured. Hive keeps the newest 500 events of each, in memory only, and nothing once the console stops watching it.
- What it captures is **redacted**: no prompt or reply text, commands, tokens or sign-in details. Names of tools and files, session ids and the numbers (usage, plan limits, costs) stay. That holds while your console shows text too: **Developer console: show prompt, reply and command text** changes only what you see.

### Advanced settings <a id="advanced-settings"></a>

**Settings → Advanced** lists details Hive relies on that a coding agent's CLI update can break, and timeouts and limits for power users: the question each CLI asks before trusting a folder, the text that means it's ready, the messages that mean its sign-in was refused, the fields it sends plan limits in, what its screen shows while it works or once a turn is done, how its footer shows each permission mode (Claude Code's and Copilot's), the labels of Codex's `/permissions` menu, how Hive tells a safety classifier stopped an agent's response (Claude Code's note and question), the timeouts Hive gives its hooks and the `hive` tools, how long a stopped Copilot gets to exit by itself, whether right-click in Copilot panes goes to Copilot (see [Keyboard shortcuts](#keyboard-shortcuts)), the longest an agent's wait lasts, how many lanes the Progress panel shows, and whether the developer console shows prompt, reply and command text (see [The developer console](#the-developer-console)). Hive's defaults work with the CLI versions it was tested with; change one only when something stops working after a CLI update, or Hive's notes say to.

- The list is on tabs: **Hive**, then each CLI (**Claude Code**, **Codex**, **GitHub Copilot**), and it opens on the tab you last chose. A CLI you turned off in Settings → Providers keeps its tab, dimmed and marked **off**, with a line atop its values saying so: they apply once you turn it on. **Filter** it, or tick **Modified only**; Settings' own search and Modified only find them too. While any of these filters it, each tab shows how many of its values match, the list shows a tab with matches (searching for a value's name, such as `codex.trust.prompt`, opens its tab), and clearing them goes back to the tab you had. **Show developer features** is first on the Hive tab: it is in `advanced.json` too, as `hive.showDeveloperFeatures`.
- Each value shows its name (as `advanced.json` has it) and what it is for. Change it in its box: a changed one is marked, with its **default** beside it and **Reset** to put it back. A value Hive can't use (a regular expression that doesn't compile, a number out of range) is refused; the box says why.
- A value for a CLI also says when it no longer finds what that CLI was recorded showing ("It doesn't find what Claude Code 2.1.296 showed"): fine when the CLI changed, which is usually why you changed it.
- A value for a CLI (what Hive reads from it, or a timeout Hive gives it) records the CLI version it was made for, also when you edit `advanced.json` by hand. After that CLI updates, the value is flagged (**Made for Claude Code 2.1.296; 2.1.300 is installed now**): check it still works, then click **Keep for 2.1.300**, or **Reset** it if the new version no longer needs it.
- Some values a workspace can set for itself (**This workspace**), such as the lanes the Progress panel shows; empty uses the list's. A switch there is a list: its first choice follows Settings' value and shows it (**on (Settings')**), then **on** and **off**; choose the first again to follow Settings once more. (**Agents per project** is now the workflow option **Maximum agents**: see [Maximum agents](#several-agents-in-one-project).)
- **Settings → Advanced → Reset all settings** puts these back to their defaults too (a workspace's own values stay).
- Hive keeps them in **advanced.json** in its profile folder (`%APPDATA%\Hive`): **Open advanced.json** opens it in your editor. Hive reads it again whenever it changes. A value in it that Hive can't use is listed as **not valid**, and Hive uses the default meanwhile; if the file itself can't be read (a missing comma), Hive keeps what it read before and says so, and the list can't change values until the file is fixed.
- Timeouts and hook settings apply to sessions started afterwards (restart a running agent); the patterns, and Copilot's exit grace, apply at once. **Disable hardware acceleration** and **Save a memory snapshot if Hive is about to run out of memory** apply when Hive restarts: once you change it, it offers **Restart Hive**, which quits as **File → Exit** does (asking about running agents) and starts Hive again.
- **Workflow rules are enforced** (on by default) is the one switch for all of a project's workflow rules at once, in every project. On, Hive keeps what each project's options ask for as cards move, and when it sees a card's work merged by hand outside Hive it acts on it: a card whose change is already in its target is moved to Done (marked *outside the rules* if its review hadn't passed). Off, cards move wherever you put them and nothing is refused, and Hive only records what happened to each card's work, as before 1.0. It applies at once.
- **Usage history: keep full detail for** (14 days) is how long after a session ends the usage history keeps when each of its requests happened, to the second. After that it keeps 1-hour steps, so `.hive/usage.db` stays small; totals and costs don't change (see **Usage history** under [Long conversations](#long-conversations)).
- **Help → Copy Diagnostics** lists which values you changed (their names, not what you changed them to).
- The Hive Assistant can explain them and, if you let it change settings, change them when you ask.

#### Hive's changes to the CLIs <a id="hives-changes-to-the-clis"></a>

Hive changes some of what each CLI does in the sessions it starts, never by editing the CLI's own config files. Each such change can be turned off in **Settings → Advanced**, under its CLI. All are on by default, as Hive has always done; turning one off applies to sessions started afterwards, and its description says what Hive can no longer do without it.

- **Claude Code → Resume the Assistant with its current instructions** (`claude-code.override.promptSnapshotOff`): when the Assistant's conversation resumes, Hive passes `--system-prompt-snapshot off`, so the Assistant follows its role and Control settings as they are now. Off, Claude Code keeps the instructions it recorded when the conversation began, until it compacts. `--system-prompt-snapshot` in **Extra arguments** wins either way.
- **Codex → Set Codex's terminal title** (`codex.override.terminalTitle`): Hive gives Codex's sessions their title items (**Terminal title items**) and tells from the title when Codex needs you. Off, your own `[tui].terminal_title` applies and Hive tells when Codex needs you from its hooks instead, as for Codex versions before 0.160. Codex's setup in Provider Setup keeps Hive's title, which it waits on.
- **GitHub Copilot → Don't let Copilot update itself** (`copilot.override.noAutoUpdate`): Hive passes `--no-auto-update` and sets `COPILOT_AUTO_UPDATE=false` for its sessions and when it checks Copilot's sign-in and models, so a download never changes Copilot between a session's restarts; **Update** in Provider Setup updates it when you choose. Off, Hive sets neither, and Copilot updates itself as it would outside Hive (your own `COPILOT_AUTO_UPDATE` still applies).
