- **GitHub Copilot → Include every MCP server's instructions** (`copilot.override.allMcpInstructions`): Hive passes `--allow-all-mcp-server-instructions`, without which Copilot leaves the hive server's instructions (Hive's guidance for agents) out of its system prompt, and those of the other servers its agents get. Off, Copilot includes only the servers it allows by itself, and Hive gives its guidance as custom instructions instead.
- **Pre-approve Hive's tools** (Codex, Copilot: `<cli>.override.preApproveHiveTools`) and **Pre-approve the Assistant's Hive tools** (Claude Code): Hive's own `hive` tools run without the CLI asking, in every mode; for the Hive Assistant, only those its **Control** setting lets it use without asking, so writing a shared note still asks (on Codex, in its **Approve for me** mode, Codex's reviewer decides instead). Claude Code agents' Hive tools are never pre-approved. Off, the CLI asks before Hive's tools as its own permission mode says. Either way, what an agent or the Assistant may do with them is decided by Hive (the Agent API and Control), and whether a session has them at all by the workspace's MCP settings.
- **Only the workspace's MCP servers** (each CLI: `<cli>.override.mcpIsolation`): Hive's sessions get the MCP servers the workspace gives them, and Hive turns the CLI's own off for them (Claude Code's `--strict-mcp-config`; Codex's `enabled=false`; Copilot's `--disable-builtin-mcps` and `--disable-mcp-server`, its built-in GitHub servers included). Off, the servers in your own CLI config and the project's load beside them, as outside Hive. A workspace server, Hive's `hive` above all, still wins over one of yours with the same name: on Codex, which merges two servers of one name, Hive's settings replace each of yours for `hive`. The one exception is a `hive` of yours reached over HTTP: Codex can't run it beside Hive's, and Hive can't leave it out without editing your config, so Codex agents don't start until you rename yours (Hive says in which file). Claude Code may first ask you to approve a project's new servers, as it does outside Hive.
- **GitHub Copilot → Keep GH_TOKEN and GITHUB_TOKEN from Copilot** (`copilot.override.stripGitHubTokens`): Hive leaves those two variables, which other tools set, out of Copilot's environment, so a token left there never signs Copilot agents in to another account; `COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN` is kept. Off, Copilot gets them and signs in with them before its own login. Hive checks Copilot again when you change it, so **Provider Setup** shows the sign-in its sessions will use.
- **Pass right-click to Claude Code**, **to Codex** and **to Copilot** (`<cli>.mouse.rightClickToCli`, off by default): right-click in a pane is Hive's copy and paste. Turned on, right-clicks go to the CLI instead, at once. Codex and Copilot track the mouse themselves; Claude Code doesn't at its prompt, so there a right-click then does nothing (Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V still copy and paste).
- **Claude Code → Hive's status line in place of yours** (`claude-code.override.statusLine`): Hive gives Claude Code a status line of its own, which sends Hive the model, context, cost and plan limits for the agent's footer and shows nothing under the input. Off, the status line in your Claude Code settings (yours, the project's, or a `--settings` in Extra arguments) shows again: Hive's command reads the status first, then runs yours with the same input Claude Code gives it. With no status line of yours, nothing changes.
- **Hive's hooks**, with no switch: they are how Hive knows what each agent is doing (working, waiting for you, finished), and they carry file locks, the lines Hive types and inbox notices, so Hive doesn't run a CLI without them; a CLI you need to run without hooks is better in a plain terminal. Your own hooks still run: Claude Code gets yours after Hive's, and a `--settings` that sets `disableAllHooks` stops the agent from starting, saying what to change.
- **Codex: your own hooks run beside Hive's**, with no switch: Codex keeps the hooks in your `config.toml` apart from the ones Hive passes on its command line, and runs both; your hooks stay trusted, and Hive trusts only its own (checked with Codex 0.163.0). A `-c hooks.…` of yours in Extra arguments replaces Hive's hook for that event.
- **Codex runs without its shared background server** (`--no-daemon`), with no switch: Codex uses that server only when it is given no configuration on its command line, and Hive gives every session its own (its hooks, MCP servers and guidance). A session on that server would also keep working after **Stop**.
- **Hive → Sessions say their terminal is xterm-256color** (`hive.sessions.terminalType`): every CLI's session starts with `TERM=xterm-256color`, as Hive's terminal is, and without Windows Terminal's `WT_SESSION`, so the CLIs draw the same whether you opened Hive from the Start menu or from a terminal (Claude Code draws `❯` rather than `>`; Codex and Copilot look the same either way). A `TERM` in your own environment or a connection's is kept. Off, sessions get the environment Hive was opened with.
- **The model and effort** Hive passes (`--model`, `--effort` and each CLI's equivalents) are already yours to leave out: when no model or effort is chosen for the agent, its project or the CLI's settings page, Hive passes neither and the CLI uses its own.

Under **Hive**, **Hive's guidance for agents** (`hive.guidance`, on): with Hive's tools, each project agent gets Hive's guidance (how to use the tools, the board and handovers, and the project's latest handover), as the hive server's instructions on Claude Code and Copilot, and on Codex after the `developer_instructions` Codex itself would use (yours, a profile's or a trusted project's, as Codex reads them for the agent's own arguments), which Hive no longer replaces. If Codex can't say which those are (**Reading a session's instructions: timeout** under Codex), Hive never replaces them: the agent starts without Hive's guidance, and the Assistant with its role alone, and Hive tells you once. Off, agents get Hive's tools without the guidance, from their next session. The Hive Assistant always gets its own guidance and role: without them it would be just another agent, so there is no switch for them.

The Hive Assistant's MCP servers are chosen per workspace in its own view ([Servers for the Hive Assistant](#servers-for-the-hive-assistant)); the Advanced switch that gave it every workspace server is gone.

Under **Hive**, seven more switches cover what Hive does with a pane's keys and with what it types into the CLIs, the same for every CLI. Each is as Hive has always done by default, and applies at once (the start view at the Assistant's next start or compaction, a running Assistant's too):

- **Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V go to the CLI** (`hive.terminal.ctrlCVToCli`, off): in an agent's pane, Ctrl+C copies the selection (with nothing selected it goes to the CLI) and Ctrl+V pastes text, or a screenshot as an attachment. On, both keys go to the CLI, as in any other terminal; copy and paste with right-click instead, unless right-click goes to the CLI too.
- **Record builds in the journal** (`hive.journal.builds`, on): each successful build of Hive itself from its source code goes into the Assistant's journal as Hive's entry, while the journal is on with events in its scope. Off, builds aren't written, and one left while Hive was closed is dropped when the workspace opens.
- **The Assistant starts with Hive's start view** (`hive.assistant.startView`, on): while the Assistant's journal is on, Hive gives it a short view of the workspace when it starts and after each compaction. Off, it starts with none and reads what it needs with its tools.
- **Send prompts as typed** (`hive.prompt.asTyped`, off): Hive puts each prompt it types into an agent (a message, a wake, your task for an agent without Hive's tools) on one line, with runs of spaces made one and control characters left out. Invisible characters (zero-width spaces, soft hyphens, direction marks) are left out either way, but for those inside an emoji: Claude Code doesn't send a prompt with one in it until Enter is pressed again. On, Claude Code and Copilot get the text as written, line breaks included. **Codex, and a slash command in any CLI, still get each line break and tab as a space**: Hive types those key by key, where a line break would send the prompt early and a tab complete a word.
- **Typed prompts: line breaks** (`codex.prompt.newlineKey`, under Codex, Automatic): with Send prompts as typed on, what Hive presses in Codex for a line break. **Alt+Enter** or **Ctrl+J** are keys Codex takes as a new line rather than a send, so a multi-line prompt arrives as written (tried with Codex 0.164.0). **Automatic** uses Alt+Enter with a Codex version it was tried with, and spaces with others; a key you choose is used with any version. **Spaces** puts the prompt on one line, as before. If you changed Codex's own new-line keys, choose Spaces. A slash command always gets spaces.
- **All keys go to the CLI** (`hive.terminal.allKeysToCli`, off): in an agent's pane, Hive's shortcuts take their keys, apart from those the CLI uses itself. On, every key goes to the CLI except the command palette's shortcut (**Show All Commands**, Ctrl+Shift+P unless you changed it), so every command is still a keypress away. A two-step shortcut you gave the palette (such as Ctrl+K Ctrl+P) works too: its first key then stays Hive's, and any other key after it goes to the CLI. Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V have their own switch, above.
- **A CLI putting text on the clipboard** (`hive.terminal.osc52`, **Allow**): when an agent's CLI asks to put text on the clipboard just after you pressed a key or clicked in its pane (Copilot does to copy its own selection), Hive takes it. **Ask each time** shows you the text first, with **Copy** and **Don't Copy**; **Block** takes none. A CLI can never read your clipboard, whatever you choose.
- **A card's start in its own words** (`hive.prompt.rawCardStart`, off): a card started on an agent reaches it wrapped in Hive's words (which card it is, what it depends on, the docs to read first, the skill to follow). On, the agent gets only the card's title, your note and its description. Hive's wakes and messages stay as they are: they are how Hive and its agents talk.
- **Clear the CLI's draft before Hive types** (`hive.prompt.clearDraft`, on): before Hive types a prompt or **Compact**, it presses Ctrl+U, which clears what you left in the CLI's input, and again to take back a prompt it stopped part-way (in Claude Code, whose Ctrl+U clears only one row of a wrapped prompt, Hive presses Backspace for each character instead). In Claude Code, a draft that wraps onto several rows is cleared a row at a time, and Hive types only once it sees the input box empty; if it can't (Claude Code busy, or its screen not as Hive expects), it types nothing and tries again later (**Clearing the input: wait for each key**, under Claude Code, sets how long it waits for each key). Off, Hive never presses it: your draft stays in front of what Hive types and is sent with it, and **Compact** can fail behind one. Changing a Codex agent's mode still clears its input first, as its menu needs.

## Updating Hive <a id="updating-hive"></a>

Hive keeps itself up to date from its GitHub releases. By default it checks shortly after starting and every six hours, downloads a new version in the background, and installs it the next time you quit Hive — it never restarts on its own, so your agents are never interrupted.

- While a new version downloads, the status bar shows the progress; when it's ready it says **Restart to update to X.Y.Z**. Click it to see what's new.
- **Restart and Update** (in that dialog, the notification or the tray menu) closes Hive, installs the update and opens Hive again. If agents are working, Hive asks first, and you can let them finish; cancel, and the notification keeps its button for later. Stopped sessions can be resumed as usual.
- **Skip This Version** stops Hive offering that version; the next one is offered as normal.
- **Hive is now Hive Desktop** (0.5.0). The update renames the Start menu and desktop shortcuts and the entry in **Installed apps** to **Hive Desktop**. Nothing else moves: Hive stays installed in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\Hive` as `Hive.exe`, and your settings and conversations stay in `%APPDATA%\Hive`. A taskbar pin keeps working.
- **Help → Check for Updates…** checks straight away and tells you the result.
- **Settings → Updates** turns automatic checks and downloads off, chooses whether updates install **automatically when Hive quits** or **only when you choose Restart and Update**, and can include pre-release versions (betas and release candidates). With **Include pre-releases** on, a pre-release you installed is offered the newest version newer than it, whether that's a beta, a release candidate or the release itself.
- **Hive never installs an update over a copy that isn't older.** Before it installs, Hive reads the copy installed on this computer. A downloaded update is installed only when it is a newer version (1.0.0-rc.2 is older than 1.0.0). If that copy is the same version or newer (a build you made yourself, say), or Hive can't read it, Hive doesn't install the update and says why (*Hive Desktop X wasn't installed*).
- **A build you made yourself** (`npm run dist`) is shown in Settings → Updates as **Local build X (its commit)**. A published release of the same version is offered, never installed by itself: Hive tells you and **Open Release Page** opens it on GitHub. A newer release updates it as usual. Its installer also waits for another Hive installer to finish (one an update started as Hive quit) before it installs, so yours is the last one installed.

Updates are checked against their published checksum before they install.

## Notifications <a id="notifications"></a>

Hive tells you each thing once, in one place, and keeps it under the bell.

- **While you use Hive** (any Hive window focused), it shows a **pop-up** in that window: an agent finished or needs you, a lane or a card needs you, plan usage, and Hive's own messages (a warning, what an action did, an update ready). Every pop-up is also kept in the **Notifications** panel (the bell).
- **With Hive in the background**, an agent finishing or needing you, a lane's or a card's news, and plan usage come as a **Windows notification** instead, and are kept under the bell too, without a pop-up. Hive's own messages wait as a pop-up in the window, which stays until you come back to it.
- **With several Hive windows**, what an action you started says (it worked, or a warning about it) pops up in the window you started it from, even if you have moved to another by the time it finishes, so you find it under that window's bell. Everything else pops up in the window you are using.
- Never both: what you see in Hive never comes as a Windows notification as well.

When an agent finishes (for one waiting on background tasks, once they have ended) or needs input, Hive also plays a chime. Configure all of this in **Settings → Notifications**; each project can override the chime.

Agents that finish together don't flood you: they chime once, and get one notice (**3 agents finished in hive**: *Claude, Codex, Agent 3*, or across projects *hive (2), web (1)*), shown a few seconds after the last of them. An agent that starts its next turn within those seconds (Hive gave it more work as it finished) isn't told as finished: you hear from it when that turn ends, or at once if it needs you. If you turn notifications off meanwhile, it isn't shown. An agent asking for your input is always told at once, on its own.

### Pop-ups and the bell <a id="pop-ups-and-the-bell"></a>

- **Where:** bottom right by default, left of the Assistant's panel and clear of the bar under a pane, or wherever **Pop-up position** puts them (any corner, or the middle of the top or bottom edge). Several stack, the newest nearest the edge; past four, **+N more** shows the rest.
- **How long:** a pop-up goes after **6 seconds** (**Pop-ups close after**), one with buttons or an error after at least 15. It stays while the pointer or the keyboard is on it, and while its window is in the background. One for an agent **waiting for you** stays until you click it, dismiss it (×), or answer the agent, wherever you answer it (**Pop-ups for an agent waiting for you stay until handled** can make them close like the others).
- **Click** a pop-up to go to what it is about: the agent's pane, the card, or the project, in whichever window has it. From the keyboard, **Tab** to its text and press **Enter** (or Space), Tab on to its buttons or **×**, and **Esc** closes it. Its colour says what kind it is: one that needs you has the accent, a warning is amber, an error red.
- **Show pop-ups about agents for:** *All workspaces* (the default: the window you're in pops up notices from every Hive window), *This workspace*, *This project* (the project the window shows), or *None (only in the bell)*. One it leaves out is kept under the bell without popping up; with Hive in the background, Windows notifications come whatever it says.
- **The bell** (bottom of the activity bar, or **Ctrl+Alt+U**) keeps every notification this window showed or told, newest first, so "where did that go?" always has an answer there. While some are unread it shows a dot and turns orange; opening the panel marks them read. **Ctrl+Alt+U** again or **Esc** closes it (Esc in a text field, a terminal or an editor stays with them), and so does its ×; while it's open, new notifications go into its list rather than popping up. **Clear all** empties it, and closes the pop-ups still on screen with it. The **Agents that need you** list in the status bar stays the live list of who waits for you now; a notification about an agent opens its pane.

**Buttons.** Some notifications offer something to do: **End Them** for processes an agent left running, **Hand Over to…** for a long conversation, **Restart and Update**, **Resume**, **Allow**, **Release Slot…** and so on. The buttons are on the pop-up and on its row under the bell alike, and stay only while they still apply. Pressed, a button shows it is working, then the notification says what happened in its place (*Ended 3 processes*, *Answered*, *Released*); if the thing has happened another way meanwhile (you answered the agent in its terminal, the processes ended by themselves), the buttons go and it says that instead. Each button checks again when you press it: **End Them** ends exactly the processes it listed, by their id and start time, and if they have changed since it says so and points you to the agent's **⋯** menu instead of ending anything.

A notification the Hive Assistant or an agent sends you about one agent or one card (**HB4 needs an Enter in its pane**) opens it when you click it, as every notification about something does: such a notification shows a **›** after its text and highlights under the pointer, and hovering it says what it opens (*Open HB4's pane*); a plain one has neither. If the agent was removed or the card deleted since, Hive says so instead.

### When a sign-in expires <a id="when-a-sign-in-expires"></a>

All of Claude Code's agents share one sign-in, and so do all of Codex's. When it expires (or Codex's API key is refused), the CLI stops each agent's next turn. Hive then shows those agents as **Needs sign-in**, with a violet dot, in the agent row, the pane header, the Overview and the Assistant's lists. Hover one to see what the CLI said and how to sign in. You're told once for that CLI, however many of its agents stopped ("3 agents need you to sign in to Claude Code"), and **Help → Provider Setup** shows it signed out.

To sign in again:

- **Claude Code**: type `/login` in one of its agents' terminals, or use **Help → Provider Setup → Claude Code → Sign in**. The agent you signed in from carries on by itself.
- **Codex**: **Help → Provider Setup → Codex → Sign in**, or `codex login` in a terminal.

Hive notices the sign-in when an agent of that CLI works again, when Provider Setup's **Sign in** finishes, or at its next check (every minute while agents wait). The agents that are still stopped then read **Stopped while signed out**, and **Resume (n)** in the project header counts them: it types a short "carry on" into each, so a card loop picks up where it stopped. Agents that already carried on aren't prompted again. A Codex agent you prompt again right after signing in shows **working**: Hive reads Codex's refusal from its session after the fact, and that late read can't turn it back to **Needs sign-in**.

### When the connection drops <a id="when-the-connection-drops"></a>

A router restarting or Wi-Fi dropping makes every agent's requests fail. Each CLI retries for a while (Claude Code for about three minutes), then ends the turn on an error such as *API Error: Connection refused — a firewall or proxy may be blocking it (ECONNREFUSED)*, and the agent waits for you.

Turn on **Settings → Advanced → Resume agents after a connection drops** (off by default) and Hive carries such agents on by itself. The agent shows **Waiting for the connection…**, and the Progress panel has a run for it. Hive checks the CLI's own API host now and then (after 30 seconds, then less and less often, at most every 5 minutes). Once it answers, Hive gives the agent's conversation one note: *The connection dropped at 11:47 (UTC+01:00) and came back at 11:57 (UTC+01:00): check what was cut off, rerun it, and carry on.* A card loop picks up where it stopped. If you prompt the agent yourself meanwhile, Hive stops waiting.

Hive only does this for connection errors. A usage limit, an expired sign-in (see [When a sign-in expires](#when-a-sign-in-expires)) or an overloaded server is never resumed, since resuming wouldn't help.

Hive stops and tells you when the connection doesn't come back within 2 hours, or when an agent's turn ends on a connection error again after three resumes in an hour. Then other apps may reach the internet while this PC's network hasn't recovered: try renewing the network adapter's addresses (`ipconfig /renew`), flushing DNS (`ipconfig /flushdns`) or restarting the PC, then resume the agent. If the Hive Assistant is set up, it is told what was resumed and what wasn't. The limits, the address checked for each CLI and the errors each CLI counts as a dropped connection are in Settings → Advanced too.

### When a safety classifier stops an agent <a id="when-a-safety-classifier-stops-an-agent"></a>

A CLI's safety classifier can stop an agent's response part way (Claude Code's model safeguards, Codex's cyber checks), carry on with another model, or ask you what to do. Hive spots it in the agent's session and tells you, so a false positive doesn't go unnoticed and an agent doesn't carry on with a weaker model without your knowing:

- **A shield beside the agent's status** in its pane says it happened. Hover it for when, on which model, how many times this session, and the names of the agent's last few tool calls before it (only their names: never what they did, nor what was stopped), so you can tell a false positive from an agent drifting into risky work. It also says what you can do: in Claude Code, `/feedback` reports a false positive, `/model` switches back, and `/config` sets whether it switches models by itself when a message is flagged.
- **When the CLI asks you about it** (Claude Code asks whether to switch to another model), the agent is **Needs input**, and the notification and its pane say why: *a safety classifier stopped a response*, with its last tool calls. Once you answer, the agent shows **Idle after a safeguard stop: give it a nudge**, and the Hive Assistant can prompt it again.
- **When its model changes after one** (Claude Code switching by itself, or Codex rerouting a request), Hive tells you, the shield is highlighted with the model it runs on now, and the agent is in **Agents that need you** until you look at its pane. Hive also notes it on the card the agent is working on, and the Assistant sees it in the agent's activity.
- **Performance** counts the stops per agent and model (below), including one you only saw as Claude Code's question (it doesn't always record those itself). Each stop counts once, however close together two are.

Hive reads this from Claude Code's and Codex's session records. GitHub Copilot's sessions don't record it, so for Copilot agents it isn't detected.

### When Claude Code asks you something by itself <a id="when-claude-code-asks-by-itself"></a>

Now and then, after a turn in **auto mode**, Claude Code (2.1.312 and later) offers to **teach auto mode about your environment**: it would read the project, your recent Claude sessions and, if you choose, your shell history and other repositories. It puts that question on its screen without telling Hive. The choice is yours, so Hive never answers it:

- The agent shows **Needs input** with the question (*Claude Code asks: Teach auto mode about your environment?*), it is in **Agents that need you**, you're notified, and the Hive Assistant is told it waits for you.
- Hive types nothing into the agent while the question shows, and presses no Enter: Enter would answer it. Wakes and messages wait. Answer it in the agent's pane (Esc puts it off); once it's gone, the agent is idle again and what waited is typed.
- If the question took a line Hive had already typed, and the agent's input is empty once it's gone, Hive types that line again: it waits **Lost line: wait** (2 seconds, **Settings → Advanced → Hive**) to be sure the line wasn't taken after all. A line that was a message Hive can't send again is shown to you in a notification, with its words. Your own text in the input is never cleared or typed over.
- Claude Code asks again a week after **Not now**, and never after **Don't show again**. To not be offered it in Hive's sessions at all, turn off **Settings → Advanced → Claude Code → Offer auto mode's environment setup**: Hive's Claude Code agents are then started with the offer off for that launch (nothing is written to Claude Code's own files, and auto mode decides as before). `/auto-mode-setup` in an agent still runs it when you want.

A question or dialog of a CLI's own that Hive doesn't recognise can take an agent's keys the same way. Before Hive types into Claude Code, it checks that the agent's input takes a key. When three tries in a row get no answer, Hive stops typing there:

- The agent shows **Needs input** with *Something on its screen is taking its keys: answer it there, then Try again*, and its pane's header says **Its input takes no keys**. You're notified, and the Hive Assistant is told it waits for you. If the agent already waits for you on something else, that stays its reason.
- Hive types nothing more into it and presses no key there, however long it waits. Wakes and messages wait, and a card its lane would hand it waits too: nothing is retried in the meantime.
- Answer what its terminal shows, then press **Try again** beside its status (in a narrow pane, it's in the pane's **⋯** menu). Hive tries the input once. If it takes the key, the agent is idle again and what waited is typed; if not, Hive says *Its input still takes no keys*. Typing in its pane so your text shows in its input frees it too, as does the agent carrying on by itself. Your own text in the input is never cleared.

### Agents that need you <a id="agents-that-need-you"></a>

With agents working in several projects, the status bar tells you what is waiting on you: **2 need you**. It counts things, not requests: each card with requests that wait for you once, however many it has, and each agent stopped in its pane once (hover it: *2 cards and 1 agent need you*). A click shows them: first the cards (see [For you](#for-you): an approval, a decision, a question an agent asked through Hive; a click opens the card to answer it). A card with several requests, or an agent stopped on it, shows *3 questions* on its row with each one under it, answered on its own; an agent stopped on such a card (it asked one of them, or works on or reviews the card) is listed under it and not counted again. Then the other agents stopped in their own panes, oldest first, with how long each has been waiting:

- **Needs input**: an agent waiting for your answer, such as a permission. It stays in the list until you answer it.
- **Asks**: an agent that asked you a question but carries on working meanwhile (Codex can). Its pane says **has a question for you**, and it stays in the list until you answer the question in its terminal.
- **Needs sign-in**: an agent its CLI stopped because the sign-in has expired. It stays in the list until you sign in again (below).
- **Stopped by an error**: an agent its CLI stopped on an error, such as a lost API connection, with what it said (Claude Code, Codex and GitHub Copilot). It stays in the list until the agent goes on.
- **After a safeguard stop**: an agent whose model changed after its CLI's safety classifier stopped a response (see [When a safety classifier stops an agent](#when-a-safety-classifier-stops-an-agent)). It leaves the list once its pane is on screen.
Agents that only **finished** while you weren't looking at them (in another project, on another page of agents, on another tab, or while Hive was in the background) don't need you, so they aren't counted: a quieter **✓ 3 finished** beside the count tells you, and the list shows them under **Finished**. Each leaves once its pane is on screen.

Click a row to go to that agent (the Assistant's opens its panel). The **Projects** icon in the activity bar shows how many agents need you, and each project in the sidebar shows how many of its agents do.

With Hive behind other windows, its **taskbar button** shows the count too (the same number as the status bar's): a red badge over the icon, and the number before the window's title (in Alt+Tab and when you hover the button). When an agent comes to ask you something while Hive is in the background, the button flashes until you switch to it. Turn either off in **Settings → Notifications** (turning the flash off also stops one that has started).

Below them, **To review** lists worktree agents that have stopped or finished with work not merged yet, with a summary such as *3 files, +120 −40 · 2 commits*, and buttons for **Changes** and **Merge…**. They don't add to the count; one leaves the list once its work is merged or it starts working again. When nothing needs you but there is work to review, the status bar says **1 to review**.

### Sleep and shutdown <a id="sleep-and-shutdown"></a>

Agents work while you're away from the keyboard, so Hive keeps Windows from **sleeping** while any agent is working or waiting on background tasks, and lets it sleep again as soon as none is. The screen can still turn off and lock. The status bar says **Keeping the PC awake: 2 agents working** while it does. **Settings → General → Keep the PC awake while agents work** chooses *When plugged in* (the default: on a laptop's battery, it may sleep), *Always, on battery too* or *Never*.

When Windows shuts down, restarts or signs you out, Hive backs up the agents' transcripts first, in the moment Windows gives it, without holding up the shutdown. After the PC wakes from sleep, Hive reads each agent's state and plan usage again.

### When Hive closed unexpectedly <a id="when-hive-closed-unexpectedly"></a>

If Hive ended without quitting (it crashed, something ended its process, or the PC lost power), the next start says so with a notice, **Hive closed unexpectedly**: **Hive was last seen on 17 Oct at 04:03. 6 agents were running in hive and ambrosia. Nothing was resumed.** The time is when Hive last showed it was running (it notes that every minute), and the agents are those running then. The notice also says when Hive kept a crash dump of that run, and names any merge an agent was in the middle of, with the folder to check. **Resume All** resumes those agents, each in the conversation it was in; **Show Overview** shows every project's agents first. Nothing resumes on its own: to have Hive resume them by itself, turn on **Settings → Sessions → Resume agents after an unexpected close** (off by default), and the notice then says which were resumed. If the Hive Assistant is set up in the workspace, Hive also leaves it a line in its inbox, so it can tell you which card loops stalled; it doesn't restart anything itself.

What the agents had started can outlive Hive: a test run, a dev server, a copy of an app. While agents run, Hive notes what each one's commands have started (from its own record of each agent's processes, so only those), and the notice says what still runs: **2 processes the agents started are still running: npm run e2e, node serve.js.** A command an agent ran through `hive-progress` (a test run, usually) is one item, named by the title the agent gave it, with how many of its processes still run: **5 processes the agents started are still running: e2e: 196 suites in hb2 (4 processes), node serve.js.** **Stop Them** ends exactly those, each checked to be the same process it noted (a process that has since got one of their process ids is never touched), and such a run whole, with what it started since. What it can't end still runs, and pressing Stop Them again tries it again. A process of such a run that Windows lets Hive neither end nor check (one running as administrator, say) is different: once what started it has ended, Hive can no longer tell it from a process that later gets its ID, so it never tries it again; instead, each press of Stop Them says it may still be running and gives its process ID, to look for in Task Manager's **Details** tab and end there if it is still the test run's. A test run left over this way keeps its test slot meanwhile; a run waiting for the slot names it as **left over from a session that ended**. The agent's working folder (its slot) is held the same way, and Hive's process helper, which stays after Hive while those processes run, frees it for the next start once they have all ended ([A slot held after Hive closed](#upgrading-to-10)). Nothing is stopped unless you choose it, whatever **Settings → Agents → Processes left running** says: that setting is for what agents leave when they stop while Hive runs.

Quitting Hive, restarting it, installing an update or Windows shutting down or signing out never shows the notice. Help → Copy Diagnostics says whether the last run ended without quitting, and the log (**Help → Open Logs**) has the details.

### Pausing the team before an update <a id="pausing-the-team-before-an-update"></a>

Before you install an update while agents are at work, pause the team, so nothing starts again behind your back and you can see when it's safe. **Pause the Team** is in the Progress panel's Lanes, the command palette and Help → Check for Updates (**Pause the Team First**, when an update is ready and agents are working). It pauses every open workspace:

- No lane starts its next card, no agent is woken by a card event (a verdict, a review to do, its card watch) and the reviewer pool gives out no reviews; the merge queue takes no new card. Each lane shows *paused: the team is pausing*. Nothing is lost: what was held is delivered once you resume, if it is still due.
- Each running agent is told once to finish its turn, end the runs it started (tests, builds, dev servers, test copies of Hive) and commit its work. Your messages and your answers to their questions still reach them.
- The Lanes section says what still runs: an agent working or with background tasks, a run in the Progress panel, a process an agent's session left running when it stopped (a test runner, a dev server), a process a running agent's command left running with its shell gone (as Activity marks it, at once here), a slot still held. **End It…** ends such a process and what it started, after asking. With several windows open, each window ends only its own workspace's processes: another workspace's list has **Show Its Window**, which brings that window forward, where its **End It…** is. Something Hive can't check is named, and never counted as parked.
- Hive counts what it records. A server you, or an agent, started yourself from a terminal outside Hive's runs isn't in its records: end it before you install.
- Once nothing runs, it says **All parked: safe to install**, with **Install Now** when an update is ready. Hive checks again as you click it, and installs only if every open workspace is still paused and parked.

While the team is paused, starting a lane is refused, and the status bar says **Team paused**. You can still start an agent or type to it yourself. **Resume the Team** lifts the pause. After the update installs, the team resumes on its own, as below. A pause lasts only while Hive runs: whenever Hive closes (for the update, or because you quit, restart the PC or it crashes), it opens again with the team not paused. The Hive Assistant can pause and resume its own workspace's team too, and tell you when it is safe to install.

### When Hive restarts for an update <a id="when-hive-restarts-for-an-update"></a>

Installing a new version closes Hive: **Restart to Update**, or an installer you run yourself. Before it closes, Hive notes which agents were working, watching their cards or in a card loop, with each one's card, round and lane, and leaves each a line in its inbox: *Hive is restarting for an update. Your work is saved; you'll be resumed.* (An installer of this version or later asks the running Hive to close first; an older one ends it, and the next start then finds a newer version and treats it the same way.)

When Hive starts again, it resumes those agents in their conversations **one at a time**, each once the one before it has taken its note: *Hive restarted for an update (1.0.0-rc.4 → 1.0.0-rc.5) at 10:00 UTC; your session was stopped and is resumed now. You were on HI-1673, round 2, in lane 'Lanes from the bottom' (its builder). Carry on: re-arm your card watch if you were waiting.* Meanwhile their lanes don't show them as stalled. One notice then says who was resumed, and who wasn't and why: an agent you removed, one already running or since moved to a newer conversation, one whose working folder (slot) is held for other work (resume it yourself), or one that wasn't ready in time (**Settings → Advanced → Resuming after a restart: ready within**, 90 s), which Hive stops before it starts the next. If the Hive Assistant is set up, it gets a line saying the same.

To decide yourself each time, turn off **Settings → Advanced → Resume agents after an update**: Hive then offers instead, as it does after you quit (see [Quitting](#quitting)). A workspace can have its own setting (**This workspace** in Settings → Advanced), and so can the time an agent has to be ready. Hive resumes a workspace's agents once that workspace has opened and is read, and the last way Hive closed decides: if you quit while it is resuming them, the ones it hadn't finished are offered at the next start, and an update after a quit resumes them by itself.

## The system tray <a id="the-system-tray"></a>

Closing the (last) window keeps Hive running in the tray so sessions continue. The tray icon shows a red dot when an agent needs you. Its menu lists the agents that need you and those with work to review, from every window (click one to go to it), then your active projects, grouped by workspace when several windows are open. Quit from the tray menu or **File → Exit**.

### Quitting <a id="quitting"></a>

Quitting stops every running session, in every window. Nothing is lost: each conversation is kept and you can resume it next time. So Hive only asks first when an agent is **in the middle of something** (working, waiting for your answer, or waiting on background tasks it started). Otherwise it just closes and a notification tells you which sessions to resume.

When it asks, you see a table of the agents: each one's project, what it's doing, its card, and what stopping it would interrupt. You can choose:

- **Quit now**: stop everything straight away.
- **Quit when agents finish**: Hive hides and quits by itself once no agent is working or waiting on background tasks. Until then the tray menu has **Quit Now** and **Cancel Pending Quit**, and opening the window shows a banner with the same choices. If you open the window meanwhile and leave a file with unsaved changes, Hive asks about it (save or discard) before it quits, whether the last agent finished or you chose Quit Now; **Cancel** there keeps Hive running.
- **Cancel**: keep working.

If files have unsaved changes (in the Files tab, or a shared note, skill, instruction or memory file, or MCP server you're editing), Hive always asks, lists them at the top, and saves them (**Save and quit**) or discards them, as you choose. A file that changed on disk since you opened it isn't overwritten: Hive stays open so you can decide.

Tick **Don't ask again** to stop the question about sessions, or change it any time in **Settings → General → Confirm before quitting** (*When an agent is working*, *Whenever sessions are running*, or *Never*).

The next time Hive starts, it offers to resume the agents that were working, watching their cards or in a card loop when you quit: one notice, **Resume the 3 agents that were working?**, naming them. **Resume** starts each again in its conversation, one at a time, telling it Hive was quit and started again; **Don't Resume** leaves them stopped and doesn't ask again. Nothing resumes until you choose, and closing the notice (×) without choosing asks again at the next start. With several workspaces open, one that gets ready later replaces the notice with one naming every agent still offered (the earlier one says *Replaced by a newer offer*), so each choice applies to exactly the agents its notice names.

### Your commands <a id="commands"></a>

Name a command once and run it by hand, or pick it for when a workspace opens or closes, or when Hive quits: a local server or a database your agents need, a backup tool, a sync. It's like JetBrains' External Tools.

- **Where.** **Settings → General → Commands** holds Hive's own, for every workspace; **Settings → Workspace → Commands** holds this workspace's, saved in its `.hive/workspace.json`, which Hive keeps out of version control. So a repository you clone never brings commands that run by themselves.
- **A command** has a name, the command line (`cmd.exe` runs it), a folder to run in (empty: the workspace's folder) and a tick, **The commands after this need it**.
- **By hand.** **Run it now** beside it in Settings, or **Run Command: <name>** in the command palette. Hive shows it running in the same dialog as the points below.
- **Placeholders.** `{workspace}` is the workspace's folder, `{name}` its folder name, `{reason}` why it runs (`open`, `window`, `switch`, `quit`, `update` or `manual`) and `{project:api}` the folder of the project `api`; quote them where a path may hold spaces: `replica run hive --workspace "{workspace}"`. One that can't be filled where it runs (`{workspace}` as Hive quits, a project that isn't there) means that command doesn't run: Hive says so rather than run it half-filled. Settings shows what each will run, and warns beside a point's command that can't be filled there.
- **Yours alone.** Only you set them: the Hive Assistant can't change them.

#### When a workspace opens or closes, or Hive quits <a id="close-commands"></a>

Pick commands, in order, for **Settings → Workspace → When this workspace opens, run** and **When this workspace closes, run** (this workspace's commands or Hive's), and **Settings → General → When Hive quits, run** (Hive's). Commands you had before 1.0 in the close or quit setting are in the lists now, named **Close command** and **Quit command**.

- **When they run.** As the workspace opens; as it closes: you close its window, switch the window to another workspace, choose **Close Workspace**, or quit Hive with it open (two windows open means two runs, one each; closing the last window to the tray isn't a close, and nothing runs if Hive closes unexpectedly); and **When Hive quits, run** once, after every window's own.
- **Hive waits for them.** The window dims with a dialog listing them: each waiting, running (with how long, its command and its last lines of output; a long command shows its first two lines: point at it for the whole line), done, failed or skipped. **Don't wait** lets Hive go on (start the workspace's agents, switch, close the window, quit) while the rest run by themselves, in order.
- **As a workspace opens,** its agents wait: Hive starts none, and none can be started, until the list is done or you choose **Don't wait** or **Stop here**.
- **When one fails,** Hive asks: **Rerun** it, **Skip it and continue** with the rest, or **Stop here**, running none of the rest. Either way Hive goes on: a workspace opening still starts its agents. A command ticked **The commands after this need it** leads with **Stop here**, and Skip carries a warning. If you've chosen **Don't wait**, nobody is asked: the list stops after a failed command that the next ones need, and goes on after any other.
- **Closing: after Hive has finished writing.** The commands start once the workspace's board and the Assistant's journal are closed and every save Hive had under way has landed. Until then the dialog says **Waiting for <workspace> to finish saving…**. **Skip the command** there means Hive won't run them, nor **When Hive quits, run**; Hive still finishes closing the workspace. **When Hive quits, run** waits the same way for every open workspace.
- **To keep Hive from waiting at all,** write a command that starts its work and returns at once, such as `start "" "C:\Tools\backup.exe" "{workspace}"`.
- **Updates.** Restarting Hive to install an update skips the close and quit commands, unless you tick **Also when Hive restarts to install an update**.
- **Their output.** Hive keeps each workspace's last 10 runs (a run is a point's whole list) and When Hive quits's, the last 64 KB of each command's, in its profile's `close-commands` folder.
- **After a close command starts,** Hive no longer guards the workspace: if you open it again while the command still runs, the two share its files. A backup that must not see changes should copy the database with SQLite's backup or take a snapshot, or you wait for it before reopening.

## Keyboard shortcuts <a id="keyboard-shortcuts"></a>

| Action | Shortcut |
|---|---|
| Command palette | Ctrl+Shift+P |
| Go to project | Ctrl+P |
| Settings | Ctrl+, |
| New window | Ctrl+K Ctrl+N |
| Close window | Ctrl+Shift+W |
| New session / resume / stop | Ctrl+Shift+N / Ctrl+Shift+R / Ctrl+Shift+X |
| Compact the conversation | Ctrl+Alt+C |
| Switch permission mode | Ctrl+Alt+M (Shift+Tab inside the terminal) |
| Add agent | Ctrl+Alt+Shift+N |
| Focus agent 1–9 / next / previous | Ctrl+1 … Ctrl+9 / Ctrl+Alt+] / Ctrl+Alt+[ |
| Layout: one at a time, two columns, three columns, grid of four, grid of six | Ctrl+Alt+1 … Ctrl+Alt+5 |
| Next / previous agent page | Ctrl+Alt+PageDown / Ctrl+Alt+PageUp |
| Focus the session terminal | Ctrl+` |
| Next / previous project | Ctrl+PageDown / Ctrl+PageUp |
| Project tabs | Alt+1 … Alt+8 in the order they show (Session, Overview, Performance, Tasks, Sessions, Files, Attachments, Git), Alt+0 for Project Settings (not a tab); Memory, Skills, Templates and MCP (in **More ▾**) have none, but you can give them one; Ctrl+Tab / Ctrl+Shift+Tab for the next / previous tab, More ▾'s included |
| Task board / new card | Ctrl+Shift+J / Ctrl+Alt+T |
| Workspace Overview | Ctrl+Shift+O |
| Toggle sidebar | Ctrl+B (outside the terminal) |
| Compact / expand the project list | Ctrl+Alt+B |
| Notifications | Ctrl+Alt+U |
| Show / hide the Hive Assistant | Ctrl+Alt+I |
| Show / fold the Progress panel | Ctrl+Alt+P |
| Developer console (with Show developer features on) | Ctrl+Alt+J |
| External terminal in the project | Ctrl+Shift+` |
| Documentation | F1 |

In the terminal, Ctrl+C copies when text is selected (otherwise it interrupts the agent) and Ctrl+V pastes. Right-click copies the selection, or pastes when nothing is selected, in every agent's pane. **In GitHub Copilot panes, Shift+drag to select**: Copilot handles the mouse itself, so a plain drag makes Copilot's own selection, which Ctrl+C copies ("copied to clipboard") but right-click doesn't. Copilot's footer says *right-click copy*; in Hive, right-click is Hive's, as in every pane. To give it to Copilot instead, turn on **Pass right-click to Copilot** under GitHub Copilot in **Settings → Advanced**: right-click then copies Copilot's own selection, and no longer pastes. An agent's CLI can only put text on the clipboard just after you press a key or click in its pane, and can never read it; **A CLI putting text on the clipboard** under Hive in **Settings → Advanced** can have Hive ask you first, or take none. **All keys go to the CLI** there gives every key but the command palette's to the CLI. To give Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V to the CLI itself, as in any other terminal, turn on **Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V go to the CLI** under Hive in **Settings → Advanced** ([Hive's changes to the CLIs](#hives-changes-to-the-clis)): right-click still copies and pastes. See **Help → Keyboard Shortcuts** for the full list.

**Changing shortcuts.** **Settings → Keyboard Shortcuts** lists the commands on tabs: **All**, then one per category (**View**, **File**, **Project**, **Session**…), each with how many commands it has, and it opens on the tab you last chose. Click a column's header to sort by it, or pick where a shortcut is **Set**, above the table, to see only those (the tabs choose the category); in a narrow window it stays in view while the table scrolls sideways. Searching shows **All**, and each tab's count becomes its matches: click one to see only its matches (until you change the search, which shows **All** again), and clearing the search goes back to the tab you had. Click the pencil (or double-click a shortcut) and press the keys you want; press a second combination straight after for a chord such as Ctrl+K Ctrl+S. You can remove a shortcut, reset one, or reset them all. Hive warns when a shortcut is already used, and won't take keys you need for typing and editing (a key without Ctrl or Alt, Ctrl+C/V/X/A/Z/Y, Shift+Tab). A project can have its own shortcuts for project and session commands in **Project Settings → Keyboard Shortcuts**; they apply while that project is selected.

**Open a file from the terminal.** File paths an agent prints, like `src/main/app.ts:42`, are links: hover one to see it underlined, and **Ctrl+click** it to open the file in the project's [Files](#files) tab with the cursor on that line. A worktree agent's paths open in its worktree, and a path into another project in the workspace opens in that project. Only files that exist are linked; a plain click still just selects text. Paths with spaces are linked too, most reliably when they're in quotes (`"docs/my notes.md"`).

**Open a card from the terminal.** Card numbers an agent or the Assistant prints, like `#12` in "#12 is back in Review", are links to the cards on the [task board](#task-board): hover one to see it underlined, with the card's title and column, and **Ctrl+click** it to open the card. Archived cards open too. Only numbers of cards on this workspace's board are linked, so an issue number from elsewhere, a colour like `#fff` or a `#` in a web address isn't.

**Terminals out of sight.** While an agent's terminal isn't shown (Settings is open, or you're on another tab, project or page), it keeps its size: the agent goes on drawing for the width you last saw. When you come back, the terminal fits its pane again once the window has settled, and the agent redraws only if the pane's size changed meanwhile. If you were following the output at the bottom, you're still at the bottom; if you had scrolled up to read, you're still on the same line.

**Screenshots and files.** With a screenshot on the clipboard (for example from Win+Shift+S), press **Ctrl+V** in a session: Hive saves the image in the session's `.hive/attachments` folder and pastes its path, and Claude Code attaches it as `[Image #1]`. You can also **drag files** from Explorer onto the terminal: any kind of file, its path pasted, copied into `.hive/attachments` first when it comes from outside the agent's folder or is an image. They're kept per session, so you can always see what was sent (see [Attachments](#attachments)).

## Troubleshooting <a id="troubleshooting"></a>

- **"… was damaged and has been restored"** — one of Hive's settings or record files couldn't be read (for example after a hand edit). Hive went back to its last good copy (`.bak`) and kept the damaged file next to it as `.corrupt-<date>`.
- **"… is being saved by something else: nothing was changed"** — another program (an editor, a sync tool) was still writing one of Hive's files, such as a project's `project.json`, when Hive went to change it (or saved it within the last couple of seconds in a form Hive can't read yet). Hive waited a few seconds and then left the file alone rather than write over that save. Once the other program has finished saving, try again.
- **"… couldn't be read: nothing was changed"** — one of Hive's files, such as a project's `project.json`, is there but couldn't be read when Hive went to change it: another program (an antivirus scan, a backup or sync tool) had it locked, or the disk reported an error. Hive tried again for a few seconds and then left the file and its `.bak` copy as they were, rather than save over settings it couldn't see. Try again in a moment; if it keeps happening, check what has the file open. Until Hive can read such a file again, it doesn't save it.
- **"Claude Code is required" / "Codex is required"** — install the CLI from **Help → Provider Setup…**, or set its path in the provider's settings page. Having the VS Code extension isn't enough; Hive needs the standalone CLI.
- **"… is turned off"** — turn the provider on in **Settings → Providers**.
- **"Git isn't installed (or isn't on Hive's PATH)"**, or **Git** in red in the status bar — install Git for Windows 2.38 or later, or add the folder holding `git.exe` to your PATH, then **Check again** in Provider Setup: see [Git](#git).
- **Agents show Needs sign-in** — their CLI's sign-in has expired: see [When a sign-in expires](#when-a-sign-in-expires).
- **Codex asks before every command** — its Windows sandbox isn't set up: **Help → Provider Setup… → Codex → Set up**.
- **An agent couldn't start** — when its CLI quits before it has started (a setting it refuses, a broken install), the bar under its terminal turns red with **Couldn't start:** and what the CLI said, a hint where Hive recognises the problem, **Retry** and **Agent Settings…** (or **Provider Setup…**). Its header's mark turns to stalled (a square), the attention inbox lists it, and if its pane isn't on screen a notification says so. Fix the setting, then Retry; the terminal above keeps everything the CLI printed.
- **Status dots don't change** — status comes from the CLI's hooks. A Codex agent shows **Ready** once its prompt appears, and reports its session with your first message. A Claude Code agent shown **Working…** at an empty prompt is idle again a minute after its turn, when Claude Code says it is waiting for input; while Claude Code compacts by itself between turns, the agent shows **Compacting the conversation…** and then **Ready** (once the transcript shows the compaction, or after 10 minutes at most: Claude Code may prepare a compaction in the background and only say it's done when it uses it). An agent whose turn ended without Hive hearing of it is finished again once its CLI's records or its screen show the end; failing that it shows **Silent n min · state unknown**, with **Mark idle**. Each hook Hive receives is logged in `hooks.log` in the logs folder. Restart the session; if it persists, check **Help → Open Logs Folder**.
- **Agent API port in use** — change the port in Settings → Agent API.
- **Hive doesn't open, or says "Hive can't start"** — Hive's windows run sandboxed. If the folder Hive is installed in has a permission entry for a Windows app package but none for **ALL APPLICATION PACKAGES**, Windows won't let them read it, and Hive can't open. The installer and updates add that entry, and Hive adds it at start when it can. When it can't (a folder you don't own), Hive shows the command to run, with **Copy Command**: run it in PowerShell (as an administrator if needed), then start Hive again. For the usual install folder, in PowerShell: `icacls "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Programs\Hive" /grant '*S-1-15-2-1:(OI)(CI)(RX)'`.
- **"Hive's window stopped and was reloaded"** — the window's page crashed (memory, a graphics driver, a bug). Your agents run outside it, so they kept going: Hive reloaded the window, which reconnects to their terminals as **View → Reload** does. Unsaved edits in the Files tab are lost, and the note says how many. If it crashes again within a minute, Hive asks instead: **Reload**, **Open Logs** or **Quit Hive**. If the window stops responding, after a few seconds Hive offers **Wait** or **Reload**, and the question goes away if the window recovers.
- **The window is blank, flickers or crashes (a graphics driver)** — turn on **Settings → Advanced → Disable hardware acceleration** and click **Restart Hive**: Hive then draws without the graphics card, and its terminals without WebGL. If the window doesn't draw at all, quit Hive (from its tray icon) and start it once with `--disable-gpu`, for example from **Run** (Win+R): `"%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\Hive\Hive.exe" --disable-gpu`; then turn the setting on. **Help → Copy Diagnostics** says whether hardware acceleration is on, and why not.
- **"This tab ran into a problem"** — something in that view failed, for example on an unusual file. Your sessions keep running. Click **Try Again** or switch to another tab; if it keeps happening, **Open Logs** has the details for a bug report.
- **Hive closed by itself** — Hive writes how it ends to its log (**Help → Open Logs**, `hive.log`): a normal quit, an update, Windows shutting down or signing out, the PC going to sleep, and errors nothing caught. When Hive, its window or one of its helper processes crashes, Windows keeps a crash dump of it in `%APPDATA%\Hive\Crashpad\reports` (a `.dmp` file). Hive keeps the newest three and removes older ones as it starts. Dumps stay on your PC: Hive never sends them. **Help → Copy Diagnostics…** says how many there are and names the newest; if you report the crash, attach that file to the issue yourself. A log that just stops, with no crash dump, means something ended Hive from outside (or the PC lost power). Either way, the next start tells you: see [When Hive closed unexpectedly](#when-hive-closed-unexpectedly).
- **Hive closed running out of memory** — Hive's log notes how much memory it uses at start, every five minutes and as it closes, and warns when it passes 70% and 85% of its limit; **Help → Copy Diagnostics…** shows the latest of these lines. To find out what filled it, turn on **Settings → Advanced → Save a memory snapshot if Hive is about to run out of memory** and click **Restart Hive**: the next time Hive is about to run out, it writes a snapshot to `%APPDATA%\Hive\heap` before it closes, and keeps the newest two. A snapshot can be several gigabytes and holds whatever Hive had in memory (card text, transcripts, possibly tokens): it stays on your PC, so look before you share one, and turn the setting off again once you have one.
- **Hive pauses, or typing lags** — Hive's log (**Help → Open Logs**, `hive.log`) notes when its main process was busy for more than a quarter of a second (at most once a minute), with what was running then (an action of the window, an Agent API request, a hook from an agent, or a step that waits for the disk, such as saving the board or writing the log) and how long it took. What it lists ran at the same time as the pause; it may not be what caused it. Include those lines when you report it. A pause while the PC was asleep, or while all of Hive was paused, isn't counted. Pauses of several seconds with nothing listed can come from Windows making a System Restore point (it holds writes to the drive for a few seconds). The log also notes when one of Hive's file calls took more than a quarter of a second (*A file call took … to end*), waiting for a free thread or for the disk: windows that open or list slowly while another window's agents are busy show it.
- **Reporting a bug** — **Help → Copy Diagnostics…** shows a summary for a bug report: Hive's and Windows' versions, your coding agents, counts of projects and agents, the settings that change how Hive behaves, whether each of Hive's worker threads is running on its own thread (work there doesn't slow your typing), and the last 50 lines of its log. Your folders, workspace, project and agent names (also those of workspaces you opened before), card titles, what you asked agents to do, and anything that looks like a key or token are taken out, and the dialog shows exactly what will be copied. Click **Copy** and paste it into the report.
- **Where is my data?** — app settings in `%APPDATA%\Hive` (the folder kept the name Hive when the app became Hive Desktop), workspace data in `Workspace/.hive`, project data in `Project/.hive`.
- **How many sessions can run?** — as many as your machine can handle, across any number of projects. Each is a CLI process; Hive only draws the terminals you can see with the graphics card, so dozens of background sessions don't slow the window down.

It includes open-source libraries (xterm.js, Monaco, React and others) under their own permissive licences, listed with their full texts in [Third-Party Notices](../THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md); both are in the Docs view and linked from **Help → About Hive Desktop**. Electron's and Chromium's licences are in the folder Hive is installed in (`LICENSES.chromium.html`). Hive Desktop is independently developed and isn't affiliated with, endorsed or sponsored by Anthropic, OpenAI or GitHub. Claude and Claude Code are products of Anthropic; Codex and ChatGPT of OpenAI; GitHub Copilot and the Copilot CLI of GitHub. Each CLI is installed separately under its maker's terms. The provider badges beside their names are Hive's own, not the providers' logos.
